## I1 Introduction

## Gila Neta1, David A. Chambers1, Aaron Carroll2

## 1National Cancer Institute, Rockville, MD, USA; 2AcademyHealth, Washington, DC, USA

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):I1
In the 18th year of the Annual Conference on the Science of Dissemination and Implementation in Health, co-hosted by the National Institutes of Health (NIH) and AcademyHealth, we embraced this milestone year of entering “adulthood” through the conference theme Realizing the Benefits of Dissemination & Implementation Science. This year’s theme addressed our existing and future efforts to maximize the benefits of D&I science through plenary sessions highlighting stories of impact and strategies to manage ongoing change.
This year’s conference had the largest turnout in the event’s history, with nearly 1600 registrants. In collaboration with our co-sponsor, the Patient Centered Outcomes Research Institute (PCORI), the conference convened researchers, practitioners, and other partners from across the United States and globally in National Harbor, MD on December 14–17, 2025.
Plenary sessions included two memorable keynote speakers from outside of the D&I research community. John Green, author of Everything is Tuberculosis and The Fault in Our Stars, opened the conference with a powerful story of impact in addressing the tuberculosis epidemic. Vic Strecher, Professor of Health Behavior and Health Equity at the University of Michigan School of Public Health, closed the second day with a thoughtful reflection on the value of purpose to guide dissemination and implementation strategies. New this year, we created an open call for submissions on impact of D&I Science to be included in a plenary session. The resulting panel, Realizing the Benefits of D&I Science: Stories of Impact, featured eight short stories describing how D&I science led to measurable and sustained health and service delivery improvements, economic or payment reforms, or policy change. Stories spanned a range of health interventions, settings, and populations, and explored different types of benefits that can be derived from D&I studies:Increasing the reach of effective addiction health services via an evidence-based implementation strategy (Becker)
From Cath Labs to Rural Hospitals: Implementing the Relational Playbook to Advance Workforce Resilience in the VA (Gilmartin)
From research to action: Harnessing implementation science to move the needle in reducing firearm injury and mortality in the United States (Hoskins)
Integrating NCD and HIV Care in Nigeria: The MAP-IT Study as a Scalable Policy-Aligned Implementation Model (Ogedegbe)
Dynamic Sustainment of Evidence-Based Health Promotion in Behavioral Health Settings (Aschbrenner)
In church, people witness to save souls, in the Witness Project we witness to save lives! Impacting lives for 36 years (Erwin)
Spreading Healthcare Access, Activities, Research and Knowledge (SHAARK): A Decade of Partnership between Implementation Scientists and Operational Leaders to Support the National VA Learning Health System (Jackson)
Population Health Impact of Incorporating Lung Cancer Screening Education in State Tobacco Quitlines (Lowenstein)

Finally, the plenary panel, Safeguarding the Benefits of D&I Science: Strategies to Manage Ongoing Change, wrapped up the conference with a discussion exploring strategies to buffer from or address derailment of D&I studies. Speakers addressed ways to maximize design flexibility, capture context, and better anticipate and respond to unexpected events during the conduct of D&I studies.
Conference participants included researchers, practitioners, and other partners worldwide, including 11 low- and middle-income countries (LMICs) in sub-Saharan Africa, Latin America, and Asia. We were pleased to welcome 156 trainees, five patient scholarship recipients, and 13 presenters from LMICs, whose broad range of perspectives added greatly to conference discourse. We were able to award 11 travel scholarships to various participants globally and domestically included in the conference program.
In addition to plenary sessions, the conference included concurrent podium and poster sessions, discussion forums, and multiple networking events. The call for abstracts generated 1111 submissions, including individual paper presentations, posters, and panel presentations spread across nine thematic tracks. Those tracks provide an organizational structure for this supplement, which includes 166 abstracts from the concurrent paper and panel sessions, representing a variety of dissemination and implementation research funded by our conference sponsors and other agencies, organizations, and systems. The additional 739 abstracts from the poster sessions are not included here but can be viewed at https://academyhealth.confex.com/academyhealth/2025di/meetingapp.cgi/ModulePosterSessions/0.
Six pre-conference workshops, with 392 total registrants, featured topics including methods for implementation strategies optimization, artificial intelligence, implementation mapping, the use of CFIR, and planning for sustainment. NIH staff also hosted a pre-conference workshop on navigating D&I grant submissions. The conference also featured a pre-recorded orientation to D&I with live Q&A for those new to the field, an evening musical gathering based on attendee song requests, a “first timers” session, fun run, poster walks, meet the editors, ancillary meetings, and daily morning coffee chats with D&I experts facilitating open discussions about key priorities for the field. The networking sessions were hugely popular and well attended, allowing attendees to connect with the leaders in the field.
We look forward to welcoming attendees to the 19th Annual D&I Science conference on December 13-16, 2026, at the Gaylord Convention Center in National Harbor, MD.

## Behavioral health

## S1 Testing multi-level implementation strategies for two evidence-based autism interventions in children’s mental health services and schools: outcomes and mechanisms of a statewide hybrid type 3 trial

## Lauren Brookman-Frazee1, Aubyn Stahmer2, Scott Roesch3, Anna Lau4, Elizabeth Rangel5

## 1ImplementatioN Science and Team Effectiveness in Practice (IN STEP) Children’s Mental Health Research Center, San Diego, USA; 2University of California Davis, Sacramento, CA, USA; 3San Diego State University, San Diego, CA, USA; 4University of California, Los Angeles, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 5SDSU/UC San Diego Joint Doctoral Program in Clinical Psychology, San Diego, CA, USA

## Correspondence: Lauren Brookman-Frazee (lbrookman@health.ucsd.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S1

Background: Children with autism are served in multiple public service systems. Evidence-based interventions (EBIs) have been developed, but are not routinely delivered. Identifying effective implementation strategies is critical to ensuring that EBIs reach families and children. The TEAMS project was a cluster randomized hybrid type 3 implementation-effectiveness trial testing the effects of two implementation strategies when paired with AIM HI (An Individualized Mental Health Intervention for Autism) in mental health programs (Study 1) and CPRT (Classroom Pivotal Response Teaching) in classrooms (Study 2). The TEAMS Leadership Institute (TLI) implementation strategy targeted implementation leadership and climate, and TEAMS Individualized Provider Strategy (TIPS) targeted provider motivation and engagement. This presentation reports the effects of the implementation strategies on implementation and child outcomes and on implementation mechanisms.

Methods: Fifty programs/districts across four training cohorts were enrolled. Organizations were randomized to receive a leader-level strategy, provider strategy, both strategies, or neither strategy (EBI provider training only). Leader and provider participants were recruited from enrolled programs/districts and child participants were recruited from providers’ caseloads or classrooms. Data from a total of 387 providers and 385 children were analyzed. Outcomes were assessed over 6 months. Provider outcomes included provider EBI certification and observed EBI fidelity. Clinical outcomes were standardized caregiver report measures (ECBI, PDD-BI). Mechanisms included the Implementation Leadership Scale, Implementation Climate Scale, and the Implementation Support Strategies scale, the Evidence-Based Practice Attitudes Scale, and Provider Motivation for Training Scale.

Findings: TLI was associated with significantly higher EBI fidelity compared to non-TLI (B = 0.37, p = 0.04). Moreover, a statistically significant TLIXTime interaction was found for child outcome T-scores (B = -10.47, p = 0.03), with a significant reduction in T-scores across time only for those in the TLI condition. TLI engaged implementation climate, but not implementation leadership. Additionally, TLI was associated with significantly greater use of implementation support strategies used by programs and school districts. TIPS did not engage the hypothesized mechanism of provider motivation for training, actual training engagement, or general EBP attitudes.

Implications for D&I Research: Findings support the effectiveness of leader focused strategies to promote implementation and clinical outcomes of autism EBIs in multiple public service systems and for multiple EBIs.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S2 Implementation under pressure: how motivation and context shape EBP delivery in community settings during a multisite learning collaborative

## Sean Snyder, Michaela Harris, Vanessa Liang, Jessica Fitts, Robert Franks

## The Baker Center for Children and Families, Boston, MA, USA

## Correspondence: Sean Snyder (seanesnyder@gmail.com)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S2

Background

Implementation readiness and capacity are critical but often underexamined determinants of successful uptake of evidence-based practices (EBPs) in behavioral health settings. This study presents findings from a mixed-methods evaluation of a 16-month learning collaborative supporting five community mental health agencies implementing the Modular Approach to Therapy for Children with Anxiety, Depression, Trauma, or Conduct Problems (MATCH). The study aimed to examine changes in agency-level readiness and capacity over time and identify contextual factors shaping those trajectories.

Methods

A repeated-measures design was used to assess readiness and capacity at four time points using the Readiness Thinking Tool. These data were triangulated with field-based qualitative observations coded using the Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research (CFIR) observation tool, offering a dynamic view of contextual factors influencing implementation. We also analyzed site-specific implementation outcomes, including number of clinicians trained, EBP sessions delivered, and clients reached.

Findings

Across all five sites, general capacity declined over the implementation period. However, four sites showed increased motivation, suggesting sustained commitment despite resource challenges. End scores for capacity and motivation were mixed: three sites ended with below-average capacity, while two maintained higher levels; motivation was high at endpoint in three sites and low in two. The number of clinicians trained ranged from 8 to 19 per site, with average MATCH sessions delivered per clinician ranging from 39 to 130. Client exposure to the intervention ranged from 7 to 13 sessions on average. Even among lower-capacity sites, MATCH was implemented, though volume and consistency varied.
Qualitative findings revealed that motivation may buffer the negative impact of declining capacity. Sites with strong internal processes and team cohesion were more likely to sustain training and service delivery. Conversely, external instability and staff turnover disrupted implementation.

Implications for D&I Research: These findings underscore the need to tailor implementation support based on each site’s evolving readiness, internal dynamics, and external pressures. Motivation emerged as a potential protective factor in sustaining implementation, even in the context of declining infrastructure. Monitoring both agency capacity and motivation over time, alongside contextual observations, may help inform run-time implementation strategies and improve implementation outcomes in real-world settings.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S3 Individualized quality improvement interventions catalyze improvements in women’s behavioral healthcare delivery across primary care settings with varying behavioral healthcare infrastructure

## Busra Ceviren1, Melissa Leonard1, Seuli Bose Brill2, Rachel Gordon2, Lisa Christian2, Bethany Panchal2, Colleen Waickman2, Alicia Rooney1, Abigail Ezzeldin1, Wendy Groznik1

## 1The Ohio Colleges of Medicine Government Resource Center, Columbus, OH, USA; 2The Ohio State University Wexner Medical Center, Columbus, OH, USA

## Correspondence: Busra Ceviren (busra.ceviren@osumc.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S3

Background

Focus on ME: Mental Health is Essential Health aims to improve care related to anxiety and depression among women of reproductive age (WRA) in primary care (PC) settings across Ohio through a statewide quality improvement (QI) learning collaborative. The collaborative evaluated the differential effect of behavioral health (BH) evidence-based quality improvement (EBQI) between statewide PC practices with and without integrated BH.

Methods

The Focus on ME collaborative, using Institute for Healthcare Improvement methodology, engaged multidisciplinary PC teams in structured QI activities to enhance anxiety and depression care for women aged 18–44. Twenty PC sites participated in phase 2, receiving an evidence-based toolkit developed by experts with a workflow for screening and recommended interventions based on symptom severity. QI activities included site-level QI coaching informed by an interactive data dashboard to guide intervention testing and monthly webinars with peer learning.

Findings

Of the twenty participating sites, 9 had integrated BH services and 11 did not. The project included 17,641 unique patients across baseline and implementation periods. Of these, 43.8% were Medicaid beneficiaries, 23.4% non-Hispanic Black, 47.3% non-Hispanic White, and 16.5% Hispanic/Latina.
During baseline, sites without integrated BH care had significantly lower anxiety and depression screening and appropriate care rates compared to those with integrated BH care (p < 0.001). In the implementation period, screening rates improved at both site types, with greater gains at sites without integrated care (24.2% for anxiety, 7.8% for depression) than integrated care sites (13.5% and 2.4%). The initiative helped close the gap by raising screening and care rates at non-integrated sites to levels comparable with integrated care sites.

Implications for D&I Research

Integrated BH in primary care remains an evidence-based model, but most U.S practices have not adopted it due to policy, payment, and workforce barriers. Findings suggest that context-tailored QI methodology can improve BH screening and treatment across sites with varied capacity. For certain measures, this approach can help practices with limited integration approximate metrics achieved by fully integrated environments. Personalizing QI interventions to local context can catalyze change across diverse practices.

Primary Funding Source: Ohio Department of Medicaid and Ohio Department of Children and Youth

## S4 Comparing the impacts of a base versus enhanced implementation bundle on fidelity to a complex evidence-based practice

## Sonya Gabrielian1,2, Kristina Cordasco2,3, Camilla Cummings2, Gracielle Tan4, Maharshi Rawal2, Lisa Edwards5, Edwin Jacobo6, Erin Finley5,7

## 1University of California, Los Angeles David Geffen School of Medicine, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 2Veterans Health Administration, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 3David Geffen School of Medicine, University of California, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 4Central Occupational Medicine Providers, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 5VA Greater Los Angeles Healthcare System, Center for the Study of Healthcare Innovation, Implementation and Policy (CSHIIP), Los Angeles, CA, USA; 6UCLA/VA Center of Excellence on Veteran Resilience and Recovery, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 7Long School of Medicine, University of Texas Health Science Center, San Antonio, TX, USA

## Correspondence: Sonya Gabrielian (sonya.gabrielian@va.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S4

Background

Implementation of Critical Time Intervention (CTI)—a complex, evidence-based, and time-limited case management practice for persons with behavioral health disorders—can substantively improve housing stability among homelessness-experienced adults. We conducted a hybrid type 3 implementation-effectiveness trial to compare two strategies for supporting CTI implementation in 32 community-based agencies that serve homeless-experienced Veterans. As fidelity to CTI influences its effectiveness, we examined the impacts of these strategies on CTI fidelity.

Methods

We used the Replicating Effective Programs (REP) implementation bundle to support all 32 agencies to achieve fidelity to CTI, while accommodating agency-level adaptations to fit local contexts. Half (n = 16) the agencies also received 9 months of external facilitation (“enhanced REP [E-REP]”), i.e., tailored supports to adopt CTI in routine care processes. To compare the impacts of REP versus E-REP on CTI fidelity, we measured agency-level CTI fidelity 12 months after REP initiation. Specifically, we assessed adherence to CTI’s core components over the prior 6 months by integrating data from: a CTI implementation self-assessment completed by implementing providers; 90-min videoconferences with providers to review clinical documentation of two “exemplar cases”; and field notes from provider narratives during these videoconferences. Each agency was classified as having adequate versus inadequate CTI fidelity (adherent to ≥ 5 of 9 core CTI components).

Findings

Over half (59.4%, 19/32) of agencies agreed to participate in fidelity assessments. Of these agencies, 10 (52.6%) received REP and 9 (47.3%) received E-REP. We were unable to assess fidelity for 1 of the 9 E-REP agencies due to recent provider turnover and inability to review exemplar cases. Agencies that received REP alone were less likely than agencies that received E-REP (50.0%, 5/10; 87.5%, 7/8, respectively) to have adequate CTI fidelity.

Implications for D&I Research

Strategies that initiate deliberate and interactive problem-solving to support evidence-based practice implementation (e.g., external facilitation) may improve practice fidelity and ultimately patient outcomes. In this study, though E-REP was a higher intensity approach to support CTI implementation, external facilitation may have improved practice fidelity by catalyzing provider and organizational changes. Tailored and flexible supports may be useful to address site-level challenges in implementing complex behavioral health interventions with high fidelity.

Primary Funding Source: Department of Veterans Affairs

## S5 Implementing Written Exposure Therapy (WET) for PTSD in underserved primary care settings: a matrixed multiple case study

## Ariella Korn1, Chloe Gomez2, Andrea Cassells3, Tameir Holder3, T.J. Lin3, Mishael Lopez3, Jonathan Tobin3,4, Stephanie Williamson2, Eunice Wong2, Brian Marx5,6, Denise Sloan5,6, Lisa Meredith2

## 1RAND, Boston, MA, USA; 2RAND, Santa Monica, CA, USA; 3Clinical Directors Network, Inc. (CDN), New York, NY, USA; 4The Rockefeller University Center for Clinical and Translational Science, New York, NY, USA; 5Veterans Health Administration, Boston, MA, USA; 6Boston University School of Medicine, Boston, MA, USA

## Correspondence: Ariella Korn (akorn@rand.org)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S5

Background: Access to evidence-based treatment for PTSD in underserved primary care settings needs improvement. Written Exposure Therapy (WET) is a promising evidence-based treatment because it is brief (six weekly sessions), easy for therapists to learn and deliver with high fidelity, and is well-tolerated by patients. However, understanding WET implementation in primary care is limited. We examined variation in the implementation of WET for PTSD in Federally Qualified Health Centers (FQHCs) within the context of collaborative care, a team-based and measurement-based health care delivery model.

Methods: We used a multiple case study approach with data from the Building Experience for Treating Trauma and Enhancing Resilience (BETTER) study: a hybrid type 1 cluster RCT that randomized 12 FQHCs to collaborative care alone or collaborative care plus access to WET. Patients (n = 722) were enrolled in three waves between 2022–2025. Guided by RE-AIM and CFIR, we analyzed data from the study’s care management registry, surveys, and interviews to identify patterns in implementation success across the six WET sites.

Findings: Patient WET completion rates were low-to-moderate across sites (range 19–42%), with no differences by delivery mode (telehealth, in-person, hybrid). The site with greatest treatment completion had highest scores for health center leadership engagement. The FQHC with lowest treatment completion had low scores for learning climate, prioritization of patient needs, and available resources, relative to other sites. Median treatment duration was similar across five of six sites (range 6.3–7.1 weeks), reflecting high fidelity to WET protocol timeframes. The sixth FQHC, with longer treatment duration (median 8 weeks), had the fewest therapists and mostly delivered WET in person. Across sites, patients who completed WET had clinically significant reductions in PTSD symptom severity from baseline to after the last session, as assessed during treatment with the PTSD Checklist for DSM-5 (PCL-5). Staff reported implementation barriers at the patient-level (e.g., unmet social needs), provider-level (e.g., discomfort delivering exposure-based therapy), and systems-level (e.g., limited resources, staffing capacity).

Implications for D&I Research: Findings suggest that WET implementation success within FQHC collaborative care is contingent on patient engagement throughout the course of treatment. Effective strategies are needed to mitigate multi-level barriers to PTSD treatment among the primary care population.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S6 SAFER: development and initial implementation of a patient decision aid for firearms lethal means safety planning

## Jeffrey Smith1, Michelle Mengeling2, Brian Cook3, James Torner3, Mark Vander Weg3, Alison Hamilton4, Jonathan Platt3, Amanda Heeren5, Lindsey Fuhrmeister5, Anne Sadler5

## 1Veterans Health Administration, Little Rock, AR, USA; 2CADRE (152), Suite 42–260, Veterans Health Administration, Iowa City, IA, USA; 3University of Iowa, Iowa City, IA, USA; 4Veterans Health Administration, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 5Veterans Health Administration, Iowa City, IA, USA

## Correspondence: Jeffrey Smith (Jeffrey.Smith6@va.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S6

Background: Firearms are the leading method of suicide death in women Veterans, and the firearm suicide rate among women Veterans is nearly 1.5 times higher than that of non-Veteran women. Development and implementation of interventions tailored to reduce firearm suicide risk among women Veterans are under-studied. This presentation describes development and initial implementation of a patient decision aid (PtDA) to support firearms lethal means safety planning (an evidence-based suicide prevention strategy) for women Veterans.

Methods: Utilizing a 3-phase, iterative co-design process involving 60 women Veterans and 26 providers, we developed a PtDA and complementary resources (toolkit, implementation playbooks) to promote safe firearm storage and suicide prevention. The tools were designed to provide Veterans with information tailored to women to support informed safety choices and to facilitate Shared Decision-Making (SDM) between Veterans and providers. Mixed-methods survey data on the PtDA’s acceptability and utility were collected from Veterans and providers. Women Veterans living in rural areas were over-sampled as they represent a high-risk population for firearm suicide.

Findings: Women Veterans reported high satisfaction with the PtDA’s information, tone, and preventive approach. Most (70%) indicated that SDM with a provider made them more likely to identify and act on a safety plan than if they had received the PtDA alone. Nearly all (95%) agreed that providers should routinely use SDM when discussing firearm safety and suicide risk. All would recommend SDM to other Veterans, and 70% indicated their safety plan made them feel safer. Among providers, all reported that the PtDA improves women Veterans’ knowledge about firearm suicide risk, and three-fourths reported the PtDA decreases barriers for providers in discussing firearms and suicide risk with women Veterans.

Implications for D&I Research: D&I science emphasizes the importance of end-user engagement. This work produced a women Veteran-tailored PtDA for firearms safety planning that Veterans and providers find usable and beneficial, demonstrating feasibility for framing/structuring firearm suicide risk discussions and safety planning in ways that are acceptable to both Veterans and providers. Further work is needed to scale-up use of the PtDA to assess its potential for broader impact in promoting firearms lethal means safety planning.

Primary Funding Source: Department of Veterans Affairs

## S7 Evaluating the adoption, sustainment, and clinical effectiveness of a brief counseling intervention for patients experiencing intimate partner violence in VHA

## Katherine Iverson1, Kelly Stolzmann1, Tara Brunswick1, Erin Mangan1, Abigail Krout2, LeAnn Bruce2, Julianne Brady1

## 1Veterans Health Administration, Boston, MA, USA; 2Veterans Health Administration, Washington, DC, USA

## Correspondence: Katherine Iverson (katherine.iverson@va.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S7

Background: Routine screening for intimate partner violence (IPV) is required for reproductive-aged women and is recommended for other patient groups in the Veterans Health Administration (VHA). VHA facilities are also required to offer trauma-informed services for patients experiencing IPV. Recovering from IPV through Strengths and Empowerment (RISE) is an evidence-based brief counseling intervention for individuals experiencing IPV that is being implemented through a national training and implementation support program in VHA.

Methods: An IRB-approved program evaluation of VHA’s national implementation of RISE is ongoing. The program employs a train-the-trainer model with implementation support via external facilitation. Training consists of a one-day virtual training and six months of clinical consultation with a regional trainer, during which time clinicians complete three cases with fidelity to become certified. Drop-in clinical consultation, one-on-one implementation support meetings, and advanced training workshops with CEUs are offered routinely by the training and implementation support program. During certification, clinicians complete a program evaluation summary following each case to report patient outcomes. An annual survey is distributed to certified clinicians (89% response rate) to assess the use of RISE over time and barriers/facilitators to implementation. We used these program evaluation data sources to examine the adoption and sustainment of RISE (June 2021- September 2024), as well as health outcomes of paramount importance to patients experiencing IPV: self-efficacy (GSS) and depression (CES-D).

Findings: 415 clinicians have been certified in RISE, with 90% of VHA Medical Centers having at least 1 certified clinician onsite. The majority (93%) of clinicians report sustained delivery of RISE at least one year post-certification. Additionally, RISE improves patient outcomes in routine care, with significant and clinically meaningful improvements in self-efficacy [t(704) = 19.44, p < 0.0001, d = 0.73] and depressive symptoms [t(582) = -15.02, p < 0.0001, d = 0.62] from the first to the last RISE session. Program evaluation data indicate that witnessing the positive impacts of RISE on patient well-being, along with regular use of implementation supports (e.g., attending drop-in consultation), facilitates sustainment.

Implications for D&I Research: Developing a strong, multifaceted training program for an IPV-focused intervention with ongoing implementation support for clinicians can achieve adoption and sustainment of RISE, resulting in improved outcomes for individuals experiencing IPV.

Primary Funding Source: Department of Veterans Affairs

## S8 Predictors of workforce turnover in wraparound care coordination: implications for implementation

## Jonathan Olson1, Tony Bonadio2, Heidi Melz3, Jessica Lim2, Grace Metz2

## 1University of Washington, Seattle, WA, USA; 2University of Connecticut, Hartford, CT, USA; 3James Bell Associates, Arlington, VA, USA

## Correspondence: Jonathan Olson (jro10@uw.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S8

Background

Current implementation science models underscore the importance of a trained and dedicated workforce to support the implementation of evidence-based practices within behavioral health systems. However, recent data suggest that turnover rates within this group are high, averaging over 30% across different segments of the workforce. Such turnover results in significant challenges within provider organizations that can lead to poor implementation outcomes and decreased service availability.

Methods

We examined workforce turnover within provider organizations that implement Wraparound Care Coordination, an intensive intervention for youth with complex behavioral health needs. We surveyed 210 care coordinators and 67 administrators from 65 provider agencies within 12 states in the United States. Questions focused on organization-level turnover rates, perceived predictors of turnover, organizational climate, and impact of turnover on practice outcomes.

Findings

Results suggest an average turnover rate of 30% among Wraparound providers, with about a third of current employees reporting that they are actively considering new employment. Approximately 30% of administrators reported that their agencies are chronically understaffed, and that this has had a negative impact on workload, administrative costs, morale, and quality of services. Direct service providers reported that commitment to the families they serve and dedication to Wraparound’s mission were more important reasons for staying in their jobs than salary, even though average salaries for this group were low (M = $39,000). Results of a hierarchical linear model were consistent with such reports. Statistically significant individual-level predictors of turnover behaviors included perceptions of an unsupportive organizational climate (b = -0.324, t(108) = -3.66, p < 0.001) and job-related stress (b = 0.289, t(108) = 4.63, p < 0.001); and a significant organization-level predictor was being understaffed (b = -0.340, t(36) = -2.93, p = 0.006). Adjusted salary was unrelated to the outcome (b = 0.00, t(40) = -1.04, p = 0.304).

Implications for D&I Research

Together, these findings suggest that turnover rates among Wraparound providers are similar to those in other behavioral health fields. These high rates have a negative impact on staffing, morale, and service provision. Characteristics of the organization and job stress appear to be more important predictors of turnover than salary. Such findings underscore the importance of providing a supportive workplace to attract and retain a talented workforce.

## S9 Implementation of a peer-support suicide prevention program in the United States Air Force

## William Bevens1,2, Nicole Stadnick1,2,3, Gregory Aarons1,2,3, Bryan Yates4, Anthony Pisani4, Peter Wyman4

## 1University of California, San Diego, La Jolla, CA, USA; 2Child and Adolescent Services Research Center, San Diego, CA, USA; 3University of California, San Diego ACTRI Dissemination and Implementation Science Center, La Jolla, CA, USA; 4University of Rochester Medical Center, Rochester, NY, USA

## Correspondence: William Bevens (wbevens@health.ucsd.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S9

Background: In response to increasing military suicides, the Wingman Guardian Connect (WGC) program was developed through collaboration between the US Department of the Air Force (DAF) and University of Rochester (UR). WGC is a group network enhancement suicide prevention model where facilitators teach Air Force personnel skills for managing career challenges while building group social bonds and cohesion. In preparation for an Air Force-wide scale-up, WGC is being evaluated in an implementation-effectiveness type 1 randomized controlled trial in nine US bases that involves each base transitioning to independence from direct UR oversight. This study explored barriers and facilitators to implementation using the Exploration, Preparation, Implementation, Sustainment (EPIS) framework, and describes the role of UR as a purveyor in supporting base-transitions.

Methods: Periodic reflections were conducted for each base at implementation launch with UR personnel who co-facilitated WGC delivery. Focus group interviews were completed across nine enrolled bases with two groups: 1) enlisted and civilian facilitators; 2) staff and leadership within Integrated Primary Prevention Workforce offices (IPPWs) responsible for managing WGC implementation and sustainment. Interview guides were developed based on EPIS and rapid qualitative analysis was used for analysis. Outer context was defined as departments within the DAF and the inner context were individual bases (base leadership and IPPWs).

Findings: Inner context leadership supported successful transitions through the recruitment and training of local facilitators and promoting schedule flexibility for Air Force personnel to participate in training. IPPWs who reported supportive inner context leadership also described greater independence to enact their mission and demonstrated proactive recruitment, delivery, and expansion of WGC. As purveyors, UR acted as a relational tie by building leadership support in the inner context, creating mission-alignment between outer and inner contexts, and supporting the de-implementation of prior resiliency programs.

Implications for D&I Research: This work described the functions, forms and importance of an academic bridging organization’s activities to engage outer context systems and facilitate their interactions with inner context actors in this multilevel military context. Several factors that influenced implementation process through purveyor relationships were identified. Data collection for this work is expected to be completed by late September 2025.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S10 Seven randomized trials support the effectiveness of the Leadership and Organizational Change for Implementation (LOCI) strategy in diverse national and international contexts

## Gregory Aarons1,2,3,4,5, Karina Egeland6, Ane-Marthe Skar6,7, Mark Ehrhart8, Marisa Sklar1,5,9,10, Nathaniel Williams11, Lauren Brookman-Frazee3,12, Aubyn Stahmer13,14

## 1ImplementatioN Science and Team Effectiveness in Practice (IN STEP) Children's Mental Health Research Center, San Diego, CA, USA; 2Implementation Science, London, United Kingdom; 3Child and Adolescent Services Research Center, San Diego, CA, USA; 4UC San Diego, La Jolla, CA, USA; 5UC San Diego Altman Clinical and Translational Research Institute Dissemination and Implementation Science Center, La Jolla, CA, USA; 6Norwegian Center for Violence and Traumatic Stress Studies, Oslo, Norway; 7Norwegian Institute of Public Health, Oslo, Norway; 8University of Central Florida, Orlando, FL, USA; 9UC San Diego Child and Adolescent Services Research Center, San Diego, CA, USA; 10University of California, San Diego, La Jolla, CA, USA; 11Boise State University, Boise, ID, USA; 12University of California San Diego, La Jolla, CA, USA; 13University of California Davis, Sacramento, CA, USA; 14Mind Institute, Sacramento, CA, USA

## Correspondence: Gregory Aarons (gaarons@health.ucsd.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S10

Background

There is a need for building research evidence for implementation strategies. However, many implementation strategies have not been tested and most lack replications. This presentation will describe multiple replications and adaptations of the Leadership and Organizational Change for Implementation (LOCI) strategy in different contexts and countries. Studies took place in diverse contexts, including community mental health clinics, schools, substance use disorder treatment facilities (multiple US states including California, Arizona, Oregon, Idaho, Nevada), mental health clinics within health trusts (Norway).

Methods

Five randomized trials including cluster randomized and stepped-wedge designs were used in these studies. The LOCI strategy was adapted and tested and provided in some projects by the original LOCI development team (Aarons, Ehrhart, Sklar) and in other studies were translated and delivered by local teams in Norway (Egeland, Skar) that adapted the strategy for language and cultural relevance, and for autism intervention implementation in community mental health and schools (Brookman-Frazee, Stahmer) using a team-based approach with select core components of the strategy. The EBPs implemented included trauma focused treatment for posttraumatic stress disorder (PTSD; Cognitive Therapy for PTSD, Eye Movement Desensitization and Reprocessing (EMDR), Trauma-Focused Cognitive Behavioral Therapy (TFCBT), Motivational Interviewing, Measurement Based Care, Classroom Pivotal Response Training (CPRT), and Individualized Mental Health Intervention for Autism (AIM HI).

Findings

All five studies found positive effects of the LOCI strategy on implementation determinants, mechanisms, and outcomes including implementation leadership, implementation climate, and evidence-based practice reach. Three of the trials also tested clinical outcomes and demonstrated improvement, including reduced problem behaviors and/or improvement in symptoms among youths.

Implications for D&I Research

Assessment of implementation strategy adaptation and replication adds to the rigor of implementation science and advances understanding of core components and nuances of strategies. Effective implementation leadership is critical in creating a positive implementation climate and subsequent implementation outcomes, including reach, fidelity, and patient outcomes. Considering multiple studies in different national and international system and organizational contexts and ways of delivering LOCI advances the science of implementation. Even with these differences, five randomized trials in different service settings in the US and Norway all found positive outcomes.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S11 Readiness assessment as a D&I strategy: informing universal depression screening implementation in schools

## Cynthia Blitz1, David Amiel1, Itzhak Yanovitzky2

## 1Rutgers University, Somerset, NJ, USA; 2Rutgers University School of Communication & Information, New Brunswick, NJ, USA

## Correspondence: David Amiel (david.amiel@rutgers.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S11

Background: Adolescent depression rates have increased dramatically over the past decade, yet screening rates remain low even in schools, where near-universal screening rates can be achieved. In New Jersey, ten school districts were selected to pilot a program designed to implement universal depression screening for adolescents in grades 7–12, with parent consent. In line with D&I principles, we conducted a readiness assessment to understand the acceptability, feasibility, barriers, and facilitators for universal adolescent depression screening prior to the state's rollout.

Methods: This mixed-methods study surveyed school-based mental health professionals (n = 70) and interviewed key informants (n = 15). The survey assessed current practices, perceived feasibility, acceptability, and anticipated barriers; results were analyzed using descriptive statistics, chi-square tests to examine differences across practitioner sub-groups, and a logistic regression model to predict practitioner readiness. Interviews explored implementation requirements and necessary supports and were analyzed using a six-step thematic analysis.

Findings: While 62% of practitioners perceived adolescent depression as common in their schools, fewer than 5% reported routine screening practices. Practitioners strongly endorsed universal screening as feasible (87%), effective for identifying depression (86%), and research-based (82%). However, they anticipated significant barriers: limited capacity for follow-up services (63%), insufficient staffing and resources (61%), lack of standardized instruments (61%), and challenges providing real-time intervention (60%). Fewer than 40% expected parent support. Interviews revealed critical needs for explicit procedural guidance, dedicated funding, staff training, clarity on responsibilities following positive screens, and strategies for engaging parents. Practitioners emphasized that successful implementation would require comprehensive infrastructure support beyond screening mandates.

Implications for D&I Research: This study demonstrates how pre-implementation assessments can maximize the impact of evidence-based interventions by proactively identifying key implementation considerations. Findings provide actionable intelligence for policymakers to address challenges and enable implementers to benefit from practitioner-informed program design. Future D&I research examining if and how such assessments ultimately influence implementation success and sustainability is promising and is underway within this initiative. Such assessments represent a valuable D&I strategy for complex and system-level mental health interventions, enabling proactive resource planning and stakeholder engagement to realize the full public health impact of evidence-based practices.

Primary Funding Source: William T. Grant Foundation

## S12 Characterizing outer contexts in behavioral health using policy mapping

## Genevieve Graaf1, Mimi Choy-Brown2, Abigail Ross3

## 1University of Texas at Arlington, Arlington, TX, USA; 2University of Minnesota, St.Paul, MN, USA; 3University of Pennsylvania, School of Social Policy and Practice, Philadelphia, PA, USA

## Correspondence: Genevieve Graaf (Genevieve.graaf@uta.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S12

Background: Outer context implementation drivers are underspecified in behavioral health settings, yet have significant impacts on uptake and sustainment for empirically-supported interventions (ESIs). The service context of home and community-based services (HCBS) for children and youth (CY) with behavioral health needs involves complex coordination and financing across multiple public sectors, professionals, and service settings—contributing to low uptake of ESIs. Leveraging existing policy and financial infrastructure may increase uptake and sustainability of ESIs. Policy characteristics are enumerated in public documents, but challenges arise in interpreting, codifying, and tracking these policies over time. This study describes the use of policy mapping to systematically identify and describe outer context features that shape behavioral HCBS for CY: 1) financed services 2) minimum provider qualifications, and 3) authorized service settings.

Methods: This longitudinal policy mapping study focused on HCBS Medicaid waiver programs for CY with behavioral health needs. Data was collected from approved state applications filed between 2006 and 2023 (N = 138 documents; K = 19 states). A data extraction tool was developed, piloted, and refined after a line-by-line review of each document. Multi-stage focused coding was guided by two established taxonomies. Matrices organized data by state and year to aid comparison.

Findings: Waiver-funded services and authorized settings varied across states (n = 19) and over time while provider qualifications for each type of service did not. Services offered included case management (n = 19), respite care (n = 19), independent living training (n = 9), wraparound care coordination (n = 5), and crisis response (n = 5). Provider qualifications: Most services had minimum education requirements. Only 6 services (11% of total services authorized), required a graduate degree. Providers without a graduate degree often required supervision by providers with Master’s degrees. Settings: Most services (83% in 8 states) were authorized for delivery in home, community, or vocational settings.

Implications for D&I Research: Policy mapping can facilitate maximum task-technology fit by characterizing structural and financial features of service environments to assist in matching ESIs to real-world contexts. ESI adaptations can aid implementation where ESIs are incongruent with policy and service contexts. For example, findings highlight the potential for scaling EBIs through task-shifting to non-clinicians working in community settings.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S13 Mental health organizations’ perceived needs for implementation science-based support to prepare for, implement, and sustain effective practices

## Elizabeth Walker1, Denise Williams2, David Katz3, Robin McGee1, Sean Karyczak4, Annie Lenoir5, Olga Vitruk6, Lydia Chwastiak6, Ricardo Canelo2, Heather Gotham2

## 1Rollins School of Public Health, Emory University, Atlanta, GA, USA; 2Stanford University School of Medicine, Palo Alto, CA, USA; 3Department of Global Health, University of Washington, Seattle, WA, USA; 4Department of Psychiatric Rehabilitation and Counseling Professions at Rutgers, The State University of New Jersey, Piscataway, NJ, USA; 5Texas Institute for Excellence in Mental Health at The University of Texas at Austin’s Steve Hicks School of Social Work, Austin, TX, USA; 6Department of Psychiatry and Behavioral Sciences, University of Washington, Seattle, WA, USA

## Correspondence: Heather Gotham (gothamh@stanford.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S13

Background: Although implementation science can accelerate mental health practice change, it has not historically been the basis for federally-funded technical assistance (TA) centers. Launched in October 2024, the federally-funded Center for Mental Health Implementation Support (CMHIS) provides TA to community-based organizations and agencies in all US states and territories. We provide implementation science-based support and resources structured around selection, implementation, and sustainment of effective mental health practices. We conducted feedback sessions on the needs and priorities of our audiences related to implementing services to inform the design of CMHIS’s support and resources.

Methods: We conducted 17 virtual qualitative discussion groups with participants from state and territory agencies, direct service organizations, and TA Centers. We designed the semi-structured discussion guides by mapping CMHIS’s eight core topics (community engagement, contextual determinants, implementation strategies, continuous quality improvement, measurement-based care, dissemination and social marketing, implementation outcomes evaluation, sustaining service delivery) to the Exploration, Preparation, Implementation, and Sustainment (EPIS) framework. We used rapid qualitative analytic techniques to synthesize data and develop themes on the core topics and TA delivery. We identified themes on important elements, challenges and facilitators, opportunities to develop TA, and cross-topic connections.

Findings: The discussion groups included 107 participants from 36 states and territories and 75 unique organizations. All core topics resonated with participants’ experiences. We identified broader themes related to the importance of partnerships, aligning leadership and staff, collecting and using meaningful data, and navigating workforce challenges. Each core topic was cross-linked with one to four other topics. For example, participants were interested in TA on how to develop sustainment plans, including how to evaluate effectiveness and implementation outcomes related to sustainment. Participants also valued TA that is participant-centered, in-depth (e.g., bundled implementation strategies), and engaging (e.g., peer sharing, problem-solving).

Implications for D&I Research: Our findings are shaping CMHIS’s TA offerings and resources with the goal of making dissemination and implementation science more accessible to leaders and staff to support implementation of effective mental health practices. Lessons learned for designing and delivering TA include a focus on co-creation, peer learning, and bundled implementation strategies to enhance sustainable practice change that can improve mental health service delivery.

Primary Funding Source: Substance Abuse and Mental Health Services Administration

Background: In 2023, the NIH funded Achieving Maternal Engagement and Transforming Health through Implementation Science and Training (AMETHIST@Penn), focused on implementation science (IS) consultation, community engagement, dissemination, training, and capacity building for the maternal health equity focused IMPROVE initiative.

AMETHIST’s research aim, to evaluate barriers and facilitators to high quality IS maternal health equity research, is underway.

Implications for D&I Research: A centralized hub facilitates consultation, technical assistance, and training in IS for maternal health equity research. This structure could be replicated in other networks to improve access to D&I expertise.

## S15 Incorporating a tool for tracking implementation processes and outcomes into maternal health community implementation programs (CIPs)

## Juan Villamar

## Westat, Bethesda, MD, USA

## Correspondence: Juan Villamar (juanvillamar@westat.com)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S15

Background: The Maternal Health Community Implementation Program (MH-CIP) and IMPROVE-CIP seek to improve preventable maternal morbidity and mortality outcomes by funding community-academic teams to conduct community-engaged implementation research. As part of our administrative, scientific, and technical support, Westat developed a CIP IS log that integrates data from the Longitudinal Implementation Strategy Tracking System (LISTS), strategy tracking efforts, and community engagement activities. Here, we highlight how we used IS log data to understand the ways in which implementation science is being used in maternal health.

Methods: We implemented LISTS to capture: strategy specifics (Proctor specification, individual, multifaceted, blended); determinants targeted/leveraged by strategies (matched to an IS theory, model, framework), and the strategy outcome(s) selected for evaluation. CETAC held three to five, 1-h virtual meetings with 4 community-academic teams between August 2024 and January 2025 to collect this data. LISTS data for 3 IMPROVE-CIP teams and retrospective community engagement data for 4 MH-CIP and 4 IMPROVE-CIP teams will be collected in the Fall of 2025.

Findings: We documented 102 determinants (52 facilitators, 67 barriers) that were matched to the updated CFIR (CFIR), 54 determinants (27 facilitators, 27 barriers) matched to PRISM and CFIR, and 12 determinants (6 facilitators and 6 barriers) mapped to EPIS domains and factors. A total of 29 strategies (13 blended, 11 discrete, and 5 multifaceted) were implemented by MH-CIP teams. The most common implementation outcomes selected for evaluation were fidelity (20) and adoption (12), followed by cost (5), sustainability (4), reach (3), acceptability (3), penetration (2), maintenance (2), and feasibility (1). Community engagement data linked the timing of strategy implementation (exploration, preparation, implementation, sustainment) with the role (govern, advise, implement, consult) and sector (e.g., health system, community-based, faith-based) of community partners. Trust, bidirectional learning, and financial distribution are to be collected in late 2025.

Implications for D&I Research: The CIP IS log is a new meta-science approach to organize LISTS data, strategy tracking, and community engagement activities conducted by MH-CIP and IMPROVE-CIP teams. By triangulating IS and community engagement data collected over time, the IS log supports systematic tracking and reporting, while also underscoring the central role that community partners have in implementation studies.

## S16 Improving data standardization and strengthening implementation research capacity in maternal health through an NIH IMPROVE Data-Hub

## Andreea Creanga

## Johns Hopkins School of Medicine, Baltimore, MD, USA

## Correspondence: Andreea Creanga (acreang3@jhu.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S16

Background: The NIH-funded Maternal Health Data Innovation and Coordination Hub (Data-Hub) works across 10 Centers of Excellence (CoE) in maternal health research to foster collaboration and support standardized data collection and analysis, thereby strengthening implementation research capacity. Between September 2023 and July 2025, we implemented key activities and tracked CoEs’ engagement with the Data-Hub alongside changes in research capacity.

Methods: With our expert panel, we conducted a landscape analysis and two modified Delphi processes to develop common data elements (CDEs) for maternal health implementation research. We disseminated a CDE implementation toolkit including REDCap forms, a data dictionary, a checklist for ethical considerations, and data safety protocols. We offered a collaboration portal and training opportunities, including a “data mosaic activity” to answer common CoE research questions. We measured CoE engagement via CoE-specific administrative connections and activity attendance. We quantified CoE engagement using a score of 1 for each CoE-specific connection or activity, and 1 if CoE-affiliate attended a specific training; and a score of 3 for each data mosaic submission. Surveys administered in 2024 and 2025 used Holden's research capacity and culture (RCC) tool to rate CoE affiliates’ skill levels from 1(low) to 10(high) on 17 research aspects.

Findings: Our expert panel recommended CDEs for 36 biomedical and 22 psychosocial maternal health constructs, and CoEs are in various stages of integration using our CDE implementation toolkit. Total engagement scores ranged between 15 and 45, with scores < 20 for two CoEs, 20–30 for five CoEs, and > 30 for five CoEs. We found improvements in RCC mean scores between 2024 (N = 82; score = 7.2) and 2025 (N = 122; score = 7.5), yet overall lower scores for early-stage and minority-group investigators.

Implications for D&I Research: Our Data-Hub supports data standardization and cross-CoE learnings in the field of maternal health implementation research. While the research is ongoing, process measures of engagement demonstrate practical ways to measure capacity development across a maternal research network. By working alongside CoEs in the development of CDEs and promoting ongoing engagement, we seek to strengthen quality and generalizability in maternal health implementation research, in order to have the most widespread impact on maternal outcomes at a national scale.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S17 “They know what they are doing”: mechanism mapping to explicate how task shifting can improve HIV care in pluralistic settings

## Misha Hooda1, Gabriel Nuwagaba2, William Bugeza2, Constance Birungi2, Maureen Tushabe2, Sylvia Natukunda2, Srija Gogineni1, Denis Nansera2, Winnie Muyindike2, Juliet Mwanga-Amumpaire2, Radhika Sundararajan1

## 1Weill Cornell Medicine, New York, NY, USA; 2Mbarara University of Science and Technology, Mbarara, Uganda

## Correspondence: Misha Hooda (mih4006@med.cornell.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S17

Background: Task-shifting is an established implementation strategy used to expand access and uptake of evidence-based care. However, in global settings, where informal and biomedical providers often operate concurrently in medically pluralistic systems, task shifting efforts frequently confront tensions between care systems. The mechanisms through which task shifting generates collaborative change in such environments remain underexplored. We use qualitative data from an ongoing clinical trial to generate a mechanism map explicating causal pathways through which task shifting with traditional healers (TH) can advance care in pluralistic contexts.

Methods: In July 2023, 22 TH in Southwestern Uganda received trained by HIV clinicians on HIV prevention, ART adherence, stigma reduction, and HIV self-testing. Between July 2023 and November 2024, we conducted longitudinal qualitative interviews with 22 TH and 20 healthcare workers at baseline and 9-months post training implementation. We employed reflexive thematic analysis to examine relationship transformation. Contextual determinants were organized using CFIR domains, and causal pathway diagramming informed the development of a mechanism map linking components of task shifting to proximal and distal implementation outcomes.

Findings: Harmonizing initially dissonant relationships between HIV clinicians and TH is foundational to effective task shifting in the study setting. We identified three distinct pathways that successfully transformed contentious relationships into bidirectional partnerships:

Legitimization pathway: integration strategies fostered professional identity and validation of TH (“we’ve been remembered”)
Competency pathway: structured HIV training facilitated mutual respect through shared knowledge and capability recognition (“they know what they’re doing”)
Relational pathway: liaison systems fostered trust through consistent two-way communication (“we are like family”)

These mechanisms catalyzed strengthened healer–clinician relationships, evidenced by increased professional trust, improved communication, and deeper social connectedness. This proximal implementation outcome is crucial to achieving successful system, clinical, and distal implementation outcomes.

Implications for D&I Research: This study demonstrates how task shifting activated interpersonal mechanisms—such as trust-building, role recognition, and communication—to achieve proximal relationship outcomes critical for intervention success in pluralistic systems. By mapping causal pathways, we offer a replicable method for analyzing and optimizing task shifting programs in other contexts. These findings highlight the value of targeting relational dynamics as core mechanisms in contexts where informal and biomedical providers intersect.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S18 The missing link? Exploring communication quality as a driver of sustainability capacity in D&I science

## Sara Malone1, Luke Zabotka1, Veronica Chaitan1, Samantha Hayes2, Rebeca Caires2, Miguel Aguilar Barragán2, Maricela Robles-Murguía2, Adolfo Cardenas-Aguirre2, Bobbi Carothers3, Jocelyn Rivera4, Virginia McKay3, Dylan Graetz2, Asya Agulnik2

## 1Washington University in St. Louis, St. Louis, MO, USA; 2St. Jude Children's Research Hospital, Memphis, TN, USA; 3Center for Public Health Systems Science, Brown School, Washington University in Saint Louis, St. Louis, MO, USA; 4Hospital Infantil Teletón de Oncología (HITO), Querétaro, Mexico

## Correspondence: Sara Malone (sara.malone@wustl.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S18

Background

While implementation strategies have been widely explored to improve the integration of evidence-based practices (EBPs), less attention has been paid to how EBPs are sustained. One example of an understudied mechanism is communication quality. Communication has been theorized as important, however, its role as a driver of sustainability capacity has been underexplored. This study examines the relationship between communication quality and sustainability capacity in resource-variable hospitals using an EBP, pediatric early warning scores (PEWS).

Methods

As part of a longitudinal study on PEWS sustainability, we conducted a cross-sectional survey with clinicians in variably resourced hospitals. Communication quality was assessed using CritCom—a reliable, multilingual instrument with six domains: actionable, clarity, tone, empowerment, leadership, and teamwork. Sustainability capacity was measured using the Clinical Sustainability Assessment Tool, including seven domains: workflow integration, engaged staff, engaged partners, monitoring/evaluation, implementation/training, organizational readiness, and outcomes/effectiveness. Additionally, we collected data on individual demographics and site characteristics. Mixed effects modeling was used to examine the relationship between communication and overall sustainability capacity, adjusting for individual and site-level factors.

Findings

1,288 individuals from 14 countries and 47 hospitals completed the survey. Participants worked in all hospital areas (wards, ICU, emergency department); most were nurses (58.1%) and physicians (33.9%). The overall median CritCom score was 4.133/5 max, IQR [3.867–4.700], and the median CSAT score was 4.250/5 max, IQR [3.928–4.733]. Individuals in leadership positions perceived both communication quality and sustainability capacity as higher than bedside staff. Similarly, physicians scored both constructs higher than their nursing colleagues. There was a strong, positive correlation between overall communication quality and sustainability capacity (0.58, p < 0.0002). Mixed-effects modeling revealed that, after controlling for individual and setting-level variables, high perceptions of communication quality were significantly associated with overall clinical sustainability.

Implications for D&I Research

Communication quality is a crucial, yet underexplored determinant of EBP sustainability. Targeting communication can improve overall site capacities, thereby enhancing implementation efforts and ultimately leading to fully realized D&I benefits over time. These results highlight the importance of embedding a focus on team communication in implementation efforts and contribute to a systems-informed understanding of how structural dynamics influence implementation outcomes.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S19 Reimaging mechanisms and functions: reframing the purpose behind implementation strategies to embed equity and relationality

## Julia Moore, Sobia Khan

## The Center for Implementation, Toronto, ON, Canada

## Correspondence: Julia Moore (julia.moore@thecenterforimplementation.com)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S19

Background

There has been significant interest in exploring exactly how implementation strategies foster change. Much of this work has focused on advancing theory and empirical evidence linking strategies with mechanisms of change. While these efforts are essential and ongoing, there remains a pressing need to support professionals in understanding the underlying functions of the strategies they use. Additionally, existing conceptualizations of functions have been critiqued for: being top-down, overlooking relational aspects of change, lacking equity considerations, and reflecting Global North assumptions. To address these limitations, we reimagined implementation functions as “intentions”: the purpose and relational grounding of implementation strategies that more accurately represent the diverse ways strategies support change across people and contexts.

Methods

Using an iterative, mixed-methods approach, we developed the intentions framework through five key activities:

literature can on functions and mechanisms;
engagement with ten implementation research experts;
integration of intentions described in practice; and
back mapping intentions for 101 implementation strategies (ERIC list and other sources).

We built on Michie et al.’s nine intervention functions, implementation support practitioner functions, and the literature on equity and relationships in implementation. The strategy-intention mappings were then linked to individual and contextual determinant frameworks.

Findings

The resulting framework includes 35 intentions. These intentions articulate the underlying purpose behind implementation strategies, including relational, equity-oriented, and systems-level intentions often underrepresented in existing taxonomies. Some intentions align with categories already described in the literature, such as those focused on knowledge and skill-building (educate, build skills) and reinforcement and regulation (remind, restrict). Other intentions emphasize relational and equity-oriented processes that foster connection, inclusion, and psychological safety (cultivate trust, create belonging, diversify, be culturally/socially responsive). Others center on internal and reflective processes that shape how individuals and teams approach implementation (shift mindset, be reflexive).

Implications for D&I Research

Expanding our understanding of the underlying intentions of implementation strategies can help us better elucidate how implementation strategies work. Adding intentions that emphasize the relational nature of most implementation strategies can help us assess mechanisms of change more accurately. To our knowledge, this is the first attempt informed by implementation science and practice to broaden our scope of knowledge and theory on strategy intentions.

## S20 Evolving strategies through community-engaged implementation: mid-study findings from the Turtle Island Tales project

## James Merle1, Emily Tomayko2, Teresa Warne2, Alexandra Adams2, Paul Estabrooks1

## 1University of Utah, Salt Lake City, UT, USA; 2Montana State University, Bozeman, USA

## Correspondence: James Merle (james.merle@utah.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S20

Background: Turtle Island Tales is a culturally grounded wellness promotion program implemented in partnership with Native American communities. The study prioritizes community engagement by embedding a Project Steering Committee (PSC) and Community Implementation Teams (CITs) as co-leaders in designing and executing implementation strategies.

Methods: Using the Longitudinal Implementation Strategy Tracking System (LISTS) method, we conducted bi-monthly meetings, which served as checkpoints for tracking strategy additions, modifications and discontinuations across seven communities. We provide a mid-study report on the dynamic strategy changes. During the study planning phase, strategies were pre-specified to promote reach, adoption, implementation, and maintenance, as documented in planning artifacts.

Findings: The initial proposal included three bundled strategies with 51 embedded strategies. Through the LISTS process, these three bundles were retained, but the total number of strategies was consolidated into 30 discrete, specified actions. To date, there are 22 active strategies, eight that have been discontinued or completed, four modifications that have been tracked, and eight strategies have been added. Key changes included material development (e.g., binders, bulk ordering), logistical and funding solutions (e.g., site visits, community-secured funding), integration with Head Start systems and educational standards, and emergent delivery adaptations. Although many strategies retained their original labels, such recruitment and sustainability planning and facilitation, their operationalizations diverged from initial specifications. For example, centralized recruitment strategies evolved into localized, CIT-led social media campaigns. These shifts reflect real-time responsiveness to community priorities, implementer capacity, and contextual constraints.

Implications for D&I Research: For implementation science, this study illustrates how LISTS can function as a participatory learning mechanism to support and document strategy evolution, particularly in culturally grounded, community-engaged D&I efforts. This mid-study report highlights the importance of flexible, community-driven implementation planning; although strategies were presumably well-specified during study planning, LISTS tracking revealed that community engagement often reshaped those strategies in unanticipated yet contextually valid ways. The LISTS process led to expanding and contracting the discrete actions within bundled strategies. These findings underscore the value of ongoing, structured community involvement to enhance fit and feasibility and to re-specify strategies over time in alignment with local needs and systems.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S21 Ongoing consultation and technical assistance can increase implementation research knowledge among teams conducting HIV-related implementation research

## Nanette Benbow

## Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine, Chicago, IL, USA

## Correspondence: Nanette Benbow (nanette@northwestern.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S21

Background: As part of the evaluation of the CHESHIRE network, we assessed whether activities and support provided by Implementation Science (IS) Hubs to the NIH-funded Ending the HIV Epidemic (EHE) administrative supplements led to increases in implementation research knowledge among the funded EHE project teams.

Methods: We analyzed self-reported survey data collected by the NIH-funded Implementation Science Coordination Initiative (ISCI) from EHE projects during their supplement funding periods (between August 2021 and February 2025). To assess changes in IS knowledge and competencies, ISCI distributed the surveys before project initiation and after project completion (i.e., pre- and post-surveys), asking teams to rate their current level of expertise (beginner, intermediate, advanced) on each of 10 implementation research competencies (103 of 107 projects completed both). We compared the proportions of projects by expertise level in the pre- and post-surveys and described the change in expertise level (increase [move from beginner to intermediate/advanced or from intermediate to advanced], no change, or decrease [moved from advanced to intermediate/beginner or from intermediate to beginner).

Findings: In the pre-surveys, across the 10 competencies, the median percentage of EHE projects rating their knowledge as “beginner” was 26%, “intermediate” was 51%, and “advanced” was 21%. More of the projects had advanced knowledge at baseline of “describing the importance of stakeholder perspectives”; more of the projects had “beginner” knowledge of “describing relationships between organizational dimension and implementation research”. Among projects with beginner or intermediate knowledge in the pre-survey, there was an increase in knowledge from pre- to post: across the 10 competencies, a median 41% of projects (range by competency: 30%-58%) demonstrated an increase in implementation research knowledge. The competencies with the greatest proportionate increase in knowledge from pre- to post-survey were “determine when participatory research is appropriate for implementation” (57% of projects increased knowledge) and “identify and develop sustainable partnerships for implementation research” (58% of projects increased knowledge).

Implications for D&I Research: Being supported by an organized network of IS hubs providing technical assistance and consultation may lead to increased IS research knowledge among teams conducting HIV-related implementation research.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S22 The impact of a collaborative implementation science mentoring network: the mentee perspective

## Mary Louise Millett

## University of Texas Health Houston, Houston, TX, USA

## Correspondence: Mary Louise Millett (mary-louise.e.millett@uth.tmc.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S22

Background: CHESHIRE represents a novel model for HIV researchers across institutions in the United States, with hub IS experts providing technical assistance and other support for EHE-funded researchers. Little is known about how grantees interact with and benefit from a network delivering implementation science (IS) mentorship, collaboration, and educational opportunities. This qualitative study evaluated the impact of CHESHIRE hub technical assistance activities on mentee IS research capacity and the integration of IS methods into their EHE-funded projects.

Methods: We conducted 14 semi-structured interviews with investigators of EHE-funded projects. Four CHESHIRE-affiliated researchers interviewed grantees assigned to hubs. The interview guide explored engagement, the quality of support, factors influencing involvement, IS competency development, and hub impact on research execution. We also sought recommendations to improve the network’s structure and support. Interviews were conducted via Zoom or Microsoft Teams, recorded, and transcribed. Analysis was undertaken in Atlas.ti. One researcher developed a preliminary codebook inductively using three transcripts. Two additional researchers independently coded two more transcripts using the draft codebook. The team compared interpretations, revised definitions, and reached consensus on a shared structure. All remaining transcripts were coded independently. The team met regularly to resolve discrepancies. We then developed higher-order themes by analyzing patterns and drawing on illustrative reflections that captured common experiences or offered distinct insights.

Findings: Our analysis identified three overarching themes: (1) factors shaping engagement, (2) forms of engagement, and (3) perceived impact of the network. Participants described how institutional constraints and competing demands shaped their engagement. Interactions included tailored consultations with mentors, webinars, and communities of practice that fostered shared learning. Grantees viewed the network as helpful for IS learning and strengthening project design and execution. They recommended reexamining how grantees are assigned to hubs to facilitate engagement and establish compatible mentor–mentee pairings.

Implications for D&I Research: These findings provide guidance for structuring capacity-building models that are responsive to real-world research needs. Sustained investment in models like CHESHIRE is critical to supporting IS in HIV research and sustaining evidence-based HIV interventions.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S23 Fostering scientific relationships through the CHESHIRE network: a social network analysis

## Alexandra Morshed

## Rollins School of Public Health, Emory University, Atlanta, GA, USA

## Correspondence: Alexandra Morshed (alexandra.morshed@emory.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S23

Background: Targeted capacity building—through training, mentorship, and connected learning networks—is crucial for expanding the reach of HIV services. When capacity-building initiatives lead to increased interdisciplinary collaborations and knowledge sharing, there is potential to accelerate the integration of implementation science (IS) into HIV research, facilitating the faster rollout of evidence into practice. As part of an evaluation of CHESHIRE, this study describes and maps scientific collaboration across the network and evaluates changes following receipt of EHE funding.

Methods: We conducted a retrospective, one-group, pre-post design study to characterize and assess changes in scientific relationships among IS Hub teams and EHE supplement principal investigators (MPIs) using an online survey (administered January-May 2025) and social network analysis methods. We developed a contact list using publicly available NIH data for each supplement grant, information from IS Hubs and ISCI, and conducted internet searches to identify missing/undeliverable email addresses. We excluded MPIs if they were on leave, retired, or reported no involvement in the supplement project. Of 265 identified network participants, 152 people (57.4%) completed the survey. Respondents were presented with the full roster of invited individuals and asked to identify their professional contacts, whether they had a scientific relationship with them before and after receiving IS Hub or EHE supplement funding, the type of scientific relationship, and the frequency of contact.

Findings: The collaboration network (265 people) consisted of 205 (77.4%) EHE supplement grantees, 42 (15.8%) IS Hub team members, and 18 (6.8%) people who were both. Twenty-six (18.6%) of survey respondents rated their IS expertise as beginner, 73 (52.1%) as intermediate, and 41 (29.3%) as advanced. Among respondents, 26.7% sought IS advice outside of CHESHIRE. The overall number of scientific relationships was greater following receipt of EHE funding, with a greater average change in number of relationships in earlier CHESHIRE cohorts (years 2019 and 2020) than later cohorts (years 20212023).

Implications for D&I Research: Public investment in catalyzing scientific relationships is crucial for advancing HIV evidence translation into practice. CHESHIRE successfully facilitated new relationships that have the potential to accelerate research translation and evidence integration.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S24 The World Health Organization's global research agenda on Knowledge Translation (KT) and Evidence-informed Policy-making (EIP): development process, results, and next steps

## Robert Terry1, Jennifer Callaghan-Koru2, Bastien Kolt3, Tanja Kuchenmüller3, Ahmed Mandil4

## 1The Special Programme for Research and Training in Tropical Diseases (TDR), Geneva, Switzerland; 2University of Arkansas for Medical Sciences, Fayetteville, AR, USA; 3World Health Organization, Geneva, Switzerland; 4University of Alexandria, Alexandria, Egypt

## Correspondence: Robert Terry (terryr@who.int)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S24

Background: The quantity and quality of global knowledge translation (KT) research has grown in recent decades but its findings are still underused in health policy-making. Challenges include: a mismatch between the KT products available and the needs of decision makers, fragmented and duplicative studies, and limited and uncoordinated funding. To address this, WHO developed a Global Research Agenda for KT (GRA) that was published in May 2025.

Methods: The agenda development process followed WHO’s priority-setting guidance and included literature reviews, an online survey, a modified Delphi exercise, and two online consultations, overseen by an advisory committee (2023–2025). More than 130 global KT experts from 38 countries, identified from an open call, participated in the process. Two rounds of Delphi were completed to identify and then rank research priorities based on the outcome of the research having impact, improving inclusivity, and being feasible. In addition, experts allocated tokens to research areas based on perceived importance.
