Findings: The Global Research Agenda on KT and EIP [1] identified 19 research priorities under three themes: 1) Interventions; 2) Barriers, facilitators, and opportunities; and 3) Methods, standards, measurements, theories, and frameworks. Highly-ranked research areas include: identify, develop, and assess strategies and approaches to institutionalize evidence production, translation, and use (Theme 1); analyze factors determining the engagement of decision-makers in evidence uptake (Theme 2); and, explore innovative and evolving technologies that support KT/EIP, such as Artificial Intelligence (Theme 3). The Agenda was launched during a global webinar in May 2025.

Implications for D&I Research: WHO will disseminate this agenda to funders and research partners to catalyze and guide investments in KT/EIP research. As part of the implementation process, TDR launched an open call for research proposals aligned with the GRA. The response was unprecedented, underscoring the strong demand for KT research: By the close of the call, 430 full submissions were received from 58 low- and middle-income countries. Further details on the funded research projects will be shared in this presentation.

Primary Funding Source: World Health Organization

Reference

A project summary, including the research agenda, can be accessed here: https://cdn.who.int/media/docs/default-source/campaigns-and-initiatives/epivnet/gra-project-summary-and-progress-report_may-2025.pdf?sfvrsn=31bc0e9d_3

## S25 Realizing the benefits of D&I: a roadmap for integrating artificial intelligence with implementation science

## Fernando Bruno1, Karen Plevock Haase1, Lilian Perez2, Shahnaz Khan1

## 1National Institutes of Health, Bethesda, MD, USA; 2San Diego State University, San Diego, CA, USA

## Correspondence: Fernando Bruno (fernando.bruno@nih.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S25

Background: Integrating artificial intelligence (AI) and implementation science (IS) presents a critical opportunity to realize the full benefits of D&I by accelerating the delivery and uptake of evidence-based interventions and improving population health outcomes. Among the benefits, AI could streamline data analysis, predict care needs, and automate tasks. However, without ethical safeguards, AI might perpetuate bias and widen health disparities. To explore optimal integration of AI and IS, the Center for Translation Research and Implementation Science (CTRIS) convened a multidisciplinary Think Tank to examine key challenges and identify future directions.

Methods: The Think Tank, held in December 2024, brought together clinicians, IS researchers, AI experts, and public health professionals divided into four groups:Ethical and Equitable Use of AI in IS
Cutting-Edge Applications of AI in IS
Data Management and Privacy
Future Innovations in AI and IS

Each group identified gaps, challenges, and opportunities within their domain. Discussions were analyzed to identify cross-cutting prioritized themes and actionable strategies.

Findings: Key themes emerged across domains:

Ethical use: Community-engaged co-design, plain-language explaining how AI models work, and continuous bias monitoring are essential for fostering trust and fairness in AI-enabled implementation.

Applications: Tools such as chatbots and large language models show promise in enhancing care delivery and reducing clinician workload, but their use is limited to resource-rich settings.

Data and Privacy: Federated learning enables secure model training across sites, yet challenges around data governance and authenticity remain unresolved.

Future Directions: Participants proposed interdisciplinary “AI + IS studios,” integration of AI training into medical and public health education, and incentives for equity-focused research and development.

Cross-cutting recommendations included standardizing data practices, investing in AI literacy, and developing shared evaluation frameworks (e.g., CONSORT-AI, RE-AIM) for responsible implementation.

Implications for D&I Research: AI can help close the evidence-to-practice gap, but only with intentional, community-centered design and infrastructure. The Think Tank identified critical areas for capacity-building and collaboration, including piloting federated-learning approaches, co-creating solutions with communities most impacted by health disparities, and establishing national guidance for unconventional data verification. These insights offer a roadmap for embedding AI into D&I research to maximize real-world impact.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S26 Mapping the landscape of dissemination and implementation science across the CTSA Consortium: a multi-domain environmental scan

## Jing Li1, Naomi Duffort1, Larry Hearld1, Kristine Hearld1, Renee Heffron1, Bertha Hidalgo1, Katherine Mills2, Michael Mugavero1, Salisa Westrick3, Lisa Zubkoff1

## 1University of Alabama at Birmingham, Birmingham, AL, USA; 2Tulane University, New Orleans, LA, USA;3Auburn University, Auburn, AL, USA

## Correspondence: Jing Li (jingli2@uabmc.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S26

Background

Dissemination and Implementation (D&I) science plays a central role in accelerating the translation of biomedical discoveries into health impact. The NIH Clinical and Translational Science Awards (CTSA) Program supports over 60 hubs to advance translational research infrastructure and practice, with D&I Science prominently featured as a goal of the CTSA funding mechanism. Currently, the degree to which D&I science is integrated within CTSA hubs varies, warranting a comprehensive assessment, to guide growth and evolution.

Methods

We conducted a structured environmental scan of all 66 CTSA hubs using publicly available web content and published documents. Data were synthesized across six domains: (1) D&I core, (2) consultation services, (3) training grants and career development, (4) structured educational offerings, (5) pilot funding, and (6) inter-institutional partnerships. Descriptive summaries and qualitative synthesis identified trends, gaps, and models of excellence.

Findings

D&I infrastructure across the CTSA network is heterogeneous. Currently, 38% of hubs (n = 25) maintain a dedicated D&I core, 20% (n = 13) offer some services without a formal structure, while 42% of hubs show no visible D&I infrastructure. Consultation services are available at 53% of the hubs (n = 35), typically covering study design, framework selection, and grant development; however, their structure varies widely, ranging from brief office hours to multi-step, team-based models. Training opportunities are limited: just 12% (n = 8) of hubs provide formal D&I training grants (e.g., K12 or T32), and only 15% (n = 10) offer D&I-focused coursework or graduate certificates. Most CTSAs do not integrate D&I into broader translational career development programs. Ten hubs offer pilot funding for D&I (typically $2,000- $50,000). Engagement in D&I collaborative events (e.g., seminars, symposia, interest groups) is reported at 37% of CTSAs. Although 48 CTSAs have partner collaborators, with most maintaining 2–4 active partnerships, none provided information about D&I-specific collaborations.

Implications for D&I Research

This scan reveals an inconsistent yet a promising direction for D&I science across the CTSA network. Gaps in training, consultation, and coordination may limit equitable growth and impact. Given the CTSA’s mission of accelerating the translation of discoveries into population health impact, the CTSA is then ideal program for building D&I by identifying best practices, supporting under-resourced hubs and strengthening implementation efforts nationwide.

## S27 Building capacity among external facilitators in a longitudinal implementation initiative

## Taylor Harris1,2, Ron Calderon3, Sona Hovsepian3, Erin Finley3, Sonya Gabrielian4

## 1VA Center for the Study of Healthcare Innovation, Implementation and Policy, VA Greater Los Angeles Healthcare System, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 2Veterans Health Administration, North Hills, CA, USA; 3Veterans Health Administration, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 4University of California, Los Angeles David Geffen School of Medicine, Los Angeles, CA, USA

## Correspondence: Taylor Harris (taylor.harris2@va.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S27

Background: External facilitation (EF) is widely recognized as an effective strategy for implementing complex, multi-faceted evidence-based practices (EBPs). Within longitudinal implementation initiatives, novice external facilitators often initially rely on didactic training, and gradually evolve towards highly partnered, creative and relationship-driven exchanges with implementing teams. However, this process of iteratively building capacity is not well understood. Drawing from a four-year national implementation initiative of Critical Time Intervention (CTI, a case management EBP) at 20 community-based agencies serving homeless-experienced Veterans (HEVs), we sought to identify key practices (e.g., activities, resources) to build external facilitators’ capacity.

Methods: Three novice external facilitators supported CTI implementation across three consecutive cohorts. Over 7 months, facilitators conducted weekly 30-min EF sessions with each agency. Periodic reflections (n = 24), a method of guided discussions for documenting contextual implementation information, conducted with facilitators, captured their learning experiences and capacity building practices. Rapid qualitative analysis identified key processes that enhanced facilitators’ capacity.

Findings: In periodic reflections, facilitators highlighted the importance of comprehensive training in CTI and EF which provided foundational skills and bolstered role clarification. Weekly debriefs (i.e., meetings to address implementation challenges and share best practices) with the EF team and reflective writing exercises (i.e., templated notes following each EF session) were salient supports that enhanced facilitators’ capacities to respond to implementing teams’ needs. Facilitators felt weekly debriefs served as a cohesion activity that reinforced implementation goals, prevented facilitator burnout, and encouraged innovative thinking. Reflective writing promoted facilitators’ recognition of biases, critical thinking, and problem-solving skills. Periodic reflections also captured facilitators’ shifts to collaborative roles, showcasing their more frequent use of reciprocal dialogue and integration of contextualized and relevant evidence as time progressed. With each cohort, facilitators gained exposure to diverse case management teams which enhanced their empathy and structured thinking, facilitating richer collaborative, co-creative interactions.

Implications for D&I Research: Our findings underscore the importance of identifying and using resources that promote facilitators’ skills, adaptability and flexibility; building facilitator capacity ultimately can contribute to increased practice uptake and improved outcomes. These insights contribute to knowledge regarding successful and sustainable implementation supports, emphasizing the need for novice facilitators to be supported as they engage in longitudinal implementation efforts.

Primary Funding Source: Department of Veterans Affairs

## S28 Building impactful community-engaged research translation partnerships: twenty-plus years of the cancer prevention and control research network

## Stephanie Wheeler1, Rebecca Lee2, Mary Wangen2, Amy Tran2, Erin Laurie3, Meghan O'Leary3, Renée Ferrari3, Jingle Xu2, Alison Brenner3, Rachel Hirschey2

## 1Lineberger Comprehensive Cancer Center, UNC Chapel Hill, Chapel Hill, NC, USA; 2University of North Carolina, Chapel Hill, NC, USA; 3UNC Gillings School of Global Public Health, Chapel Hill, NC, USA

## Correspondence: Stephanie Wheeler (stephanie_wheeler@unc.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S28

Background: The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention-funded Cancer Prevention and Control Research Network (CPCRN) has existed since 2002 with additional support from the National Cancer Institute. The mission of CPCRN is to accelerate the adoption and implementation of evidence-based cancer prevention and control strategies through community engagement, enhance large-scale efforts to reach all populations to reduce the burden of cancer, and develop the dissemination and implementation workforce in cancer prevention and control. In 2002–2024, the Network included collaborating centers at a total of 19 academic institutions. The Network currently includes more than 300 members, representing 8 funded collaborating centers, as well as Affiliate Members and CPCRN Scholars from 45 additional community organizations and academic institutions. Here, we report on the scientific impact of CPCRN over its 20-plus year history.

Methods: The CPCRN coordinating center developed an online progress reporting system in 2004 to track network activities and accomplishments. Here, we summarize combined peer-reviewed publications, presentations, grant activity, and contributions to official reports, plans and policies within CPCRN through collaborating center core projects and cross-center workgroups in two time periods: 2019–2024 (corresponding to the CPCRN5 funding cycle), and 2004–2024 (since inception of the progress reporting system).

Findings: During the CPCRN5 funding cycle, members published a combined total of 969 CPCRN-related articles; since 2004, members published 2,818 CPCRN-related publications. In CPCRN5, members delivered 645 CPCRN-related presentations and 162 trainings; since 2004, members delivered 2,581 CPCRN-related presentations. Trainings supported evidence translation in outside organizations, such as Federally Qualified Health Centers (129 presentations) and other community-based healthcare providers (247 presentations). During CPCRN5, members submitted 240 CPCRN-related grant applications, of which 186 applications (~78%) were funded, totaling > $272 million in additional awards. Since 2004, CPCRN members submitted a cumulative total of 1,513 grants, of which 809 (~53%) were funded, totaling > $959 million in additional awards. In the CPCRN5 funding cycle alone, members contributed to 39 official reports, plans, and policies to support local, state, and federal research translation in cancer.

Implications for D&I Research: Large, collaborative, thematic networks like CPCRN enable impactful and productive partnerships at local, state, and federal levels.

Primary Funding Source: Centers for Disease Control and Prevention

## S29 The power of a research network: CPCRN’s role in advancing pharmacy-based cancer prevention and control through innovation and evidence synthesis

## Alison Brenner1, Mary Wangen2, Renée Ferrari3, Daniel Reuland4, Macary Marciniak5, Rachel Hirschey2, Stephanie Wheeler6, Parth Shah7, The PharmCPC Workgroup8

## 1Lineberger Comprehensive Cancer Center, UNC Chapel HIll, Chapel Hill, NC, USA; 2University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill, Chapel Hill, NC, USA; 3UNC Chapel Hill Gillings School of Global Public Health, Chapel Hill, NC, USA; 4UNC Chapel Hill, School of Medicine, Chapel Hill, NC, USA; 5UNC Eshelman School of Pharmacy, Chapel Hill, NC, USA; 6Lineberger Comprehensive Cancer Center, UNC Chapel Hill, Chapel Hill, NC, USA; 7University of Washington, Seattle, WA, USA; 8The Cancer Prevention and Control Reseach Network, Chapel Hill, NC, USA

## Correspondence: Alison Brenner (alison.brenner@unc.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S29

Background

Pharmacies are among the most accessible healthcare settings in the U.S., particularly for populations with limited access to traditional care. As pharmacists’ scope of practice has expanded over the past three decades, pharmacies are increasingly well-positioned to deliver preventive services. The Cancer Prevention and Control Research Network (CPCRN) has served as a critical platform for conceptualizing, piloting, and scaling implementation strategies. Recognizing the broader potential for pharmacies as a healthcare setting, CPCRN allowed for expanded exploration of pharmacy cancer prevention and control (CPC) activities.

Methods

Through CPCRN’s infrastructure, the University of North Carolina collaborating center explored an innovative model to deliver fecal immunochemical tests (FIT) for colorectal cancer screening through community pharmacies (PharmFIT™). We conducted formative interviews, surveys, and pilot tests and, following, expanded on the PharmFIT™ innovation to explore the potential of other CPC activities in pharmacies.

Findings

PharmFIT™ formative work demonstrated high acceptability among pharmacists, patients, and primary care providers. Pilot testing confirmed feasibility and potential for impact, with two pilot sites reporting near 100% FIT completion rates. Encouraged by the successful pilots, we successfully applied for an NCI R01 (1R01CA279010). Next, the CPCRN PharmCPC Interest Group launched a multi-site scoping review to catalog the global landscape of pharmacy-based CPC interventions, currently underway with 13 reviewers across multiple CPCRN sites. Insights from PharmFIT™ directly informed the development of a new intervention: PACTS (Pharmacist Assisted Cessation of Tobacco and Support for Screening). PACTS is a pharmacy-delivered lung cancer screening shared decision-making program, integrated with existing tobacco cessation services. Currently, we are interviewing pharmacists and physicians to understand acceptability, potential for integration with tobacco cessation in pharmacies, and key aspects of the care coordination for accessing lung cancer screening services.

Implications for D&I Research

This progression, from innovation to evidence review to expansion of intervention design, demonstrates the unique capacity of a stable research network to accelerate the D&I research pipeline. CPCRN’s sustained infrastructure enables rapid cycle development, sustained community partnerships, and knowledge translation. This model offers a replicable framework for leveraging novel healthcare settings like pharmacies to expand reach and equity in cancer prevention.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S30 Scaling what works: advancing the reach and real-world impact of cancer prevention and control interventions

## Rachel Hirschey1, Mary Wangen1, Amy Tran1, Renée Ferrari2, Rebecca Lee3, Meghan O'Leary2, Jingle Xu1, Alison Brenner4,5,6, Cam Escoffery7, Prajakta Adsul8, Stephanie Wheeler9, The Scaling What Works Workgroup10

## 1University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill, Chapel Hill, NC, USA; 2UNC Chapel Hill Gillings School of Global Public Health, Chapel Hill, NC, USA; 3University of North Carolina, Chapel Hill, NC, USA; 4Division of General Medicine and Clinical Epidemiology, University of North Carolina School of Medicine, Chapel Hill, NC, USA; 5UNC Chapel Hill, School of Medicine, Chapel Hill, NC, USA; 6Lineberger Comprehensive Cancer Center, UNC Chapel HIll, Chapel Hill, NC, USA; 7Rollins School of Public Health, Emory University, Atlanta, GA, USA; 8University of New Mexico, Albuquerque, NM, USA; 9Lineberger Comprehensive Cancer Center, UNC Chapel Hill, Chapel Hill, NC, USA; 10The Cancer Prevention and Control Reseach Network, Chapel Hill, NC, USA

## Correspondence: Rachel Hirschey (hirschey@unc.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S30

Background

Investigators from the Cancer Prevention and Control Research Network (CPCRN), in partnership with the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), developed the Scaling What Works (SWW) program to support National Comprehensive Cancer Control Program (NCCCP) sites in implementing evidence-based interventions (EBIs). SWW focused on EBIs designed to address critical gaps in cancer prevention and care: (1) Project ECHO combined with patient navigation to improve outcomes for rural cancer survivors; (2) interventions targeting cancer risk factors during adolescence; and (3) strategies to expand access to gynecologic oncology services.

Methods

A multi-phase, multi-component approach was used to build capacity at participating NCCCP sites to adapt, implement, evaluate, and sustain EBIs. Activities included focus groups, information sessions with pilot implementers, a three-day interactive symposium incorporating the Putting Public Health Evidence in Action (PPHEIA) curriculum, and a virtual learning collaborative offering ongoing technical assistance, mentorship, and peer learning. Changes in implementation competencies were assessed using pre/post paired t-tests.

Findings

A total of 89 program staff and coalition members from 41 NCCCP sites (representing 32 states, 2 tribal organizations, and 7 U.S. jurisdictions) engaged in SWW activities. Baseline assessments revealed common capacity gaps in staffing, partnerships, funding, and infrastructure, particularly in rural areas. Participants also reported low confidence in planning for EBI implementation.
Following SWW, participants demonstrated statistically significant improvements in implementation competencies across all five core learning objectives: understanding (10.2 to 12.8), adapting (13.3 to 16.8), implementing (12.9 to 17.2), evaluating (13.7 to 17.4), and sustaining (15.8 to 20.4) EBIs.

Implications for D&I Research

The SWW program demonstrates how structured, network-supported capacity-building can enhance the dissemination and implementation of EBIs in cancer prevention and control. By leveraging scalable strategies such as training, mentorship, and peer exchange, SWW strengthened implementation readiness across NCCCP sites. This model provides a replicable approach for accelerating the public health impact of D&I science, particularly in medically underserved and rural communities.

Primary Funding Source: Centers for Disease Control and Prevention

## S31 Training global D&I leaders: insights from the CPCRN Scholars Program

## Cam Escoffery1, The CPCRN Scholars Workgroup2

## 1Rollins School of Public Health, Emory University, Atlanta, GA, USA; 2The Cancer Prevention and Control Reseach Network, Chapel Hill, NC, USA

## Correspondence: Cam Escoffery (cescoff@emory.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S31

Background

The CPCRN Scholars Program was established in 2021 to prepare early-career professionals in dissemination and implementation (D&I) science to address challenges in cancer prevention and control across public health, clinical, and academic settings. Targeting graduate students, postdoctoral researchers, early-career faculty, and practitioners, the program emphasizes workforce development through applied learning, mentorship, and integration into the CPCRN. The program’s primary goal is to strengthen D&I capacity by combining knowledge-building with real-world experience and peer collaboration.

Methods

Developed by a national workgroup of CPCRN investigators, the year-long CPCRN Scholars program includes asynchronous training modules (i.e., NCI Implementation modules/Putting Public Health Evidence into Action curriculum), monthly webinars with national experts, and mentorship from experienced D&I scientists. Scholars also join CPCRN workgroups and complete a mentored project that applies D&I methods to a priority issue in cancer prevention and control. The program encourages cross-institutional learning, professional networking, and active participation in collaborative research. A mixed-methods evaluation across three Scholar cohorts (n = 62) included pre/post surveys, semi-structured exit interviews, and a nine-month alumni follow-up to assess changes in D&I knowledge, program satisfaction, and continued involvement in implementation science.

Findings

Participants reported a significant increase in the total D&I competency scores for all three cohorts for 4 overarching domains and 43 subdomains (MPre = 1.38 MPost = 1.89), particularly in theory-informed planning and adaptation of evidence-based interventions. Most CPCRN Scholars completed a mentored project and engaged in CPCRN workgroups, which facilitated further skill development and collaboration. Scholars reported gains in confidence, conceptual clarity, and ability to apply D&I approaches. Alumni have completed dissertations, presented at national conferences, published peer-reviewed manuscripts, secured faculty roles, and remained active in D&I research networks, suggesting sustained engagement in the field. About 80% remain engaged with CPCRN workgroups and investigators.

Implications for D&I Research

The CPCRN Scholars Program offers a replicable model for training early-career professionals in cancer D&I science. Findings support the value of integrating formal instruction with mentored project work and network participation. The program may inform future efforts to expand D&I capacity across systems involved in cancer prevention and population health.

Primary Funding Source: Centers for Disease Control and Prevention

## Clinical care settings: patient-level interventions

## S32 Implementation facilitation of motivational interviewing to activate pain supports (MAPS) for veterans separating from military service

## Steve Martino1,2, Marc Rosen3

## 1Veterans Health Administration, West Haven, CT, USA; 2Yale University School of Medicine, New Haven, CT, USA; 3Yale School of Medicine, Department of Psychiatry, West Haven, CT, USA

## Correspondence: Steve Martino (steve.martino@yale.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S32

Background: Over half of veterans at the Department of Veteran Affairs (VA) in the United States have musculoskeletal disorders (MSD) connected to military service. MAPS helps veterans seeking MSD VA disability compensation engage in pain care. In a 1,101 person, 8-site, 36-week, Type 1 hybrid trial, veterans receiving MAPS, compared to those in usual care, had significantly more motivation for pain care, more pain care encounters, and reduced pain intensity, pain interference, and impaired work ability. We sought to implement MAPS more widely and sustainably in VA.

Methods: We partnered with the VA’s Post-9/11 Military2VA (M2VA) Case Management Program, where case managers serve recently separated veterans, including those with MSD VA disability claims. M2VA Case Managers screen every recently separated Veteran new to VA health care for case management needs, ensuring they can connect to necessary care, services and resources. MAPS, based on motivational interviewing, is compatible with this case management approach. We used Implementation Facilitation (IF), with a Relational Coordination framework, to support case managers’ reach, adoption, and implementation of MAPS with veterans.

Findings: National M2VA leadership collaborated in the design and obtainment of an NIH-funded Type 2 hybrid trial testing the effectiveness of IF to support the use of MAPS in the program. Pre-implementation activities included presentations to key stakeholders, tailored IF activities and MAPS to fit M2VA workflow, and input from a formative evaluation. M2VA leadership recruited an employee to support MAPS implementation and helped secure 14 VA facilities to receive IF for MAPS. IF teams consisted of local pain experts as internal facilitators, M2VA program managers as champions, and pain and addiction treatment experts with IF experience as external facilitators. Eighty-eight case managers treating 30,000 new veterans annually were trained in MAPS, facilitating its use in Veteran engagement. National efforts to reduce the VA workforce have complicated MAPS implementation, though facility participation remains robust.

Implications for D&I Research: During a period of institutional change, invested national leadership, tailored IF and MAPS to fit M2VA practice, and continued organizational support through IF have been essential for sustaining MAPS and integrating M2VA into VA pain care.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S33 Barriers and facilitators to implementing a telehealth mindfulness-based intervention for pain within the VA healthcare system

## Diana Burgess

## Veterans Health Administration, Minneapolis, MN, USA; Minneapolis, MN, USA

## Correspondence: Diana Burgess (diana.burgess@va.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S33

Background: The objectives of the LAMP (Learning to Apply Mindfulness to Pain) trial was to test the effectiveness of two scalable, mindfulness-based interventions (MBIs) for Veterans with chronic pain and collect and describe implementation data.

Methods: Hybrid Type 1 effectiveness-implementation trial of two mindfulness-based, telehealth interventions, delivered via self-paced and group-based formats. We collected pre-implementation data from multi-level stakeholders, including leaders from Veterans Affairs (VA) program offices who would be responsible for administering LAMP. Analysis focuses on key facilitators and barriers to implementation, organized using the Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research.

Findings: Trial results showed that both MBIs were effective at improving pain and pain-related outcomes among Veterans (N = 811). We are now implementing the self-paced LAMP in VA within pain management teams, and there is a pending project to implement LAMP through virtually delivered academic detailing services in rural sites. This success in moving LAMP into clinical practice was informed by implementation science, including development of multi-level stakeholder relationships, who helped identify barriers and facilitators and provided input on adaptation of MBIs to fit the local context. The LAMP intervention was perceived as addressing an important need and aligned with VA priorities, with the self-paced MBI seen as low-resource and relatively easy to implement. LAMP was also seen as credible because it was studied with VA patients and published in a recognized journal. Outer setting facilitators were its alignment with Congressional mandates, the opioid epidemic, and ability to be delivered to rural Veterans via telehealth. The process of implementation was facilitated by ongoing engagement with operational partners, stakeholders, and champions, including through collaboration within the Pain Management Collaboratory (PMC). Outer setting barriers included federally driven plans to significantly restructure VA and reduce the VA workforce. These outer setting barriers negatively affected the inner setting, with project partners and champions facing competing priorities and uncertainties.

Implications for D&I Research: Results underscore the importance of close and ongoing collaboration with operational partners, and the benefits of a structure such as the PMC that facilitates collaboration, as well as potential outer setting barriers.

Primary Funding Source: Department of Veterans Affairs

## S34 Whole health options and pain education: new findings from the wHOPE trial and experiences using dissemination and implementation strategies

## Karen Seal

## San Francisco VA Health Care System, San Francisco, USA

## Correspondence: Karen Seal (Karen.Seal@va.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S34

Background: Chronic pain is a significant problem within the Veterans Health Administration (VA). VA has promoted the “Whole Health” model, a patient-centered approach emphasizing multi-modal non-pharmacological, complementary integrative health (CIH) approaches (e.g., yoga, mindfulness) and interdisciplinary care for chronic pain. To date, only observational evidence supports the use of the Whole Health model in clinical care. In this 12-month pragmatic Type 1 hybrid implementation-effectiveness trial, 764 veterans with chronic pain were enrolled across 6 VA healthcare systems. Results showed that a Whole Health Team approach was superior to Cognitive Behavioral Therapy for Chronic Pain and Usual VA Pain Care in improving pain interference and other key secondary outcomes (e.g., pain severity, pain treatment satisfaction). With these positive trial results, we aimed for broader and sustainable implementation within the VA system.

Methods: The VA Office of Patient-Centered Care that developed the Whole Health model and the Office of Pain Management that funds Pain Management Teams have facilitated virtual meetings with VA clinical and operational study partners to disseminate results. Evidence-Based Quality Improvement (EBQI) methods have guided the presentation of trial results and solicitation of partner input regarding scaling of the Whole Health Team approach in VA clinical care settings. Emergent themes are analyzed using rapid qualitative methods.

Findings: To date, the research team has completed several EBQI meetings with the following operational partners: national-level VA leadership, local VA facility leadership in Pain Management and Whole Health, and volunteer wHOPE clinicians at each study site. Meetings to obtain veteran input are planned, and results from a budget impact analysis are forthcoming. In presenting trial results favoring the Whole Health Team approach for chronic pain management that includes a Whole Health coach conducting Personalized Health Planning in collaboration with pain and CIH clinicians, trial partners have suggested strategies to make incremental budget-neutral changes to existing Pain Management Teams to incorporate the Whole Health model.

Implications for D&I Research: Ongoing VA and veteran partner input and buy-in using D & I strategies shows promise in further scaling the Whole Health Team approach to improve chronic pain management across the VA healthcare system.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S35 Building toward national implementation: findings and outcomes of the Cooperative Pain Education and Self-management (COPES) Expanding Treatment for Real-world Access (Extra) trial

## Alicia Heapy

## Veterans Health Administration, West Haven, CT, USA

## Correspondence: Alicia Heapy (alicia.heapy@va.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S35

Background: Cognitive behavioral therapy for chronic pain (CBT-CP) is a first line nonpharmacological treatment that improves pain and functional outcomes, but access barriers and treatment burden reduce uptake. A clinician-guided asynchronous version of CBT-CP called COPES is effective for improving pain related outcomes but the best way to implement it in the VA system is unknown. Over several trials we evaluated implementation strategies to address patient and system barriers to the use of COPES culminating in the COPES ExTRA Type 1 hybrid implementation-effectiveness trial of 764 veterans with chronic pain enrolled across 9 VA healthcare systems. COPES was superior to standard CBT-CP in improving pain interference, pain intensity, depressive symptoms, and sleep. COPES participants attended significantly more sessions, waited fewer days to begin treatment, and had lower healthcare costs in the year following treatment compared to standard CBT-CP. Based on these results, we pursued wider implementation of COPES in VA.

Methods: A series of prior trials implementation trials shaped the selection and refinement of implementation and treatment delivery strategies used in COPES ExTRA. Sustained partnership with the VA Pain Management, Opioid Safety and PDMP Program (PMOP) provided additional information about VA system needs. Collaboration with the VA Virtual Care QUERI resulted in a new VA-funded hybrid Type 3 implementation trial to evaluate implementation strategies tailored for deploying COPES in rural community-based clinics.

Findings: COPES ExTRA used a combination of implementation strategies identified in prior COPES trials. These included centralized coordination and treatment delivery, patient self-referral mechanisms, and low-burden electronic health record alerts that trigger during clinical visits to improve referral. Based on the strength of COPES ExTRA findings and with the support of PMOP, COPES is now available in VA sites across six Northeastern states with a plan for eventual national implementation. The ongoing QUERI trial will leverage advanced implementation strategy selection methods (e.g., concept/intervention mapping, causal pathway diagrams) to optimize implementation of COPES in rural settings.

Implications for D&I Research: Efforts are underway to scale COPES for systemwide implementation. Successive implementation studies, bundling implementation strategies, and sustained partnership with PMOP have critically informed successful implementation in the complex, nationwide VA system.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S36 Scaling an evidence-based electronic patient reported outcomes (ePRO) program to improve cancer care across a multi-state health system: a system-level approach

## Ursula Wright, Jessica Engelbrecht, Jay Carlson, Dana Haynie, Kerry Bommarito, John Mohart

## Mercy Health, St. Louis, MO, USA

## Correspondence: Ursula Wright (Ursula.Wright@Mercy.Net)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S36

Background: Electronic patient-reported outcomes (ePROs) have demonstrated improvement in symptom management and reducing ED visits and hospitalizations among oncology patients. In 2023, Mercy Health, a multi-state health system, launched its AI-driven, symptom-focused, SMS based ePRO program for adult patients with non-leukemic cancers receiving chemotherapy and identified as highest risk for hospitalization. As a participant in the Patient-Centered Outcomes Research Institute’s Health Systems Implementation Initiative (HSII), Mercy is applying dissemination and implementation (D&I) science to expand ePRO monitoring across three domains: physical function, behavioral health, health-related social needs, while broadening patient reach and access.

Methods: Mercy’s expanded program targets adult outpatients (18 +) receiving chemotherapy alone or with adjuvant therapy across 57 oncology-related clinics across four states. Multi-modal patient engagement (SMS, MyChart, automated calls) enhances accessibility, while automated alerts integrated into Mercy’s single-instance EHR enable real-time clinical monitoring and response. Implementation is guided by the RE-AIM and PARIHS frameworks. A quasi-experimental cohort design compares outcomes between ePRO responders and non-responders. Key system-level strategies include structured design and go-live process, clinician training, standardized workflows, and real-time outcome tracking.

Findings: Early findings (Nov.2023-Dec.2024) identified 6,627 unique patients completed 49,014 surveys with 7% triggering escalation, most commonly severe pain (67%). Responders demonstrated 0.8 percentage point reduction in ED visits within 30 days of chemotherapy (14.8% vs.15.6%). Engagement was highest among women, married patients, and patients in Oklahoma communities; lower response among patients aged 76 + . Mercy’s expanded program will measure improved symptom control and physical functioning and assuring equitable reach across demographic groups. Early implementation efforts focused on program co-design with clinicians and patients and workflow standardization.

Implications for D&I Research: Mercy’s system-level implementation demonstrates how large, diverse health systems can scale evidence-based interventions. Mercy demonstrates how system-level strategies, including integrating patient-centered technology, standardizing workflows and leveraging a unified EHR infrastructure, can proactively improve symptom management through early communication with the clinical care team—reducing ED utilization. It contributes to the D&I science by showing how structured implementation processes such as early stakeholder engagement, change management, and real-time monitoring can be effective for operationalizing and sustaining evidence-based complex interventions in real-world clinical settings, and providing a replicable model for other health systems.

Primary Funding Source: Patient-Centered Outcomes Research Institute

## S37 Cost-effectiveness analysis of digital medicine support models for alcohol use disorder

## Xiang Li1, Randall Brown2, Alice Pulvermacher1, Andrew Quanbeck3

## 1University of Wisconsin Madison, Madison, WI, USA; 2University of Wisconsin School of Medicine & Public Health, Madison, WI, USA; 3University of Wisconsin – Madison, Madison, WI, USA

## Correspondence: Xiang Li (xiang.li2@wisc.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S37

Background

Alcohol use disorder (AUD) is a major public health issue, contributing to substantial morbidity, mortality, and economic burden. Despite its prevalence and impact on health, many patients with AUD (particularly those with mild-to-moderate AUD) go unrecognized or do not receive appropriate intervention. Digital health apps offer scalable solutions, but health systems require rigorous evaluations to inform adoption. This paper presents a cost-effectiveness analysis from a randomized clinical trial comparing three digital health support models for AUD: self-monitored (SM) use (no human support), peer-supported (PS) use (1:many support by peer coaches), and clinically integrated (CI) use (1:1 support by a certified health coach).

Methods

From a health system perspective, we estimated the delivery costs of each model and assessed effectiveness based on reduction in heavy drinking days (4 + drinks for women, 5 + drinks for men). A cost-effectiveness analysis was conducted to evaluate tradeoffs and inform decision-making around program adoption at varying scales of implementation.

Findings

Among 558 participants, all 3 models statistically significantly reduced heavy drinking days from baseline, with annualized reductions of 46, 54, and 75 days per patient for the SM, PS, and CI groups, respectively. Per-patient costs were $103, $953, and $688, respectively. Average cost-effectiveness (cost per reduced heavy drinking day) was $2.24 (SM), $17.65 (PS), and $9.17 (CI). Compared to SM, the incremental cost-effectiveness ratio for CI was $20.17 per additional reduced drinking day; the PS model was dominated. Although between-group differences were not statistically significant, these results suggest potentially meaningful clinical and economic differences.

Implications for D&I Research

Economies of scale are important to consider for health systems implementing peer supported or clinically integrated use. Health coaching represents the most intensive and effective option, with relatively low startup costs but high marginal costs for each patient. High startup costs are required to implement monitoring and response systems for peer-supported use, making it only feasible to implement at scale (i.e., for patient populations above 500). Individuals may have different preferences regarding the level of support provided in using a digital health tool; thus, providing a range of support models may allow for digital health tools to better meet diverse needs of a population.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S38 Microcosting the implementation of extended-release buprenorphine in rural settings

## Amelia Bennett1, Philip Jeng1, Danielle Ryan1, Larissa Mooney2, Yih-Ing Hser3, Sean Murphy1

## 1Weill Cornell Medicine, New York, NY, USA; 2UCLA David Geffen School of Medicine, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 3University of California, Los Angeles, Los Angeles, CA, USA

## Correspondence: Amelia Bennett (agb4001@med.cornell.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S38

Background: Approximately 24% of the US population live in rural counties, 35% of which are classified as high-need regarding access to medications for opioid use disorder (MOUD). Extended-release buprenorphine (XR-BUP) may decrease barriers to rural MOUD, thanks to fewer required doses than sublingual buprenorphine (SL-BUP). The present study aims to estimate the “real world” resources/costs necessary to implement and sustain an XR-BUP intervention in rural settings.

Methods: The parent study is a randomized controlled pilot trial evaluating the effectiveness and feasibility of XR-BUP in rural settings. Participants were randomized 2:1 into XR-BUP or SL-BUP for an intervention period of 14 weeks. An activity-based microcosting analysis was conducted at 3 sites, 2 being primary care facilities with SUD providers and one specialty outpatient recovery clinic. The microcosting analysis entailed semi-structured interviews with relevant personnel to understand the resources required to implement and sustain each intervention. Resources were divided into fixed start-up (supplies, initial staff trainings, etc.), time dependent (recurring meetings, certifications), and variable (participant treatment costs). Variable cost estimates assumed a census of 48 patients annually, per- site, assuming all patients moved from SL-BUP to XR-BUP in a real-world setting. Sensitivity analyses were run varying the percentage of those who transition from SL-BUP to XR-BUP. Nationally representative costs were assigned to each resource unit.

Findings: First-year implementation costs were estimated to be an average of $54,032. Subsequent year sustainment costs were estimated to be $52,413. On average, Fixed start-up costs were $1,619 and time dependent costs were $15,867 across sites. Average annual variable costs were $36,545. The analysis utilized a “real-world”, clinic-level perspective, excluding research and medication costs and assessing the costs of treating patients in a real-world setting. Assuming 48 patients transitioned from SL-BUP to XR-BUP, we found the additional cost per participant on XR-BUP was $71, an overall average difference of $3,434 to maintain a patient population of 48 on XR-BUP versus SL-BUP.

Implications for D&I Research: A comprehensive understanding of the resources required to support rural MOUD will allow stakeholders to mitigate financial risk and uncertainty. Well-informed implementation will assist decision makers in promoting effective and sustainable integration of XR-BUP in rural settings.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S39 Implementation outcomes of nicotine replacement therapy sampling in community dental practices: a national dental practice-based research network cluster-randomized trial

## Sarah Helseth1, Christopher Dunne1, Devyn Fernholz2, Douglas Levy3, Brad Rindal4, Tamara Lischka5, Pearl Fang2, Arthur Truong6, Denis Nyongesa5, Sarah Basile7, Amanda Gillesby8, Pat Ragusa9, Victoria Thomas10, Rita Cacciato10, Dorota Kopycka-Kedzierawski10, Emily Welle11, Matthew Carpenter12, Sandra Japuntich13, The National Dental PBRN Collaborative Group14

## 1Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine, Chicago, IL, USA; 2Hennepin Healthcare Research Institute, Minneapolis, MN, USA; 3Massachusetts General Hospital, Boston, MA, USA; 4HealthPartners Institute, Bloomington, MN, USA; 5Center for Health Research, Portland, OR, USA; 6Kaiser Permanente Center for Health Research, Portland, USA; 7HealthPartners Institute for Education and Research, Bloomington, MN, USA; 8HealthPartners Institute, Bloomington, USA; 9University of Rochester Medical Center, Rochester, NY, USA; 10University of Rochester Medical Center, Rochester, USA; 11Hennepin Healthcare Research Institute, Minneapolis, USA; 12Medical University of South Carolina, Charleston, USA; 13Hennepin Healthcare, Minneapolis, MN, USA; 14None, Birmingham, AL, USA

## Correspondence: Sarah Helseth (sarah.helseth@northwestern.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S39

Background: Oral health provider (OHP) best practices recommend Assessing tobacco use; Advising smokers to quit; and Referral to state quitlines (AAR). AAR connects smokers to counseling but not medications. Giving free Nicotine Replacement Therapy samples (NRTS) has reduced smoking in primary care; it has not been examined in community dental contexts.

Methods: We present feasibility and acceptability outcomes from a hybrid type 1 cluster-randomized trial conducted in the National Dental Practice-Based Research Network (NationalDentalPBRN.org). Practices delivered AAR and were randomly assigned to provide either a 2-week supply of NRTS or an electric toothbrush (ET). NRTS participants included 36 OHPs (n = 23 dentists & dental therapists, n = 13 hygienists) and 486 patients who smoke. Providers rated Organizational Readiness to Implement Change (ORIC), Feasibility (FIM) and Acceptability (AIM) of Intervention Measures, and Intervention Appropriateness (IAM) at pre- and post-implementation. Patient acceptability was a post-intervention satisfaction questionnaire. A subsample of OHPs (n = 15) and patients (n = 11) completed interviews about their experiences with tobacco cessation, NRTS, and future sustainability and scale-out; coding followed best practices.

Findings: Overall, 50% of NRTS patients used either nicotine patches (39%) or lozenges (32%); they reported liking the patch (M = 5.6/10, SD = 2.7) and lozenges (M = 5.1/10, SD = 3.0), indicating acceptability. OHP-reported readiness was high but decreased post-implementation (pre-post mean difference 3.2, p = 0.009); dentists (pre-post mean difference 4.3, p = 0.025) drove the effect, vs hygienists (mean difference 1.19, p = 0.186). OHPs rated NRTS feasible, acceptable and appropriate; scores did not change post-implementation (ps > 0.377). However, NRTS practices recruited fewer participants than ET practices (p = 0.03). Interviewees liked NRTS and found it appropriate for dental settings. Despite instructions to recruit all comers, OHPs noted challenges with recruitment of patients not ready to quit. Patient- and provider-level barriers to sustainability and ideas for scale out were discussed.

Implications for D&I Research: OHPs are ideal to support smoking cessation. Despite strong acceptability, dentists reported lower feasibility post-implementation; hygienist-reported feasibility was steady, indicating role-specific barriers among OHPs that may require additional supports. Patients also noted barriers to longer-term NRT use, indicating NRTS may benefit from enhancement to sustain NRT use.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S40 Insights from a rollout implementation trial of a multi-level strategy for improving depressive symptoms in cardiac patients: the iHeart DepCare hybrid II trial

## Pooja Sonikar1, Jennifer Mizhquiri Barbecho1, Nohora Ospina1, Samantha Simantiris2, Meredith Diamond1, Daniela Suarez-Rebling3, Siqin Ye1, Joseph Schwartz1, Nathalie Moise1

## 1Columbia University Irving Medical Center, New York, NY, USA; 2Care Access, New York, USA; 3Columbia University, New York, NY, USA

## Correspondence: Pooja Sonikar (ps3335@cumc.columbia.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S40

Background: Guidelines recommend preference-driven depression treatment to reduce the risk of recurrent cardiac events. We applied behavioral and implemenation science methods to develop IHEART DEPCARE, a multi-level, theory-informed strategy centered on a digital patient activation tool (eSDM tool) to support patient decision-making. We aimed to assess the strategy’s impact and offer insights into rollout implementation trial and hybrid II designs.

Methods: Due to COVID-19 and limitations in EHR depression screening data, we adapted our stepped-wedge design to a pre-post, single-arm, open-label rollout across four sites (eight primary care and cardiology clinic clusters) randomized to implementation timing. English- and Spanish-speaking adults (≥ 21 years) with CHD and PHQ-9 ≥ 10 were enrolled between 2019–2024 and completed baseline and 6-month follow-up surveys. As a cluster entered post-implementation, behavioral health providers received problem-solving/learning collaboratives (LCs), clinicians received education and preference alerts, and patients received the eSDM tool. Outcomes included Beck Depression Inventory (BDI, primary), decisional conflict, patient activation, treatment barriers, and 6-month self-reported treatment use. Multi-level logistic regression accounted for four clusters. Implementation determinants were assessed using CFIR 2.0 via LC transcripts, clinician interviews, and patient exit surveys.

Findings: Of 2,118 patients approached, 2,086 were screened and 336 enrolled (181 pre-, 155 post-implementation); mean age 67.8 (SD 12.1); 39% Non-Hispanic White, 13.7% Non-Hispanic Black, 17% Other, 11.9% Missing; 47% had a high school education or less. No demographic differences were observed pre- vs post-implementation. There were no significant changes in BDI (17.1 vs. 18.6), patient activation (58.9 vs. 61.7), or new treatment at 6 months (31 vs. 41). Decisional conflict was significantly lower post-implementation (25.1 vs. 36.9). Qualitative data showed the intervention prompted clinician follow-up (e.g., referrals, care adjustments) and facilitated communication between patients, providers, and care teams.

Implications for D&I Research: Our study demonstrates the limited effect of a theory-informed eSDM tool on depressive symptoms and treatment use in real world cardiology and primary care settings. It may be that key multi-level barriers were difficult to overcome, particularly in the context of COVID19, specialty settings, and complex cardiac patient populations. Nonetheless, we demonstrate novel statistical approaches to adapting and analyzing rollout trials and potential mechanistic targets for future research.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S41 Comparing two strategies for implementing evidence-based care for women Veterans: a hybrid type 3 trial using the Stages of Implementation Completion®

## Erin Finley1,2, Alison Hamilton2,3, Ariel Lang4,5, Tannaz Moin2,6, Bevanne Bean-Mayberry2,6, Lisa Saldana7, Rebecca Oberman2, Ismelda Canelo2, Karissa Fenwick2, La Shawnta Jackson8, Rachel Lesser2, Melissa Farmer2

## 1Long School of Medicine, University of Texas Health Science Center, San Antonio, TX, USA; 2Veterans Health Administration, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 3David Geffen School of Medicine, University of California, Los Angeles, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 4Veterans Health Administration, San Diego, CA, USA; 5University of California San Diego, San Diego, CA, USA; 6UCLA David Geffen School of Medicine, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 7Chestnut Health Systems-Lighthouse Institute, Eugene, OR, USA; 8Veterans Health Administration, Los Angeles, USA

## Correspondence: Erin Finley (erin.finley@va.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S41

Background: The Department of Veterans Affairs (VA) EMPOWER 2.0 Quality Enhancement Research Initiative (QUERI) conducted a cluster-randomized hybrid type 3 effectiveness-implementation trial comparing Replicating Effective Programs (REP), a lighter touch strategy involving quarterly technical assistance, and Evidence-Based Quality Improvement (EBQI), a more intensive strategy involving monthly facilitation. These strategies supported implementation of three evidence-based practices (EBPs) focused on virtual care for women Veterans: Telephone Lifestyle Coaching (TLC) and asynchronous Diabetes Prevention Program (DPP), to prevent cardiovascular disease and diabetes, respectively; and Reach Out, Stay Strong Essentials (ROSE), to prevent postpartum depression. We report trial findings comparing progression to implementation competence across 21 VA sites enrolled 2021–2024.

Methods: The Stages of Implementation Completion® (SIC) measures progress across diverse EBPs, settings, and strategies. We developed an adapted EMPOWER-SIC capturing implementation activities across each EBP (TLC, DPP, ROSE) and strategy (REP, EBQI). Sites were randomized to either REP or EBQI and, based on geographic cluster, offered the opportunity to implement either (1) TLC and/or ROSE; or (2) DPP and/or ROSE. On trial completion, we assessed sites’ progression to “implementation competence,” defined as achieving the pre-set patient enrollment goal for each EBP (TLC/DPP: 40 women; ROSE: 10 women).

Findings: Among 6 TLC sites, 67% of REP sites and 33% of EBQI sites progressed to competence. Among 14 DPP sites, 33% of REP sites and 50% of EBQI sites progressed to competence. Among 21 ROSE sites, 45% of REP sites and 50% of EBQI sites progressed to competence. Across EBPs, both REP and EBQI supported progression to competence for some sites, although site-level challenges related to staffing, varied size of patient population, role scope and coordination, and competing demands remained significant. TLC sites faced the additional challenge of a shortened timeline for enrollment, as the contract for TLC services was canceled unexpectedly.

Implications for D&I Research: While implementation trials remain the gold standard for assessing the effectiveness of distinct implementation strategies, these findings underscore the challenges of real-world implementation. Both REP and EBQI were successful in achieving implementation competence in some sites. DPP and ROSE were more complex interventions and implementing sites may have derived additional benefit from the higher-intensity EBQI strategy.

Primary Funding Source: Department of Veterans Affairs

## S42 Implementing structured interdisciplinary bedside rounds to improve team-based care for hospitalized oncology patients: a co-designed quality improvement initiative

## Patricia Litkowski1, Nino Naskidashvili1, Mark Williams2, Jing Li3

## 1Washington University in St. Louis, Saint Louis, USA; 2Washington University in St Louis, St Louis, USA; 3University of Alabama at Birmingham, Birmingham, AL, USA

## Correspondence: Jing Li (jingli2@uabmc.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S42

Background: Hospitalized oncology patients require highly coordinated, team-based care. Hospitalist-led interdisciplinary bedside rounds (IDRs) have the potential to improve communication, care planning, and patient-centered decision-making. Structured models on medical floors such as the Interprofessional Teamwork Innovation Model (ITIM) and the TEMP checklist have demonstrated positive outcomes. However, implementing IDRs in high-acuity oncology settings poses challenges, including workflow disruptions and inconsistent team engagement.

Methods

This initiative was conducted on the Oncology Hospital Medicine co-management units at an academic medical center using a structured, three-phase process:

Context Assessment: Conducted workflow observations, individual interviews with frontline clinicians, and engaged operational and clinical leaders to identify barriers and opportunities.
Model Design & Development: Co-designed the Oncology I-TEAM Care Model, a structured IDR approach tailored to the unit’s clinical and operational needs. Developed standardized rounding protocols and conducted targeted training.
Pilot Implementation: Initiated a mini-pilot, followed by a three-month, weekly, iterative pilot. The project coordinator documented rounds and gathered real-time feedback from frontline to support continuous adaptation. The pilot included over 500 inpatients, 26 hospitalists, ~ 60 nurses, and additional interdisciplinary team members (pharmacists, case managers, social workers). Physician and nurse focus groups were held to assess feasibility, acceptability, and perceived impact.

Findings: Nurses, pharmacists, and case managers attended Oncology I-Team 68%, 73%, and 77% respectively, while hospitalist facilitating all rounds discussion. The average time in patient room was 6 min (SD 3 min) and the average total rounding time for 13 patients was 65 min (SD 19 min). Clinicians reported that I-TEAM improved communication, strengthened care coordination, and supported more patient-centered decision-making. Reported implementation barriers included scheduling constraints, variable team participation due to staffing and clinical interruptions, and time constraints. The co-design process and ongoing engagement were critical in adapting solutions to these challenges. Pilot data suggested acceptability and feasibility, with qualitative findings supporting its impact on team collaboration and care experience.

Implications for D&I Research: This project illustrates the value of structured, iterative implementation and frontline co-design in adapting complex care interventions. The model shows promise for scalability and provides a practical framework for advancing patient-centered, team-based oncology care in hospital settings.

Primary Funding Source: Barnes-Jewish Hospital Foundation Grant

## S43 Implementation of medication adherence assessments in VA primary care clinics led to better chronic medication adherence for high-risk patients

## Timothy Anderson1,2, Stephen Frochen3, Franya Hutchins1,4, Natasha Torrence1, Susan Stockdale3, Elvira Jimenez3,5, Michelle Wong3, Evelyn Chang3,5, Ashok Reddy6,7, Ann-Marie Rosland1,8

## 1Veterans Health Administration, Pittsburgh, PA, USA; 2University of Pittsburgh School of Medicine, Pittsburgh, USA; 3Veterans Health Administration, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 4University of Pennsylvania Perelman School of Medicine, Philadelphia, PA, USA; 5University of California Los Angeles, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 6Veterans Health Administration, Seattle, WA, USA; 7University of Washington, Seattle, WA, USA; 8Division of General Internal Medicine, University of Pittsburgh School of Medicine, Pittsburgh, PA, USA

## Correspondence: Ann-Marie Rosland (Ann-Marie.Rosland@va.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S43

Background: Medication regimen complexity among patients with multimorbidity can lead to inconsistent use of medications for chronic conditions. The VA High-RIsk VETerans (RIVET) QUERI Program implemented the Medication Adherence Assessment (MAA) evidence-based practice for high-risk primary care patients. We assessed adherence barriers detected and effects on chronic medication adherence.

Methods: The MAA assessed adherence barriers in five domains (mental health/substances, health literacy, cognitive impairment, health beliefs, socioeconomic) and prompted clinicians with strategies to address barriers detected. Patients empaneled to participating primary care teams with > 90th-percentile hospitalization-risk score were targeted; some sites focused further on patients with recent ED visits/hospitalizations or uncontrolled chronic conditions. Assessments were delivered by primary care staffAll patients receiving MAA and using at least one chronic medication class (cardiovascular, diabetes, antidepressant, respiratory) at baseline were matched 1:3 to controls based on demographics, risk score, medication class, and site. Cohort entry date was the MAA receipt date, or closest primary care visit date within 30 days for controls. Continuous proportion of days covered (PDC) assessed adherence at the drug class-level in the 6 months pre- and 6 months post-entry date. Difference-in-differences (DID) analyses compared change in adherence between groups, accounting for covariates and within-patient clustering.

Findings: 68 MAA patients at 6 sites (mean age 72 years, 85% male, 82% white) were matched to 202 controls. MAA identified health belief concerns most often (91% patients) and mental health/substance concerns least often (16%). Adjusted mean baseline PDC was 71.1% (95%CI, 58.3% to 84.0%) for MAA and 72.3% (95%CI, 60.6% to 85.3%) for controls. Post-period PDC declined in MAA (-4.6%; 95%CI -8.8%, -0.4%) and controls (-11.2%; 95%CI -13.7%, -8.8%), but the decline was significantly smaller in the intervention group (DID 6.7%; 95%CI 1.8%, 11.5%). Similar trends were observed across medication categories.

Implications for D&I Research: Delivery of MAA to high-risk patients by primary care team members in real-world settings was associated with detection of barriers to adherence and slower declines in chronic medication adherence over time. Health systems could find implementation of complex chronic care management practices particularly beneficial when targeted to the highest-risk patients in their population.

Primary Funding Source: Department of Veterans Affairs

## S44 Intervention fidelity as a driver of implementation success: lessons from the thrive transitional care program

## Heather Brom1,2, Jennifer Gil1,2, J. Margo Brooks Carthon1,2

## 1University of Pennsylvania School of Nursing, Center for Health Outcomes and Policy Research, Philadelphia, PA, USA; 2The Leonard Davis Institute of Health Economics, Philadelphia, PA, USA

## Correspondence: Heather Brom (hmbrom@upenn.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S44

Background

Fidelity measures are often inconsistently reported in many implementation studies, despite their importance in evaluating the effectiveness of complex interventions. Proper fidelity assessment ensures interventions maintain their effectiveness in new contexts while advancing equitable care delivery. This study evaluates fidelity to Thrive, an equity-centered, evidence-based transitional care program providing 30-day post-discharge support to Medicaid-insured individuals. Thrive includes wraparound services, home health, interdisciplinary case management, and extended engagement with hospital-based providers. The objective of this study is to (1) evaluate adherence, dosage, and timeliness of Thrive, and (2) examine whether variations in fidelity explain differences in 30-day readmissions, emergency department (ED) visits, primary care (PC), and specialty care (SC) utilization.

Methods

We conducted an intervention fidelity evaluation during two stepped-wedge randomized control trials of Thrive (March 2023-May 2025) at three teaching hospitals in Philadelphia, Pennsylvania. Fidelity measures were developed using expert consensus, including adherence (8 core components), dosage (up to 4 weeks), and timeliness (home health within 2 days of discharge). Descriptive statistics characterized fidelity measures. Chi-square and t-tests assessed relationships between fidelity and 30-day readmissions, ED visits, PC and SC visits post-discharge.

Findings

Among 308 Thrive participants, intervention fidelity was high, mean adherence was 7.2 components per patient (SD = 0.6), 81.5% received all four weeks (dosage) and 43.2% received timely home health within two days of discharge. Higher adherence was associated with increased PC visits (7.4 vs. 7.2 components; p < 0.05). Patients without readmissions more often received a social needs screening (99.2% vs. 93.7%; p < 0.01). Patients receiving more weeks of Thrive had more PC and SC visits (3.8 vs. 3.6 weeks, p < 0.05). There were no differences in ED utilization by fidelity.

Implications for D&I Research

This study demonstrates the benefits of fidelity monitoring in complex interventions. High Thrive fidelity was achieved, with increased dosage linked to enhanced PC and SC engagement, an essential connection for vulnerable populations. Results suggest that variations in fidelity may drive outcome differences among participants receiving the same intervention. Incorporating fidelity monitoring during implementation of complex interventions can reinforce protocols and promote equitable outcomes. These findings provide methodological guidance for future implementation studies seeking to demonstrate and sustain intervention benefits across diverse healthcare settings.

Primary Funding Source: Rita and Alex Hillman Foundation

## S45 Tailoring site-specific implementation strategy plans for a cesarean risk calculator trial: a mixed-methods approach

## Rebecca Hamm1,2, Rebecca Clark3,4, Mucio Delgado1,5, Elizabeth Howell5, Sarah Hulse5, Alicia Hussey5, Lisa Levine1,5, Mary Steele5, Alexandra DeRita6, Cara Dolin7, Brett Einerson8, Michelle Moniz9, Jessica Page10, Abigail Ramseyer11, Dr. Meghan Lane-Fall12

## 1Leonard Davis Institute of Health Economics, Philadelphia, PA, USA; 2Perelman School of Medicine, University of Pennsylvania, Philadelphia, PA, USA; 3University of Pennsylvania School of Nursing, Center for Health Outcomes and Policy Research, Philadelphia, PA, USA; 4The Leonard Davis Institute of Health Economics, Philadelphia, PA, USA; 5University of Pennsylvania Perelman School of Medicine, Philadelphia, PA, USA; 6Henry Ford Macomb Hospital, Clinton Township, MI, USA; 7Cleveland Clinic, Cleveland, OH, USA; 8University of Utah, Salt Lake City, UT, USA; 9University of Michigan, Ann Arbor, MI, USA; 10Healthcare Delivery Institute, Office of Research, Intermountain Healthcare, Murray, UT, USA; 11University of Michigan Health Sparrow, Lansing, MI, USA; 12Columbia University in the City of New York, New York, NY, USA

## Correspondence: Rebecca Hamm (Rebecca.feldmanhamm@pennmedicine.UPenn.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S45

Background: Our prior single-site study demonstrated that patient and clinician awareness of individualized patient cesarean risk, facilitated by a novel calculator, was associated with reductions in maternal morbidity and cesarean rates. To prepare for a 14-site stepped-wedge type I hybrid effectiveness-implementation trial of this calculator, we developed a multi-phase mixed-methods approach to create context-specific implementation strategy plans that center the opinions of front-line clinicians.

Methods: Guided by CFIR 2.0 and the Implementation Research Logic Model, our embedded sequential mixed-methods study (QUAN- > QUAL; 8/2023–9/2024) systematically identified implementation determinants at 14 diverse labor and delivery sites to inform selection of ERIC strategies most likely to achieve successful local implementation. Strategies were categorized as “set” (required across sites), “optional” (potentially beneficial based on preliminary qualitative data), or “emergent” (responsive to newly identified site-specific barriers). First, we established site-based leadership teams (3–15 obstetricians, midwives, nurses, doulas, and patient/community partners). Next, pre-implementation clinician surveys across sites assessed barriers/facilitators, and perceived benefit/feasibility of four “optional” strategies: (1) preparing patients as active consumers (outpatient calculator discussion), (2) enhancing patient uptake (preliminary risk score outpatient), (3) electronic health record integration, and (4) visual aids for counseling. QUAN data were presented to site-based leadership teams to generate preliminary plans. These plans were refined, including the selection of emergent strategies, and finalized through clinician focus groups at each site.

Findings: Frontline clinicians participated meaningfully across the survey (QUAN, n = 437) and focus groups (QUAL, 21 groups, n = 92). Cross-cutting barriers reported by clinicians included time constraints, buy-in, and competing priorities. Site-specific barriers also emerged, e.g. minimal physician/midwife presence on nurse-driven labor units for risk counseling. Perceived benefit/feasibility of the “optional” strategies varied across sites; leadership teams’ preliminary plans combined these ratings with personal judgement. Focus groups were crucial for finalizing plans and identifying “emergent” strategies, such as development of a direct-to-patient educational video for use at nurse-driven sites.

Implications for D&I Research: This innovative methodology allowed development of 14 context-specific implementation strategy plans that center frontline perspectives prior to a national trial. Ongoing work will evaluate how these tailored plans impact calculator penetration and outcomes after trial completion, offering a scalable model for future multi-site implementation research.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S46 Improving care for older surgery patients at hospital-to-home transitions through participatory adaptation applying CFIR, ADAPT, and FRAME

## Sevdenur Cizginer1,2,3, Ferhat Yildiz4, Matthew Vrees5, Christy Cauley4, Rocco Ricciardi4, Donna Ron6, Steve Bartels4

## 1Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, USA; 2Division of Palliative Care and Geriatric Medicine, Massachusetts General Hospital, Boston, MA, USA; 3Center for Aging and Serious Illness, Massachusetts General Hospital, Boston, MA, USA; 4Massachusetts General Hospital, Boston, MA, USA; 5Brown University, Providence, RI, USA; 6Meir Medical Center and Tel Aviv University, Kefar Sava, Israel

## Correspondence: Sevdenur Cizginer (scizginer1@mgh.harvard.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S46

Background: Hospital-to-home transitions pose significant risks for older surgical patients, yet evidence-based transitional care models remain largely unadapted for surgical populations. This implementation gap persists despite proven models for medical patients. Our study addresses this by systematically adapting and implementing a transitional care intervention for surgical patients using mixed methods in light of implementation science frameworks.

Methods: We employed a multi-step implementation science process. First, guided by a Logic Model approach, we used mixed methods to systematically integrate core components of an evidence-based medical care transitions intervention (CTI) with an inpatient geriatric surgery co-management program (OSCAR), creating the OSCAR-S model. Second, we conducted 42 in-depth interviews with national stakeholders representing 14 disciplines to achieve three objectives: (1) identify implementation determinants using the Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research (CFIR), (2) adapt OSCAR-S using the ADAPT framework to enhance intervention-context fit, preserving core intervention functions, and (3) identify and apply optimal implementation strategies. Throughout this process, we systematically documented all adaptations using the FRAME. Data analysis employed mixed methods and framework matrix approaches.

Findings: Key adaptations to the OSCAR-S included initiating the care transition planning from the pre-surgery phase, integrating telehealth, virtual mentoring from a geriatrician, surgical-specific red flags in coaching materials, and modified workflow integration for surgical patients. Major implementation determinants included model’s comprehensiveness and established connections between nurse coach and surgical team as facilitators. Barriers included competing clinical priorities, cross-system care coordination challenges, and time-intensive patient needs. Implementation strategies addressed these determinants through structural and process adaptations (e.g. embedding nurse coaches in surgical clinics, process redesign including post-implementation feedback loops). As the next step, we will conduct feasibility implementation effectiveness trial of OSCAR-S program guided by RE-AIM framework.

Implications for D&I Research: Implications for D&I Research: This study applied a systematic approach to adapt non-surgical interventions for surgical contexts while preserving their core components, utilizing established frameworks to identify implementation strategies during the adaptation phase. The findings demonstrate that implementation research methods are feasible and contribute to improving care for older surgical patients, particularly in complex interventions spanning multiple care transitions.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S47 Adaptation & testing of an integrated shared decision-making tool for combined family planning and HIV prevention counseling implementation

## Katherine Anderson1, Anandi Sheth2, Whitney Rice1, Alexandra Morshed3, Jessica Sales4

## 1Emory University Rollins School of Public Health, Atlanta, GA, USA; 2Emory School of Medicine, Atlanta, GA, USA; 3Rollins School of Public Health, Emory University, Atlanta, GA, USA; 4Emory University, Rollins School of Public Health, Atlanta, GA, USA

## Correspondence: Katherine Anderson (kmande9@emory.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S47

Background

Approximately 20% of new HIV infections in the United States occur among women, primarily from heterosexual contact. These infections are preventable. Title X clinics provided sexual health (SH) care to > 2 million sexually active women in 2023, but performed fewer than half as many HIV tests. Still, in 2023, Title X clinics diagnosed ~8% of new HIV cases in the US. While essential, HIV testing is suboptimally provided and remains a reactive approach to HIV prevention compared with proactive counseling. Title X clinicians may lack resources- such as Family Planning (FP)-HIV integrated counseling materials- to conduct recommended universal prevention counseling with or without testing. Responsively, we aimed to systematically adapt and assess perceived feasibility and acceptability of an evidence-based FP shared decision-making tool to integrate HIV prevention into FP counseling, to ultimately inform future implementation, effectiveness testing, and scaling.

Methods: Thirteen FP providers at Georgia FP clinics and 28 cisgender women in FP/SH care in Atlanta completed formative interviews. Key adaptations were identified via rapid qualitative analysis and underwent modified Delphi review by 17 FP/SH/HIV practitioners/experts. Adaptations were systematically documented using the FRAME framework; surveys assessing perceived feasibility/acceptability with 30 practitioners and 40 SH care-engaged women will inform final adaptations by 10/2025. Activities were exempt from IRB approval.

Findings: Providers endorsed perceived risk-based HIV prevention counseling with minimal counseling tool availability. Preferences included concise, accessible language, availability for pre-visit patient engagement, with information on HIV prevalence, prevention method comparison (cost/side effects/usage/maintenance) and guided questions. Women preferred universal counseling with minimal personalization that could indicate judgement. Expert review highlighted usefulness of comparison charts for STI/HIV and pregnancy prevention modalities, physical materials with QR codes, counseling checklists, and conversation prompts. Information on PrEP side effects, maintenance visits/testing, effectiveness, and comparison to condoms were prioritized. Responsive adaptation was conducted, followed by preliminary feasibility and acceptability testing.

Implications for D&I Research: Title X clinics represent a high-impact environment for reaching women with HIV prevention counseling, dependent on patient- and provider-sensitive implementation tools and acceptable processes. Formative research and systematic adaptation of resources for HIV prevention are vital to acceptable implementation to reduce HIV burden domestically and globally.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S48 Evaluating the MyPath by text intervention for women Veterans: a hybrid type 1 cluster randomized controlled trial

## Lisa Callegari1, Karin Nelson1, David Arterburn2, Christine Dehlendorf3, Alison Hamilton4, Samantha Benson1, Siobhan Mahorter1, Rachel Hunter-Merrill1, Leslie Taylor1, Aarthi Yogendran1, Caroline Merkel1, Na'imah Muhammad1, Shannon Mitchell5, Sonya Borrero6

## 1Veterans Health Administration, Seattle, WA, USA; 2Kaiser Permanente Washington Health Research Institute, Seattle, WA, USA; 3University of California, San Francisco, San Francisco, CA, USA; 4Veterans Health Administration, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 5Veterans Health Administration, Pittsburgh, PA, USA; 6University of Pittsburgh, Pittsburgh, PA, USA

## Correspondence: Lisa Callegari (lisa.callegari@va.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S48

Background: Discussions about individuals’ reproductive goals and decisions are a critical component of comprehensive primary care but frequently missing from primary care visits at the Veterans Administration (VA).
