MyPath is a patient-facing web-based decision support tool designed to help users identify and discuss their reproductive goals and healthcare needs with providers. We assessed the effectiveness of the MyPath tool and evaluated one-way text messaging as a patient-facing implementation strategy.

Methods: In a hybrid Type 1 cluster randomized controlled trial with primary care provider (PCP)-level randomization, women Veterans ages 18–44 with an upcoming primary care visit received MyPath by Text prior to their appointment (intervention arm) or usual care (control arm). Sixty-six PCPs were randomized and 349 Veterans enrolled across 12 sites. Texts with a link to the tool were sent using VA’s appointment reminder system. Effectiveness was measured post-visit by self-reported occurrence of a reproductive health discussion (fertility/preconception health, contraception) at the visit. Mixed-effects logistic regression with multiple imputation was used for intent-to-treat analysis; complier average causal effect (CACE) analysis was used to account for tool non-use. Text messaging was evaluated among intervention participants by survey (n = 169) and qualitative interviews (n = 23).

Findings: No differences were found between groups in occurrence of reproductive health discussions in intent-to-treat analysis, however only 49% of intervention participants reported using MyPath. In CACE analysis, those intervention participants who used MyPath were more likely to report discussions than controls (74.4% vs. 65.5%, OR 2.81,95%CI:1.02–7.73). Intervention participants valued the ease of accessing MyPath on their phone, however some described missing or ignoring the text, not remembering to complete the tool, and being unsure if the text confirmed that their provider was willing to discuss reproductive goals.

Implications for D&I Research: Effectiveness findings in the MyPath by Text trial were limited by higher-than-expected non-use of the tool. The study, however, generated novel insights on one-way text messaging as a patient-facing implementation strategy for decision support tools. As texts alone generated only moderate use the tool, adding higher-touch strategies (e.g., secure messages in addition to text messages, direct outreach or prompting from clinical staff) could increase use and thus impact.

Primary Funding Source: Department of Veterans Affairs

## S49 Behaviorally-informed text message reminders to increase colorectal cancer screenings: a randomized quality improvement study at federally qualified health centers

## Cathy Jian1,2, Jeremy Lu1,2

## 1NYU Langone Health, New York, NY, USA; 2NYU Grossman School of Medicine, New York, NY, USA

## Correspondence: Cathy Jian (yin.jian@nyulangone.org)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S49

Background: Colorectal cancer (CRC) screening rates among patients at the NYU Family Health Centers (FHCs)—a network of Federally Qualified Health Centers in Brooklyn, NY—remain low. While guidelines recently lowered the recommended screening age to 45, the eligible population has grown, increasing the need for scalable, low-cost outreach strategies. At the FHCs, providers are prompted to order annual fecal immunochemical tests (FIT) for eligible patients, which are distributed in-person or mailed after virtual visits at no cost. Nurses provide education and follow-up phone calls on Day 7 for unreturned tests. Despite these efforts, in 2024 only 43% of FITs were completed within four weeks. Prior studies of mailed FIT campaigns suggest that reminders using behavioral insights, particularly deadlines to support planning, are more effective than standard reminders. This study evaluates whether a behavioral economics–informed, automated text messaging intervention could increase FIT completion rates and reduce outreach burden on clinic staff compared to standard phone-based outreach.

Methods: In a six-week randomized trial, 740 patients with new FIT orders were assigned to either a control group (one phone call on Day 7) or an intervention group (text messages on Days 2, 5, and 8). Messages included a deadline, and return-location details tailored to the patient’s home clinic. Texts were sent in English, Spanish, or Chinese based on the patient’s preferred language. FIT completion was measured at Days 7, 14, and 21.

Findings: Text messages significantly outperformed phone calls with higher FIT completion rates at each follow-up interval with significant differences at Day 14 (52.0% vs. 39.6%, p < 0.001) and Day 21 (57.3% vs. 50.4%, p = 0.024).

Implications for D&I Research: This study demonstrates that a behaviorally-informed, automated text message strategy can improve FIT completion in diverse primary care settings. As a simple and low-cost approach, the intervention yielded measurable improvements in preventive care uptake with minimal operational burden, including saving an estimated 6.8 staff hours per month and enabling approximately 63 additional FITs to be completed within 14 days each month if all patients received text messages instead of phone calls. Future studies will explore message variations to further increase screening uptake and reduce disparities.

## S50 Comparing the effectiveness of two mailed outreach interventions to increase colorectal cancer screening in community health centers

## Folasade May1, Suzanne Brodney2, Jessica Tuan3, Beth Glenn4, Adjoa Anyane-Yeboa5, Dr. Andrew Chan2, Sue Regan5, Sapna Syngal6, Kelley LeBeaux7, Kimberly Schoolcraft8, Gina Johnson7, Chinedu Ukaegbu6, Anjee Davis9, Beverly Moy5, Dr. Erica Warner2, David Drew5, Yuchiao Chang2, Jennifer Haas2

## 1UCLA, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 2Massachusetts General Hospital, Boston, MA, USA; 3UCLA, Los Angeles, USA; 4UCLA Kaiser Permanente Center for Health Equity, Los angeles, CA, USA; 5Massachusetts General Hospital, Harvard Med S, Boston, MA, USA; 6Dana Farber Cancer Institute, Boston, MA, USA; 7Great Plains Tribal Leaders' Heath Board, Rapid City, USA; 8Boston, USA; 9Fight CRC, Springfield, MO, USA

## Correspondence: Jennifer Haas (jennifer.haas@mgh.harvard.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S50

Background: Community health centers (CHCs) provide primary care to over 31 million low-income and uninsured individuals, yet colorectal cancer (CRC) screening rates in these settings are below the national average. Annual fecal immunochemical tests (FIT) are an accessible, inexpensive stool-based screening method. FIT-DNA, a newer option recommended every three years, offers an alternative, though not widely used in CHCs.

Methods: Two-arm pragmatic trial to compare CRC screening rates using FIT and FIT-DNA across eight CHCs, four in Boston and four in Los Angeles. Eligible participants were English or Spanish-speaking adults aged 45 to 75 due for screening. Clinics were matched by region and size, then randomized to receive mailed outreach with either FIT or FIT-DNA. A parallel site in Rapid City, South Dakota (SD) also participated in the FIT-DNA arm but lacked a comparator. In the FIT arm, patients received a mailed kit with instructions and three text message reminders over 35 days. In the FIT-DNA arm, patients received a mailed kit accompanied by the test manufacturer’s standard outreach, including phone calls, texts, and letters. The primary outcome was CRC screening completion within 90 days of kit mailing; secondary outcomes included screening at 180 days and colonoscopy follow-up after an abnormal result.

Findings: Among 5,931 patients across the three regions, 58.4% were female, 64.4% identified as Hispanic, 13.7% American Indian, 56.7% preferred Spanish, and 42.8% Medicaid insured. Baseline screening rates ranged from 24%-67%. Overall, screening completion was 24.3% at 90 days and 27.9% at 180 days. At randomized sites, 90-day screening was significantly higher in the FIT-DNA vs. FIT arm (27.9% vs. 22.6%, p = 0.019), with a meaningful uptake in both arms. In SD, FIT-DNA completion was 17.7%. Among the 126 patients with an abnormal stool test result, colonoscopy completion rate was 38.1% at 180 days; median time to colonoscopy was 69 days.

Implications for D&I Research: These findings demonstrate that mailed FIT and FIT-DNA outreach is feasible in CHCs and that FIT-DNA yields higher CRC screening participation than FIT alone, supporting broader implementation in these underserved settings. Systems are needed to address barriers to colonoscopy among those with positive stool test results.

Primary Funding Source: Stand Up 2 Cancer

## S51 A multilevel randomized clinical trial evaluating clinician training and text message reminders to increase cancer prevention from HPV vaccination among adolescents

## Stephanie Staras1, Tuo Lin1, Pengchen Wang1, Marta Hansen1, Lindsay Thompson2, Matthew Gurka3, Gabriela Sanchez1, Xiaofei Chi1, Christopher Harle4, Elizabeth Shenkman1, Georges Khalil1, Carma Bylund1

## 1University of Florida, Gainesville, FL, USA; 2Wake Forest Baptist Medical Center, Winston-Salem, NC, USA; 3University of Virginia, Charlottesville, VA, USA; 4Regenstrief Institute, Inc., Indianapolis, IN, USA

## Correspondence: Stephanie Staras (sstaras@ufl.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S51

Background: Human papillomavirus (HPV) vaccination rates among adolescents remain lower than the Healthy People 2030 goals. We evaluated the effectiveness of clinician trainings and text message reminders in increasing HPV vaccination rates among 10- to 12-year-olds.

Methods: Florida primary care clinics (n = 30) participated in a randomized trial between March 2022 and September 2023 with follow-up until October 2024. By clinic, clinicians were randomly assigned to one of three trainings: Bundled (recommended adolescent vaccines), Focused (HPV vaccine focus), and deferred. We randomized parents of patients who had not received adolescent vaccines to text message arms (Bundled, Focused, or none). Messages were sent initially (study start or 11th birthday) and with appointment reminders. We estimated Risk Ratios (RRs) for the effects of the interventions on two-year HPV vaccine initiation ( 1 dose) and up to date (UTD) (2 doses) with generalized estimating equations adjusting for clinic-level clustering.

Findings: At 2 years, 28 of 30 (93%) clinics provided vaccination records for 6,520 adolescents. Neither the training nor text message assignments were associated with vaccination rates (Table). Adolescents whose parents received text messages versus not had 17% higher initiation (95% CI 1.08—1.26) and 30% higher UTD rates (95% CI 1.09—1.56). Receiving 2 messages versus 0 increased initiation (RR = 1.43, 95% CI = 1.30—1.58) and UTD rates (RR = 2.00, 95% CI = 1.54—2.59). Among focused trained clinics, UTD rates were higher with bundled versus focused text messages (RR = 1.49, 95% CI = 1.10 – 2.33).

Implications for D&I Research: Evaluating interactions across intervention levels is important to understand interaction effectiveness.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health
 

## Clinical care settings: system-level interventions

## S52 Recruitment and retention lessons learned: EvidenceNOW in Wisconsin primary care clinics

## Edmond Ramly1, Heidi Brown2

## 1Indiana University, Bloomington, IN, USA; 2Kaiser Permanente Southern California, San Diego, CA, USA

## Correspondence: Edmond Ramly (eramly@iu.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S52

Background: EvidenceNOW – Managing Urinary Incontinence (MUI) funded implementation research to improve primary care screening and management of UI in adult women. We compared two implementation strategy bundles [streamlined practice facilitation (SPF) vs. SPF with supplemental partnership-building (SPF + PB)], on adoption and maintenance of UI screening and management in primary care practices in Wisconsin (WI).

Methods: WI primary care practices that enrolled received $600 to support project meetings during participation and $3,000 upon completion of data collection. Consenting practices designated a change team of 2–5 employees to lead the UI screening and treatment intervention for their practice and participated in at least 4 virtual or in-person SPF meetings that followed a menu-based guide to facilitate tailoring and minimize burden. Practices allocated to SPF + PB had facilitated introductions to local UI specialists and community organizations and were enrolled in an online learning community. Change in UI screening rate (from baseline to post- implementation) among clinics allocated to SPF vs. SPF + PB, was ascertained by anonymous patient surveys collected from each practice to ascertain rates of UI screening and treatment before implementation. Participating practices also provided aggregate electronic health record (EHR) data to compare the proportion of adult woman patient visits at which a UI diagnosis was documented before, during, and following implementation.

Findings: Among 38 enrolled practices, 31 (83%) completed participation: 16 (52%) non-academic health system; 7 (23%) independent; 4 (13%) academic health system; 4 (13%) FQHC/tribal clinic. Disenrollment reasons included clinic closure (n = 2), lack of bandwidth for the project (n = 2), inability to collect patient surveys (n = 2), and lack of alignment with practice goals (n = 1). While 8 clinics closed during the study timeframe, only 2 (25%) disenrolled; EHR data were available for 6/8 (75%). Practices affiliated with health systems faced administrative delays; independent practices progressed rapidly through implementation and achieved higher sustainment. Rates of UI screening increased from pre- to post-implementation; ongoing analyses will be presented at the meeting.

Implications for D&I Research: Streamlined practice facilitation (SPF) allowed for tailoring without excessive burden, and effectively improved UI screening and treatment in diverse primary care practices. Minimizing burden facilitates ongoing sustainment, even in the face of clinic closures.

Primary Funding Source: Agency for Healthcare Research and Quality

## S53 Implementation of urinary incontinence management in primary care – a sustainable approach

## Sarah Koopman Gonzalez

## Case Western Reserve University, Cleveland, OH, USA

## Correspondence: Sarah Koopman Gonzalez (sjk98@case.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S53

Background: EMPOWER partnered with 76 primary care practices (PCP) across an integrated health care system to improve diagnosis and care for Urinary Incontinence (UI) among women.

Methods: Participating PCPs were invited to participate in education about UI management and each site was asked to provide a paper UI screener (International Consultation on Incontinence Questionnaire – Urinary Incontinence Short Form (ICIQ-UISF)) and a pamphlet with a link to the screener to all women at every visit. At the end of the intervention, each site was offered a menu of options to sustain screening including the following: paper screeners, integration of the screener in the EMR, training of a staff member as a “Continence Advisor”, having a “Champion” provider, information resources for patients and providers, and a referral list for pelvic floor physical therapy and urology specialists. The implementation team engaged with each practice regularly and the frequency and content of these engagements were documented in a detailed log. Sustainability was prioritized in qualitative assessments of engagement.

Findings: Each site had an average of four contacts regarding sustainability (range 2–6). Additionally, the implementation team had six meetings with leadership of the integrated health system primary care institute which included a discussion of sustainability. These contacts included a study introductory meeting with medical directors and onboarding visits with each site which included asking for opinions and suggestions on sustainability. Suggestions included integration of the screener into the EMR. At the end of the intervention period, each site in operation was contacted about sustainability options and in-person visits were conducted with 65 of the sites to offer the menu of sustainability options. Only 12 sites requested one or more of the options.

Implications for D&I Research: Despite an emphasis upon sustainability of management in contact with individual practices, few projects expressed a strong interest in available options to promote sustainability. Nevertheless, the health system has integrated the UI screener into the EMR, which notifies the primary provider when the patient has a score indicating greater than mild symptoms. Understanding the factors besides continued contact around sustainability for individual clinical sites is important for future D&I research.

Primary Funding Source: Agency for Healthcare Research and Quality

## S54 Health system implementation of an automated screening and education tool in primary care for women’s urinary incontinence

## Stephen Persell

## Northwestern University, Feinberg School of Medicine, Chicago, IL, USA

## Correspondence: Stephen Persell (spersell@nm.org)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S54

Background: Urinary Incontinence (UI) is prevalent but under-identified despite its impact on quality of life, mental health and physical function.

Methods: Within a regional health system, a voluntary, clinic-level implementation strategy was offered to 74 primary care practices. Workflow included an EHR-delivered pre-visit questionnaire, in-room screening, interactive educational online module and additional components. Interrupted time series analysis was used to assess changes in management before and after implementation.

Findings: Implementation occurred between January 2023 and December 2024; 43 of 74 practices implemented the workflows. Among 102,041 women with eligible visits, 72,009 (71%) completed screening. Of those, 16.5% reported bothersome symptoms, and 9.1% expressed interest in treatment. Screening rates were high across practices with some variation. More screening was completed using the pre-visit questionnaire than during rooming. The regions of the health system used two different workflows for rooming—one used interruptive alerts, and the other used a passive EHR checklist. Rooming-based screening was more likely to occur with the interruptive alerts. Uptake of the patient-facing module was modest. The mean (SD) clinic-level rate of UI diagnosis increased from 4.2 (1.1) per 100 encounters to 5.9 (1.4), p = 0.011 and annual rate of change in diagnosis was higher post implementation, increasing from 0.58 (95% CI 0.37–0.78) to 1.12 (95% CI 0.66–1.59) per 100 encounters (p = 0.033). Rates of pelvic floor physical therapy orders increased on average (SD) from 0.4 (0.3) to 1.1 (0.7) per 100 encounters; the annual rate of change increased from 0.05 (95% CI -0.03–0.13) to 0.36 (95% CI 0.18–0.54) per 100 encounters (p = 0.001). Referrals to subspecialists increased from 0.5 (0.2) to 0.8 (0.4) per 100 encounters. The rate of new UI medication prescribing was low before and during the program and did not change.

Implications for D&I Research: Implementation of standard EHR-supported workflows led to high rates of UI screening but the adoption was not mandated, and a substantial proportion of practices opted not to adopt. Patients at the adopting practices had high rates of screening and in turn increased their diagnosis of UI and corresponding referrals to pelvic floor physical therapy and subspecialists which appeared to be sustained during the first year after implementation.

Primary Funding Source: Agency for Healthcare Research and Quality

## S55 Effectiveness of a stepped implementation strategy for improving suicide risk screening and evaluation in the Veterans Health Administration: results from a sequential multiple assignment randomized trial

## Nazanin Bahraini1, Bridget Matarazzo1, Jeri Forster1, Trisha Hostetter1, Steve Dobscha2, Catherine Barry3, Katherine Dollar4, Lisa Brenner1, Edward Post5

## 1Veterans Health Administration, Aurora, CO, USA; 2Veterans Health Administration, Portland, OR, USA; 3Veterans Health Administration, Palo Alto, CA, USA; 4Veterans Health Administration, Syracuse, NY, USA; 5Veterans Health Administration, Ann Arbor, MI, USA

## Correspondence: Bridget Matarazzo (Bridget.matarazzo@va.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S55

Background

Suicide prevention remains a critical priority within the Veterans Health Administration (VHA). Despite establishing requirements for universal suicide risk screening and evaluation through the VA Risk ID Initiative, initial uptake of this program varied significantly across VHA facilities. This study evaluated the effectiveness of a stepped implementation strategy using a Sequential Multiple Assignment Randomized Trial (SMART) design to optimize implementation approaches based on facility performance.

Methods

We conducted a three-phase SMART trial involving 138 VHA facilities in partnership with the VA Office of Suicide Prevention. During Phase 1, Implementation as Usual (IAU) was provided to all facilities for three months, which included education & training, quality assurance measures, and technical assistance. During Phase 2, facilities not meeting 70% screening adherence were randomized to continue IAU (n = 69) or receive augmented Audit & Feedback (A/F; n = 69) for eight months. In Phase 3, non-responding A/F sites (n = 57) were randomized to continue A/F only (n = 29) or to receive additional External Facilitation (EF; n = 28). Primary outcomes included measures of adherence for suicide screening and follow-up evaluation (rate and timeliness).

Findings

No facilities met adequate screening targets after Phase 1 (IAU alone). Following Phase 2, sites receiving A/F (n = 69) showed significantly greater improvement in annual screening adherence (p < 0.0001) compared to those receiving IAU alone (n = 69). Among non-responding A/F sites in Phase 3 (n = 57), those that accepted and received EF (n = 17) demonstrated significantly greater improvement in timely screening (i.e., completed when due, p = 0.02) compared to those who continued to receive A/F alone. Sites achieving adequate screening after A/F successfully maintained performance when stepped back to a less intensive A/F strategy, with no significant differences between facilities randomized to continue A/F (n = 6) or switch to A/F Light (n = 6).

Implications for D&I Research

This SMART trial demonstrates the value of adaptive implementation strategies for improving suicide screening adherence in VHA. Audit and feedback provided meaningful benefits over standard implementation approaches, while external facilitation offered additional value for persistently struggling sites. Furthermore, the stepped approach allows for efficient resource allocation while maintaining performance gains, providing an evidence-based framework for scaling suicide prevention initiatives across large healthcare systems.

Primary Funding Source: Department of Veterans Affairs

## S56 Using co-designed Roll-out Implementation Optimization (ROIO) to enhance implementation in a hybrid type-2 comparative effectiveness RCT to prevent health care worker burn-out

## Stephen Bartels1, Giselle Perez-Lougee2, Hao Trieu Weinmann2, Aimee Huang2, Julie Levison2, Kelly Irwin2, Nicholas Oreskovic3, David Krane2, Anna Wilson2

## 1Massachusetts General Hospital, Harvard Medical School, Boston, NH, USA; 2Massachusetts General Hospital, Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, USA; 3Massachusetts General Hospital, Harvard Medical School, 02114, MA, USA

## Correspondence: Stephen Bartels (sjbartels@mgh.harvard.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S56

Background: Front-line healthcare workers in high stress, understaffed, public sector settings serving individuals with complex needs are at high-risk of burn-out, anxiety, depression, missed workdays, and turnover. Evidence-based practices have been shown to be effective in improving resiliency and reducing workplace stress, however factors contributing to burnout are also systemic barriers to participation, including multiple competing demands, managing challenging clinical and social needs, and understaffing. We hypothesized that overcoming system-wide barriers to implementing health care worker burnout interventions will be facilitated by adaptations co-designed with community providers applying roll-out implementation optimization (ROIO).

Methods: We conducted a system-level, hybrid type-2 effectiveness-implementation RCT with n = 184 group homes in two behavioral health provider organizations for persons with serious mental illness and developmental disabilities randomized to either 6-sessions of group-based integrated resiliency training and task sharing (IRTTS: n = 92 group homes) or 9 months of workplace improvement learning collaboratives (WILC n = 92 group homes). Participatory co-design and roll-out optimization (ROIO) was employed in phase two and three including weekly implementation dashboards monitoring staff uptake and participation reviewed with providers (audit and feedback) followed by problem-solving sessions identifying barriers to participation and co-designing and implementing adaptive solutions. Implementation outcomes include uptake and participation by eligible staff and group home managers.

Findings: Phase one IRTTS uptake: 28% of group home staff; mean participation 19%. WILC uptake: 74% of group home managers; mean participation 42%. Phase 2 and 3 (respectively) with audit and feedback and ROIO participatory co-designed adaptations: IRTTs uptake 38% and 53% of staff with mean participation 36% and 48%; WILC uptake 82% and 77% with mean participation 76% and 66% of managers. Co-designed adaptations included offering make-up, booster, and evening sessions; and combined WILC sessions across the two participating agencies. Staff reported increased feasibility, and acceptability of IRTTS and WILC following adaptations of IRTTS and WILC through ROIO.

Implications for D&I Research: ROIO co-designed adaptations contribute to overcoming systemic barriers to improve implementation uptake and participation in interventions to prevent burnout in health care providers serving individuals with complex needs in public sector behavioral health organizations.

Primary Funding Source: Patient-Centered Outcomes Research Institute

## S57 Budget impact analysis of the balanced opioid initiative: a cluster randomized trial aimed at de-prescribing opioids for chronic pain in primary care settings

## Andrew Quanbeck1, Xiang Li2, Alice Pulvermacher2, Randall Brown3

## 1University of Wisconsin – Madison, Madison, WI, USA; 2University of Wisconsin Madison, Madison, WI, USA; 3University of Wisconsin School of Medicine & Public Health, Madison, WI, USA.

## Correspondence: Andrew Quanbeck (arquanbe@wisc.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S57

Background

This study compared the budget impact of four de-implementation strategies designed to promote guideline-concordant opioid prescribing per the Centers for Disease Control’s opioid prescribing guidelines for chronic pain.

Methods

This cluster randomized trial (involving two health systems, 32 primary care clinics, and 268 clinicians) used a hybrid type 3 sequential multiple-assignment design. De-implementation strategies targeted the system, clinic, and prescriber levels. All clinics received system-level quarterly educational meetings with monthly audit/feedback reports. At month 3, half were randomized to practice facilitation, a clinic-level strategy (in addition to the system-level strategy). At month 9, half the clinics were again randomized to add prescriber peer consulting, a prescriber-level strategy, resulting in 4 groups: system, system/clinic, system/prescriber, and system/clinic/prescriber. The primary outcome was change in mean morphine milligram equivalent (MME) dose. Secondary outcomes included adherence to opioid risk mitigation metrics (urine drug testing, treatment agreements, pain/function screening, and mental health screening). We calculated the cost of delivering each implementation strategy and budget impacts associated with changes in healthcare utilization, including pairwise comparisons between groups.

Findings

Among 8978 patients (57.3% female; mean age 58.3 years), the primary contrast between the system/clinic/prescriber vs. system strategies showed statistically significantly changes in mean MME (-2.4 mg/d, 6% reduction), increased pain/function screening (5.4%), decreased urine drug screening (–7.3%), and decreased treatment agreements (–6.7%). Implementation costs per clinic ranged from $4,416 (system), $5,610 (system/clinic), $7164 (system/prescriber), and $8,358 (system/clinic/prescriber). With healthcare utilization changes incorporated, the system strategy’s adjusted per-clinic costs rose from $4,416 to $10,908, yielding the highest budget impact due to increased healthcare utilization; conversely, budget impact for clinics in the system/clinic/prescriber group were largely unchanged at $8,703. The system/clinic strategy had the lowest budget impact at $7,266; system/prescriber’s budget impact was $9,625.

Implications for D&I Research

Higher-intensity de-implementation strategies significantly reduced MME dose and increased pain/function screening, while decreasing treatment agreements and urine drug screening. Somewhat paradoxically, the lowest cost strategy (system) led to more costly downstream healthcare utilization, resulting in the highest budget impact. Adding clinic- and prescriber-level strategies may help health systems reduce reliance on opioids for chronic pain with lower budget impact than providing system-level strategies alone.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S58 Running the gauntlet to attain sustainable impact by embedding interventions into health systems: addressing feasibility constraints of systems and the priorities of many masters

## Allison L'Hotta1, Katy Trinkley2, Russell Glasgow3, Amy Huebschmann4

## 1University of Colorado Anschutz Medical Campus, Aurora, CO, USA; 2UCHealth, Aurora, CO, USA; 3Adult and Child Center for Outcomes Research and Delivery Science (ACCORDS), Aurora, CO, USA; 4University of Colorado School of Medicine, General Internal Medicine, General Internal Medicine, Aurora, CO, USA

## Correspondence: Allison L'Hotta (ALLISON.LHOTTA@CUANSCHUTZ.EDU)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S58

Background

A cornerstone of implementation science is to align delivery of programs with the context of real-world settings and recipients. For system-level interventions, this requires balancing fidelity to core functions for research rigor with local adaptations. Digital health interventions integrated into the electronic health record (EHR) face additional feasibility constraints and organizational approval chains that influence the decision to adopt. Based on a successful case example of EHR integration, our objectives were to: 1) align with key health system decision-maker priorities and characteristics and 2) define implementation strategies to overcome feasibility constraints to EHR integration.

Methods

We report our experience integrating a digital health patient intervention, My Own Health Report (MOHR), into the EHR of a large academic health system. Core functions of MOHR included: 1) flag health risks; 2) prompt patient to set goals for priority risks. Using Practical, Robust Implementation and Sustainability Model (PRISM), we examined how multi-level factors, and the contextual infrastructure influenced the decision to adopt and integrate in the EHR.

Findings

Four organizational teams and approval processes were critical to MOHR’s adoption and EHR integration: 1) Clinician leaders in primary care, 2) EHR informatics, 3) Learning Health System, 4) EHR Patient Experience. While clinician leaders prioritized MOHR adoption due to alignment with value-based care metrics (e.g., blood pressure control), they could not ultimately approve its adoption due to the informatics team’s limited bandwidth. A Learning Health System leader identified an alternate partner due to the alignment of MOHR with their priorities to increase Wellness goal setting for patients: the EHR Patient Experience team. To meet the EHR Patient Experience team’s requirement for non-clinical, Wellness-focused domains, MOHR was adapted from its original 17 risks to four health risks while still maintaining the MOHR intervention core functions. Additionally, representation of the MOHR results in the EHR required tailoring to both system feasibility constraints and the preferences and needs of clinicians and patients.

Implications for D&I Research

Integration into the EHR has tremendous potential for impact by supporting system-wide implementation and sustainability. For successful implementation, researchers must align interventions with health system priorities, adapt to partner preferences and constraints, while maintaining fidelity to core functions.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S59 Enhancing the implementation of clinical practice and office systems changes in primary care: the impact of practice facilitation from the STop UNhealthy Alcohol Use Now (STUN) trial

## Sean Riley1, Sarah Birken2, Leslie Brouwer1, Marcella Boynton3, Colleen Barclay4, Debbie Grammer5, Chris Weathington5, Monique Mackey6, Mary McCaskill7, Adam Zolotor8, Kimberly Shoenbill4, Sam Cykert9, Darren DeWalt10, Daniel Jonas1

## 1The Ohio State University Wexner Medical Center, Columbus, OH, USA; 2Wake Forest University School of Medicine, Winston-Salem, NC, USA; 3University of North Carolina, Chapel Hill, NC, USA; 4University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill, Chapel Hill, NC, USA; 5University of North Carolina, Chapel Hill, USA; 6Area L AHEC, Rocky Mount, NC, USA; 7North Carolina Area Health Education Centers, Chapel, USA; 8University of North Carolina, Chapel Hill, Chapel Hill, NC, USA; 9University of North Carolina School of Medicine, Chapel Hill, NC, USA; 10UNC Chapel Hill, Chapel Hill, NC, USA

## Correspondence: Sean Riley (sean.riley@osumc.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S59

Background: Implementation of evidence-based interventions in primary care remains challenging, due to variability in implementation policies and practices. Practice facilitation (PF), which provides quality improvement coaching, electronic health record support, and structured implementation guidance, may address these barriers by standardizing processes and building organizational capacity. Nested within the STop UNhealthy Alcohol Use Now (STUN) trial, this analysis evaluates whether PF improves clinical practice and office systems changes to support the adoption of alcohol screening and counseling.

Methods: A cohort of small-to-medium primary care practices in North Carolina received 12 months of PF. Monthly changes were measured using the Key Driver Implementation Scale (KDIS), a validated tool assessing the extent to which practices have implemented quality improvement (QI) activities aligned with the Chronic Care Model (range, 0–45). KDIS evaluates progress across six domains: Clinical Information Systems, Standardized Care Processes, Optimized Team Care, Patient Self-Management Support, Leadership Support, and Fiscal Health. Linear mixed-effects models were used to assess KDIS scores over 18 months, with baseline alcohol screening rate (high [≥ 50%] vs. low [< 50%] of adult patients screened at baseline) tested as a moderator.

Findings: Twenty-one practices serving 54,294 adult patients reported implementation data. There were statistically significant linear and quadratic effects for time (month; ps < 0.001). Marginal effects indicated a predicted KDIS score of 18.7 at baseline, which incrementally increased over the study period by ~9 points, sustaining a predicted total score of ~28 points during the post-implementation period (months 12–18). We also observed a significant interaction between time and baseline alcohol screening rates, where practices with higher baseline rates showed faster and greater increases in KDIS scores compared to those with lower rates.

Implications for D&I Research

PF in the STUN trial led to statistically significant improvements in clinical practice and office system changes that support implementation. Notably, these effects were greatest in clinics with higher baseline screening rates, suggesting that existing infrastructure may amplify the impact of PF on implementation policies and practices. Future research should evaluate whether PF yields comparable improvements across other preventive services and settings, while clarifying the mechanisms that drive and sustain these changes over time.

Primary Funding Source: Agency for Healthcare Research and Quality

## S60 Embedding UI care into PC: lessons from the evaluation of a multi-site implementation initiative

## Shona Olalere Oluwatola1, Nipher Malika2, Gabriela Alvarado3, Rachel Burns4, Sangeeta Ahluwalia2

## 1RAND, Santa monica, CA, USA; 2RAND Corporation, Santa Monica, CA, USA; 3RAND Corporation, Washington, DC, USA; 4RAND, Santa Monica, CA, USA

## Correspondence: Shona Olalere Oluwatola (oolalere@rand.org)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S60

Background

Urinary incontinence (UI) is a prevalent yet underdiagnosed condition among adult women, often overlooked in primary care settings. To address this gap, five federally-funded grantees implemented system-level interventions aimed at integrating evidence-based, non-surgical UI care into routine primary care workflows. Conducted across 269 sites nationally, this initiative focused on improving screening, diagnosis, and management of UI by embedding changes in practice operations, staff roles, and patient engagement strategies. Here we present lessons learned from the multi-site evaluation on the system-level strategies and factors that supported adoption and early implementation of UI management in primary care.

Methods

Over three years, our evaluation team conducted 36 data collection sessions including semi-structured interviews, focus groups with grantee leaders, practice facilitators, and clinicians as well as practice site visits. All interviews were audio-recorded, transcribed verbatim, coded on Dedoose, and analyzed using a thematic analysis approach. All five grantees submitted quarterly quantitative data on participating practices, providers, and patients.

Findings

Using the RE-AIM framework to guide interpretation, we identified key themes. For Reach, practices varied in patient outreach, with higher engagement and higher likelihood of meeting target practice and patient recruitment goals in sites with leadership buy-in, greater staff capacity and prior experience with quality improvement initiatives. In terms of Effectiveness, the initiative improved provider awareness and shifted UI from a neglected issue to one addressed through structured care. Adoption of UI screening and management revealed that one-size does not fit all and practices and healthcare systems need to find what works for them and their patients. Implementation varied across sites, with practice facilitators adapting tools to practice workflows. Under Maintenance, some practices sustained changes, while others struggled due to turnover, competing demands, or limited tool integration.

Implications for D&I Research

This study underscores the value of tailoring implementation supports—such as practice facilitation, training materials, and workflow tools—to the needs, capacity, and culture of each site. Practices that adapted tools to their local context and engaged motivated staff were more likely to sustain screening and care. In contrast, grantees facing leadership engagement gaps reported greater adoption challenges. D&I strategies should address variation in staffing, leadership, and infrastructure to enhance effectiveness.

Primary Funding Source: Agency for Healthcare Research and Quality

## S62 Co-development of an intrinsic capacity management programme and its implementation strategies in primary care for mildly frail older adults in Singapore: application of implementation science frameworks and tools

## Ezra Ho1, Siew Fong Goh1, Qing Wang1, Mimaika Luluina Ginting1, Jonathan Gao1, Woan Shin Tan1, Yew Yoong Ding1, David Ng Wei Liang2, Jonathan Ng Choon Aik2, Sing Cheer Kwek3, Richard Hui Jor Yeong3, Sinead Wang Zhen4, Chirk Jenn Ng4,5, Grace Sum1

## 1Geriatric Education & Research Institute, Singapore, Singapore 2National Healthcare Group Polyclinics, Singapore, Singapore; 3National University Polyclinics, Singapore, Singapore; 4SingHealth Polyclinics, Singapore, Singapore; 5Duke-NUS Medical School, Singapore, Singapore

## Correspondence: Yew Yoong Ding (ding.yew.yoong@geri.com.sg)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S62

Background

To translate evidence-based complex healthcare innovations into practice, integrating programme co-development and tailoring of implementation strategies from the outset are critical to ensure contextual fit, sustainability, and scalability. This study serves two purposes: 1) To translate Singapore’s 2023 National Frailty Strategy and the World Health Organization Integrated Care for Older People (WHO ICOPE) into practice by co-developing an intrinsic capacity management programme in five public primary care clinics; and 2) To co-select, co-specify, and co-develop tailored implementation strategies for each clinic.

Methods

Stakeholders (clinic leadership, healthcare workers, and older adults) from 5 primary care clinics participated in co-development meetings over a 6-month period to establish consensus on the programme’s core functions and delivery. The Framework of Actions for Intervention Development guided development through problem definition, stakeholder consensus, function selection and programme prototyping. Concurrently, contextual determinants (barriers and enablers) were elicited from interviews and focus groups with the stakeholders was guided by the updated Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research (CFIR). The co-selection of implementation strategies was guided by the CFIR-Expert Recommendations for Implementing Change matching tool and literature-informed criteria (relevance, improvement opportunity, feasibility, difficulty, validity). Each strategy was specified using Proctor’s framework (actor, action, action target, temporality, dose, justifications, implementation outcomes).

Findings

37 stakeholders (8 administrators; 10 clinicians; 10 nurses; 9 allied health practitioners) reached consensus on the core functions of the innovation across clinics, including six-domain IC screening (vision, hearing, mobility, vitality, cognition, psychological well-being) for older adults of Clinical Frailty Scale score 4–5, further clinical assessment, and referral pathways to health and social care services. Each clinic developed adaptable forms of delivery of core functions, including clinical assessment tools, protocols, workflows, and referral partners. Additionally, each clinic selected different combinations of 2 to 5 strategies to mitigate anticipated barriers and leverage anticipated enablers. These comprised educational meetings, mobilizing champions, implementation team meetings, patient nudging using text messages, and altering incentive structures. Strategies were specified to facilitate operationalization and fidelity tracking.

Implications for D&I Research

Integrating systematic stakeholder-driven co-development with tailored implementation strategies provides a comprehensive implementation science approach to accelerate the translation of national policies into clinical practice in ways that are sustainable and scalable.

Primary Funding Source: Geriatric Education and Research Institute

## S63 Co-creation during crisis: harnessing implementation science to achieve impact

## Christine Fahim1, Jamie Boyd2, Adrienne Chan3, Allison McGeer4, Sharmistha Mishra5, Stefan Baral6, Jennifer Gommerman7, Anne-Claude Gingras8, Rob Delatolla9, Claire Oswald10, Kim Gilbride11, Mario Ostrowski12, Keelia Quinn de Launay13, Alyson Takaoka13, Liana Sikharulidze14, Sharon Straus13

## 1Li Ka Shing Knowledge Institute, Unity Health Toronto, Toronto, ON, Canada; 2Knowledge Translation Program, Li Ka Shing Knowledge Institute, St. Michael’s Hospital, Unity Health Toronto, Toronto, ON, Canada; 3Sunnybrook Health Sciences Centre, Toronto, Canada; 4Sinai Health System, Toronto, Canada; 5Li Ka Shing Knowledge Institute, St. Michael's Hospital, University of Toronto, Toronto, Canada; 6Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health, Baltimore, MD, USA; 7Dept of Immunology, University of Toronto, Toronto, Canada; 8Sinai Health System, Lunenfeld-Tanenbaum Research Institute, Toronto, Canada; 9Faculty of Engineering, University of Ottawa, Ottawa, Canada; 10Dept. of Geography and Environmental studies, Toronto Metropolitan Universityal, Toronto, Canada; 11Dept. of Chemistry and Biology, Toronto Metropolitan University, Toronto, Canada; 12Dept. of Laboratory Medicine and Pathobiology, University of Toronto, Toronto, Canada; 13Li Ka Shing Knowledge Institute, St. Michael’s Hospital, Toronto, ON, Canada; 14Toronto, Canada

## Correspondence: Christine Fahim (christine.fahim@unityhealth.to)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S63

Background: People living and working in long-term care and retirement homes were among the most affected by COVID-19, yet had limited ability to participate in research during the pandemic response. We describe the development and impact of Wellness Hub, which evaluated the impact of tailored strategies to address COVID-19 challenges and facilitated SARS-CoV-2 research.

Methods: Using the Theoretical Domains Framework, we rapidly interviewed 91 leaders from 47 homes to identify their COVID-19 support needs, which spanned infection prevention and control (IPAC), vaccine uptake and confidence, and staff mental health. We assigned 48 homes to an intervention arm (tailored resources + guided facilitation) and 24 to a control arm (generic resources) and evaluated the impact on challenge resolution using a poisson regression (rate/10 challenges, rate-ratio,95% CI). We formed a multidisciplinary network that spanned > 20 research and clinical disciplines and included front-line home staff and caregivers, to define immunity research questions pertaining to SARS-CoV-2. We conducted an immunity study that collected dried blood spots, demographic questionnaires, and other serosamples from residents, their caregivers, staff, and their household members. We also designed a wastewater study to track SARS-CoV-2 in congregate care settings to inform outbreak prevention measures.

Findings: We delivered 38 original educational resources, 50 education events, 22 community of practice meetings and funding supports to 72 LTCH/RH. Homes in the intervention arm were two times more likely to resolve COVID-19 challenges (2.02; 95% CI 1.50–2.70, p < 0.0001); IPAC challenges (1.85; CI 1.25–2.73, p = 0.0002) and four times more likely to resolve vaccine challenges (rate-ratio 3.78; CI 1.97–7.25, p = < 0.0001). Wellness Hub data advanced knowledge on SARS-CoV-2 seroprevalence and antibody response to mRNA vaccinations and were used to shape the national COVID-19 vaccine schedule in Canada. We implemented wastewater surveillance in 12 care settings and 20 neighborhoods where staff lived; data were fed back to homes and public health authorities to prompt implementation of outbreak prevention measures.

Implications for D&I Research: We used implementation research methods to rapidly co-design an adaptive support strategy that addressed homes’ needs, while facilitating the conduct of multidisciplinary research among marginalized staff and residents at risk of infection and associated morbidity and mortality.

Primary Funding Source: COVID-19 Immunity Task Force

## S64 Impact of the acute inpatient medicine – high reliability, learning environment, and workforce development initiative (AIM-HI) in Rural Veterans Health Administration Hospitals

## Heather Gilmartin1,2, Brigid Connelly2, Marguerite Daus2,3, Edward Hess2, Amy Ladebue2, John P. Nolan Jr.4, Paige Perry5, Heidi Sjoberg2, Soumya Subramaniam2, Melver Anderson6, Chelsea Leonard2,7

## 1Colorado School of Public Health, Aurora, CO, USA; 2Veterans Health Administration, Aurora, CO, USA; 3University of Colorado Anschutz Medical Campus, Division of General Internal Medicine, Aurora, CO, USA; 4Independent Researcher, El Dorado, AR, USA; 5Veterans Health Administration, Seattle, WA, USA; 6Veterans Health Administration, Washington D.C., DC, USA; 7University of Colorado Anschutz Medical Campus, Aurora, CO, USA

## Correspondence: Heather Gilmartin (heather.gilmartin@va.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S64

Background: Rural healthcare systems often struggle with limited resources, leading to provider shortages and higher patient mortality which necessitate innovative workforce development strategies. AIM-HI is an evidence-based workforce development program designed to foster supportive learning environments within Veterans Affairs (VA) rural hospital medicine teams to enhance culture and workforce well-being.

Methods: AIM-HI is a type II hybrid implementation study employing a convergent mixed methods approach to evaluate the Relational Playbook, a 6-month virtual team training program for clinical leaders (physician, nursing, social work, pharmacy). Guided by VA’s learning healthcare system model, implementation strategies were tailored for rural settings and included behavioral nudges, learning collaboratives, and leadership coaching. In 2023, AIM-HI was implemented in 3 rural VA hospitals with hospital medicine programs and expanded to 7 more in 2024. The primary outcome was AIM-HI’s acceptability, appropriateness, and feasibility with secondary outcomes on leader, staff, and learning environment impacts, all evaluated through surveys and interviews. Quantitative data were summarized, while qualitative data were thematically analyzed and triangulated with quantitative data.

Findings: From 2023 to 2024, AIM-HI enrolled 100 clinical leaders across 10 VA hospitals. Our first cohort (n = 24) rated AIM-HI’s implementation as acceptable, appropriate, and feasible (range 4.0–4.3; n = 11/24; 46%). They reported that Playbook practices enhanced team dynamics, increased engagement, required minimal time, and delivered significant benefits. The second cohort (n = 76) expressed a substantial need for the program and reported greater job satisfaction, reduced burnout, and stronger desire to stay in VA, suggesting effective leadership promotes psychologically safe, high-functioning teams. Both cohorts experienced engagement challenges from clinical demands. Facilitators included leadership coaching and learning collaboratives. Barriers were staffing shortages and increased workloads requiring a focus on patient care versus team development.

Implications for D&I Research: AIM-HI is a feasible and impactful rural hospital medicine workforce development program that supports clinical leaders’ professional growth and well-being. Despite barriers, the program's positive impact on staff relationships and work environments highlights its potential to optimize the VA workforce to meet rural Veterans’ needs. This work shows how D&I science can drive scalable, sustainable solutions to strengthen leadership and workforce resilience.

Primary Funding Source: Department of Veterans Affairs

## S65 Health system-level implementation support strategies for an evidence-based cervical screening intervention in Ghana

## Nadia A. Sam-Agudu1, Emily Frueh2, Obed Cudjoe1, Alex Dankyi1, Nancy Ebu Anyan1, Sebastian Ken-Amoah1, Patrick K. Akakpo1, Dorcas Obiri-Yeboah3, Matthew Asare2

## 1University of Cape Coast, Cape Coast, Ghana; 2Baylor University, Waco, TX, USA; 3University of Cape Coast School of Medical Sciences, Cape Coast, Ghana

## Correspondence: Nadia A. Sam-Agudu (agudu@umn.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S65

Background

The HOPE-inG study is a Type 2 hybrid implementation-effectiveness trial evaluating cervical cancer (CC) screening uptake among women living with HIV (WLWH) in Ghana. The HOPE multicomponent intervention consists of socio-behavioural communication messaging using the 3R Model (Reframing, Reprioritising, Reforming), HPV self-sampling, and patient navigation. We elucidate implementation strategies that addressed determinants of HOPE implementation at the health systems level.

Methods

This was the Preparation phase in our Exploration, Preparation, Implementation, and Sustainment (EPIS) framework-informed trial. We used a four-step nominal group technique (Generate ideas, Round-robin sharing, Discussion, and Voting) to rapidly collect data on barriers and facilitators from 4 nominal groups: WLWH (intervention recipients; N = 16), healthcare workers (intervention deliverers; N = 16), government officials (N = 17), and health facility managers (N = 16) (policy/decision-makers). Determinants were organized using the Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research (CFIR) and matched to implementation strategies in the Expert Recommendations for Implementing Change (ERIC). We focused on determinants and corresponding implementation strategies relevant to healthcare workers and health facility managers. We named these implementation strategies “implementation support strategies” (ISS), defined as implementation strategies most relevant to implementation support, which is concerned with moving research into practice through activities like training, providing tools and ongoing monitoring and evaluation.

Findings

Twenty-nine highly-ranked determinants (13 facilitators, 16 barriers) were identified across all CFIR domains: Inner Setting (12), Outer Setting (6), Implementation Process (6), Innovation (2), and Individuals (4). These determinants were matched to 73 non-unique implementation strategies. A total of 24 unique ISS specific to implementation support were identified. The top seven ISS were: Conduct ongoing training; Audit and provide feedback; Conduct educational meetings; Facilitation; Identify and prepare champions, Use data experts; and Use data warehousing techniques.

Implications for D&I Research

Implementation science promotes adoption and sustainability of EBIs by guiding the process of moving research to practice. We identified ISS as key implementation strategies to facilitate this process for the evidence-based HOPE intervention. These ISS included training, supervision and monitoring, and mentoring/technical assistance. The ISS categorization allows for identification and further evaluation of implementation strategies specifically addressing implementation support and bridging factors between the Inner implementation and Outer policy setting at the health systems level.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S66 Sustainability of evidence-based strategies for HPV vaccination in a large, urban Federally Qualified Health Center

## Jennifer Tsui1, Samantha Garcia1, Michelle Shin2, Emily Dang1, Morgan Gill1, Artur Galimov1, Zehui He3, Sharon Hudson4, Benjamin Crabtree5, Lourdes Baezconde-Garbanati1, Lawrence Palinkas6

## 1University of Southern California, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 2University of Washington, Seattle, WA, USA; 3University of Southern California, Los Angeles, USA; 4AltaMed Health Services Corporation, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 5Rutgers Robert Wood Johnson Medical School, New Brunswick, NJ, USA; 6UC San Diego, La Jolla, CA, USA

## Correspondence: Jennifer Tsui (Tsuijenn@usc.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S66

Background: Numerous HPV vaccination evidence-based strategies (EBS) have been implemented in recent years, but a major challenge is integration and sustainment of EBS within dynamic clinical settings long-term. Our prior work evaluated implementation of HPV vaccination EBS in safety-net settings. We now evaluate longer-term sustainability outcomes and factors that impact sustainability for three EBS implemented in a large, urban Federally Qualified Health Center.

Methods: Using a mixed-methods design, we applied the Rapid Assessment Procedure-Informed Clinical Ethnography (RAPICE) approach and Shelton’s RE-AIM extension to guide sustainability assessment April-June 2025 (18 months after implementation). The initial implementation study collected data at baseline (Oct-Dec 2022) and 12-months (Nov-Dec 2023). EBS implementation (Dec 2022-Nov 2023) included: provider communication training, clinic staff education/workflow training, and audit-and-feedback with leaders and clinic champions. We assessed continued use and impact of EBS across sites and clinical roles. We also used the Clinical Sustainability Assessment Tool (CSAT) to identify factors that lead to sustainability.

Findings: A total of 81 surveys (39 providers, 42 clinic staff) and 25 qualitative interviews (10 providers, 9 clinic staff, 5 system leaders) were completed. Surveys showed sustained EBS impact on discussion of HPV vaccination starting at age 9 (baseline: 63%, 12-month: 82%, sustainability: 88%) and high confidence in addressing parental vaccine concerns (baseline: 36%, 12-month: 48%, sustainability: 69%). Participants reported high CSAT factors for the provider communication training: easy to use (100%), integrated well into clinical workflow (85%) and can be used consistently (85%). CSAT factors for staff trainings were also high (> 89% across similar measures). Qualitative data showed that while there was agreement all EBS were important, survey and interview findings showed mixed perspectives on interest in sustaining provider trainings. In contrast, there was strong support for integrating staff trainings into onboarding or self-learning tools within nursing education workflows.

Implications for D&I Research: Our findings show engagement with providers, staff, and system leadership can inform how to sustain EBS for HPV vaccination beyond a research-funded intervention period. Adequate co-design from initial implementation and routine feedback with stakeholders within clinics can provide inputs that accelerate long-term system adoption and sustainability of interventions.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S67 Engaging practice champions to develop a system-wide implementation plan for hypertension management at primary care practices at UPMC

## Elaine Kwok

## UPMC Center for High-Value Health Care, Pittsburgh, PA, USA

## Correspondence: Elaine Kwok (kwoke@upmc.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S67

Background

UPMC created a standardized process to more efficiently plan for practice changes across its integrated payer-provider system as part of the PCORI Health System Implementation Initiative. This presentation outlines the application of this process, particularly the engagement with practice champions, to develop an implementation plan for hypertension evidence-based practice (EBP) adoption in primary care practice (PCP) settings.

Methods

First, a summary of the hypertension EBP was shared with health system leaders from primary care, EHR/IT infrastructure, data analytics, quality improvement, and payer teams to assess priority and feasibility for system-wide implementation. After the EBP was considered both important and feasible, independent outreach was conducted with each PCP group to assess interest in EBP adoption, and to discuss contextual factors and timelines. Lastly, interested practice groups were encouraged to identify a practice champion, a frontline provider from within each practice group, who was willing to work with the implementation team to design an implementation plan.

Findings

The process secured leadership support and built a strong business case for system-wide hypertension EBP adoption. Thirteen PCP groups and their practice champions informed a feasible, phased implementation approach involving 130 sites. Early adoption sites (N = 30) tended to be affiliated with academic or research hospitals (e.g., through residency training programs or previous research collaborations) and have strong leadership and administrative support, and agreed to help pilot and finalize new clinical workflow and EHR modifications. Mid-adoption sites (N = 50) tended to have existing quality improvement initiatives and/or dedicated quality improvement officers, and have established incentive programs with payers based on blood pressure control rates, and were willing to onboard once all EHR modifications are finalized. Sites preferring later participation (N = 50) cited staff shortages and burnout, limitations in data infrastructure, and concerns about workflow changes; therefore, they preferred to start implementation after outcomes data become available from early adoption sites.

Implications for D&I Research

System-wide implementation of EBP can be complex, but establishing a standardized process and engaging frontline practice champions helped the implementation team consider multi-level factors that determined the scope, pace, and milestones to facilitate EBP adoption in different contexts.

Primary Funding Source: Patient-Centered Outcomes Research Institute

## S68 Applying a transformative healthcare cyle to aid in informing interventions and developing a stakeholder driven, economically sustainable implementation plan for uncontrolled hypertension at primary care practices, urgent care and emergency departments

## Joyce Kerpchar

## AdventHealth, Orlando, FL, USA

## Correspondence: Joyce Kerpchar (joyce.kerpchar@adventhealth.com)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S68

Background

AdventHealth applied the Transformative Healthcare Cycle—a standardized, enterprise-wide framework—to support evidence-based decision-making and implementation of hypertension management strategies. As part of the PCORI Health System Implementation Initiative, this model was used to guide intervention selection, implementation planning, and sustainability across primary care, urgent care, and emergency department settings.

Methods

The framework includes four phases: (1) Design – establishing urgency, engaging stakeholders, and aligning with strategic priorities; (2) Pilot – generating early wins and addressing barriers; (3) Implement – scaling successful practices; and (4) Sustain – embedding changes into organizational culture. During the Design phase, a comprehensive assessment evaluated the alignment of the PCORI Hypertension Initiative with AdventHealth’s clinical priorities. This included analysis of population reach, supporting evidence, payor mix and overall economic feasibility. Findings were presented to leadership to inform intervention selection and build consensus.

Findings

Presenting clinical and economic impact data helped secure leadership support and informed a phased implementation plan. Analysis showed that patients presenting to emergency and urgent care settings with uncontrolled hypertension could benefit from a structured management program. A total of 385 clinical sites were identified—275 primary care practices and 128 urgent/emergency care locations. Based on feedback from a multidisciplinary steering group, implementation was staggered, beginning with primary care pilot sites. Dashboards are being developed to monitor clinical outcomes and economic feasibility, enabling real-time evaluation and resource planning.

Implications for D&I Research

This study illustrates how structured frameworks can guide leadership and clinical teams in the selection, implementation, and long-term sustainability of evidence-based interventions. The integration of timely economic evaluations into the implementation strategy, along with real-time tracking of both clinical and economic outcomes, supports adaptive decision-making and promotes sustainability across diverse care settings.

Primary Funding Source: Patient-Centered Outcomes Research Institute

## S69 Development of hypertension clinical decision support tools as an implementation strategy at WellSpan Health

## Nicole Hurst

## WellSpan Health, York, PA, USA

## Correspondence: Nicole Hurst (nhurst@wellspan.org)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S69

Background

Through participation in the PCORI Health System Implementation Initiative, WellSpan Health developed a standardized, interdisciplinary team-based approach to guide large-scale implementation initiatives. This presentation describes the application of that approach to the design and development of a digital technology, hypertension-focused clinical decision support (CDS) tools within Epic, WellSpan’s electronic health record (EHR). These tools, including a SmartSet orderset and an Our Practice Alert, were selected as core implementation strategies to promote care standardization and support pragmatic evidence-based care delivery across primary care.

Methods

An interdisciplinary team composed of primary care clinicians, informatics, data analytics, pharmacy, nursing, quality improvement, and community health leaders collaboratively led CDS tool development. The team reviewed current workflows, regional variation in clinical practice, and national hypertension guidelines to shape the tools’ structure and content. Key components included evidence-aligned lab and medication ordering, referral pathways, documentation templates, and embedded patient education. User-centered design principles ensured the tools were intuitive, clinically relevant, and integrated into varied workflows. Stakeholder perspectives were essential in uncovering workflow nuances, clinical preferences, and adoption barriers. Feedback gathered through usability testing, live demonstrations, and iterative refinement cycles informed both the design and implementation planning.

Findings

CDS tool development revealed variation in hypertension care across WellSpan’s 63 primary care sites. Several unintended workflow disruptions surfaced, including role misalignment and friction caused by overlapping or nonintuitive tools. Interdisciplinary collaboration was key to surfacing and addressing these issues in real time. The process improved alignment with frontline operations and supported strategies to build provider trust. CDS tools were intentionally aligned with the expansion of Remote Patient Monitoring, embedding workflows and decision support to enhance hypertension management. Implementation planning emphasized in-person facilitation and relational engagement as key strategies to support adoption and build capacity across care teams.

Implications for D&I Research

Digital technologies, such as CDS tools, can support implementation at scale. Leveraging user-centered design through interdisciplinary collaboration strengthens stakeholder engagement, supports workflow integration, and enhances sensitivity to context and the nuances of care delivery. Iterative development, guided by diverse perspectives and responsive to variability across clinical settings, can produce more acceptable, feasible, and adaptable tools, better positioned for successful implementation and sustained use.

Primary Funding Source: Patient-Centered Outcomes Research Institute

## S70 Implementing the National Hypertension Control Initiative in 350 Federally Qualified Health Centers from 2021 to 2023

## Eduardo Sanchez

## American Heart Association, Dallas, TX, USA

## Correspondence: Eduardo Sanchez (eduardo.sanchez@heart.org)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S70

Background

In December 2020, the Office of Minority Health (OMH) and Health Resources & Services Administration (HRSA) announced the National Hypertension Control Initiative (NHCI) a three-year implementation project to improve blood pressure (BP) control in 500 Federally Qualified Health Centers (FQHCs). The 500 FQHCs (roughly one-third of all FQHCs) were afforded funding from HRSA to provide self-measured blood pressure (SMBP) devices for patients with hypertension (HTN), and the American Heart Association (Heart) was awarded $32 million through a 3-year cooperative agreement with OMH and HRSA to provide training and technical assistance (T&TA) for 350 of the 500 FQHCs to clinical care teams on implementation of evidence-based practices (EBP) to improve BP control. This abstract describes the implementation strategies to support adoption of EBP to improve BP control in 350 FQHCs in the lowest quartile for BP control.

Methods

A three-year T&TA curriculum based on the AMA MAP™ framework (measure accurately, act rapidly, partner with patients), ongoing assessment of clinical care practices and BP control, and feedback mechanisms were the key implementation strategies selected.
The clinical T&TA integrated a core medical educational curriculum, evidence-based tools and resources, and evaluation processes. T&TA was delivered through webinars, workshops, office hours, structured forums, and one-on-one coaching. T&TA was modified and tailored to address the evolving competencies and needs of the FQHCs. The webinar-based curriculum initially focused on hypertension screening, diagnosis, and management and the addition of technology webinars focused on SMBP devices, electronic health records, and remote patient monitoring. The activities spanned three years, with distinct goals and interventions for each year.

Findings

In year 3, 94% of FQHCs reported having a documented protocol or process for BP measurement, 79% using a BP treatment protocol, and 97% having an SMBP program. In the 350 FQHCs receiving T&TA, BP control rates increased from 51.1% in 2020 before NHCI to 60.9% in 2023 (19.2% relative improvement). By comparison, BP control improved 11.4% in the other FQHCs.

Implications for D&I Research

T&TA is a highly scalable implementation strategy. Further analysis will help delineate facilitating factors and barriers to BP control improvement in the participating FQHCs.

Primary Funding Source: Office of Minority Health

## S71 Planning for sustainability: implementing a severe hypertension safety bundle in outpatient maternity care

## Jennifer Leeman1, Aparna Kachoria2, Alasia Ledford2, Shelby Smith-Janey2, Maihan Vu2, Narges Farahi2, Johanna Quist-Nelson2, M. Kathryn Menard3

## 1UNC Chapel Hill, School of Nursing, Chapel Hill, NC, USA; 2University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill, Chapel Hill, NC, USA; 3The University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill, Chapel Hill, NC, USA

## Correspondence: Jennifer Leeman (jleeman@email.unc.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S71

Background: Severe hypertension increases risk for serious pregnancy-related complications. The AIM Severe Hypertension During Pregnancy and Postpartum Period Safety Bundle (HTN Bundle) has demonstrated effectiveness at improving early detection and treatment of severe HTN in inpatient settings but has not been tested in the outpatient setting. To address this gap, we adapted the HTN Bundle for the outpatient setting (O-HTN), tailored strategies to support implementation (training, facilitation, simulation, and education materials), and are testing implementation and effectiveness in 19 clinics. We report findings on O-HTN Bundle sustainability for the study’s first complete cohort of clinics (n = 7).

Methods: Using an explanatory sequential mixed-methods design, we administered the Clinical Sustainability Assessment Tool (CSAT) to clinic implementation team members (n = 20, 74% response rate) during month 10 of the 12-month implementation phase. We then engaged each implementation team (n = 7) in a semi-structured discussion of factors contributing to their CSAT results (month 11) and in meetings to plan for sustainability (month 12). Descriptive statistics were used to summarize data from the CSAT. Two team members used content analysis to code transcripts and meeting notes and met to reconcile findings.

Findings: Mean scores on each of six CSAT domains ranged from 5.48 to 6.42 on a 7-point scale. Themes from qualitative findings identified barriers and facilitators within each domain. For example, for the CSAT domain “organizational readiness”, barriers included high staff turnover rates, competing priorities, and limited coordination across levels within a clinic network. For “monitoring & evaluation”, barriers included difficulty accessing electronic health record (EHR) data and limited capacity for quality improvement. For the domain “engaged staff and leadership”, facilitators included having a provider champion, leadership support, and a being a small clinic. Implementation teams identified a range of strategies they would use to sustain implementation (e.g., conduct team debriefs following a severe HTN event).

Implications for D&I Research: Sustaining interventions beyond active implementation is challenging. We applied mixed methods to identify barriers to sustainment and strategies clinics might use to overcome them. Findings will inform refinement of existing implementation strategies and the need for new strategies targeting factors beyond the clinic (e.g., network leadership and EHR systems).

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S72 Understanding determinants influencing longitudinal pathways from implementation to sustainment of moral injury groups in the Veterans Health Administration: realizing the importance of “liminal” sustainment

## Andrea L. Nevedal1, Marilla Widerquist2, Maria Arasim2, Caitlin M. Reardon3, Jason Nieuwsma4, Melissa Smigelsky4, Summer Anderson4, Heather A. King5,6, Brandolyn White7, Keith Meador4, George Jackson4,8

## 1Veterans Health Administration, Ann Arbor, MI, USA; 2VA Ann Arbor Healthcare System, Ann Arbor, MI, USA; 3VA Center for Healthcare Evaluation, Research, and Promotion, Pittsburg, PA, USA; 4Durham Veterans Affairs (VA) Health Care System, Durham, NC, USA; 5Duke University School of Medicine, Durham, NC, USA; 6Veterans Health Administration, Durham, NC, USA; 7Durham VA Health Care System, Durham, NC, USA; 8University of Texas Southwestern Medical Center, Dallas, TX, USA

## Correspondence: Andrea L. Nevedal (Andrea.Nevedal@va.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S72

Background: People experience moral injury (MI) when violations of deeply-held moral beliefs (e.g., killing someone in combat) lead to feelings of guilt, shame, or loss of meaning. The Veterans Health Administration (VA) developed MI-groups co-led by mental health providers and healthcare chaplains. We conducted a trial to compare an organizational network strategy called the Dynamic Diffusion Network (DDN) to technical assistance (TA) at sites implementing MI-groups. Our objective was to understand determinants influencing longitudinal pathways from implementation to sustainment of MI-groups.

Methods: Thirty-three semi-structured interviews were conducted among 18 eligible MI-group co-facilitators from nine VA sites (4 = DDN, 5 = TA) during pre-implementation and post-implementation of MI-groups. One-year post-implementation, 16 sustainment surveys were conducted. The updated Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research (CFIR) and the CFIR Outcomes Addendum informed rapid directed content analysis of qualitative interview and survey data (determinants indicated in parentheses). Outcomes included: anticipated implementation, actual implementation, anticipated sustainment, and actual sustainment.

Findings: Of the nine sites, six (2 = DDN, 4 = TA) had consistent successful pathways from anticipated implementation to actual sustainment to anticipated future sustainment. Three of the nine sites (2 = DDN, 1 = TA) had inconsistent pathways. Two DDN sites had inconsistent pathways because they successfully implemented MI-groups but had “liminal” sustainment (neither fully sustained as intended nor discontinued). One TA site had an inconsistent pathway because they anticipated successful implementation but did not complete implementation/discontinued MI-groups. Facilitators to implementation/sustainment included having DDN support (Implementation Facilitator); providers referring Veterans to MI-Groups (Engaging); co-facilitators to lead MI-Groups (Opportunity); and support for implementation (High-Level Leaders). Barriers to implementation/sustainment included losing a co-facilitator without having a replacement (Opportunity); insufficient eligible Veterans to refer (Recipient Need); and insufficient support for implementation (High-Level Leaders). Among DDN sites in “liminal” sustainment, one anticipated future sustainment as “likely” if replacing a co-facilitator (Opportunity) and the other anticipated future sustainment as “neither likely/unlikely” because of insufficient eligible Veterans for MI-group referral (Recipient Need).

Implications for D&I Research: Most sites were consistently successful throughout implementation to sustainment of MI-groups. However, findings point to important future work defining and understanding sites in “liminal” sustainment, which may include enhancing earlier support and internal collaboration for those experiencing organizational change or needing adaptations.

Primary Funding Source: Department of Veterans Affairs

## S73 Who needs to lead? Dimensions of leadership needed for system-wide, sustainable, integrated behavioral health implementation

## Ann M. Nguyen1, Alexandra Williams2, Lisa Mikesell3, Anushka Purkayastha1, Stephanie Marcello4, Agota Szabo5, Ralph Gigliotti6, Benjamin F. Crabtree7

## 1Rutgers Center for State Health Policy, New Brunswick, NJ, USA; 2Rutgers Robert Wood Johnson Medical School, New Brunswick, NJ, USA; 3Rutgers University, New Brunswick, NJ, USA; 4Rutgers University Behavioral Health Care, Piscataway, NJ, USA; 5Centre of Expertise on Global Governance, The Hague University of Applied Sciences, The Hague, Netherlands; 6Rutgers Center for Organizational Leadership, New Brunswick, NJ, USA; 7Department of Family Medicine and Community Health, Rutgers Robert Wood Johnson Medical School, New Brunswick, NJ, USA

## Correspondence: Ann M. Nguyen (anguyen@ifh.rutgers.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S73

Background: Despite the availability of comprehensive integrated behavioral health (IBH) protocols, IBH has not been widely adopted across U.S. health systems. The literature has identified that a lack of effective leaders is a key barrier to successful IBH implementation. Powell et. al (2015) have recognized the critical role of leaders in intervention implementations in three discrete implementation strategies (i.e., inform local opinion leaders; have leaders mandate change; recruit/designate/train for leadership). Our study fills a gap in IBH implementation by examining who needs to lead IBH for successful system-wide, sustainable IBH implementation.

Methods: Qualitative in-depth interviews were conducted to identify the role, characteristics, and experiences of leaders in IBH implementation. We interviewed a national sample of prominent IBH leaders and experts (n = 41, 82% response rate), which included distinguished researchers, primary care providers with intensive IBH experience, behavioral health providers, IBH implementers, and senior policy influencers from a wide range of healthcare organization types. Rapid analysis during data collection and subsequent immersion-crystallization were conducted to ensure thematic saturation and identify themes.

Findings: Interviewees identified multiple dimensions of leadership that must be engaged to institute system-wide IBH adoption. Leaders must be engaged at the individual clinic level as well as the system level. Within each clinic, frontline staff and clinic leadership collectively serve as change advocates and gatekeepers.
