There must also be engagement from health system C-suite leaders, who can embed IBH into the system’s strategic priority setting and allocation of needed resources and incentives. Systems where IBH has not been sustained lacked C-suite engagement. Additionally, leaders from both the primary care and behavioral health disciplines must work together to create a shared mental model.

Implications for D&I Research: As the field of D&I moves toward sustainable system-level interventions, it is critical that we hone implementation strategies that focus on a diversity of leaders at all levels. This study advances the knowledge on leadership dimensions that need to be engaged for IBH, which undoubtedly apply to other systems-level interventions. Our findings also reveal the critical role of C-suite leadership in fostering longevity in the changes. A focus on C-suite interventions merits further development.

Primary Funding Source: Agency for Healthcare Research and Quality

## S74 Sustainability through financing and leadership: a mixed-methods study of tobacco treatment programs in NCI-designated cancer centers

## Miranda Reid1, Magda Montague1, Sofia Anrecio1, Jennifer LeLaurin1, Carma Bylund2, Mara Minion3, Gonghao Liu4, Ji-Hyun Lee2, Edmond Ramly5, Donna Shelley6, Jamie Ostroff7, Graham Warren8, Ramzi Salloum1

## 1University of Florida College of Medicine, Gainesville, FL, USA; 2University of Florida, Gainesville, FL, USA; 3Carbone Cancer Center, University of Wisconsin-Madison, Madison, WI, USA; 4University of Florida Health Cancer Center, Gainesville, FL, USA; 5Indiana University, Bloomington, IN, USA; 6Department of Population Health, New York University School of Medicine, New York, NY, USA; 7Memorial Sloan Kettering Cancer Center, New York, NY, USA; 8University of Kentucky, Lexington, KY, USA

## Correspondence: Miranda Reid (miranda.reid@ufl.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S74

Background: Tobacco cessation markedly improves cancer treatment outcomes. As part of the National Cancer Institute (NCI) Cancer Moonshot program, the Cancer Center Cessation Initiative (C3I) funded 52 NCI-Designated Cancer Centers to promote the development of sustainable tobacco treatment programs (TTPs) from 2017–2021. This analysis characterized leadership and funding strategies used by centers to address TTP sustainability.

Methods: In 2023–2024, TTP leaders at 47 centers completed surveys on sustainability strategies and outcomes. TTPs at 20 of these centers also completed semi-structured interviews on TTP sustainability and adaptation. TTPs were selected for interviews to represent variation in program reach, geographic region, and perceived sustainability based on survey responses. For quantitative data, descriptive statistics were used to characterize use of implementation strategies, core program components, and funding sources. For qualitative data, three authors conducted a thematic analysis using the constant comparative method. Using a concurrent triangulation design (QUAL + quan), analytical integration occurred after initial parallel analysis.

Findings: Analytical integration revealed three core themes: 1) Right-sizing to ensure financial sustainability: in order to sustain funding, TTPs created a program whose scope was sustainable in their context by discontinuing or never starting core program components that were expensive to maintain (e.g. 34.8% maintained interactive voice response) and instead investing in lower-maintenance forms (e.g. 60.9% maintained electronic health record changes); 2) Reliance on institutional funding: given significant administrative hurdles, few programs (27.7%) were able to bill for services (6.4% sole source of funding, 6.4% majority source). Instead, most relied on institutional support after C3I funding ended (44.7% sole source of funding, 19.2% majority source); and 3) Wide use of leadership engagement strategies: TTPs engaged leadership and maintained their support after funding ended by continuing to meet regularly with leaders and champions (74.5%), aligning reported metrics with leadership priorities (55.3%), and marketing their program internally (66.0%).

Implications for D&I Research: TTP’s ability to leverage leadership support and design programs of appropriate scope allowed them to access institutional funding when billing was not feasible. These strategies may be relevant in other implementation contexts, especially in areas where clinical revenue generation is limited.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S75 Designing and adapting a quality improvement project to enhance life-sustaining treatment documentation in VA home-based primary care

## Jennifer Kononowech1, Kate Magid2, Leah Haverhals2,3, Jazmin Beltran2, Cari Levy2,3

## 1Veterans Health Administration, Ann Arbor, MI, USA; 2Veterans Health Administration, Aurora, CO, USA; 3University of Colorado School of Medicine, Aurora, CO, USA

## Correspondence: Cari Levy (cari.levy@va.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S75

Background: The Preferences Elicited and Respected for Seriously Ill Veterans through Enhanced Decision-Making (PERSIVED) intervention aimed to increase documentation of life-sustaining treatment (LST) preferences in the Department of Veterans Affairs (VA) electronic health record. PERSIVED partnered with VA Home Based Primary Care (HBPC) Programs and used audit with feedback and implementation facilitation to increase LST template completion for HBPC Veterans. We describe PERSIVED design and the impact of implementation activities tailored for sites.

Methods: HBPC programs were eligible for PERSIVED if < 50% of their patients had completed LST templates. The 6-month pre-implementation phase included interviews (n = 56) with site staff to identify champions and barriers and facilitators for completing LST templates. During the 15-month implementation phase, site champions participated in coaching calls and received feedback reports showing rates of LST template completion. Process evaluation interviews (n = 67) were conducted at months six and twelve during implementation and used to refine implementation activities. During the 12-month sustainability phase, feedback reports continued, but coaching calls ended, and sites worked independently to maintain improvements.

Findings: Eleven VA HBPC programs and 32 champions participated in PERSIVED, with 2–5 champions per site. Baseline interviews were used to design site-specific implementation plans. Feedback gathered during coaching calls and process evaluation interviews led to iterative tailored implementation activities such as adjusting the frequency of coaching calls, personalizing feedback reports, and providing education outside of regular calls. In interviews, participants mentioned the benefits of tailored feedback reports and satisfaction with the changes made to coaching calls. All 11 HBPC programs participating in PERSIVED increased LST template completion by the end of implementation (rates ranged from 64%-93%) and maintained these improvements during sustainability, suggesting that tailored audit with feedback and facilitation strategies contributed to program engagement and improvements in LST template documentation rates.

Implications for D&I Research: PERSIVED provides a case example of the design and evaluation of a quality improvement project and highlights the importance of collecting data at multiple stages to allow for continuous refinement of implementation strategies. Lessons learned can be used by implementation scientists to support design, implementation, evaluation, and sustainment of interventions conducted in complex healthcare settings.

Primary Funding Source: Department of Veterans Affairs

## S76 Costs to implement an evidence-based practice intervention for seriously ill veterans in the Department of Veterans Affairs

## Tiffany Radcliff1,2, Matthew Griffith2,3, Kate Magid2, Jennifer Kononowech4, Anne Sales4,5, Cari Levy2,3

## 1Texas A&M University School of Public Health, College Station, TX, USA; 2Veterans Health Administration, Aurora, CO, USA; 3University of Colorado School of Medicine, Aurora, CO, USA; 4Veterans Health Administration, Ann Arbor, MI, USA; 5University of Missouri, Columbia, MO, USA

## Correspondence: Tiffany Radcliff (tiffany.radcliff@tamu.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S76

Background: Evidence supports documenting life-sustaining treatment (LST) preferences in electronic medical records (EMRs) to align end-of-life care with a patient’s goals and values. The Preferences Elicited and Respected for Seriously Ill Veterans (PERSIVED) intervention aimed to improve documentation of LST preferences for Veterans receiving VA Home Based Primary Care (HBPC). This analysis assessed PERSIVED implementation costs.

Methods: Descriptive assessment of costs to implement PERSIVED at each HBPC site and Central Program was conducted. A centralized database tracked program activities, staff time, and other resources by project phase (pre-implementation, implementation, and sustainability). Personnel costs were estimated using national hourly wage data plus a standard allowance for fringe benefits and overhead costs. Costs estimates were compared across sites to assess sensitivity of the results to intervention site locations, site engagement, and team composition.

Findings: Site-level personnel costs ranged from under $1,000 to more than $3,500 per site, with another $2,250 to $4,200 in Central Program costs to support program delivery. Cost variation by site reflected differences in the types and number of staff involved and their level of participation, with clinicians having higher hourly costs than non-clinicians and some sites including more participants in intervention activities. Total personnel costs across all sites were estimated at $52,734, with approximately $22,234 for site-level personnel and $30,500 for centralized staff. Central Program resources to implement PERSIVED relied heavily on core staff who delivered the intervention across sites; their efforts included maintaining systems for documenting intervention activities, recruiting site-level champions, coaching sites, and adjusting the intervention. HBPC site-level costs were primarily driven by VA staff whose time was allocated from other duties to serve as champions or participate in the intervention. Estimated total FTE for each HBPC site ranged from 0.02 to 0.07, and from 0.05 to 0.09 FTE for Central Program support per site.

Implications for D&I Research: PERSIVED represents a potentially low-cost, high-value strategy to improve LST documentation for Veterans in HBPC. Gaining a better understanding of the resources and related costs of interventions that improve documentation of patient preferences supports better patient care quality and more informed decisions regarding resources required for broader dissemination of LST coaching interventions.

Primary Funding Source: Department of Veterans Affairs

## S77 Implementation outcomes of the Preferences Elicited and Respected for Seriously Ill Veterans through Enhanced Decision-Making intervention

## Leah Haverhals1,2, Jennifer Kononowech3, Kate Magid2, Jazmin Beltran2, Paula Langner2, John Cashy4, Cari Levy1,2

## 1University of Colorado School of Medicine, Aurora, CO, USA; 2Veterans Health Administration, Aurora, CO, USA; 3Veterans Health Administration, Ann Arbor, MI, USA; 4Veterans Health Administration, Philadelphiaa, PA, USA

## Correspondence: Leah Haverhals (Leah.haverhals@va.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S77

Background: The Department of Veterans Affairs (VA) quality improvement program, Preferences Elicited and Respected for Seriously Ill Veterans through Enhanced Decision-Making (PERSIVED), sought to improve documentation of life-sustaining treatment (LST) preferences for seriously ill Veterans. The PERSIVED team enrolled VA Home Based Primary Care (HBPC) teams in an audit with feedback and facilitation intervention using a six-month pre-implementation and 15-month implementation period. During implementation, HBPC site champions received monthly coaching calls and feedback reports showing LST template completion rates.

Methods: We conducted a difference-in-differences analysis to compare changes in monthly LST template completion rates at PERSIVED sites (n = 11 HBPC teams) versus non-intervention sites (n = 123 HBPC teams). We also conducted process evaluation interviews (n = 67) at 12-and 18-months post-enrollment with champions and site leadership and analyzed data through applying the Tailored Implementation in Chronic Diseases (TICD) framework to understand facilitators and barriers to improving LST rates.

Findings: PERSIVED HBPC teams demonstrated a significant increase in LST template completion with an overall average treatment effect of 0.212 (95% CI: 0.142–0.282), corresponding to a 21.2 percentage point increase over expected trends. Participants expressed that reviewing PERSIVED feedback reports facilitated improvements in LST template completion and that PERSIVED coaching calls and resources created accountability and improved education and awareness about requirements for LST template completion. Participants stated PERSIVED proved compatible to HBPC workflows and motivated some sites to create multi-disciplinary team processes to ensure LST template completion. Participants were confident LST template completion would continue to improve, indicating high self-efficacy, intention and motivation, and positive attitudes toward completing LST templates. Staff turnover and competing priorities were barriers at some sites.

Implications for D&I Research: Our evaluation of PERSIVED suggests audit with feedback and facilitation were effective implementation strategies for a multi-site intervention and adds to the evidence on the efficacy of these strategies. Utilizing qualitative and quantitative approaches to understand the impact of PERSIVED proved critical and provided a comprehensive picture of intervention outcomes. Evaluators and researchers should consider incorporating multiple outcome measures in large implementation projects like this to better prepare efforts to expand and encourage sustainability.

Primary Funding Source: Department of Veterans Affairs

## S78 Barriers and facilitators to sustaining improvements in documenting life sustaining treatment preferences for Veterans in home based primary care

## Kate Magid1, Leah Haverhals1, Jennifer Kononowech2, Jazmin Beltran1, Cari Levy1,3

## 1Veterans Health Administration, Aurora, CO, USA; 2Veterans Health Administration, Ann Arbor, MI, USA; 3University of Colorado School of Medicine, Aurora, CO, USA

## Correspondence: Kate Magid (kate.magid@va.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S78

Background: The Preferences Elicited and Respected for Seriously Ill Veterans through Enhanced Decision-Making (PERSIVED) intervention was developed to improve documentation of life-sustaining treatment (LST) preferences for Veterans receiving Department of Veterans Affairs (VA) Home Based Primary Care (HBPC). During the 15-month intervention, VA HBPC clinicians received monthly feedback reports and coaching from the PERSIVED team. Following implementation, programs entered a 12-month sustainability period during which feedback report data continued but coaching ceased. This abstract describes LST documentation rates of PERSIVED sites during the sustainability period and facilitators and barriers impacting sustainment.

Methods: We recruited HBPC programs with LST preference documentation rates of < 50% to participate. In total, PERSIVED included three cohorts with 11 HBPC programs and 32 champions. We conducted 10 semi-structured group interviews at months 4 (n = 26 champions) and 10 (n = 25 champions) of the sustainability period. One site declined to participate. Interviews were transcribed and analyzed via content analysis. Additionally, champions (n = 25) completed a survey about sustainability practices during month 8 of the sustainability period. We calculated the percentage of Veterans in each HBPC program with completed LST templates at the end of the sustainability period and determined which sites maintained or exceeded their highest LST template completion rate achieved during implementation.

Findings: At the end of the sustainability period, all sites maintained or exceeded the LST preferences documentation rate they achieved during implementation. Rates ranged from 75% to 96%. Champions continued to review feedback reports and document LST preferences, though use of materials provided by the PERSIVED team proved minimal. Facilitators to sustainment included leadership support, integrating LST documentation into existing processes and meetings, and training newly hired staff on LST documentation processes. Barriers included lacking ways to monitor LST documentation rates after PERSIVED ended, staffing shortages, and competing priorities.

Implications for D&I Research: Results highlight benefits of longitudinal assessments of sustainability. Given personnel and time investment of monthly coaching calls, future projects should explore coaching dosage required to sustain improvements and whether this can be tailored to each site. Barriers and facilitators to sustainability can inform sustainability planning for future interventions within health care settings.

Primary Funding Source: Department of Veterans Affairs

## Closing health gaps through implementation science

## S79 Fidelity measures for low threshold care as predictors of implementation outcomes

## Wayne Kepner1, Hannah Cheng2, Berkeley Franz3, Andrea Jakubowski4, Margaret Lowenstein5, Mark McGovern6, Elena Rosenberg-Carlson6

## 1Stanford Medicine, San Francisco, CA, USA; 2Stanford University School of Medicine, Stanford, CA, USA; 3Ohio University Heritage College of Osteopathic Medicine, Athens, OH, USA; 4Montefiore Medical Center/Albert Einstein College of Medicine, Bronx, NY, USA; 5Perelman School of Medicine, University of Pennsylvania, Philadelphia, PA, USA; 6Stanford University School of Medicine, Palo Alto, CA, USA

## Correspondence: Wayne Kepner (wkepner@gmail.com)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S79

Background: Measures of intervention fidelity are critical to assessing implementation outcomes, but such measures are often neither standardized nor validated. Low threshold care (LTC) approaches for medications for opioid use disorder (MOUD) aim to systematically remove treatment barriers and improve access. Yet no standardized implementation measures exist for LTC. We aimed to develop and test four tiered LTC indices as fidelity measures—representing the “Implementation” of RE-AIM—and examined their association with key implementation outcomes across 20 California primary care clinics initiating and expanding MOUD services.

Methods: Data come from a prospective implementation study within the Addiction Treatment Starts Here practice change collaborative (Feb 2023-June 2024). Implementation strategies included audit and feedback, learning collaborative workshops, external facilitation, and educational webinars. We developed four LTC indices (12, 5, 3, and 2 items) from the validated Integrating Medications for Addiction Treatment in Primary Care-Equity Version (IMAT-PCE) tool based on expert recommendations and federal guidelines. Implementation outcomes, assessed monthly, were informed by RE-AIM: Reach (new MOUD patients), Effectiveness (34-day retention), and Adoption (active prescribers). We used repeated-measures ANOVA for LTC change and Poisson GEE models for associations between LTC scores and implementation outcomes.

Findings: Clinics showed significant improvements in LTC scores over time. The LTC12 scale demonstrated the largest effect size (d = 1.18, p = 0.003), followed by LTC5 (d = 1.1, p = 0.009), and LTC2 (d = 0.89, p = 0.029). In terms of predictive validity, a one-point increase on the LTC3 index was associated with a 37% increase in Reach (incidence rate ratio [IRR] = 1.37, 95% CI [1.01,1.86], p = 0.047). A one-point increase on LTC2 was associated with a 24% increase in Reach (IRR = 1.24, 95% CI [1.01,1.53], p = 0.049).

Implications for D&I Research: Our findings provide preliminary empirical support for a replicable measure of LTC in primary care settings. Longer scales had greater internal consistency and were more sensitive to change over time, while brief scales showed utility for predicting patient reach. Future research should validate these measures in diverse settings and test causal relationships between fidelity of LTC and implementation outcomes.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S80 Use of a comprehensive assessment practice for high-risk primary care patients: fidelity to complex practice implementation

## Natasha Torrence1, Caroline Yoo2, Franya Hutchins1,3, Evelyn Chang2,4, Susan Stockdale2, Ashok Reddy5,6, Elvira Jimenez2, Michelle Wong2, Kelsey Cummings2, Emily Wong7, Katharine Borthwick2, Ann-Marie Rosland1,8

## 1Veterans Health Administration, Pittsburgh, PA, USA; 2Veterans Health Administration, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 3University of Pennsylvania Perelman School of Medicine, Philadelphia, PA, USA; 4University of California Los Angeles, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 5Veterans Health Administration, Seattle, WA, USA; 6University of Washington, Seattle, WA, USA; 7Veterans Health Administration, Menlo Park, CA, USA; 8Division of General Internal Medicine, University of Pittsburgh School of Medicine, Pittsburgh, PA, USA

## Correspondence: Natasha Torrence (Natasha.Torrence@va.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S80

Background: Fidelity to new practices is important to maximize effectiveness but can be difficult to enact with complex practices in real-world settings. The VA High-Risk VETerans (RIVET) QUERI Program implemented Comprehensive Assessment and Care Planning (CACP) to identify and address needs of high-risk patients in primary care (PC) clinics. The CACP guides clinicians in assessing seven domains in a synchronous patient visit: advance care planning, behavioral health, health literacy, cognitive impairment, functional limitations, acute care use, and social needs. We assessed fidelity to CACP domains, needs identified, and care delivered in response.

Methods: 13 clinics delivered CACP to 991 patients. While PC teams often adapted CACP delivery, full-fidelity was defined as completing all domains. We used EHR data generated by CACP templates to assess clinic-level average and type of domains completed and needs identified, and EHR encounters to assess need-related care received within 90-days of CACP completion.

Findings: 7 clinics had “high” CACP domain fidelity (> 6/7 domains/patient on average, < 1SD variation in domains/patient), 6 clinics had “low” fidelity (< 5/7 domains or > 1SD/patient). The behavioral health domain was most often incomplete (most skipped in 5/13 clinics), while functional limitations was most often completed (12/13 clinics). 674/991 patients had ≥ 1 need identified, with functional limitations most frequently identified (53% patients assessed), and behavioral health needs least often identified (14%). The most common care received related to a CACP-identified need was a social work encounter (40% of those with any need). Over 50% of those with an identified behavioral health need had an encounter with a mental health provider in the following 90 days.

Implications for D&I Research: While many high-risk patient needs were identified and addressed, fidelity to this multi-component practice varied, with key domains frequently skipped in some clinics. Clinicians possibly perceived that certain domains were not needed by patients, would be hard to address if detected, or were repetitive with other clinic initiatives. The least assessed domain was associated with the highest amount of care received when needs were detected, suggesting low-fidelity sites may have missed opportunities to provide needed care. Challenges of defining, communicating, and adhering to full fidelity should be addressed when implementing complex practices.

Primary Funding Source: Department of Veterans Affairs

## S81 Fidelity of HIV pre-exposure prophylaxis implementation in family planning clinics in Kenya

## Irene Mukui1, Sandra Urusaro2, Kenneth Mugwanya3

## 1University of Washington, Seattle, USA; 2Hospital of the University of Pennsylvania, Philadelphia, PA, USA; 3University of Washington, Seattle, WA, USA

## Correspondence: Irene Mukui (imukui@uw.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S81

Background

Integrating HIV pre-exposure prophylaxis (PrEP) into family planning (FP) services offers a strategic opportunity to reach women at risk of HIV, who may benefit from PrEP for HIV prevention, particularly in Africa, where their HIV risk is high. We assessed fidelity to core components of oral PrEP delivery, in a large programmatic project integrating PrEP delivery in real-world FP clinics.

Methods

Between March 21 and December 22, we conducted a stepped-wedge cluster randomized trial, integrating oral PrEP into 12 FP clinics in Kisumu, Kenya, delivered by existing FP healthcare providers. Standardized client (SC) actors conducted unannounced visits to determine whether core components of integrated PrEP services were delivered as intended and evaluate service quality. SC actors were trained on case scripts reflecting key elements: HIV risk screening, eligibility assessment, and PrEP initiation. After each visit, SCs completed checklists documenting services received: behavioral risk assessment, HIV prevention counseling, PrEP counseling, safety assessment, PrEP dispensing, quality-of-care and service experience. Service quality was rated on a 5-point Likert scale. We used descriptive analysis to summarize the proportion of visits where each component was completed.

Findings

Overall, 985 women aged ≥ 15 years-initiated PrEP. Twenty-four (12 initiation, 12 follow up) SC visits were conducted, two per clinic. Behavioral risk assessment occurred in 83% of visits; HIV prevention counseling (condom use, knowing partner HIV status, post-exposure prophylaxis, male circumcision) at all visits (100%); and PrEP counseling (daily use, repeat HIV testing, side effects, PrEP not protective against STIs) in 58% of visits. Clinical safety assessments (acute HIV symptoms, comorbidities) were conducted in 42% of visits, PrEP dispensed in 83% of visits, and 79% of clients given a return date. For service quality, providers used clear language (mean = 4.0), listened attentively (3.8), and were respectful (3.7), with no reports of judgmental behavior. Scores were lower for privacy (2.6) and providing comprehensive information (2.5).

Implications for D&I Research

In this project integrating PrEP provision into FP services, fidelity to key components of PrEP delivery and quality of services was high, with some gaps noted for improvement. PrEP delivery is feasible with high fidelity, using existing staff in African FP clinics.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S82 Acción PrEP: testing the feasibility of community co-designed implementation strategies to advance PrEP uptake among Latinos in the U.S. south

## Sarah Wilson1, Judith Montenegro2, Lisvel Matos3, Carrie Dombeck1, Carolina Mejia4, Jamilah Taylor1, Aleida Espinal2, Megan Reaves1, Cristofer Navas5, Samantha Fortier5, Amy Corneli5

## 1Duke University School of Medicine, Durham, NC, USA; 2Latino Commission on AIDS, Durham, NC, USA; 3Duke University School of Nursing, Durham, NC, USA; 4Department of Population Health Sciences, Duke University, School of Medicine, Durham, NC, USA; 5Duke University, Durham, NC, USA

## Correspondence: Sarah Wilson (sarah.wilson@duke.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S82

Background: Despite systemic inequities in pre-exposure prophylaxis (PrEP) uptake among Latinos, clinic-level implementation strategies that address health equity-related PrEP barriers are underutilized. This study aimed to test the feasibility and acceptability of community-co-developed, equity-focused strategies to improve PrEP uptake among Latinos in the southern U.S.

Methods: This study was a community/academic partnership and was conducted in Mecklenburg County, North Carolina, a jurisdiction with high HIV incidence and a rapidly growing Latino population. The Acción PrEP workgroup, a collective of local community and public health constituents with HIV/PrEP experience, co-developed PrEP implementation strategies over six months (March-August 2023), using implementation mapping, human-centered design, and participatory methods. This process yielded Acción PrEP, a package of implementation strategies that were subsequently pilot-tested at a federally qualified health center and a non-profit community clinic from March-August 2024. Data on strategy fidelity and modifications were collected. Outcomes included: acceptability of the workgroup process (via exit surveys) and feasibility and acceptability of the implementation strategies (assessed qualitatively October 2024-February 2025 via key informant interviews with 7 pilot implementers and 16 community partners).

Findings: Acción PrEP workgroup baseline and exit data indicated inclusiveness of varying identities (e.g., Latino ethnicity and lived experience of HIV/PrEP) and high levels of collaboration/satisfaction. The workgroup developed three health equity-focused strategies: a needs and assets assessment, blended implementation facilitation (combining external facilitation from a community-based organization with internal clinic facilitation), and a bilingual, culturally tailored PrEP awareness mass media campaign. Pilot-testing process data indicated: 1) 100% fidelity to the needs and assets assessment, 2) 85% average completion of weekly facilitation meetings, and 3) delayed campaign dissemination. Adaptations included fewer facilitation meetings, increased leadership engagement during implementation, and adding a sustainability planning workshop. Key informant interviews supported acceptability and feasibility. Strengths of Acción PrEP included observation-based feedback, concrete change goals, and the overall focus on Latino health equity. Barriers included insufficient time, staffing, clinic space, and challenges in identifying internal facilitators.

Implications for D&I Research: The community co-design process and Acción PrEP strategies were acceptable and feasible. Study findings underscore the need for longer implementation timelines and stronger support and protected time for internal facilitator roles.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S83 How do policymakers prioritize interventions for health benefits packages? Insights from Ethiopia

## Sali Ahmed

## University of Washington, Seattle, WA, USA

## Correspondence: Sali Ahmed (salia@uw.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S83

Background

When deciding which interventions to prioritize for health benefits packages, policymakers often need to balance multiple criteria and competing priorities. These include maximizing health outcomes, improving the distribution of health (i.e., equity), and providing financial risk protection. Little is known about how policymakers weigh these criteria, so we conducted a discrete choice experiment in Ethiopia to better understand local preferences.

Methods

Our experiment was conducted among 49 policymakers involved in developing Ethiopia's health benefits package. The experiment used a four-attribute, forced-choice design. Vignettes presented two hypothetical alternative interventions, totaling 18 vignettes. The attributes specified for the two alternatives were (1) absolute reduction in mortality, (2) severity of disease targeted, (3) age group targeted, and (4) absolute reduction in medical impoverishment (assuming full public finance). We estimated the average preference weights for each attribute using a random-parameter mixed logit model and calculated the conditional relative importance of each attribute based on our estimates.

Findings

Most respondents worked in the Ministry of Health or a local insurance agency and were men. Based on 864 total observations, all four criteria were statistically significant and varied monotonically across different attribute levels, as expected. The conditional relative importance of each of the four attributes was 30% for the targeted age group and absolute reduction in mortality, 22% for the reduction in medical impoverishment, and 19% for the severity of the disease targeted.

Implications for D&I Research

We found that Ethiopian policymakers involved in EHBP development tend to prioritize interventions that target younger age groups, especially < 5 years, and achieve substantial mortality reduction. Financial risk protection and disease severity were less critical determinants of decision-making. These findings provide empirical preference weights that can be incorporated into multicriteria decision analysis (MCDA), thereby enhancing the systematic and transparent development of EHBP. This study presents a novel stakeholder-informed method to guide priority setting. Aligning health policy decisions with local values can support the implementation of impactful policies that can close the health gaps in resource-limited settings.

## S84 Implementation of NOURISH: a clinic-community linkage model to address the social, nutrition, and health needs of pregnant persons

## Robin Ortiz1, Shilpi Mehta-Lee2, Stephanie Quintero Chacon3, Kelly Naranjo4, Eric Rochman4, Priscilla Upshaw4, Jessica Gonzalez2, Joanna Madigan5, Kathleen Hopkins5, Meleen Chuang2, Rebecca Gallager5, Brita Roy5, Antoinette Schoenthaler6, Gbenga Ogedegbe7, Christina Kim3, Rachel Gross3

## 1NYU Grossman School of Medicine, New York City, NY, USA; 2NYU Grossman School of Medicine, Brooklyn, NY, USA; 3NYU Grossman School of Medicine, New York, NY, USA; 4God's Love We Deliver, New York, NY, USA; 5NYU Langone, Brooklyn, NY, USA; 6NYU Grossman School of Medicine, New York, USA; 7Institute for Excellence in Health Equity, NYU Grossman School of Medicine, New York, NY, USA

## Correspondence: Robin Ortiz (robin.ortiz@nyulangone.org)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S84

Background: Pre- and gestational diabetes in pregnancy (GDM) poses risk for cardiovascular disease and disparities across generations. Medically-tailored meals (MTM) are an evidence-based intervention that also addresses food access, but requires clinic-community linkage (CCL) to overcome implementation barriers. God’s Love We Deliver (GLWD), a community-based organization, has historically provided registered dietician-guided MTM throughout New York for numerous chronic conditions. In 2023, a new program designed for pregnant persons with GDM launched. However, implementation was challenged by lack of direct communication with referring clinical providers, overlayed with the time-sensitive nature of GDM diagnosis and treatment within the third trimester.

Methods: To enhance the adoption of this CCL, we established the NOURISH (Novel Outreach Using caregiver-child Relationships to Impact Social and Health) initiative to identify implementation barriers and develop strategies to increase CCL referrals. A mixed method evaluation assessed pre-implementation contextual factors, harnessing the Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research (CFIR) framework, and evaluated the initial intervention implementation by quantifying referrals, enrollments (meals delivered), and dis-enrollments, and capturing provider and client feedback.

Findings: Pre-implementation work identified two referral process barriers (inner setting): 1) inefficiency (excessive clinical resources and time), and 2) bottleneck workflow (physician sign-off). Two implementation strategies were employed to address these barriers including the addition of an EPIC Electronic Health Record (EHR) SmartSet and community health worker support. Over an 18-month CCL period, referrals grew from 13 (baseline) to 208 with a sustained pattern of 10 + /month referrals, ~ 80%/month client enrollments, and consistent dependent/child enrollments (from 0 prior) after launch of the implementation strategies. Patients referred were racial/ethnically diverse (7 categories including 62% Hispanic/Latino), spoke 7 languages, and were 83% publicly insured. Positive spillover consequences included improved overall healthcare system GDM care delivery, reduction in caregiver stress for clients with children, and increases in addressed social needs (48% of CCL patients received additional social service referrals). Identified challenges inform continued work to improve data infrastructure and support program fidelity.

Implications for D&I Research: Our findings highlight that CCL partnerships can effectively expand reach and engagement in clinical pregnant populations by strengthening the referral pathways, while also generating positive spillover effects, including increased support for social needs.

Primary Funding Source: Philanthropic & Academic

## S85 Recovery incentives: examining California’s statewide implementation of contingency management for the treatment of stimulant use disorder

## Tyler Erath1, Darren Urada2, Valerie Antonini2, Avery Nork2, Brittany Bass2, Howard Padwa2, Yiyu Xing2, Edward Zakher2, Liliana Gregorio2, Anne Lee2, Savannah Gourley2, Richard Rawson1,2

## 1Vermont Center on Behavior and Health, Department of Psychiatry, University of Vermont, Burlington, VT, USA; 2Integrated Substance Use and Addiction Programs, Division of Addiction Psychiatry, University of California Los Angeles, Los Angeles, CA, USA

## Correspondence: Tyler Erath (tyler.erath@uvm.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S85

Background

Expanding the implementation of effective treatment for stimulant use disorder (StimUD) has become increasingly urgent as fatal drug poisonings involving stimulants have rapidly increased in the United States (US). Despite three decades of evidence supporting Contingency Management (CM) as the most effective treatment for StimUD, its availability in community settings has been limited to non-existent until recent. In 2023, California initiated the Recovery Incentives program, the first statewide implementation of CM for StimUD. This presentation presents implementation results from the first two years of the program.

Methods

The Recovery Incentives program is being systematically implemented using the Science-to-Service Laboratory approach. This implementation strategy includes three core training components (e.g., didactic training, performance feedback, and external facilitation) along with site readiness assessments and ongoing fidelity monitoring. The evaluation approach is organized around the RE-AIM framework. Here we report on findings to date for three evaluation components: (1) adoption (number of provider agencies delivering CM), (2) reach (number of participants in CM treatment), and (3) effectiveness (% stimulant-negative urine drug test results).

Findings

Adoption: as of June 2025, 155 provider agencies have opted to participate in the Recovery Incentives program, 100 of which are currently approved and have enrolled at least one participant for an average adoption rate of 4.4 programs per month. Reach: Between April 2023 to April 2025, 7,462 unique participants participated in the program. Effectiveness: Among 5,020 participants enrolled long enough to complete the 24-week treatment, the average retention was approximately 18 weeks; 36.3% completed all 24 weeks, 29.8% completed 12–23.5 weeks, and 33.9% completed < 12 weeks. The overall rate of stimulant-negative urine drug test results was 95.5% for all completed test and 74.9% when including unexcused absences as an assumed positive test result.

Implications for D&I Research

Findings represent outcomes from the first statewide and largest known implementation of CM for StimUD to date. Evaluation outcomes indicate strong uptake and adoption of CM into organizational service delivery, positive outcomes for the Recovery Incentive program’s reach, and strong evidence of CM effectiveness to reduce stimulant use among individuals with StimUD. Together, findings provide a potential implementation model for future use in statewide CM efforts.

Primary Funding Source: Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services

## S86 Scaling evidence-based a1c monitoring through automation and asynchronous messaging: a system-level implementation across a multi-state health system

## Ursula Wright, John Mohart, Kalli Castille, Hillary Barron, Jessica Engelbrecht, Gavin Helton

## Mercy Health, St. Louis, MO, USA

## Correspondence: Ursula Wright (Ursula.Wright@Mercy.Net)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S83

Background: Despite clear guidelines, many patients with diabetes experience delays in A1c testing, contributing to poor glycemic control. Mercy Health, a multi-state health system, implemented evidence-based automated processing of A1c orders and digital messaging to close this care gap. The program uses automated order entry and outreach via secure SMS to notify patients of overdue or uncontrolled A1c tests. The system-level intervention aligns with dissemination and implementation (D&I) science by integrating evidence-based practice into routine care through health IT infrastructure.

Methods: Guided by the RE-AIM and PARIHS frameworks, the program leverages Mercy’s unified EHR and centralized diabetes registry to identify eligible patients (ages 18–85 with A1c of ≥ 8.0 and last A1c test greater than 90 days or no A1c test in the last 365 days) across 272 primary care clinics in Missouri, Arkansas, Oklahoma, and Kansas. Providers review monthly reports before automated A1c orders are placed using standardized approved orders. Patients receive HIPAA-compliant SMS based messages with educational content and opt-out options. Implementation is supported by structured go-live process, clinician training, a communications plan, and real-time outcome tracking.

Findings: The automated process reduced manual order entry and standardized outreach while preserving provider oversight. Early implementation (Jan-Dec 2022), 24% of patients receiving automated A1c labs had the test drawn. In the most recent 12-month period (Jan-Dec 2024) 19% of patients receiving automated A1c labs had the test drawn. The program expanded by 400% over 2 years going from 14,000 patients limited to specific payor in 2022 to 60,000 patients (all payor) in 2024. Clinicians report reduced administrative burden and improved workflow. Patient messaging supports engagement, self-management, and reinforces completion of chronic disease monitoring care interventions.

Implications for D&I Research: This system-level intervention demonstrates how large, diverse health systems can operationalize evidence-based practices using automation and digital outreach to close chronic disease care gaps. Mercy’s approach—anchored in standardized workflows and a unified EHR—demonstrates how system-level strategies overcome barriers like clinical burden and patient access. This program contributes to D&I science by showing how automation and digital infrastructure supports scalable, low-burden, high-reach model supported by real-time monitoring, structured implementation and continuous feedback and audit between stakeholders.

## S87 Perspectives towards implementing an evidence-based protocol to provide timely home health and outpatient visits for Heart Failure (HF) patients after hospital discharge: early findings from the I-TRANSFER-HF trial

## Madeline Sterling1, Michael Dicpinigaitis1, Daniel Spertus2, Michelle Shum1, Joselyne Aucapina2, Joanna Bryan1, Jonathan Tobin3,4, Mangala Rajan5, Alicia Arbaje6,7, Christine Jones8, Karen Ream9, Tanya Budnikova10, Kathrine Eckstadt10, Pamela Manning9, Jessica Nensel9, Brittany Cyriacks9, Hemali Patel9, Joy Prabhakar9, Zuzanna Czernik9, Margaret McDonald11, Kathryn Bowles12,13

## 1Weill Cornell Medicine, New York, NY, USA; 2Weill Cornell Medicine, New York, USA; 3The Rockefeller University Center for Clinical and Translational Science, New York, NY, USA; 4Clinical Directors Network, Inc. (CDN), New York, NY, USA; 5Weill Cornell Medical College, New York, NY, USA; 6Johns Hopkins University, Baltimore, USA; 7Johns Hopkins University, Baltimore, MD, USA; 8University of Colorado Denver—Anschutz Medical Campus, Aurora, CO, USA; 9University of Colorado Denver—Anschutz Medical Campus, Aurora, USA; 10Berkley Home Health Care, Aurora, USA; 11VNS Health, New York, NY, USA; 12University of Pennsylvania, Philadelphia, USA; 13VNS Health, Center for Home Care Policy and Research, New York, NY, USA

## Correspondence: Madeline Sterling (mrs9012@med.cornell.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S87

Background: Timely receipt of home health care and outpatient follow-up after hospitalization improves outcomes in HF, yet only 12% of Medicare beneficiaries receive these evidence-based practices. We are conducting a Hybrid Type 1 stepped-wedge cluster RCT study to test the effectiveness and identify contextual factors for implementation across 4 hospital and home health agency (HHA) dyads. We assessed the participants’ pre-implementation perspectives and readiness for change from 2 sites.

Methods: We conducted semi-structured interviews informed by the Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research 2.0 (CFIR2.0) to determine facilitators and barriers. Organizational Readiness for Implementing Change (ORIC) scores, a 12-item survey assessing change efficacy and confidence (score range 12–60), were summarized with descriptive statistics.

Findings: We conducted 42 pre-implementation interviews across 2 hospital-HHA dyads. Site 1 is a Mid-Atlantic, private academic medical center with an integrated HHA; Site 2 is a Mountain West, public university hospital associated with a small HHA. Across both sites, HHA-based participants had greater readiness than hospital-based participants (Site 1 ORIC median 55.0 [IQR: 53.0, 59.0] vs. 41.0 [38.0, 49.5], p = 0.03; Site 2 ORIC median 57.5 [55.0, 60.0] vs. 48.5 [44.0, 52.0], p = 0.05). Unique implementation facilitators included an integrated hospital-HHA with shared EHR, a HF bridge clinic, and adaptability for home care physical-therapy visits versus nursing (Site 1), along with prior experience with a similar study, a central telecommand center, and HHA workforce capacity (Site 2). Shared facilitators included mission alignment and compatibility of the intervention with existing workflows. Unique barriers were patient characteristics, limited inpatient staff time, and access to follow-up appointments (Site 1), as well as limited inpatient education on home care and insurance denials (Site 2). Shared barriers included eligible patient identification, timely outpatient provider access, and patient refusal.

Implications for D&I Research: This is the first known implementation of a pragmatic, evidence-based intervention to improve post-discharge outcomes for HF patients across hospital-HHA dyads. Findings highlight the importance of identifying common and site-specific facilitators and barriers to tailor strategies. Sustained practice change may depend on leveraging local resources and institutional knowledge between the hospital-HHA dyads, engaging stakeholders beyond formal meetings, and promoting cross-site learning.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health.

## S88 Implementation of a multi-site financial navigation intervention in rural and non-rural oncology clinics: a hybrid II implementation-effectiveness clinical trial

## Stephanie Wheeler1, Austin Waters2, Michelle Manning3, Arrianna Planey3, Bridgette Thom3, Lisa Spees3, Sarah Birken4, Katherine Reeder-Hayes3, Camille Murray3, Kevin Pignone3, Allison Deal3, Donald Rosenstein3

## 1Lineberger Comprehensive Cancer Center, UNC Chapel Hill, Chapel Hill, NC, USA; 2Dana-Farber Cancer Institute, Boston, MA, USA; 3University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill, Chapel Hill, NC, USA; 4Wake Forest University School of Medicine, Winston-Salem, NC, USA

## Correspondence: Stephanie Wheeler (stephanie_wheeler@unc.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S88

Background: Financial toxicity (FT) among cancer survivors is prevalent and associated with worse quality of life, caregiver distress, and higher mortality. Although financial navigation (FN) has reduced FT in urban settings, evaluation of FN within geographically diverse practices is needed to reach broader populations suffering from FT.

Methods: We conducted a pragmatic hybrid II implementation-effectiveness clinical trial (Lessening the Impact of Financial Toxicity, LIFT) in 8 academic and non-academic oncology practice sites in North Carolina, adapting FN for 4 rural sites and 4 non-rural sites serving rural patients, with rurality defined using rural–urban community area (RUCA) codes greater than 3. All cancer patients within five years of diagnosis with a FACIT-COST score ≤ 22 (indicating moderate-to-severe FT) who understood English were eligible. Participants were recruited through clinic appointments, provider referral, or self-referral. FN, which consisted of comprehensive intake, 2–8 follow-up FN sessions, case management and personalized support for resource applications, was provided by local staff champions, mostly social workers, who received ~17 h training in FN, participated in monthly technical assistance calls, and joined monthly learning collaborative calls (i.e., implementation strategies). The primary effectiveness outcome was change in FACIT-COST score, pre- versus post-LIFT implementation. Implementation outcomes included uptake, fidelity, acceptability, costs, and sustainability. Here, we report on the FACIT-COST score change and uptake (# served by FN/# screened positive for FT).

Findings: Of 258 cancer survivors consented, 233 screened FT-positive. 171 cancer survivors received FN, and 125 completed all follow-up surveys. Survivors with complete data were, on average, 51.7 years (SD:14.2), more often female (65%), and non-Hispanic White (56%) or Black (36%), with 8% identifying as Hispanic; 42.4% lived in a rural area. After FN, FACIT-COST scores significantly increased by an average of 8.5 points (p < 0.0001; pre-mean score: 6.8, SD:5.1; post-mean score: 15.3, SD:10.1). Changes in FACIT-COST scores were similar for rural and non-rural survivors. Uptake was high, with 73.4% of those offered FN receiving it.

Implications for D&I Research: Social needs interventions like FN can be adapted to benefit diverse rural communities.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S89 Evaluating an implementation intervention for increasing mental health providers’ adoption of best practices with transgender youth: a nonrandomized hybrid type 2 pilot trial

## Maggi Price1, Patrick Mulkern1, Nathan Hollinsaid2, Madelaine Condon1

## 1Boston College, Chestnut Hill, MA, USA; 2Harvard University, Cambridge, MA, USA

## Correspondence: Maggi Price (maggi.price@bc.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S89

Background

Transgender youth face significant mental health inequities, and standard treatments fail to meet their needs. Gender-Affirming Psychotherapy (GAP) is a community-co-designed clinical intervention comprising evidence-based practices (e.g., using affirmed names and pronouns) designed to close this gap. To facilitate GAP adoption, we co-developed and tested an implementation intervention (bundle of implementation strategies) with key stakeholders: transgender youth, their parents, and mental health providers.

Methods

N = 78 mental health providers, N = 22 transgender youth, and N = 19 parents completed a nonrandomized hybrid type 2 trial (NCT05626231) of the implementation intervention. Data from 18 months of Electronic Health Record (EHR) records from N = 125 transgender youth were analyzed. The intervention comprised 12 implementation strategies: 8 individual-level strategies (e.g., shadow, simulate change) were embedded in an asynchronous training, and 4 inner-setting strategies (e.g., change data systems) were implemented clinic-wide. Providers were evaluated at pre-, post-, and monthly follow-up assessments for one year post-intervention. Youth and parents were evaluated weekly for 4 months post-intervention. The implementation outcomes were GAP adoption (reported by providers, transgender youth clients, and their parents) and provider knowledge of, attitudes towards, and self-efficacy using GAP. Effectiveness outcomes were transgender youth functioning, internalizing, and externalizing symptoms.

Findings

Implementation: Providers self-reported a significant increase in GAP adoption at 1-month (β = 0.80) and 1-year follow-up (β = 0.38). Transgender youth (β = 0.45) and their parents (β = 0.80) reported a significant increase in their providers’ GAP adoption after intervention implementation. Provider knowledge (β = 1.28), attitudes (β = 0.38), and self-efficacy (β = 1.17) showed significant improvement post-intervention; outcomes remained significant at 1-year follow-up (knowledge β = 1.32; attitudes β = 0.26; self-efficacy β = 1.14). Effectiveness: Transgender youth experienced a significant increase in functioning, controlling for time and covariates (β = 0.46), as reported by their parents; internalizing and externalizing symptoms results were nonsignificant.

Implications for D&I Research

This hybrid pilot demonstrated that a multilevel intervention is preliminarily effective in improving provider- and patient-level outcomes for a critical health disparities population. Researchers should consider community co-designed individual- and system-level strategies to close health disparity gaps. Finally, leveraging EHR data may be an efficient and low-burden method for analyzing patient-level outcomes.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health.

## S90 A change in STRIDE: Site-reported adaptations of an evidence-based inpatient mobility program

## Leah Zullig1,2, Connor Drake1,2, Jaime Hughes1,3, Karen Stechuchak1, Caitlin Kappler1, Ashley Choate1, Cassie Meyer1, Amy Webster1, Kelli Allen1,4, Courtney Van Houtven2, Susan Hastings1,2

## 1Veterans Health Administration, Durham, NC, USA; 2Duke University School of Medicine, Durham, NC, USA; 3Wake Forest University School of Medicine, Winston-Salem, NC, USA; 4University of North Carolina Chapel Hill School of Medicine, Chapel Hill, NC, USA

## Correspondence: Leah Zullig (leah.zullig@duke.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S90

Background: Hospital-based inactivity among older adults often leads to functional decline. While mobility programs like STRIDE (assiSTed eaRly mobIlity for hospitalizeD older vEterans) have proven effective, their implementation in clinical settings remains understudied. Program adaptation may improve implementation but requires study of how facilities modify intervention components to preserve intervention effectiveness and improve fit within the clinical context. Our objectives were to: 1) report adaptations made to the STRIDE program across Veterans Affairs Medical Centers (VAMCs) during implementation, using the Framework for Reporting Adaptations and Modifications-Enhanced (FRAME); and 2) assess the relationship of adaptations with adoption and sustainment.

Methods: This was part of the Function QUERI (QUality Enhancement Research Initiative), a type III effectiveness-implementation hybrid study. Thirty-five VAMCs were randomized 1:1 to receive foundational or enhanced implementation support. Site-level contacts completed forms at pre-implementation, six, and ten months, documenting adaptations to program eligibility, staffing, marketing, and documentation. Adaptations were analyzed using collaborative coding to determine alignment with core STRIDE components and fidelity. We defined programmatic benchmarks for adoption or sustainment at 6 or 8 months (defined as having 5 or more patients receiving STRIDE in a general medicine bed section in months 5–6 and 10 or more patients receiving STRIDE in months 7–8, respectively.

Findings: Of the 35 participating sites, 29% (10 sites) met initial adoption benchmarks at 6-months, increasing to 51% (18 sites) by eight months. Sites implemented various adaptations across four categories: patient eligibility, program enhancements and staffing model, program documentation, and program marketing and education. The adaptations were classified according to FRAME criteria including timing, planning status, decision-makers, modification type, delivery level, and fidelity consistency. All sites reported at least one adaptation. The largest number of adaptations per site was seven. We found that adaptations were positively associated with the likelihood of adopting an inpatient mobility program.

Implications for D&I Research: Our study demonstrates that sites can successfully implement fidelity-consistent adaptations, which may lead to improved implementation outcomes. Our study compliments existing research which shows that adaptations can enhance the local fit and increase uptake of interventions.

Primary Funding Source: Department of Veterans Affairs

## S91 A 3-Step implementation model for family-based HIV prevention for Black girls through academic-community partnerships

## Natasha Crooks

## University of Illinois Chicago, Chicago, IL, USA

## Correspondence: Natasha Crooks (ncrooks@uic.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S91

Background: Black girls are disproportionately impacted by HIV and STIs in the United States. Engaging Black men in family-based HIV prevention programming provides a unique opportunity to protect Black girls. Community engagement through academic partnerships can support efforts to deliver family-based programming. To support sustainability, we employed a 3-Step Implementation Model (Prepare, Rollout, and Sustain) to engage community-based organizations (CBOs) in a randomized control trial (n = 300 dyads) of a family-based program in Chicago.

Methods: CBOs partnered with an academic institution to implement IMAGE, a family-based program for Black girls and men. Guided by the determinants of EPIS, we assessed implementation factors through a mixed-methods evaluation using interviews, observations/notes, and surveys. We conducted an adapted framework analysis, organizing coded data in a matrix categorizing barriers and facilitators by low, medium, or high impact through data triangulation.

Findings: Structured, evidence-based implementation strategies were applied consistently across all CBOs (n = 8). CBO engagement levels varied and were driven by inner context characteristics. During Prepare, the most significant factors were mission alignment and fit, funding constraints, and facility availability. Four CBOs advanced to Rollout, collectively delivering 10 IMAGE groups with an average fidelity score of 83%. Successful Rollout was associated with high levels of organizational leadership, team cohesion, mission alignment, and program fit. Outer-context factors, including service environment/policies limitations and challenges with participant access, emerged as major barriers. To transition to Sustain, local political will and capacity, and organizational mission alignment and fit are necessary. Two CBOs demonstrated potential to sustain IMAGE independently, while the others required additional partnerships to address limited access to the target population.

Implications for D&I Research: This study demonstrates that applying a structured framework like EPIS with a 3-Step implementation plan can effectively facilitate the delivery of evidence-based interventions through community-academic partnerships. Key organizational and contextual factors, including mission and fit alignment, leadership, and the local environment, influence program success. Identifying and understanding these factors enables implementation scientists and community partners to develop tailored strategies to overcome barriers and maximize the impact of interventions to improve sexual and reproductive health outcomes among marginalized communities.

Confirmation: Ethics approval UIC IRB: 2023–1317

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S92 Facilitators and barriers to implementing H-HOPE (Hospital-to-Home: Optimizing the Preterm Infant Environment) in six NICUs in the USA

## Rosemary White-Traut

## Children’s Hospital of Wisconsin, Milwaukee, WI, USA

## Correspondence: Rosemary White-Traut (rwhite-traut@childrenswi.org)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S92

Abstract Text

Background

H-HOPE is an example of a major implementation science and health care gap, the failure of an evidence-based behavioral intervention to become the standard of care. H-HOPE includes parent education and infant massage, delivered by parents. Our implementation using a modified 3-step implementation model research focused on assisting nursing administration and clinical staff adopt H-HOPE for preterm infants and parents in the neonatal intensive care unit (NICU) and the transition to home. This presentation identifies key facilitators and barriers that distinguished between sustaining and non-sustaining NICUs.

Methods

Our Hybrid Design examined H-HOPE implementation effectiveness and process across six NICUs that varied in size, acuity, and location. Two pairs of NICUs were in the same hospital system. Using the Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research, we described complex implementation factors across five domains: innovation, outer setting, inner setting, individuals, and implementation process. The final analysis examined facilitators and barriers affecting the decision to sustain.

Findings

Two NICUs from the same health system made a clear decision to sustain. Powerful barriers across all NICUs, including the two sustaining sites, were innovation-related complexity and insufficient hands-on training, and the inner setting work infrastructure, which was compromised by understaffing, insufficiently oriented new hires, high patient workloads, and leadership disruption, undermining leader engagement. Barriers were linked to non-compatibility of H-HOPE within the NICU and an individual barrier of low opportunity to provide H-HOPE.
In the two sustaining NICUs, these barriers were offset by: parent-centered and developmentally focused unit culture, which supported greater endorsement of H-HOPE and greater individual capability to provide H-HOPE. These units also worked well as a team and adapted two aspects of the protocol to better fit their NICU.

Implications for D&I Research

An innovation with a strong evidence base may still present formidable implementation challenges. Interacting structural barriers reduced implementation feasibility. However, unit culture and process teamwork and adaptations allowed some NICUs to overcome these barriers and sustain H-HOPE, highlighting the importance of strong facilitators to overcome barriers. Future H-HOPE implementation should be modified by initially strengthening unit culture and infant behavior knowledge prior to more intensive and hands-on training.

Confirmation: IRBPRO00035600, Medical College of Wisconsin

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S93 Community sustainment of an effective HIV prevention program in Malawi after funding

## Kathleen F. Norr

## Children’s Wisconsin, Milwaukee, WI, USA

## Correspondence: Kathleen F. Norr (norr.kathleen@gmail.com)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S93

Background: A persistent implementation science gap is the failure of effective interventions to continue after funding ends. This success story shows that capacity-building enables post-funding community sustainment. Our six-session peer group HIV prevention intervention, Mzake ndi Mzake (Friend-to-Friend), initially implemented with NIH funding, is being sustained by communities. Program efficacy when implemented by health workers was established in prior studies. However, health worker shortages meant that the health system could not take ownership. Transferring Mzake to communities with health system support was a promising strategy that we tested. To guide implementation, we developed a simple 3-Step Implementation Model (prepare, rollout, sustain) for community use.

Methods: This effectiveness-implementation study was conducted in three communities in Phalombe District, which had high poverty and HIV rates. Using a stepped-wedge design, four repeated surveys (n = 1008), and a process evaluation were completed.

Findings: Community volunteers in all three communities successfully followed the 3-Step Model to deliver Mzake with fidelity and effectiveness. During Prepare, communities formed Coordinating Committees. During Rollout, communities offered Mzake to 1139 individuals; all but four individuals completed the 6-session program. Volunteer-led Mzake remained effective. HIV Knowledge increased and was significantly higher in the intervention group (log-odds estimate = 0.17, SE = 0.06, p-value < 0.01). Condom use at last sex increased in intervention communities compared to the control community [Adjusted Odds Ratio: 1.81 (1.13, 2.90)]. During Sustain, communities wrote proposals funded from the project. Through their projects, trained facilitators increased from 48 to 85, and Mzake reached 7,196 new people. After funding ended, communities chose to continue the program. Retired researchers and local leaders established the Ekodda Foundation to support Mzake and other initiatives. In the past three years, Mzake expanded to 6 new communities, reaching 1521 more people.

Implications for D&I Research: Filling an implementation gap, the 3-step model enabled communities to successfully implement and sustain Mzake beyond initial funding. Turning implementation over to communities has high potential to expand HIV prevention without burdening the health system, supporting long-term sustainment. Sustaining requires mutual trust and limiting researchers' control while building capacities to support the successful transfer of ownership to communities.

Confirmation: Ethics: COMREC P.10/15/1815 and UIC #2015 − 0691

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S94 Scaling up midwifery-led and evidence-based group prenatal care in Malawi

## Crystal Patil

## University of Michigan, Ann Arbor, MI, USA

## Correspondence: Crystal Patil (clpatil@umich.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S94

Background: Improving perinatal outcomes globally demands scalable implementation of evidence-based, person-centered models that retain women in care. To address this gap, we completed a rigorous hybrid effectiveness-implementation trial of Centering-based Group prenatal care (PNC) in seven clinics in Blantyre District.

Methods: We followed a 3-Step Implementation Model (Prepare, Rollout, and Sustain), and our process evaluation was guided by CFIR and RE-AIM, triangulating data from fidelity checklists, training records, clinical records, surveys, and study notes.

Findings: During Prepare, we provided capacity-building training for 299 midwives. During Rollout, 84 groups were completed with high fidelity. Compared to individual PNC, women in Group PNC reported greater satisfaction with care (Estimate = 1.21, 99% CI = 0.07, 2.35), more visits (Estimate = 0.74, 99% CI = 0.50, 0.98), greater dietary diversity (Estimate = 0.35, 99% CI = 0.17, 0.53), birth preparedness (Estimate 0.32, 99% CI = 0.16, 0.48), and consistent condom use (OR = 1.07, 99% CI = 1.00, 1.14), and less mental distress (Estimate −0.61, 99% CI = − 1.20, − 0.02). More than 80% of women preferred Group PNC for a future pregnancy. During Sustain we provided ongoing mentorship for clinic teams and monitored new and existing site progress. Implementation strategies, including capacity-building training and mentor development, provided a replicable framework for establishing new perinatal care models. Midwife mentors trained an additional 103 providers and expanded Group PNC to six new clinics. Seven of the original clinics continue to offer Group PNC and have completed 147 additional groups, reaching over 1000 women. Fidelity, feasibility, and acceptability remained high. Four of six expansion clinics continue to offer Group PNC and have completed 23 groups. In 2024, the Malawi Ministry of Health hired our project Mentors to introduce Group PNC into district-level training as part of initiating national scale-up. In at least four districts sensitized to Group PNC several clinics are now offering Group PNC.

Implications for D&I Research: This study demonstrates the value of the 3-Step Implementation Model for scaling complex, midwifery-led interventions. Results provide evidence for advancing perinatal services redesign using a replicable framework that promotes positive perinatal outcomes, equity, and sustainability through effective, context-responsive implementation.

Confirmation: Ethics approval COMREC: P.10/18/2498 and UIC IRB: 2018–0845

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S95 Bridging knowledge and collaboration: empowering public health professionals in dementia risk reduction

## Rachel Goldberger

## Alzheimer's Association, Chicago, IL, USA

## Correspondence: Rachel Goldberger (rbgoldberger@alz.org)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S95

Background: The Alzheimer's and Dementia Care ECHO® Program, hosted by the Alzheimer's Association, is a free, case-based telementoring program that uses videoconferencing to connect dementia experts with community-based professionals to increase access to dementia risk reduction, detection, diagnosis, and best-practice care management. In 2023, the Public Health Center of Excellence for Dementia Risk Reduction (Center) and the Alzheimer's Association Project ECHO Program collaborated to develop an ECHO program for state and local health officials to increase knowledge and confidence around dementia risk factors and integrate brain health messaging into chronic disease prevention programs. As evidence around dementia risk reduction continues to grow, public health professionals must understand the science and strategies to implement brain health messaging in their chronic disease prevention efforts and community health improvement plans.

Methods: To address this need, the Alzheimer’s and Dementia Care ECHO® Program for Public Health Professionals was developed. This process involved designing and writing the curriculum, identifying subject matter experts and a host state to pilot the initiative. The resulting curriculum explores evidence-based dementia risk factors associated with cognitive decline, integrates the social determinants of health framework, and demonstrates how to embed these elements into chronic disease prevention programs and community health strategies. Designed to be both informative and adaptable, it encourages collaboration among local health departments. Each session includes a 10–15-min didactic by a subject matter expert, followed by a case presentation led by one of the local health department attendees, and a group discussion. The curriculum is intentionally flexible, allowing states to incorporate unique data and experiences while bolstering participants’ understanding and confidence regarding dementia and cognitive decline risk factors.

Findings: In February 2024, the Alzheimer’s and Dementia Care ECHO® Program for Public Health Professionals pilot launched in partnership with the Tennessee Department of Health. Building on this momentum, Washington State Department of Health and Oklahoma State Department of Health launched their pilot programs in spring 2025.

Implications for D&I Research: This panel presentation will spotlight the development, implementation, and impact of these three state-led pilot series.

Primary Funding Source: Centers for Disease Control and Prevention.

## S96 Tailoring the ECHO model for state-level public health infrastructure: lessons from Tennessee, Washington, and Oklahoma

## Mickal Lewis

## Alzheimer’s Association, Chicago, IL, USA

## Correspondence: Mickal Lewis (midlewis@alz.org)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S96

Background: Through the Alzheimer’s and Dementia Care ECHO® Program for Public Health Professionals, three states, Tennessee, Washington, and Oklahoma, participated in the ECHO series to build public health capacity around dementia risk reduction. Each state adapted the core curriculum to align with its unique public health infrastructure, workforce needs and strategic priorities.

Methods: The Tennessee Department of Health (TDH) launched its pilot in spring 2024, and used it to further bolster their strong commitment to elevating dementia as a statewide public health priority. With support from the Tennessee Commissioner of Health, and leadership across TDH’s Community Health Services and Central Office divisions, TDH was able to ensure alignment with existing chronic disease prevention programs and increase recruitment.

Findings: As a result, 98 individuals, representing 34 counties, one metro, and seven individuals representing Central Office, inclusive of 3 divisions participated in the pilot. The series showcased effective communication, strategic alignment, and integration of brain health messaging into public health planning. The Washington State Department of Health launched in spring 2025, focusing on cross-jurisdictional collaboration and peer learning. Designed to integrate dementia messaging into existing public health programs, the series covered topics ranging from social determinants of health to action planning and community engagement. As a result, 31 participants from 15 health departments engaged in the series, enriching both the learning experience and practical application. The Oklahoma State Department of Health prioritized rural and underserved communities by partnering with aging services and community-based organizations. The pilot supported 14 participants and 10 health departments, bringing valuable insights from local contexts and highlighting opportunities for community-level integration.

Implications for D&I Research: Across all three states, adaptation processes were guided by implementation of science principles, including community engagement, iterative feedback, and alignment with existing chronic disease prevention programs. Strong leadership support and strategic alignment were key drivers of successful implementation. This multi-state pilot illustrates how the ECHO model can be flexibly adapted while maintaining core fidelity, offering a replicable approach for other jurisdictions and contributing to the growing field of dissemination and implementation science.

Primary Funding Source: Centers for Disease Control and Prevention

## S97 Evaluating the impact of the Alzheimer’s and Dementia Care ECHO® Program for Public Health Professionals: a multi-state assessment

## Olivia Woodward

## Emory Centers for Public Health Training and Technical Assistance, Atlanta, GA, USA

## Correspondence: Olivia Woodward (olivia.woodward@emory.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S97

Background: The Alzheimer’s and Dementia Care ECHO® Program for Public Health Professionals, was piloted to assess its capability to address dementia risk reduction in public health settings. To evaluate the program’s impact, the Public Health Center of Excellence on Dementia Risk Reduction partnered with the Emory Centers to evaluate changes in knowledge, confidence in taking action, and collaboration around dementia risk reduction among participants from public health agencies in Tennessee, Washington, and Oklahoma. The evaluation of this multi-state pilot also captured participants’ intentions to implement learnings from the series in their work and suggestions for improvement.

Methods: After the conclusion of the multi-state pilot, evaluation data was aggregated for comprehensive analysis. Across the three pilot states, 143 individuals from 62 public health agencies participated in the 6-week series. Participants represented a wide range of roles within their agencies; nurse (26%, n = 37) and health educator (22%, n = 32) were the top two roles among the sample. Almost all respondents (92%, n = 99) were affiliated with a local/regional or state health department. Ninety-five percent of participants (n = 85) attended at least four of the six ECHO sessions.

Findings: Evaluation surveys were administered before the first session and after the conclusion of the six-weeks. Survey respondents demonstrated a statistically significant increase in knowledge (+45%) and confidence (+29%) among dementia risk reduction topics. Additionally, almost all respondents (85%) reported more frequent discussions, and over half (62%) reported more frequent collaboration on dementia risk reduction activities after the series. Most respondents (72%) planned to implement learnings from the series immediately or within 30 days. Respondents reported integrating brain health into existing programs and strengthening new or existing interdepartmental collaboration, and increasing outreach to raise awareness of modifiable risk factors as a result of the series. Participants also provided recommendations for improving the series, such as expanding topics and increasing accessibility.

Implications for D&I Research: Overall, 93% of respondents were satisfied with the ECHO series, finding it an effective way to develop their capacity to implement dementia risk reduction activities.

Primary Funding Source: Centers for Disease Control and Prevention

## S98 Task shifting evidence-based care to lower cadre health care workers: the case for working with traditional healers to deliver HIV testing in South Africa

## Carolyn Audet1, Praises Mnisi2, Amanda Mwelase2, Ryan Wagner2, Wonderful Mabuza2

## 1Vanderbilt University Medical Center, Nashville, TN, USA; 2University of the Witwatersrand, Johannesburg, South Africa

## Correspondence: Carolyn Audet (carolyn.m.audet@vanderbilt.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S98

Background: Task shifting of care delivery from professionals with higher levels of training to those with less formal training is a commonly employed implementation science strategy. In low- and middle-income countries, task shifting strategies can increase the reach of evidence-based practices, including HIV testing.

Methods: We recruited and trained traditional healers from ten clinical catchment areas in rural South Africa to conduct HIV counseling and testing services with their clients. Training followed national guidelines. Healers who successfully passed their coursework and exams completed surveys about implementation feasibility, acceptability, and appropriateness of healer-initiated HIV testing. All were provided with materials (HIV test kits, symptom screening tools), linked with their local facility to ensure smooth referral of patients and contacted weekly by a study team to facilitate collection of data.

Findings: One hundred and forty-seven healers successfully completed each of the training components (94%).
