All healers agreed with the statements, “I welcome healer led HIV counseling and testing in my community” and “Healer led HIV counseling and testing seems like a good idea given the culture of this area.” When looking to the potential to address barriers to testing, 98% agreed or strongly agreed with the statement, “Healer led HIV counseling and testing will help address barriers to HIV counseling and testing.” The majority (87%) agreed or strongly agreed with the statement “I trust that the health system will respect the work I do to identify people living with HIV.” Over four months, healers consulted 1,297 clients; 918 (71%) agreed to receive allopathic testing and screening services. Of those tested, 43 (5%) were newly diagnosed with HIV.

Implications for D&I Research: Task shifting HIV counseling and testing services to lower cadre workers, even those not formally acknowledged by the health system, can increase access to vital testing and treatment services for those who otherwise avoid seeking care at allopathic health services.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S99 Bridging the gap: a pilot training and referral program for traditional healers to early breast cancer detection in Tanzania

## Autumn Beavers1, Elizabeth Msoka2, Theresia Edward Mwakyembe3, Irene Masue3, Ayesiga Herman4, Blandina T. Mmbaga2, Lily Gutnik5

## 1University of Alabama—Birmingham, Birmingham, AL, USA; 2Kilimanjaro Clinical Research Institute, Moshi, Tanzania, United Republic of; 3Kilimanjaro Christian Medical College, Moshi, Tanzania, United Republic of; 4Kilimanjaro Christian Medical Collegenjaro, Moshi, Tanzania, United Republic of; 5The University of Alabama at Birmingham, Birmingham, AL, USA

## Correspondence: Lily Gutnik (lgutnik@uabmc.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S99

Background: Breast cancer mortality remains disproportionately high in low- and middle-income countries (LMICs) due to delayed diagnosis. In Tanzania, where many individuals seek care from traditional healers (THs), these practitioners may be uniquely positioned to support early detection. This study evaluated the feasibility and outcomes of a breast health training and referral pathway for traditional healers in rural Tanzania.

Methods: Guided by the Adoption domain of the RE-AIM framework, this study assessed real-world uptake and integration of breast health knowledge and referral practices among THs. Six registered THs (3 male, 3 female; mean age 53) completed a five-day in-person training on breast health, clinical breast exams, and referral protocols. Pre- and post-training knowledge assessments were administered. Following training, traditional healers referred patients to Kilimanjaro Christian Medical Centre (KCMC), a tertiary hospital. Referrals were made based on their clinical judgment and identification of suspicious breast findings, informed by the knowledge and skills acquired during the breast health training program. Monthly focus group discussions were conducted to evaluate implementation and identify barriers.

Findings: Before training, 67% of THs had no formal breast cancer education, though 83% reported some awareness. Median knowledge scores increased from 41% to 74% post-training (p = 0.01). Over six months, 48 patients were referred, and 13 (27%) presented to KCMC (median age: 48). Of these, nine had a prior breast cancer diagnosis, including six with metastatic disease. Themes from focus group discussions included patient-level referral barriers, use of training content, referral decision-making, and collaboration with the biomedical system. Barriers included cost of care, fear, stigma, and spousal influence. Healers reported applying their knowledge to educate communities, screen asymptomatic individuals, and expand practice. They valued their engagement with the biomedical system, felt more integrated into formal health networks, and expressed interest in sustaining referrals and expanding outreach.

Implications for D&I Research: Traditional healers, as trusted community health providers, represent a promising resource for early breast cancer detection in Tanzania. Strengthening their knowledge and referral skills may offer a scalable, community-based strategy for improving early detection and reducing diagnostic delays in LMICs.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S100 Ugandan traditional healers can help achieve the ‘last mile’ in HIV epidemic control

## Radhika Sundararajan1, Misha Hooda1, Srija Gogineni1, Denis Nansera2, Carolyn Audet3, Winnie Muyindike2, Juliet Mwanga-Amumpaire2

## 1Weill Cornell Medicine, New York, NY, USA; 2Mbarara University of Science and Technology, Mbarara, Uganda; 3Vanderbilt University Medical Center, Nashville, TN, USA

## Correspondence: Radhika Sundararajan (ras9199@med.cornell.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S100

Background: Only 75% of people living with HIV (PLWH) in rural Uganda are virally suppressed – far below the 95% target set by UNAIDS. This gap illustrates the need for tailored implementation strategies to identify PLWH and support their linkage and retention in care. Traditional healers (TH) are frequently the initial source of healthcare in rural communities. Using a task-shifting strategy, Ugandan TH were trained to identify PLWH with unsuppressed HIV viral load at their practices and (re)link them to HIV services.

Methods: We trained 66 TH in Mbarara, Uganda to screen for adult PLWH clients through 1) facilitating HIV self-testing for those without testing in the prior 12 months and no prior HIV diagnosis; and 2) evaluating ART adherence using the CASE Adherence Index Questionnaire (CAIQ). Fidelity of these procedures was monitored via structured observations. Clients with a reactive self-test or CAIQ score < 10 underwent HIV-1 viral load (VL) testing. All virally unsuppressed PLWH (VL > 200 copies/mL) were referred to a local HIV clinic to (re)initiate care. A sample of 20 PLWH were interviewed post-enrollment to assess their experiences.

Findings: Between September 2023-July 2025, 277 PLWH were enrolled across 66 healer practices. Among our participants, about half were male, median age was 34 years, and median viral load at enrollment was 97,910 copies/mL. Most were newly diagnosed; of these, 48% (n = 81) the healer facilitated test was their first lifetime HIV test. Most participants (re)linked to care within 30 days – more than half within 7 days. Qualitative interviews illustrate that task shifting HIV testing and linkage to care with TH was successful due to ease of access, perceived confidentiality, and trusting interpersonal relationships. These qualities were noted in contrast to clinic-based services.

Implications for D&I Research: A task shifting strategy with informal health providers like traditional healers has the capacity to fill important service delivery gaps in HIV care. About half of newly diagnosed individuals received their first HIV test through a healer, and those previously disengaged were successfully relinked to HIV care. Traditional healers can complement formal health services and play a crucial role in achieving the ‘last mile’ to end the HIV epidemic.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S101 Transforming access to mental health care through collaboration with traditional and faith healers in Nigeria

## Akinyinka Omigbodun1, Jibril Abdulmalik2, Olayinka Egbokhare2, Obafemi Jegede2, Kafayat Aminu2, Swaran Singh3, Olayinka Omigbodun4

## 1College of Medicine, University of Ibadan, Ibadan, Nigeria; 2University of Ibadan, Ibadan, Nigeria; 3University of Warwick, Warwick, United Kingdom; 4Department of Child and Adolescent Psychiatry, and Centre for Child and Adolescent Mental Health, College of Medicine, University of Ibadan, Ibadan, Nigeria

## Correspondence: Akinyinka Omigbodun (omigbodun@yahoo.com)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S101

Background: Help-seeking for mental illness in Nigeria is pluralistic, with patients frequently seeking care from traditional and faith healers (TFH) instead of formal health services. While TFHs can be important sources of support, help-seeking through them can lead to delays in accessing the necessary biomedical and psychological treatment. Therefore, collaborative approaches are widely advocated to improve access to formal mental health care and reduce delays and other disadvantageous practices of TFHs, whilst retaining the valued aspects of their activities.

Methods: The TRANSFORM project aims to improve access to care and outcomes of serious mental illness among people living in disadvantaged communities by developing an innovative collaborative care model with TFHs in Nigeria. Following in-depth studies into the local population’s understanding of the explanatory models of illness and care, two training packages were developed and implemented to allow TFHs to identify serious mental illnesses, such as psychosis and suicidality, and refer patients to formal health and social services in the community. Training workshops were conducted in May 2024 and a pilot study was launched in July 2024.

Findings: Twenty TFHs were trained to screen their clients for mental illnesses and urgently refer those with signs of serious mental illness for care at local health facilities. Since launch of the screening program, four patients were identified as having signs of severe mental illness and were referred for urgent evaluation. Of these, three engaged with care at the health facility: two were diagnosed with Schizophrenia while one was diagnosed with psychosis with comorbid substance abuse. All three initiated treatment. One concern brought up by TFHs following the training was that was that the patients would not receive prompt attention in the health facility; however, among those urgently referred, all received care and medical stabilization immediately.

Implications for D&I Research: TFHs can be trained to recognize both common and serious mental health problems. Task-shifting mental health screening to TFHs can increase the reach and acceptability of evidence-based mental health treatments among communities who may otherwise avoid formal biomedical care. This approach is currently being scaled up across other communities in Nigeria.

Primary Funding Source: National Institute for Healthcare and Research (UK)

## Global dissemination and implementation science

## S102 Sustained mental health outcomes from the youth readiness intervention: a four-year follow-up study in Sierra Leone

## Wijnand Van Den Boom, Kathryn Noon, Abdulai Jawo Bah, Matias Placencio-Castro, Theresa Betancourt

## Research Program on Children and Adversity, Boston College, Chestnut Hill, MA, USA

## Correspondence: Theresa Betancourt (theresa.betancourt@bc.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S102

Background: Sustaining evidence-based mental health interventions (EBIs) in conflict-affected settings requires innovative implementation strategies. The Youth Readiness Intervention (YRI), a group-based EBI integrating cognitive behavioral/interpersonal/mindfulness approaches, was embedded within a youth entrepreneurship program in Sierra Leone as part of a 2019 Hybrid Type II implementation-effectiveness cluster-randomized trial. Guided by the EPIS (Exploration, Preparation, Implementation, Sustainment) framework, the intervention was implemented using the Collaborative Team Approach (CTA), a quality improvement strategy that supports non-specialist providers through coaching, supervision, fidelity monitoring, and iterative Plan-Do-Study-Act (PDSA) cycles. PDSA cycles were used throughout implementation to iteratively identify and address barriers (e.g., poor attendance) and tailor delivery to rural contexts. The original trial (N = 1,151 youth, 528 third-party reporters) found improvements in depression and anxiety among YRI + ENTR participants, along with better work/training performance reported by community leaders. This study presents findings from a four-year follow-up assessing the sustainability of those mental health outcomes.

Methods: A stratified random subsample of 584 youth was assessed across three study arms: control, entrepreneurship training only (ENTR), and entrepreneurship plus YRI (ENTR + YRI). Outcomes included emotion regulation, psychological distress, and (interpersonal) functioning. Linear mixed-effects models were used to assess the long-term effects of the intervention, and structural equation modeling examined whether COVID-19-related economic and relationship stressors mediated these outcomes.

Findings: At four-year follow-up, ENTR + YRI youth sustained modest but significant reductions in depressive symptoms (β = -0.057) and combined depression/anxiety symptoms (β = -0.047), compared to controls. No sustained effects were found for daily functioning or emotion regulation. ENTR-only participants did not differ from control and ENTR + YRI participants. COVID-19-related economic stressors partially mediated the relationship between intervention group and psychological distress.

Implications for D&I Research: We demonstrate that structured, team-based implementation strategies, such as CTA, guided by the EPIS framework, can support sustained mental health benefits over time. The durability of effects during the COVID-19 pandemic suggests that the YRI helped promote coping mechanisms that enhance psychological resilience. Findings contribute evidence toward the scalable use of D&I strategies (e.g., EPIS) in low-resource settings such as Sierra Leone.

Primary Funding Source: Global Development Services International

## S103 Evaluating sustainment of an evidence-based intervention, Pediatric Early Warning Systems (PEWS), to identify deteriorating pediatric oncology patients in Latin America

## Asya Agulnik1, Sara Malone2, Samantha Hayes1, Rebeca Caires1, Miguel Aguilar Barragán3, Maricela Robles-Murguía1, Sayeda Islam4, Adolfo Cardenas-Aguirre1, Maria Puerto-Torres1, Kim Prewitt5, Bobbi Carothers5, Douglas Luke6, Dylan Graetz1, Virginia McKay5

## 1St. Jude Children's Research Hospital, Memphis, TN, USA; 2Washington University in St. Louis, St. Louis, MO, USA; 33. Casa de la Amistad para Niños con Cáncer, Mexico City, Mexico; 4Washington University in Saint Louis, St. Louis, MO, USA; 5Center for Public Health Systems Science, Brown School, Washington University in Saint Louis, St. Louis, MO, USA; 6Center for Public Health Systems Science, Washington University in St. Louis, St. Louis, MO, USA

## Correspondence: Asya Agulnik (Asya.Agulnik@STJUDE.ORG)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S103

Background: Pediatric early warning systems (PEWS) are evidence-based interventions that aid in the early identification of clinical deterioration in hospitalized children. Sustaining PEWS is crucial for improving patient outcomes. However, few studies successfully measure long-term use of evidence-based interventions, particularly in resource-variable settings. In this study we evaluate PEWS sustainment across diverse hospitals providing childhood cancer care in Latin America and Spain.

Methods: We employed a longitudinal mixed-method design with hospitals participating in Proyecto Escala de Valoración de Alerta Temprana (EVAT), a quality improvement collaborative focusing on PEWS implementation. Hospitals were included if they successfully implemented PEWS between 2018 and 2024. Data on PEWS sustainment were collected for 2 months after PEWS implementation completion and every 6 months from July 2021 to December 2024 (1–6 time points/center). PEWS sustainment was operationalized as > 85% fidelity on three core components: 1) using PEWS with every vital sign assessment, 2) correct PEWS scoring, and 3) adhering to the PEWS algorithm. Local teams collected data via chart review. Hospital organization type (oncology, general, etc.), public/private funding type, nurse:patient ratio, and World Bank country income levels were reported by site leads. Sustainment distributions and descriptive comparisons across hospital characteristics over time were examined.

Findings: As of December, 2024, 61 Proyecto EVAT centers successfully implemented PEWS. Of these, 49 (80.3%) centers participated which completed PEWS implementation 0–6 years previously, contributing a total of 136 evaluation time points. PEWS sustainment was generally high. Only 6 centers were non-sustaining by at least one measure of fidelity during 9 individual timepoints (9/136 or 6.6%); only 1 center was non-sustaining across all 3 indicators at a single timepoint. Sustainment was high across centers regardless of organization and funding types, nurse:patient ratios, and country income-levels over time.

Implications for D&I Research: This study operationalizes and longitudinally assesses sustainment of an evidence-based intervention across resource-diverse hospitals, providing a template for a low-burden methodology to study intervention use over time. Findings demonstrate that PEWS is largely sustainable over time across resource-diverse centers of varying country, hospital, and unit characteristics. Future work will identify components of clinical capacity for sustainability related to challenges sustaining PEWS.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S104 A framework driven in-depth assessment of SAIA implementation fidelity at scale

## Carmen Hazim1, Mery Agostinho2, Christopher Akiba3, Kristjana Ásbjörnsdóttir4, Fernando Amaral2, Joana Coutinho5, Jonny Crocker1, Maria Cruz2, Fatima Cuembelo6, Aneth Dinis1, Sarah Gimbel7, David Katz8, Isaias Ramiro9, Kenneth Sherr1

## 1University of Washington, Seattle, WA, USA; 2Comité para Saúde de Moçambique, Chimoio, Mozambique; 3RTI International, Seattle, USA; 4University of Iceland, Reykjavik, Iceland; 5Comité para Saúde de Moçambique, Beira, Mozambique; 6Eduardo Mondlane University, Maputo, WA, Mozambique; 7University of Washington School of Nursing, Seattle, WA, USA; 8Department of Global Health, University of Washington, Seattle, WA, USA; 9Comité para Saúde de Moçambique, Maputo, Mozambique

## Correspondence: Carmen Hazim (chazim@uw.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S104

Background

The Systems Analysis and Improvement Approach (SAIA) uses systems engineering tools of cascade analysis, process mapping, and quality improvement in monthly cycles to optimize prevention of mother-to-child HIV transmission (PMTCT) services. To better understand SAIA implementation at scale, we developed and applied an in-depth assessment of SAIA fidelity from a recent three-wave stepped wedge cluster randomized trial (SAIA-SCALE) which tested a district-led model of SAIA delivery in 36 facilities in Manica Province, Mozambique.

Methods

The conceptual framework for implementation fidelity by Carroll et al. (2007) was used to develop a multi-component, SAIA-specific fidelity measure that captures adherence to content, coverage, frequency, and duration of an ideal SAIA cycle. Using implementation tracking data captured during SAIA-SCALE, we assessed each individual fidelity component, quantified fidelity per facility-month as a composite classification (“high” or “low”) and determined each facility’s overall “fidelity experience” (“high” defined as > 85% of facility-months with high fidelity). Finally, we explored associations between SAIA fidelity and trial wave, implementation phase, and facility characteristics such as urbanicity, staffing, and previous quality improvement training using logistic regression.

Findings

High fidelity was observed in 73% of 864 facility-months of SAIA implementation with high levels of adherence to SAIA cycle frequency (99%) and coverage (91%). Duration could not be assessed due to lack of data. Among the eight content sub-components assessed, process mapping (84%) and clear workplan tasks (83%) were the least frequently fulfilled. Wave 1 facilities had on average lower proportions (63%) of facility-months with high fidelity compared to wave 2 (81%) and wave 3 (88%) facilities. Furthermore, compared to wave 1, odds of high-fidelity experience were 2.46 times greater [95% CI = 1.05–6.44] for each subsequent wave. High-fidelity experience was also more common among facilities with previous quality improvement training [OR: 5.68; 95%CI: 0.85–38.08].

Implications for D&I Research

Evaluations of fidelity to implementation strategies are important to contextualize program effectiveness but are rare andoften lack standardization. As the first in-depth analysis of an expanded definition of fidelity to SAIA, these findings may be used further to investigate predictors of high fidelity and assess whether high fidelity moderates the effect of SAIA on service-level and patient health outcomes of PMTCT.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S105 Stakeholder-prioritized implementation strategies for scale-up and sustainability of a stepped-care tool for HIV care among youth

## Sarah Hicks1, Nok Chhun1, James Kibugi2, Patricia Odhiambo3, Tobias Odwar3, Alphonce Oguok3, Bryan Weiner1, Kawango Agot2, Mark Ayallo3, Kristin Beima-Sofie1

## 1University of Washington, Seattle, WA, USA; 2Impact Research and Development Organization, Kisumu, Kenya; 3IMPACT Research & Development Organization, Kisumu, Kenya

## Correspondence: Sarah Hicks (smd722@uw.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S105

Background

Interventions are more successful when relevant stakeholders are engaged in decision-making. The nominal group technique (NGT) is a prioritization method designed to equitably distribute decision-making power and enable stakeholders to drive the research process.

Methods

We conducted a modified NGT workshop to obtain diverse stakeholder perspectives and prioritize implementation strategies to support scale-up and sustainability of a stepped-care intervention for adolescents and young adults living with HIV (AYLHIV) in Kenya. Using a sequential explanatory mixed methods design, we conducted surveys with healthcare providers at 12 facilities participating in the Data-informed Stepped-care (DiSC; NCT05007717) study and hosted a multi-day stakeholder workshop to prioritize barrier-matched implementation strategies pre-identified by providers using the Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research (CFIR). Workshop participants ranked strategies on feasibility and effectiveness using a stepwise elimination process to prioritize a final set of strategies that aligned with the Expert Recommendations for Implementing Change categories. Facilitator debrief reports and participant evaluations were used to assess participants’ decision-making and workshop effectiveness.

Findings

The workshop was highly acceptable to participants and enabled efficient identification of 10 implementation strategies. In the pre-workshop survey, providers rated 18 pre-identified strategies aligned with CFIR across measures of feasibility, impact, acceptability, and appropriateness. Workshop participants ranked and rated these same 18 strategies and an additional 7 strategies generated during the workshop (n = 25). Highly prioritized strategies included prioritization of AYLHIV with mental health and viral suppression challenges, improving provider training and resources, and continuous quality improvement meetings throughout stepped-care scale-up. Facilitator debrief reports highlighted the importance of cost-effectiveness, strategy alignment with current programs/systems, and technology access as key influencers. The majority of participants (82.5%) felt that appropriate strategies were identified. Workshop design elements (two-day format; implementation science education session) were viewed favorably in qualitative evaluations.

Implications for D&I Research

Given the diversity of contexts in a national scale-up campaign, there is a need for prioritization approaches that are less prescriptive and allow for identification of diverse implementation strategies that can be selected and optimized for local contexts. Future work is needed to evaluate the impact of using stakeholder-informed strategies over traditional standard-of-care strategies to support national scale-up progresses.

Primary Funding Source: Adolescent HIV Prevention and Treatment Implementation Science Alliance (AHISA)

## S106 Harnessing implementation research methods to strengthen neonatal care improvement: lessons from NEST360

## Kylie Dougherty1, Nebiyou Hailemariam2, Hannah Mwaniki3, Robert Tillya4, Salome Okunade5, Aynalem Hailemichael Frew6, Christine Bohne7, Lisa Hirschhorn8

## 1Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine, Chicago, USA; 2Rice University, Addis Ababa, Ethiopia; 3Aga Khan University, Nairobi, Kenya; 4Ifakara Health Institute, Dar Es Salaam, Tanzania, United Republic of; 5NEST360 Nigeria, Abuja, Nigeria; 6Emory University, Saving Little Lives, Addis Ababa, Ethiopia; 7Rice University, Houston, USA; 8Center for Dissemination and Implementation Science, Northwestern University, Chicago, USA

## Correspondence: Kylie Dougherty (kylie.dougherty@northwestern.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S106

Background

NEST360 is a multi-country alliance in Kenya, Malawi, Nigeria, Tanzania, and Ethiopia working to reduce neonatal mortality in sub-Saharan Africa by improving small and sick newborn care (SSNC). During Phase 1 (2019–2023), NEST360 introduced facility-level quality improvement (QI) and cross-facility data use to increase coverage of key evidence-based interventions (EBIs) such as kangaroo mother care, phototherapy, CPAP, and hypothermia prevention. In Phase 2 (2024–2029), NEST360 integrated implementation research (IR) methods to understand implementation barriers, strategy selection, and synthesize cross-country lessons to improve care coverage and strengthen EBI quality and scale. This abstract describes how IR was used to adapt and strengthen NEST360 implementation.

Methods

Using a multi-country, mixed-methods approach, NEST360 developed implementation research logic models (IRLMs) for specific program activities (e.g., device qualification) and broader program-wide implementation. Barriers and facilitators were identified across the SSNC Implementation Toolkit, and facility-level change ideas were mapped to ERIC (Expert recommendations for implementation change). Implementation outcomes were measured using Proctor’s framework and RE-AIM. Elements of these same frameworks were embedded into QI resources such as change packages. Data sources included facility assessments, routine programmatic data, and stakeholder interviews across sites and global stakeholders.

Findings

IR methods provided a structured lens to identify cross-cutting and context-specific implementation barriers, including workforce, family involvement, and infrastructure, and link them to targeted strategies including integrating QI, biomeds, data and clinical mentorship, increasing spread of cross-country lessons, and adaptation to device maintenance activities. Three IRLMs were developed to illustrate overall program implementation, QI activities, and device functionality initiatives. A comprehensive change package covering five EBIs and aligned with IR frameworks was shared with all NEST-supported hospitals. Implementation outcomes helped to uncover factors contributing to early reductions in neonatal mortality in supported hospitals.

Implications for D&I Research

Integrating IR methods into program implementation offers a context-sensitive model for spreading program learning to improve quality-of-care delivery in SSNC. The NEST360 experience shows how aligning global health efforts with IR frameworks can amplify locally generated solutions and inform scale-up. This work also adds explanatory depth to standard program outcomes, such as changes in mortality, increasing program replicability.

Primary Funding Source: Bill and Melinda Gates Foundation

## S107 Describing the expansion of tele-dementia services to Veterans in Guam and American Samoa through applying the Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research 2.0

## Leah Haverhals1, Marisa-Francesca Brodrick2, Marika Blair Humber2, Christine Gould2

## 1Veterans Health Administration, Aurora, CO, USA; 2Veterans Health Administration, Palo Alto, CA, USA

## Correspondence: Leah Haverhals (Leah.haverhals@va.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S107

Background: Guam and American Samoa have some of the highest proportions of United States Veterans per capita, and many who served during wartime are rapidly aging. The Veterans Health Administration (VA) has clinics on these Pacific Islands for primary and mental health services; however, specialty care is delivered via telehealth, by VA providers traveling to the islands, through beneficiary travel (Veterans traveling), or via community care. In 2023, the VA regional telehealth hub (Clinical Resource Hub [CRH]) in California developed dementia evaluation and management services through their virtual geriatrics team. This abstract describes factors influencing implementation of these services to Veterans in Guam and American Samoa.

Methods: From February-June 2025, we interviewed VA leadership (n = 3), staff and providers at Guam and American Samoa clinics (n = 10), and the VA CRH team (n = 6) by video conferencing technology or in-person during a site visit to Guam in March 2025. Interviews were recorded and transcribed. Two implementation frameworks guided analyses: the Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research (CFIR) 2.0 and the He Pikinga Waiori (HPW) Implementation Framework. Analysts coded data to CFIR and HPW constructs and created role-level matrices summarizing barriers and facilitators to implementing virtual services.

Findings: Barriers included unreliable internet connectivity affected by weather and navigating the substantial time difference. Translation of health recommendations and adjustment of neuropsychological tests administered were also needed. Initial planning to identify staff at clinics facilitated strong communication, as did collaborations with clinic and CRH leadership to create clinics and grids to streamline processes. Partnering with the clinic telehealth-care technician during visits requiring timed assessments proved essential to connect the CRH neuropsychologist by video. Such across-clinic partnerships led to more referrals, improved abilities to address Veterans’ behavioral needs and diagnoses, and increased caregiver support. Veterans and caregivers in the Pacific Islands valued consistent relationships, and staff hoped services would continue.

Implications for D&I Research: Applying the CFIR 2.0 and HPW frameworks proved effective in describing barriers and facilitators to implementing tele-dementia and geriatrics services to Veterans in Guam and American Samoa. As there are similar care needs in Saipan and surrounding islands, identifying these factors so they can be addressed and care improved is essential.

Primary Funding Source: Department of Veterans Affairs

## S108 Acceptability and feasibility of UNICEF's Service Delivery Framework for improving pediatric HIV service delivery in Mozambique, Nigeria, and Uganda

## Susan Nakubulwa1, Geofrey Basalirwa2, Chinye Osa-Afiana3, Herminio Cossa4, Christopher Lubega2, Patience Kukundakwe1, Gertrude Nanyonjo2, Eleanor Moffatt5, Evaezi Okpokoro6, Nanlesta Pilgrim5, Brenda Okech2, Nadia A. Sam-Agudu7,8

## 1Mildmay Research Centre Uganda, Kampala, Uganda; 2Uganda Virus Research Institute (UVRI) and the International AIDS Vaccine Initiative (IAVI), Entebbe, Uganda; 3International Research Center of Excellence, Institute of Human Virology, Nigeria, Abuja, Nigeria; 4Manhiça Health Research Centre (Centro de Investigação em Saúde de Manhiça, Manhica, Mozambique; 5ViiV Healthcare, Durham, NC, USA; 6International Research Center of Excellence, Institute of Human Virology Nigeria, Abuja Federal Capital Territory, Nigeria, Abuja, Nigeria; 7Institute of Human Virology Nigeria, Abuja, Nigeria; 8University of Minnesota Medical School, Minneapolis, MN, USA

## Correspondence: Nadia A. Sam-Agudu (agudu@umn.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S108

Background: The Pediatric Breakthrough Partnership (PBP) was formed to implement UNICEF’s 2019 Service Delivery Framework’s (SDF’s) implementation strategy package for improving pediatric HIV service delivery. The SDF incorporates strategies for system strengthening, demand creation, service delivery models, monitoring and evaluation, and adaptation and learning. We investigated the SDF’s acceptability and feasibility in Mozambique, Nigeria, and Uganda.

Methods: We used a mixed methods approach to collect data in six districts across the three countries from January 2021-December 2023. Quantitative data was collected via survey questionnaires based on the Acceptability and Feasibility of Intervention Measures (AIM/FIM). The AIM and FIM each have 4 questions graded on a five-point Likert scale (max score = 5). An overall mean score was determined for each question and averaged for all 4 questions; the mean (standard deviation) for the group was then calculated. Qualitative data was collected via focus group discussions and in-depth/key informant interviews. Quantitative and qualitative evaluations were guided by the REAIM and CFIR frameworks, respectively. We used purposive sampling to identify participants based on their involvement in pediatric HIV services.
Findings: We recruited 191 participants delivering and receiving pediatric HIV services: 56 (29.3%) from Mozambique, 62 (33.0%) from Nigeria and 73 (38.2%) from Uganda. Survey findings showed an overall mean score (SD) of 4.5 (0.7) for acceptability and 4.4 (0.7) for feasibility of SDF strategies implemented by the PBP. Qualitative findings indicated high acceptability; particularly for HIV testing and ART dispensing in mobile brigades. Feasibility was noted through innovative strategies of peer lay/professional provider education and training. However, challenges including stigma, limited community HIV/ART knowledge, limited community leadership involvement and facility overcrowding were highlighted.
Implications for D&I Research: UNICEF’s SDF package of evidence-based implementation strategies provides an opportunity for high burden, low resourced countries to consolidate efforts and solve problems in pediatric HIV program implementation. We found a high level of acceptability and feasibility for the SDF in Mozambique, Nigeria and Uganda. However, qualitative findings revealed community and system-level barriers. Further scale-up to similar settings should incorporate studies or solutions that evaluate and/or address these challenges to optimize adoption and sustainability of SDF innovations in pediatric HIV service delivery.

Primary Funding Source: ViiV Healthcare

## S109 Challenges and adaptations in community mobilization for clean cookstove implementation in Lagos, Nigeria: FRAME-IS guided approach

## Oghenebrozie Enakeno1, Shivani Mishra2, Deborah Onakomaiya3, Grace Apapa1, Precious Tettehfio1, Oluwadamilola Opadotun4, Ayodeji Omoyeni5, Donald Ezinwanne1, Temitope Akindeire1, Weixi Chen6, Temiloluwa Fagbemi5, Nafesa Kanneh2, Angela Aifah3, Gbenga Ogedegbe2, Erin Hade7, Christopher Olopade8, Kikelomo O. Wright1

## 1Lagos State University, Lagos, Nigeria; 2Institute for Excellence in Health Equity, NYU Grossman School of Medicine, New York, NY, USA; 3Section for Global Health, Department of Population Health, NYU Grossman School of Medicine., New York, NY, USA; 4Lagos State University, Nigeria, Nigeria; 5Lagos State University College of Medicine, Lagos, Nigeria; 6Department of Biostatistics, NYU Grossman School of Medicine, New York, NY, USA; 7Department of Population Health, New York University Grossman School of Medicine, New York, NY, USA; 8University of Chicago School of Medicine, Chicago, IL, USA

## Correspondence: Deborah Onakomaiya (deborah.onakomaiya@nyulangone.org)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S109

Background: Household air pollution (HAP) from traditional biomass fuels in peri-urban Lagos, Nigeria, increases the risk of poor cardiovascular health. Implementing Clean Fuel–Clean Stoves (CF-CS) to reduce HAP and help prevent hypertension, the leading risk factor for cardiovascular disease, faces barriers including cultural resistance, high cost, and logistical constraints. Community mobilization (CM) fosters ownership, empowers individuals, and builds collective capacity, increasing accessibility and adoption. We document challenges and adaptations in CM for a CF-CS intervention, using the Framework for Reporting Adaptations and Modifications to Evidence-based Implementation Strategies (FRAME-IS).

Methods: In a cluster-randomized trial, we enrolled 1,248 adults from 624 households across 32 peri-urban communities in Lagos to promote CF-CS with liquefied petroleum gas (LPG) or ethanol stoves. Implementation challenges and modifications were recorded through team notes, engagement logs, and input from community health extension workers, health education officers, and mobilizers. Trained staff organized data into FRAME-IS modules, systematically categorizing adaptations across pre-implementation, implementation, and post-implementation phases.

Findings: We identified 15 key implementation challenges for the CF-CS intervention, including inaccurate mapping of households, participant unavailability, skepticism due to prior research experience, cultural resistance to adopting new stoves, logistical barriers in riverine areas, and participant displacement. In response, 15 adaptations were implemented: two in the pre-implementation, 11 during the implementation, and two in the post-implementation. Adaptations included community reassessment, revised eligibility criteria, sensitization sessions, safety workshops, extended field operations, and re-engagement strategies for displaced participants. These adaptations involved structural changes to implementation strategies and the addition of new supportive activities.

Implications for D&I Research: Systematic, culturally sensitive modifications enhanced community mobilization for the CF-CS intervention in peri-urban Lagos, Nigeria. FRAME-IS enables structured documentation of adaptations, addressing cultural, logistical, and economic barriers, and improving intervention feasibility. Overall, these adaptations improved enrollment, retention, and timely data collection. Despite these efforts, economic barriers and stove stacking persisted as challenges. These findings highlight the value of FRAME-IS in guiding adaptive implementation strategies and present a transferable model for advancing implementation science in global health and environmental Interventions.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S110 An implementation toolkit for integrating hypertension management into HIV care in Nigeria using the knowledge to action framework

## Nafesa Kanneh1, Shivani Mishra1, Afua Siaw1, Nina Uzoigwe2, Deborah Onakomaiya3, Weixi Chen4, Oluwatosin Odubela5, Daniel Henry6, Angela Aifah3

## 1Institute for Excellence in Health Equity, NYU Grossman School of Medicine, New York, NY, USA; 2NYU Grossman School of Medicine, New York, NY, USA; 3Section for Global Health, Department of Population Health, NYU Grossman School of Medicine., New York, NY, USA; 4Department of Biostatistics, NYU Grossman School of Medicine, New York, NY, USA; 5The Nigerian Institute of Medical Research, Lagos, Nigeria, Lagos, Nigeria; 6Cardiovascular Research Unit, University of Abuja., Abuja, Nigeria

## Correspondence: Nafesa Kanneh (Nafesa.Kanneh@nyulangone.org)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S110

Background

In Nigeria, the dual burden of HIV and hypertension demands integrated care approaches. However, implementation strategies are rarely packaged in ways that support adaptation or scale-up, resulting in fragmented efforts and limited cross-site learning. Drawing on two National Institute of Health (NIH)-sponsored implementation studies in Akwa Ibom and Lagos (UH3HL154498 and R01HL147811), we developed a practical implementation toolkit to address this gap.

Methods

Using the Knowledge-to-Action (KTA) framework—a model outlining how evidence is created and applied in practice—we mapped implementation strategies across knowledge creation and action phases. Inputs included training materials, fidelity tools, stakeholder engagement structures, communication approaches, and policy translation mechanisms. The toolkit was informed by discussions with two research staff members from the implementation teams of both studies to ensure comprehensive representation of all strategies.

Findings

The final toolkit includes 17 tools across five domains—training, workflow integration, community engagement, quality monitoring, and policy alignment—mapped to the KTA framework. For practical guidance, it features standardized training modules, screening cards, and fidelity checklists to support routine hypertension-HIV integration. Contextual adaptations include role adjustments for community health workers, locally tailored training content, and site-specific retention strategies such as radio jingles in Akwa Ibom and SMS reminders in Lagos. Communication tools include WhatsApp peer learning groups, policy briefing templates, and community materials to support engagement across clinical, community, and policy levels.

Implications for D&I Research

This implementation toolkit provides a replicable, flexible model for integrating noncommunicable disease services into HIV care in resource-limited settings. Guided by the Knowledge-to-Action (KTA) framework, the toolkit supports the integration of hypertension management into HIV care by providing practical guidance, contextual adaptations, and communication strategies to inform researchers, community-based organizations, and policymakers working on similar health challenges By documenting and packaging locally adapted strategies across the full KTA cycle, the toolkit supports cross-context learning and accelerates the translation of implementation research into practice. This work highlights the role of toolkits as both implementation and dissemination mechanisms—bridging research and real-world delivery to advance the goals of Dissemination and Implementation (D&I) science.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S111 Adapting the HealthMpowerment (HMP) mobile application using the ADAPT-ITT model for HIV prevention and treatment services among youth in Africa

## Marie-Claude Lavoie

## University of Maryland School of Medicine, Baltimore, MD, USA

## Correspondence: Marie-Claude Lavoie (mlavoie@ihv.umaryland.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S111

Background: Adaptation plays a critical role in implementation science (IS) by optimizing the fit of evidence-based interventions to local contexts and populations. Adaptation is especially relevant in digital health, where technologies, user preferences, and needs evolve. Yet, methodological approaches for guiding and documenting adaptations are seldomly included. We describe a multi-phase process, grounded in an IS framework, and results for the adaptation of a digital intervention, the HealthMpowerment (HMP) app, for HIV prevention and treatment for sexual and gender minority (SGM) youth in Africa.

Methods: Guided by the Assessment, Decision, Administration, Production, Topical Experts, Integration, Training, and Testing (ADAPT-ITT) IS model, we adapted HMP for SGM youth in Zambia, Malawi, Kenya, and Nigeria. We conducted eight focus group discussions (FGDs) with SGM youth (N = 40) living with or at risk for HIV and four with other collaborators (clinicians, peers, policymakers) (N = 29) to inform app adaptations. Subsequently, we conducted beta testing with 40 SGM youth to assess the app's usability using a validated scale. Data were collected from June 2024 to April 2025. Rapid qualitative and descriptive analyses were conducted. We used the Mobile App Rating Scale (MARS) to summarize the adaptations.

Findings: HMP adaptations were mainly within the MARS's Functionality and Information Quality domains. Functionality enhancements included adding content in local languages and integrating WhatsApp. Information Quality adaptations focused on expanding resources (e.g., mental health, economic strengthening) and ensuring that healthcare referrals made via the app were to SGM-competent providers. Engagement adaptations centered on increasing support and reducing response times from providers through the app, along with added gamification. Safety was prioritized and addressed by creating an emergency lockout code. For Subjective Quality, participants reviewed the app favorably for its PrEP/ART adherence support. The results from the beta-testing indicated that > 86% of participants agreed/strongly agreed that the HMP app was easy to use, and 89% felt confident using it. Overall, 61% agreed/strongly agreed that they used the app frequently.

Implications for D&I Research: Guided by the ADAPT-ITT model, the HMP app was collaboratively and iteratively adapted with SGM youth and others. This methodological approach enhances HMP's functionality, quality, and safety features, responding to users' needs.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S112 Using FRAME to characterize provider-identified adaptations to integration of a stepped care intervention for perinatal mood and anxiety disorders in maternal-child-health clinics in Kenya

## Agnes Karingo Karume1, Asterico Neema1, Yuwei Wang2, Anne Kaggiah1, Nancy Ngumbau1, Alexandra Rose3, Lincoln Pothan2, Anna Larsen3, Linnet Ongeri4, Amritha Bhat3, Dorothy Mangale5, John Kinuthia1, Anjuli Wagner2, Keshet Ronen2

## 1Kenyatta National Hospital, Nairobi, Kenya; 2University of Washington, Seattle, WA, USA; 3UW Medicine, Seattle, WA, USA; 4Kenya Medical Research Institute, Nairobi, Kenya; 5Washington University, St Louis, MO, USA

## Correspondence: Agnes Karingo Karume (karumeagnes@gmail.com)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S112

Background

In Kenya, perinatal depression and anxiety are common yet often untreated due to limited access to mental health specialists and inadequate routine screening. The Integrated Perinatal Mental Health (IPMH) Project evaluates a stepped-care package: universal screening, lay-delivered Problem Management Plus (PM +), and telepsychiatry. Successful implementation requires contextually appropriate adaptations to ensure feasibility, acceptability, and fidelity in maternal-child-health clinic settings. Our objective was to elicit and describe adaptations to the IPMH interventions.

Methods

Between February-June, 2025 we monitored provider-driven adaptations through structured group problem-solving meetings facilitated by the study team at 10 maternal-child health clinics in western Kenya. Health providers participated in five sessions per site to identify implementation challenges and propose solutions. Adaptations to the intervention package were coded using the Framework for Reporting Adaptations and Modifications-Expanded (FRAME) and to Evidence-based Implementation Strategies (FRAME-IS).

Findings

Thirty-one unique adaptations were identified (intervention = 26, strategies = 5) with 30 (97%) being context-related, primarily involving format changes to align with clinical workflows. One adaptation focused on provider training to enhance competency in delivering the intervention. Primary adaptation goals included improving feasibility (n = 11, 35%), reach (n = 7, 23%), fit with recipients (n = 6, 19%) and retention (n = 5, 16%). Most adaptations were driven by organizational barriers (n = 16, 52%), followed by recipient-related constraints (n = 14, 45%). Most targeted the intervention recipients—perinatal women (n = 21, 68%) versus facility staff (n = 10, 32%). The study team co-developed four cross-site adaptations in response to provider concerns: training to support women with pregnancy/infant loss, streamlining PM + for missed sessions, appointment cards, and provider diaries. Screening adaptations (n = 12, 40%) like reorganizing clinic space and adding privacy curtains aimed to enhance privacy. PM + and telepsychiatry adaptations (n = 14, 45%) focused on appointment coordination, using reminder calls and revised provider schedules to reduce missed sessions and improve retention. Most adaptations were adopted (97%).

Implications for D&I Research

Provider-identified adaptations targeted improved integration into routine clinic practices primarily aiming to reduce barriers to intervention feasibility and reach. These findings underscore the importance of responsive, context-driven adaptations to enhance adoption of integrated mental health services. Systematically capturing frontline worker feedback and supporting iterative adaptations can enhance fit, equity, and long-term sustainability.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S113 Effects of the 4 youth by youth HIV prevention intervention on sexually transmitted infection testing uptake among adolescents and young adults in Nigeria: findings from a stepped-wedge cluster-randomized trial

## Peter Kalulu1, Oluwakorede J Adedeji2, Olufunto A. Olusanya1, Chiyere Arinze1, Temitope Ojo1, Ashley Bardon1, Hong Xian3, Titilola Gbaja-Biamila4, Chisom Obiezu-Umeh3, David Oladele5, Ucheoma Nwaozuru6, Adesola Z. Musa4, Collins O. Airhihenbuwa7, Nora Rosenberg8, Rhonda BeLue9, Donaldson F. Conserve10, Jason J Ong11, Lei Zhang11, Susan Nkengasong12, Weiming Tang13, Olugbenga G. Ogedegbe14, Joseph D. Tucker15, Oliver C. Ezechi4, Juliet Iwelunmor1

## 1WashU Medicine, St. Louis, MO, USA; 2University of Ilorin, Kwara, Nigeria; 3Saint Louis University, Saint Louis, MO, USA; 4Nigerian Institute of Medical Research, Lagos, Nigeria; 5Nigerian Institute of Medical Research, Yaba, Lagos, Nigeria; 6Wake Forest School of Medicine, Winston-Salem, NC, USA; 7Georgia State University, Atlanta, GA, USA; 8University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill, Chapel Hill,, NC, USA; 9The University of Texas at San Antonio, San Antonio, TX, USA; 10George Washington University, Washington, DC, USA; 11Melbourne Sexual Health Centre, Carlton, Australia; 12London School of Hygiene & Tropical Medicine, London, United Kingdom; 13University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill, Chapel Hill, NC, USA; 14New York University School of Medicine, New York, NY, USA; 15London School of Hygiene and Tropical Medicine, London, United Kingdom

## Correspondence: Peter Kalulu (kalulu@wustl.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S113

Background: Sexually transmitted infections (STIs) pose a major public health issue globally, particularly among adolescents and young adults (AYAs). In Nigeria, high STI rates combined with low testing are exacerbated by fear, social issues, and limited access to services. This study evaluated the effectiveness of youth-led, participatory interventions known as 4 youth by youth (4YBY) on increasing STI testing among AYAs in Nigeria.

Methods: We used participatory action research, which included a crowdsourced open call, designathon, apprenticeship bootcamp, and participatory learning communities with trained facilitators, to co-develop a youth-friendly 4YBY intervention aimed at increasing the adoption of 1) 4YBY HIV self-testing bundles, containing STI testing instructions, and 2) peer support and technical assistance. The youth-friendly 4YBY intervention was implemented using a stepped-wedge cluster randomized trial across 32 local government areas in 14 states, covering four of Nigeria's geopolitical zones. The 4YBY intervention was sequentially rolled out across clusters over a 24-month period. Data were collected through surveys and clinical records, analyzed using generalized linear models, and reported with the RE-AIM framework to assess the effectiveness of the 4YBY intervention in increasing STI testing, including for syphilis, gonorrhea, and chlamydia.

Findings: A total of 1,500 AYAs (50.20% males and 49.80% females), aged 14–24, were reached across 32 LGAs, with most having completed secondary education (68.6%), primarily from Igbo (36%) and Yoruba (30%) ethnic groups. Baseline STI testing uptake was low, with syphilis at 3.16%, gonorrhea at 3.29%, and chlamydia at 2.08%. After 24 months, there were significant increases: syphilis testing increased to 43.25%, gonorrhea to 42.94%, and chlamydia to 43.01% (p < 0.0001 for all outcomes). Adjusted risk ratios indicated a strong effect of the youth-friendly 4YBY HIV prevention intervention: syphilis (RR 17.83), gonorrhea (RR 14.99), and chlamydia (RR 29.24).

Implications for D&I Research: Co-designed with youth through peer support and participatory learning, the 4YBY intervention achieved high adoption and reach of STI testing, demonstrating potential for long-term maintenance of improved health behaviors among AYAs. These findings highlight the importance of youth involvement in creating effective health interventions and support scaling youth-led, participatory strategies to enhance STI testing in similar settings.

Primary Funding Source: Eunice Kennedy Shriver National Institute of Child Health & Human Development of the National Institutes of Health.

## S114 Quantifying healthcare worker preferences for HIV service delivery features using best–worst scaling in KwaZulu-Natal, South Africa

## Noelle Le Tourneau1, Aaloke Mody1, Ellen Mukwekwerere2, Buyisile Chibe3, Franco Musiello4, Kombatende Sikombe5, Zaynab Essack6, Anjali Sharma5, Elvin Geng1, Alastair van Heerden6, Lindsey Filiatreau1

## 1Washington University in St. Louis, St. Louis, MO, USA; 2Washington University in St. Louis, St Louis, MO, USA; 3Human Sciences Research Council, Pietermaritzburg, South Africa; 4Wits Health Consortium, Witwatersrand, South Africa; 5Centre for Infectious Disease Research in Zambia, Lusaka, Zambia; 6Human Sciences Research Council, Sweetwaters, South Africa

## Correspondence: Noelle Le Tourneau (lnoelle@wustl.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S114

Background: As ART coverage has rapidly expanded across sub-Saharan Africa, preventing treatment interruptions after initiation and ensuring continued engagement in care are key to achieving sustained viral suppression. Understanding which HIV care service delivery features matter most to healthcare workers (HCWs) can inform program operations to improve retention and support clients upon re-engagement in care.

Methods: We used a best–worst scaling (BWS) survey, a preference methodology, to understand how HCWs rated and prioritized different care delivery features for supporting client engagement in HIV care at 36 clinics in KwaZulu-Natal Province, South Africa between May 13, 2024, and October 28, 2024. BWS was used to quantify the relative importance of 13 service delivery features (attributes) in HIV care (e.g., flexible refills, drug pick-up coordination, friendly providers, privacy) selected from the literature and reviewed by community members. Mean preference weights (MPWs), calculated from participants' most and least selected relevant barriers among 4 options across 13 choice sets, were rescaled using Hierarchical-Bayes estimation (out of 100) and compared across healthcare worker cadre.

Findings: Among 261 HCWs surveyed (81.2% female, median age of 42 years (IQR 35–50), 64.8% professional HCW, 16.5% lay HCW, and 18.0% administrative staff, with 74.7% working in their position for 6 or more years), the highest-rated service delivery feature for clients in HIV care was 1) patient privacy during visits (MPW: 15.13, 95% CI 14.53–15.75), followed by 2) timely viral load monitoring and sharing results with clients (12.76, 95% CI 12.11–13.42), and 3) providers that are kind when clients return to clinic after time away from care (9.59, 95% CI 9.11- 10.06). HCWs ranked features such as hotline support (5.00, 95% CI 4.60–5.41), community delivery of ART (4.91, 95% CI 4.54–5.30), and flexible appointment scheduling (4.22, 95% CI 3.92–4.53) lowest. Preferences were consistent across healthcare worker cadre.

Implications for D&I Research: This best–worst scaling study highlights the importance of strategies to sustain engagement that should strengthen confidentiality, lab result timing and discussion with clients, while providing kind reception to returning clients and tailoring additional support for populations facing structural barriers. Preference heterogeneity underscores the need for differentiated care models responsive to both client and HCW priorities.

Primary Funding Source: Gates Foundation

## S115 Decolonizing and innovating implementation of suicide prevention: results from a mixed-methods hybrid type II pilot cluster randomized controlled trial by mothers for mothers in rural Pakistan.

## Ashley Hagaman1, Gul Saeed1, Sidra Mumtaz2, Javeria Tanveer2, Erum Hamid2, Sundus Rehman2, Noor Ul Huda2, Atif Rahman3, Siham Sikander3

## 1Yale School of Public Health, New Haven, CT, USA; 2Global Institute of Human Development, Islamabad, Pakistan; 3University of Liverpool, Liverpool, United Kingdom

## Correspondence: Ashley Hagaman (ashley.hagaman@yale.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S115

Background: Low- and middle-income countries account for 75% of global suicide deaths but produce less than 10% of research. South Asia has the highest rate of female suicide fatalities worldwide. In Pakistan, peer-led maternal mental health interventions have proven effective; however, structural and social stigma impede suicide disclosure and care-seeking and no community-level suicide prevention trials exist. Leveraging Pakistan’s existing maternal mental health infrastructure, this study evaluated the feasibility, acceptability, and fidelity of a co-designed, decolonized, peer-led maternal suicide prevention intervention.

Methods: Following extensive co-design with a mother-led community advisory board of individuals with lived experience of suicide, we conducted a mixed-methods, hybrid type II cluster randomized controlled trial (cRCT) in rural Pakistan. Enhanced usual care included primary care integrating WHO mhGAP depression and suicide prevention protocols. The intervention, delivered by 11 Peer Mothers, included decolonized safety planning and six months of home visits. Implementation strategies tested included (1) peer-delivered brief interventions and (2) image-based, storytelling-driven safety planning, diverging from Western, literacy-dependent models. Implementation outcomes were measured through longitudinal periodic reflections, CFIR2.0 qualitative interviews, and culturally adapted quantitative tools assessing feasibility, acceptability, appropriateness, and fidelity. Clinical outcomes included suicidal ideation/behaviors, depression, help-seeking, and novel culturally-derived suicide risk measures.

Findings: The cRCT met all pre-determined milestones for feasibility, acceptability, and fidelity, attaining high recruitment (88%), retention (88% for mothers, 91% for peers), and strong peer-delivered intervention fidelity and competency. Intervention participants demonstrated improved clinical and help-seeking outcomes. Facilitators included collaborative learning with health workers and family engagement. Barriers included structural constraints (e.g., high turnover of medical officers) and complex family and community negotiations. Our triangulated mixed-methods data and decolonized approach enhance understanding of complex implementation and innovate on standard implementation measures.

Implications for D&I Research: D&I approaches are critical to co-designing implementation models for resource-strained, culturally diverse settings. Findings indicate that Peer-led intervention delivery is a feasible and acceptable model for suicide prevention among mothers in Pakistan. We contribute to the evidence base for culturally embedded, community-led suicide prevention implementation strategies in LMICs, reinforcing the potential of peer-led models to scale high quality access to mental health services.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S116 How to listen to multiple voices: moving from ethnography to implementation mapping

## Ana Baumann1, Sarah Burack2, Comfort Asante3, Aliness Mwilaba-Dombola3, Sam Miti3, Christian Musirikare3, Lusungu Musimuko3, Diana Mwanza3, Tasha Ngalande3, Kelvin Kapungu3, Lindsey Kaufman1, Thembekile Shato4, Petronella Singogo3, Michelle Silver5, Jean Hunleth6

## 1Washington University in St. Louis, St. Louis, MO, USA; 2Washington University in St. Louis, Saint Louis, USA; 3National Health Research & Training Institute, Ndola, Zambia; 4Washington University in St. Louis, St Louis, USA; 5Washington University in St. Louis, St. Louis, USA; 6Department of Surgery (Division of Public Health Sciences) and Alvin J. Siteman Cancer Center, Washington University School of Medicine, St. Louis, MO, USA

## Correspondence: Ana Baumann (abaumannwalker@wustl.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S116

Background: Scholars have long advocated for the engagement of different voices from the community in the selection and development of implementation strategies. Several methodologies exist to support the incorporation of multiple voices, including ethnography. In the Reaching for Equity in Adolescent Care through HPV vaccination (REACH) study, we drew on ethnographic methods to explore the contextual factors that shape implementation of HPV vaccination and engage different community member (adolescents, healthcare providers, household members, and community leaders) in three healthcare settings (rural, urban, and peri-urban) in Zambia. This study aims to show how we moved from ethnography to implementation mapping with a constant question in our mind: who is benefiting from this intervention?

Methods: We focused on understanding the experience of HPV vaccination at multiple levels, as well as from a range of perspectives. We did so through participant observation, key informant interviews, focus group discussions, and participatory workshops, including mapping and roleplays. Findings were organized and analyzed in multiple ways to honor the different methods used, including but not limited to inductive and deductive coding of interview and focus group transcripts and fieldnotes, drawing on theory from anthropology and childhood studies, as well as the different versions of the Consolidated Framework of Implementation Research. Data were triangulated, member checked with the community, and the team used reflexivity to reflect on the process of designing performance objectives and outcomes. Causal pathway diagrams for each strategy were designed to further clarify mechanisms of change. We held meetings with our Research Advisory Board and partners to gather feedback on the feasibility and impact of the proposed strategies.

Findings: The ethnographic methods have provided rich understanding of the contextual factors, including the discrepancies and commonalities across the different voices. Reflexivity helped examine team process and bias. Through implementation mapping we selected three bundled strategies: education, monitoring of HPV vaccine, and monitoring of resources.

Implications for D&I Research: While scholars have advocated for listening to community members when selecting and designing strategies, the field of D&I could benefit from further guidance on decision making processes when information from different voices is discrepant.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S117 The full circle: global health reciprocal innovation of task-sharing strategies to address uncontrolled hypertension in the United States

## Makeda Williams

## National Institutes of Health, Bethesda, MD, USA

## Correspondence: Makeda Williams (willimak@mail.nih.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S117

Background: NHLBI supports rigorous domestic and global implementation research that advances implementation of evidence-based interventions (EBI) that address heart, lung, blood, and sleep (HLBS) disorders in the United States and worldwide. NHLBI stimulates research to generate knowledge regarding optimal implementation and sustainability of EBIs, strategies, and guidelines to reduce the domestic and global burden of HLBS disorders that creates unparalleled opportunities for reciprocal innovation. This talk will overview NHLBI’s global health research strategy, which supports implementation research to build capacity in low- and middle-income countries, spur data science innovations appropriate to local settings, and develop technologies that can be used at the community level to address public health needs.

Methods: This strategy includes five pillars: (1) leverage partnerships/collaborations; (2) innovate implementation science; (3) advance mutually-beneficial priorities; (4) support training and career development; and (5) advance emerging areas of science. Annually, NHLBI completes a portfolio analysis of its investment in global health research grants to characterize progress and opportunities. Recently, NHLBI surfaced exemplars that illustrated real and potential impacts of reciprocal innovation, notably moving innovations from global to domestic contexts.

Findings: NHLBI’s strategic partnerships with entities such as Fogarty International Center, UnitedHealth, and Global Alliance for Chronic Diseases have resulted in unique opportunities to leverage implementation research through the lens of reciprocal innovation to address shared health priorities. Given an estimated one billion adults worldwide suffer from hypertension, including nearly half of all adults in the United States, with many living in low-resource settings, NHLBI has funded several hypertension-focused global implementation research trials. NHLBI’s support of scaling up healthcare delivery innovations to address the global burden of hypertension has proved crucial. Two trials—one in Ghana and one in NYC–resulted in reduced blood pressure through task-shifting, practice facilitation, lifestyle counseling/health coaching, and medication adherence support.

Implications for D&I Research: Trials such as these underscore the return on investment in reciprocity, bidirectional learning, and innovation through strategic partnerships and community engagement. Global health research offers unique benefits of reciprocal innovation such as improved treatment and health care delivery options for Americans dealing with health issues that are a burden globally.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S118 Task-shifting for hypertension control: a cluster trial in Ghana informing reciprocal innovation in the U.S.

## Olugbenga Ogedegbe

## NYU Langone Health, Institute for Excellence in Health Equity, New York, NY, USA

## Correspondence: Olugbenga Ogedegbe (Olugbenga.Ogedegbe@nyulangone.org)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S118

Background: Limited access to care and physician shortages remains a critical barrier to hypertension control in sub-Saharan Africa. The Task Shifting Strategy for Hypertension Control (TASSH) in Ghana demonstrated that training nurses to deliver cardiovascular care within community clinics can effectively reduce systolic blood pressure (SBP). This trial has since informed the Advancing Long-term Improvements in Hypertension Outcomes through a Team-based Care Approach (ALTA) trial in New York, which adapts a similar task-shifting model to address persistent hypertension disparities among underserved patients. To inform scalable, equity-focused solutions in both global and U.S. health systems, we evaluated the comparative effectiveness of health insurance coverage (HIC) alone versus TASSH plus HIC among patients with uncontrolled hypertension in Ghana.

Methods: In a pragmatic cluster randomized implementation trial, 32 community health centers in Ghana were randomized to HIC alone or TASSH + HIC. A total of 757 patients were enrolled and followed for 24 months. Both groups received health insurance and scheduled nurse visits, but only the TASSH group received structured nurse-led cardiovascular risk management, lifestyle counseling, and medication initiation/titration. The primary outcome was change in SBP at 12 months; secondary outcomes included BP control and lifestyle changes.

Findings: At 12 months, SBP reduced significantly in both groups, with a greater reduction in the TASSH + HIC group (−20.4 mmHg; 95CI −25.2 to −15) compared to HIC alone (−16.8 mmHg; 95CI −19.2 to −15.6), yielding a significant between-group difference (−3.6 mmHg; p = 0.021; 95CI −6.1 to −0.5; p = 0.021). BP control improved similarly in both groups; lifestyle behaviors showed minimal change.

Implications for D&I Research: The TASSH + HIC strategy led to greater SBP reduction than HIC alone, with comparable BP control and minimal lifestyle change across groups. Findings from this trial reinforce the effectiveness of nurse-led, task-shifting strategies when paired with structural enablers like insurance coverage to reduce SBP in resource-constrained settings. As the foundational model for the ALTA trial in New York, TASSH demonstrates how global implementation strategies can drive reciprocal innovation and inform equity-focused hypertension control efforts across both low- and middle-income countries and underserved U.S. populations.

Primary Funding Source: New York University Langone Health

## S119 From global insights to local impact: using practice facilitation and task-shifting to improve hypertension control in a Federally Qualified Health Center

## Antoinette Schoenthaler

## NYU Grossman School of Medicine, New York, USA

## Correspondence: Antoinette Schoenthaler (antoinette.schoenthaler@nyulangone.org)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S119

Background: Federally Qualified Health Centers (FQHC) provide essential care to medically vulnerable communities but face barriers to implementing complex hypertension interventions due to workforce and resource constraints. Adopting innovations from global settings, like task-shifting, supported by practice facilitation, can help FQHCs optimize workforce roles and workflows to implement evidence-based interventions. Drawing on the Task Shifting Strategy for Hypertension Control in Ghana trial, we evaluated the effectiveness of practice facilitation versus usual care in implementing a team-based intervention (ALTA) for improved blood pressure (BP) control among 652 patients with uncontrolled hypertension at a New York City FQHC.

Methods: We conducted a stepped-wedge cluster randomized trial in six urban primary care practices from 2019 to 2024. All practices began with usual care, followed by a 6–12-month pre-implementation phase before transitioning to the 12-month implementation phase. Practice facilitators supported ALTA implementation, which included medical assistants screening patients for medication nonadherence, clinicians referring patients for home BP monitoring and nurse-led virtual health coaching, and structured documentation and monitoring of patients’ progress. The primary outcome was implementation fidelity, assessed using mixed methods, guided by Proctor’s Implementation Outcomes Framework. The clinical outcome was the proportion of patients achieving BP < 140/90mmHg at the end of the implementation phase.

Findings: Mean patient age was 61.9 (SD 13.6) years, 56.7% were female, 59.5% identified as Hispanic, and 29.2% as Black. Implementation adherence varied across roles: medical assistants screened 53.1% of eligible patients (1,649/3,103) for medication nonadherence; clinicians referred 72.8% (1,200/1,649) for home BP monitoring and health coaching, and 54.3% (652/1,200) enrolled. Of those enrolled, 91.4% (596) had ≥ 1 health coaching session, and 94.5% (563) had ≥ 1 follow-up session. Median implementation dose was six coaching sessions over 5.5 months, with 2.1 BP readings per week. Implementation quality was high, with comprehensive documentation of sessions. In the adjusted model, the implementation phase was associated with 60% higher odds of BP control compared to usual care (z-value = 2.29, p = 0.02).

Implications for D&I Research: This study demonstrates that task-shifting, an effective strategy in global settings, can be successfully adapted through reciprocal innovation and practice facilitation support to optimize workforce capacity and workflows to implement evidence-based interventions in urban safety-net settings.

Primary Funding Source: New York University Langone Health

## Health policy dissemination and implementation science

## S120 Jurisdictional profiles to plan for policymaker engagement, fit, and scalability of implementation efforts

## Elle Pope1, Sofia Morales2, Jair Hurta2, Maxwell Krieger3, Katherine Dunham3, Brandon Marshall4, Xiao Zang5, Erika Crable6

## 1University of California Los Angeles, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 2University of California San Diego, La Jolla, CA, USA; 3Brown University, Providence, RI, USA; 4Brown University School of Public Health, Providence, RI, USA; 5University of Minnesota, Minneapolis, MN, USA; 6University of California, Los Angeles, Los Angeles, CA, USA

## Correspondence: Elle Pope (ellefpope@gmail.com)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S120

Background: Implementation strategies that respond to local health policy environments and address current needs of policymakers are hypothesized to enhance implementation success and sustainment goals. Barriers to policymakers’ use of research are well documented; however, there is little guidance for identifying key policymakers or framing research to effectively engage them in implementation activities. This study developed an interdisciplinary methodological approach—jurisdictional profiles (JPs)—to identify policymakers in 6 jurisdictions/states (Massachusetts, Minnesota, Missouri, New York City, Nevada, Rhode Island) and engage them in the implementation of jurisdiction-tailored simulation models supporting evidence-informed decisions to prevent overdoses. Simulation models use local data to identify optimal combinations of harm reduction services (e.g., naloxone, drug checking, syringe services) but are only effective if policymakers trust and embed models in decision-making processes. We hypothesize that JPs will guide tailoring of the simulation model to enhance model fit and scalability into decision-making processes, and implementation of evidence-informed harm reduction services.

Methods: JPs are living documents updated throughout the 4-year study to include: 1) Political landscape and decision-making authority for overdose prevention (identified through document review of jurisdiction websites); 2) Political rhetoric about harm reduction services (identified through discourse analysis of news/press releases to understand evidence framing); 3) Sentinel policy surveillance of the legal status harm reduction services to inform which to include/exclude in jurisdiction-specific models; 4) Jurisdiction data sources, dashboards, locally de/prioritized statistics to include in the model; 5) Concurrent harm reduction efforts (identified through policy document review). A structured ethnographic template is used to evaluate the utility/relevance of JPs and policymakers’ trust in simulation models.

Findings: To date, JPs were created for 6 jurisdictions to guide EPIS Framework Planning activities. JPs yielded initial lists of policymakers to engage based on their decision-making authority and political support for harm reduction efforts, and informed how to tailor recruitment emails, presentation materials, and simulation models presented in co-design meetings. To date, one co-design meeting occurred (Missouri) which included 12 policy decision-makers. By December 2025, we plan to conduct 6 co-design meetings.

Implications for D&I Research: JPs provide an interdisciplinary approach to support implementation scientists in planning for policy-engaged, tailored implementation efforts to achieve fit and scalability outcomes.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S121 Strategic translators: using the interactive systems framework to map the role of intermediaries in tobacco policy implementation

## Ana Herrera, David Timberlake, Denise D. Payan

## University of California, Irvine, Irvine, CA, USA

## Correspondence: Denise D. Payan (dpayan@hs.uci.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S121

Background: Intermediaries, which include public health officials, community advocates, and government staff, can serve a pivotal role in translating health policy to practice. Examining their contributions (i.e., interpretation, adaptation, and/or communication), the challenges they encounter, and the supports they require is critical to develop effective strategies to support policy implementation.

Methods: We conducted key informant interviews (N = 19) with individuals who implemented state and local flavored tobacco sales restrictions (FTSRs) in California. Data collected focused on strategies used to implement FTSRs, barriers/challenges experienced, and resources needed. We applied the Interactive Systems Framework (ISF) as an organizing lens to categorize strategies and develop a typology that highlights the hybrid roles of intermediaries. This framework informed our interpretation of how these strategies were perceived to impact implementation.

Findings: Policy implementation relied on coordinated, system-level strategies that addressed distinct yet interdependent functions. Strategies within the Synthesis and Translation System included developing educational materials, translating surveillance data for public use, and conducting media campaigns for health and policy education to enhance public understanding, bolster community support, and improve stakeholder policy awareness. The Support System included community engagement, technical assistance, advocacy, convening, and legal interpretation. These were described as key actions to strengthen local capacity, align sectors, and encourage compliance. The Delivery System involved direct enforcement activities through compliance checks, surveillance of tobacco use, and legislative development. While participants affirmed the value of these strategies, they also identified persistent challenges (i.e., limited resources, lack of enforcement capacity, unclear policy language, fragmented agency coordination, community mistrust). Participants highlighted the need for sustained funding, stronger interagency coordination, targeted policy education, and enhanced accountability in enforcement and stakeholder engagement to bolster effective policy implementation.

Implications for D&I Research: This study advances dissemination and implementation science by empirically mapping intermediary-led strategies, revealing hybrid roles that span ISF systems, and emphasizing the importance of centering intermediary perspectives in policy implementation research. Findings underscore that intermediaries actively shape implementation outcomes and overall policy impact by bridging informational, procedural, and relational gaps.

Primary Funding Source: California Tobacco-Related Disease Research Program of the University of California, Grant Number [T32KT4713]

## S122 Implementing increased Medicaid funding for Mobile Crisis Teams under the American Rescue Plan Act: a cross-state comparative analysis

## Jacob Jorem1, Ej Kelley2

## 1Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, USA; 2Columbia University, New York, NY, USA

## Correspondence: Ej Kelley (ejk2204@cumc.columbia.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S122
