Background: Background: Mobile Crisis Teams (MCTs) play a crucial role in providing community-based responses to behavioral health crises across the United States. To reduce this variation in access to and funding for MCTs, the American Rescue Plan Act (ARPA) of 2021 introduced increased Medicaid funding to strengthen and standardize MCTs for states opting in. Despite these efforts, little is known about how ARPA has impacted MCTs or the facilitators and barriers to its implementation across states opting in–among them Colorado, Massachusetts, New York, and North Carolina.

Methods: We conducted qualitative semi-structured interviews with 16 purposefully sampled stakeholders, including state- and city-level Medicaid officials and both urban and rural MCT providers. Interviews were conducted between February and May 2025 in Colorado, Massachusetts, New York, and North Carolina selected to represent variation in geography, politics, and Medicaid system. The transcribed interviews were analyzed thematically, and informed by the innovation and outer setting domains with relevant constructs of the Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research (CFIR).

Findings: The limited adaptability of ARPA’s requirements of 24/7 availability and two-person multidisciplinary teams limited the uptake of the increased Medicaid funding in New York and North Carolina. In contrast, Massachusetts and Colorado successfully integrated ARPA funding by aligning it with existing or parallel Medicaid behavioral health crisis system reforms. The 24/7 availability requirement seemed particularly challenging in rural areas, where low call volumes made sustaining adequate response teams challenging. Staffing shortages emerged as the primary barrier to meeting ARPA requirements. Key facilitators included strong interagency collaboration, supported by enabling legislation and robust community engagement. Effective referral networks linking MCTs to various levels of the crisis system, including 988, emergency departments, inpatient and outpatient settings, and law enforcement, also facilitated implementation.

Implications for D&I Research: This study highlights how federal funding depends on state-level system readiness and infrastructure alignment for effective implementation. Findings underscore the importance of policy adaptability and robust infrastructure for effective implementation. They also highlight the value of using implementation frameworks to assess how states translate federal behavioral health policy into practice—and how funding streams can be leveraged to sustain services across diverse contexts.

Primary Funding Source: The Commonwealth Fund

## S123 Using an environmental policy scan to assess population prioritization and equity in COVID-19 public health strategies in Ontario, Canada

## Claire Gapare1, Sierra Sweet1, Lames Danok2, Jamie Boyd2, Stefan Baral3, Adrienne Chan4, Antoinette Handley5, Sulaiman Lakoh6, Sharmistha Mishra7, Bailah Molleh8, Beate Sander9,10, Stephen Sevalie6,8, Sharon Straus11, Christine Fahim11

## 1Knowledge Translation Program, Li Ka Shing Knowledge Institute, St. Michael’s Hospital, Unity Health Toronto, Toronto, Canada; 2Knowledge Translation Program, Li Ka Shing Knowledge Institute, St. Michael’s Hospital, Unity Health Toronto, Toronto, ON, Canada; 3Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health, Baltimore, MD, USA; 4Sunnybrook Health Sciences Centre, Toronto, Canada; 5Dalla Lana School of Public Health, Toronto General Hospital Research Institute, Toronto, ON, Canada; 6Department of Medicine, College of Medicine and Allied Health Sciences, University of Sierra Leone, New England, Freetown, Sierra Leone; 7Li Ka Shing Knowledge Institute, St. Michael's Hospital, University of Toronto, Toronto, Canada; 8Sustainable Health Systems Sierra Leone, Freetown, Sierra Leone; 9Institute of Health Policy, Management and Evaluation, University of Toronto, Toronto, Canada; 10Health Systems and Policy Research Collaborative Centre, University Health Network, Toronto, Canada; 11Li Ka Shing Knowledge Institute, Unity Health Toronto, Toronto, ON, Canada

## Correspondence: Christine Fahim (christine.fahim@unityhealth.to)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S123

Background: Income, housing and occupation shaped the unequal risk of infection, transmission, and disease severity during the COVID-19 pandemic. We assessed whether Ontario, Canada’s COVID-19 policies were implemented uniformly or prioritized specific populations, and whether health equity was explicitly referenced.

Methods: We conducted an environmental scan of provincial and federal public health and socio-economic COVID-19 strategies in Ontario (January 2020–May 2022) using the Canadian Institute for Health Information (CIHI) COVID-19 Intervention Timeline and a grey literature search. Strategies were screened independently by two researchers and grouped into 10 CIHI-defined categories (e.g., “Vaccines”, “Closures/Openings”). Three researchers double coded the strategies as either “uniform” (applied universally across the populations or contexts without the intent to reduce disproportionate risks of acquisition, transmission, and/or severity if infected) or “prioritized” (intended to reduce disproportionate risks of acquisition, transmission, severity if infected, and/or socioeconomic impacts – such as those related to occupation, housing or income – of COVID-19 for specific populations or settings. Coding was verified by a full team review. We examined whether strategies explicitly referenced equity in their rationale.

Findings: Of 819 strategies identified, 615 (394 provincial, 221 federal) met inclusion criteria; 460 (74.8%) were uniform and 155 (25.2%) prioritized. Strategies related to “Distancing,” “Travel,” and “Public Information” were entirely uniform (n = 134, 100%), followed by “Closures/Openings” (n = 106, 94.6%) and “Financial/Economic” supports (n = 67, 62.0%). “Health Services” (n = 18, 54.5%) and “Vaccine” (n = 45, 48.4%) strategies were often prioritized. Prioritized populations included people in congregate care settings, Indigenous communities, people with disabilities, and others experiencing heightened risk. Of the prioritized strategies, 32.5% explicitly cited equity considerations; other strategies also used equity-oriented language but were implemented uniformly. Some uniform strategies were later replaced with prioritized strategies due to unintended negative impacts (e.g., school closures on mental health).

Implications for D&I Research: Most COVID-19 public health strategies in Ontario were uniformly implemented. Although more targeted strategies—such as vaccine rollout—emerged later, these were not consistently prioritized. These findings highlight the need to embed co-creation and community-informed approaches into pandemic preparedness, so equity is prioritized early and responsive strategies are in place for those most at risk.

Primary Funding Source: New Frontiers Research Fund—Special Call 2022 (NFRF)

## S124 Implementation of the ban on the sale of ultra-processed foods to minors in Oaxaca

## Carolina Pérez Ferrer1, Mariana López Santiago1, Raúl Ulises Hernández Ramírez2, Carlos Manuel López García1, Alma Lizeth Alvarado López1, Anna K. Porter2, Leith Soledad León Maldonado1, Donna Spiegelman2, Tonatiuh Barrientos Gutiérrez1

## 1Instituto Nacional de Salud Pública, Cuernavaca, MR, Mexico; 2Yale School of Public Health, New Haven, CT, USA

## Correspondence: Carolina Pérez Ferrer (carolina.perez@insp.mx)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S124

Background

In 2020, the Mexican state of Oaxaca enacted a law to ban the sale of unhealthy foods and beverages to minors. The law was revised in 2024 to eliminate the prohibition in stores but maintained it in schools. We aimed to explore the law’s adoption, acceptability, appropriateness, feasibility and implementation barriers and facilitators over time.

Methods

This multi-study project employed random sampling of schools in Oaxaca’s Central Valleys and convenience sampling of stores nearby sampled schools. The project included three components: (1) mystery clients to assess law adoption (unsuccessful purchase attempts); (2) a survey on perceptions of acceptability, appropriateness, and feasibility among stakeholders using validated 5-point ordinal scales; and (3) semi-structured interviews with stakeholders to identify implementation barriers and facilitators, guided by the Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research. Data was collected in both schools and stores in 2023 (baseline) and again in schools in 2025.

Findings

At baseline, the law was adopted in 48.3% of the 58 selected schools and in 1.5% of the 65 nearby stores. Overall, 105 stakeholders completed the surveys at baseline: 55 in schools and 50 in stores. The mean acceptability scores were 4.4 in schools and 3.9 in stores. For appropriateness, they were 4.1 and 3.6, respectively. Feasibility received lower mean scores, with 3.8 in schools and 3.3 in stores. There was little change over a year in schools with adoption at 50%, and mean scores of 4.3, 4.1 and 3.7 for acceptability, appropriateness and feasibility respectively. Interviews with 20 stakeholders revealed implementation barriers including inadequate law communication, retailers' economic interests, lack of champions, and no enforcement.

Implications for D&I Research

The initial age-of-sale law may have been partially adopted by schools but not by stores in Oaxaca. The level of adoption, acceptability, appropriateness, and feasibility of the law was similar after its revision to focus on schools only. While the law seems to be generally acceptable among stakeholders, especially schools, there were several barriers identified that affected its implementation. Barriers should be addressed with implementation strategies to improve adoption and maintenance of the law and achieve its potential public health impact.

Primary Funding Source: Medical Research Council (MRC)

## S125 Beyond the letter of the law: a CFIR-informed analysis of street-level enforcement of flavored tobacco sales restrictions

## Ana Herrera1, David Timberlake1, Alec Chan-Golston2, Denise D. Payan1

## 1University of California, Irvine, Irvine, CA, USA; 2University of California, Merced, Merced, CA, USA

## Correspondence: Denise D. Payan (dpayan@hs.uci.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S125

Background: Inconsistent or limited enforcement of flavored tobacco sales restriction (FTSR) policies can compromise policy effectiveness and unintentionally reinforce or exacerbate inequities. These policies are typically enforced by a range of actors, including law enforcement, public health agencies, and other municipal officials. Enforcement plays a critical role in shaping implementation outcomes such as reach, fidelity, sustainability, and equity. However, limited attention has been paid to how health policy enforcement is operationalized and adapted by various actors and across diverse settings.

Methods: We conducted key informant interviews with 14 implementation and 5 enforcement agents, including representatives from law enforcement, public health, and municipal offices across California to investigate local enforcement of FTSRs. Guided by the Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research (CFIR), we used a thematic analysis approach to code transcript data, focusing on determinants across the Outer Setting, Inner Setting, Individuals Involved, and Process domains. We paid particular attention to equity-influencing factors (e.g., differential enforcement practices and approaches) to understand how enforcement is shaped and adapted in local contexts, and how these adaptations influence implementation outcomes.

Findings: Enforcement of FTSRs was shaped by multilevel factors that directly influenced equity and implementation outcomes. Outer setting conditions, including structural inequities, sociocultural norms, retail dynamics, and variability in tobacco control infrastructure, led to uneven enforcement across jurisdictions. Internally, enforcement agencies varied in their access to resources, knowledge, leadership engagement, and alignment with organizational goals, affecting the reach, consistency, and sustainability of efforts. Perceptions of agency authority, capacity, and institutional prioritization revealed role misalignments that constrained fidelity and equity. Finally, process-level factors such as retailer engagement strategies, interaction quality, and enforcement structures further shaped implementation outcomes and overall FSTR impact, underscoring the need for coordinated, equity-focused enforcement approaches.

Implications for D&I Research: This study extends CFIR by integrating an explicit equity lens into the analysis of FTSR enforcement. By identifying how multilevel and intersecting factors shape enforcement processes and outcomes, the findings offer insights into how procedural, structural, and relational dynamics influence the reach, fidelity, sustainability, equity, and broader policy impact. By making equity consequences of enforcement visible, this work advances a framework promoting equitable enforcement and effective public health policy implementation.

Primary Funding Source: California Tobacco-Related Disease Research Program of the University of California

## S126 Doula care for Medicaid beneficiaries in Virginia

## Desirae Leaphart1, Alison Cuellar2, Hong Xue2, Panagiota Kitsantas2

## 1NORC at the University of Chicago, District of Columbia, DC, USA; 2George Mason University, Fairfax, VA, USA

## Correspondence: Desirae Leaphart (ddel5r@gmail.com)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S126

Background: To address rising maternal mortality, maternal morbidity, and racial inequities in maternal care, states are increasingly opting to include doulas as a licensed provider category in the state and, further, to include doula services as a benefit under their Medicaid programs. This study evaluates the implementation of Virginia's Medicaid doula policy and concludes with lessons learned.

Methods: This mixed-methods study used Virginia Medicaid claims, state-certified doula registry data, American Community Survey data, and qualitative interviews with doula Medicaid providers. Quantitative analyses included spatial mapping to assess geographic alignment between doula provider locations and Medicaid-enrolled pregnant individuals, as well as descriptive statistics summarizing service utilization patterns in Medicaid claims data from 2022 to 2025. The qualitative component explored implementation experiences from the perspective of nine practicing Medicaid doulas through semi-structured interviews. All study procedures were reviewed and approved by the George Mason University Institutional Review Board.

Findings: There were 130 state-certified doula Medicaid providers in Virginia at the time of the study. Spatial analysis identified regions of Virginia with high and low concentrations of doula Medicaid providers relative to where pregnant Medicaid beneficiaries are located. Claims analysis revealed that fewer than half of certified doulas billed for services between 2022 and 2025, with most claims being for postpartum services. The qualitative research identified eight key themes that highlight policy implementation lessons regarding administrative burdens, reimbursement rates, and awareness among Medicaid beneficiaries of their eligibility for doula care.

Implications for D&I Research: This study offers actionable insights that could be applied to other states implementing or scaling Medicaid doula policies. Implementation strategies should include working collaboratively with doulas to ensure targeted recruitment in regions with high need but less doulas available; more support for doulas regarding the Medicaid enrollment and reimbursement processes; enhanced collaboration with healthcare providers, case managers, and use of social media and community events to raise awareness among Medicaid beneficiaries about their doula benefits; and adjusting reimbursement rates and the amount and length of billable visits to reflect doulas' scope of work. These lessons can inform broader efforts to expand equitable access to doula care through Medicaid.

## S127 Understanding factors influencing HPV vaccination policy implementation in Kenya

## Priya Sarma, MS1, Cynthia Kairu2, Lynette Kamau2, Ramatou Ouedraogo2, Doris Njomo, PhD3, Peter Szilagyi4, Risa Hoffman5, Jonathan Purtle6, Corrina Moucheraud7

## 1New York University School of Global Public Health, New York City, USA; 2African Population and Health Research Center, Nairobi, Kenya; 3Kenya Medical Research Institute, Nairobi, Kenya; 4University of California Los Angeles, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 5David Geffen School of Medicine at the University of California, Los Angeles, USA; 6Health Management & Policy, New York University School of Global Public Health, New York, NY, USA; 7New York University School of Public Health, New York, USA

## Correspondence: Corrina Moucheraud (cm6512@nyu.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S127

Background: In 2019, Kenya launched a national human papillomavirus (HPV) vaccination program targeting girls aged 9–14 – but vaccine uptake remains low, with only 26% of eligible girls having received at least one dose. This study aimed to generate knowledge about how HPV vaccination policies are implemented by different stakeholder groups and determinants that influence policy implementation success in Kenya.

Methods

We conducted 21 key informant interviews (KIIs) with policymakers from the Kenyan Ministry of Health; county-level health officials; and representatives from implementing agencies, multilateral organizations (e.g., UNICEF), and non-governmental organizations supporting HPV vaccination.
Researchers from the African Population and Health Research Center led the interviews, with a guide informed by the Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research (CFIR). Questions focused on facilitators and barriers to implementing HPV vaccination policies (the Kenya Cancer Policy 2019–2030, the Kenya National Cancer Control Strategy 2023–2027, and the Kenya National Immunization Policy Guidelines 2023) per domains of the CFIR.

Findings

In the outer setting, low public awareness of HPV vaccination was cited as a major barrier to implementation. Religious leaders were frequently mentioned as influential actors in both facilitating and hindering vaccine uptake. Respondents also perceived threats to long-term sustainability of HPV vaccination policies due to insufficient government financing, reliance on external donors for vaccine supply, and lack of sustained political commitment, including recent changes in the global landscape.
In the inner setting, limited collaboration between government agencies at the county-level (e.g., the Ministries of Health and Education) hindered school-based vaccine delivery. This included lack of joint planning and weak ownership of the vaccination agenda by some schools. Irregular review meetings and multiple demands on health worker time further constrained consistent implementation, particularly in reaching out-of-school and nomadic girls.
In the process domain, health worker training about HPV vaccination and performance reviews tied to county-level immunization targets enabled adaptive planning to implement these policies.

Implications for D&I Research

This study highlights important enablers and barriers for HPV vaccine policy implementation. These data can be used to assist policymakers and implementers to better design strategies that fit Kenya’s unique social and health system context to increase HPV vaccine uptake.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## Models, measures, and methods

## S128 The next generation of implementation strategy testing

## Cara Lewis1, Shahnaz Khan2, George Mensah1

## 1Center for Translation Research and Implementation Science; National Heart, Lung, and Blood Institute, National Institutes of Health, Bethesda, MD, USA; 2National Institutes of Health, Bethesda, MD, USA

## Correspondence: Cara Lewis (cara.lewis@nih.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S128

Background: The value of implementation science depends on promising findings of strategies, or the methods used to improve adoption, implementation, sustainment, and scale-up of evidence-based interventions. Implementation strategy taxonomies have proliferated, supporting selection and operationalization of strategies, and curation of their evidence. Yet, the majority of studies included in a 12-year systematic review did not sufficiently test strategies to draw conclusions about their impact. This presentation offers methodological insights to advance the field.

Methods: We completed a portfolio analysis of 181 competing implementation science awards issued by the National Heart, Lung, and Blood Institute from 2014–2024. We coded implementation strategies to characterize awards by reviewing their specific aims using a consensus coding approach. We categorized discrete strategies per the Expert Recommendations for Implementing Change (ERIC) taxonomy published in 2015. We used a content analysis approach to build upon the ERIC taxonomy while maintaining parsimony that allowed for new categories, new discrete strategy labels, and revised definitions. We documented challenges to using the taxonomy, missed opportunities for strategy testing, and exemplars.

Findings: Across 181 grants, 70% of the ERIC strategies were used; with 45 strategies tested in fewer than 20 studies, and 34 strategies tested in fewer than 10 studies. Assess for readiness and identify barriers and facilitators was coupled with tailor strategies in 34% of studies, with adapt intervention included in 65% of those studies. Engage consumers, utilize financial strategies, and change infrastructure were among the least used categories. To adequately reflect tested strategies, we developed one new category–improve intervention fidelity and access–and 15 new strategies across new and existing categories. We observed six opportunities for advancement: (1) Iterate taxonomies more frequently to evolve with the field; (2) Expand strategy characterizations to explicitly include function, and not simply form; (3) Provide granular abstraction of strategies for replication; (4) Select core strategies based on intervention and setting; (5) Distinguish between the intervention and strategy; and (6) Invest in underutilized strategies given promising putative mechanisms.

Implications for D&I Research: This portfolio analysis highlights methodological advances that could refine strategy testing to improve selection, replication, impact, transparency, and scalability.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S129 Navigating complexity in implementation science clinical trials: towards a structured framework for risk stratification

## Veronica Tutse-Tonwe1, Nicole Nyack1, Michelle Culp2, Debbie Vitalis1

## 1Center for Translation Research and Implementation Science; National Heart, Lung, and Blood Institute, National Institutes of Health, Bethesda, MD, USA; 2National Institutes of Health, Bethesda, MD, USA

## Correspondence: Veronica Tutse-Tonwe (veronica.tonwe@nih.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S129

Background: The goal of clinical trial monitoring is to ensure appropriate oversight of participant safety and data integrity. The 1998 NIH Policy for Data and Safety Monitoring recognizes that, “all trials, even those that pose little likelihood of harm, should consider an external monitoring body”. Monitoring should align with the trial’s nature, size, and complexity. Implementation science (IS) clinical trials present unique challenges due to complexity of design, recruitment, and outcomes, yet limited guidance exists for tailoring oversight and risk assessment to IS. A systematic, tailored approach to IS trial oversight is needed.

Methods: We conducted a portfolio analysis of the NHLBI, Center for Translation Research and Implementation Science (CTRIS) funded IS clinical trials from 2013–2024 to (1) characterize grants, (2) identify proposed oversight processes (related to risk assessment and monitoring strategies), and factors that may impact risks; and (3) develop a structured IS clinical trial risk stratification framework. We analyzed a subset of 62 grants for our in-depth portfolio analysis on clinical trial oversight. Using a structured coding framework, coders extracted data on eight variables from the NIH grants database.

Findings: CTRIS-funded IS clinical trials revealed substantial variability across study designs, consent practices, oversight mechanisms and risk reporting. Most studies employed hybrid designs (52%) with cluster and stepped-wedge approaches (63%), underscoring implementation study design complexities that are frequently not considered when assessing risks. Oversight processes included an independent monitor, DSMB, or simply the IRB, but details of their execution were inconsistently documented. Most studies lacked clarity around adverse event reporting and potential risks descriptions, identifying participant study risk as “minimal”, primarily citing confidentiality. Consent approaches varied; most used a multi-level recruitment consenting process, written consent at the patient-level, and waiver-based consent for provider/system-level trials.

Implications for D&I Research: Traditional models of clinical trial risk assessment do not sufficiently account for the ethical, logistical, and operational dimensions unique to IS trials, yet these findings highlight critical gaps in oversight management and risk stratification. A structured framework that integrates trial complexity, recruitment levels and size, nature and real-world context is critically needed.

## S130 Social network alteration as implementation strategies: a secondary analysis of empirical studies

## LauraEllen Ashcraft1, Christina Yuan2, Katherine Pickard3, Alicia Bunger4, Sobia Khan5, Reza Yousefi Nooraie6

## 1Veterans Health Administration, Philadelphia, PA, USA; 2Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health, Baltimore, MD, USA; 3Emory University School of Medicine, Atlanta, GA, USA; 4The Ohio State University, Columbus, OH, USA; 5The Center for Implementation, Toronto, ON, Canada; 6University of Rochester, Rochester, NY, USA

## Correspondence: LauraEllen Ashcraft (lauraellen.ashcraft@pennmedicine.upenn.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S130

Background

There is a growing recognition that relationships are foundational to implementation. Yet, the mechanisms and packaging for integrating social networks as implementation strategies remain unclear. This study examines how experimentally tested implementation strategies incorporate network alteration activities (i.e., intentionally changing the structures and functions of social networks) and identifies underexplored opportunities for advancing network interventions in implementation research.

Methods

We included 129 experimental implementation studies identified in a recently published systematic review (Ashcraft et al., 2024). Preliminarily, we included 66 of the 129 studies based on the highest frequency of ERIC strategies pertaining to network alteration. Reviewers assessed implementation strategy descriptions for network alteration strategies corresponding to Bunger et al.’s (2023) typology (creating groups, changing actors’ networking skills, changing actors’ awareness of social networks, changing actors’ motivation for networking, changing actors’ prominence, changing network composition, changing environments to facilitate networking, targeting specific ties). We met regularly to ensure consistency and consensus. We captured the frequency of each network alteration type and their combinations, and packaging with other implementation strategies.

Findings

Network alteration activities are frequently embedded within multi-component strategies but are rarely tracked as primary strategy components, inconsistently identified, and rarely assessed. The most common network alteration strategy was creating groups (n = 35; e.g., group training and formation of implementation teams), followed by changing network composition (n = 25; e.g., an external facilitator joining local teams), and changing actors’ prominence (n = 21; e.g., the identification and promotion of local champions and internal leads). The least common strategies were changing actors’ motivation and awareness, and changing the environment. The forthcoming analysis will analyze the packaging of network strategies and their association with RE-AIM outcomes, extracted from the original systematic review.

Implications for D&I Research

This analysis generated a crosswalk linking network alteration activities to implementation strategies and identified gaps for future methodological work. By clarifying how social network alterations are operationalized within implementation activities, we aim to expand the "frontiers" for designing and evaluating socially and contextually responsive implementation strategies by better incorporating social structures and dynamics in the intervention design and implementation planning.

## S131 The Dissemination Research Logic Model (DRLM) for planning and executing dissemination projects: application to the Adolescent Medicine Trials Network for HIV Interventions

## Katherine Merrill1, Kristi Gamarel2, Lisa Hightow-Weidman3, Sybil Hosek4

## 1University of Illinois Chicago, Chicago, IL, USA; 2University of Michigan School of Public Health, Ann Arbor, MI, USA; 3Florida State University, Tallahassee, USA; 4University of Illinois Chicago, Chicago, USA

## Correspondence: Katherine Merrill (kgm@uic.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S131

Background: The implementation research logic model (IRLM) is a widely used tool for enhancing the clarity and rigor of implementation projects, but no such tool exists in dissemination science. We set out to create a dissemination research logic model (DRLM) and apply it to the 25-year-old Adolescent Medicine Trials Network for HIV Interventions (ATN).

Methods: We began by articulating the key components of the dissemination process: the “thing” (i.e., program/practice/guideline) being disseminated and its source, goal and audience, determinants, strategies, channels, mechanisms of change, and desired outcomes. We retrospectively applied the model to several completed ATN projects, generating variations of the model. Here, we illustrate its application to the ATN 113 Protocol (Project PrEPare), the first major adolescent-specific pre-exposure prophylaxis (PrEP-Truvada) demonstration project in the U.S for adolescent men who have sex with men ages 15–17.

Findings: The “thing” disseminated was a guideline based on findings from the ATN 113 (the source)—i.e., that PrEP is safe, acceptable, and feasible, and effective in preventing HIV for adolescents with high medication adherence. The ATN sought to make PrEP available for adolescents through policy change, targeting policymakers. To overcome dissemination strategies, the network used a set of strategies to disseminate through various channels (e.g., conference presentations, peer-reviewed publications). These led to improved knowledge and awareness among policymakers (i.e., mechanisms), which led to three dissemination outcomes: 1) a change in policy through the FDA’s expansion of Truvada PrEP use for adolescents; 2) updates to CDC guidelines to recommend PrEP for adolescents; and 3) updates to insurance coverage policies to cover PrEP for adolescents. Dissemination outcomes precipitated increased PrEP prescription for adolescents (i.e., implementation outcome) and subsequently reduced HIV incidence among adolescents (i.e., individual outcome).

Implications for D&I Research: The DRLM’s application to ATN 113 clarifies the pathways through which the guideline disseminated led to changes in dissemination outcomes (i.e., policy changes), changes in implementation outcomes, and ultimately improved health outcomes. Our presentation of this example and others from the ATN will inform future researchers’ dissemination projects. The DRLM will help realize the benefits of dissemination science, which has to date received notably less attention than the field of implementation science.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S132 Demystifying theories, models and frameworks: when, why, and how to integrate more than one

## Christine Kowalski

## Veterans Health Administration, Ann Arbor, MI, USA

## Correspondence: Christine Kowalski (christine.kowalski@va.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S132

Background: Theories, Models and Frameworks (TMF) are tools that, together, represent a common language, allow cross comparisons, and ensure we consider relevant variables that help explain implementation processes, successes or failures. However, selecting one or more TMF(s) remains challenging due to an overwhelming number of options and lack of salient points for selection. This presentation will describe the rationale for selecting two or more TMFs and some beneficial tools to support selection.

Methods: The 5 overarching TMF categories will be explained along with why to use a TMF, including to describe and/or guide the process of translating research into practice, to understand and/or explain what influences implementation outcomes –i.e., explain why an intervention works or doesn’t work, to evaluate implementation, to improve generalizability, to identify barriers or understand context, and to select/identify implementation strategies. Multiple resources will be shared that will help participants select a TMF that is a good fit for a research or quality improvement study. How and when to select more than one TMF will be explored, including how to carefully use multiple TMFs temporarily throughout your QI project or study including for planning, implementation, and evaluation—and how to use TMFs to understand what happened and why.

Findings: When used together TMFs can enhance effectiveness by uncovering relationships between constructs and mechanisms that influence implementation success. For example, multiple TMFs can allow system-level process factors and individual-level process factors. When using two TMFs it is important to check for redundancies and eliminate. Combinations can also be used to make the leap from a determinants list only, to elucidating impacts on outcomes.

Implications for D&I Research: Resources for where to turn for help will be shared. Attendees will gain an understanding of how to pick a TMF that is a good fit for an operational project or research question and gain a fundamental understanding of when and why you might need to use more than one TMF in conjunction. By ensuring that more practitioners and researchers understand thoughtful integration of more than one TMF we can maximize the benefits of dissemination and implementation science.

Primary Funding Source: Department of Veterans Affairs

## S133 Evaluating legal services for housing-unstable veterans: a combined use of RE-AIM and i-PARIHS

## Bo Kim1,2, Sonia Rupcic3, Samantha Ryan1, Angie Moussa4, Sophia Bartels4, Ida Griesemer5, Amanda Midboe6,7,8, Jack Tsai9,10, Beth Ann Petrakis4

## 1Veterans Health Administration, Boston, MA, USA; 2Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, USA; 3University of Pittsburgh, Pittsburgh, PA, USA; 4Veterans Health Administration, Bedford, MA, USA; 5Veterans Health Administration, White River Junction, VT, USA; 6Veterans Health Administration, Menlo Park, CA, USA; 7University of California Davis School of Medicine, Davis, CA, USA; 8Stanford University School of Medicine, Stanford, CA, USA; 9Veterans Health Administration, San Antonio, TX, USA; 10University of Texas Health Science Center at Houston, Houston, TX, USA

## Correspondence: Bo Kim (bo.kim@va.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S133

Background: Legal issues are major but addressable social determinants of health. The Department of Veterans Affairs’ Legal Services for Homeless Veterans and Veterans At-Risk for Homelessness (LSV-H) grant program funds legal aid to address barriers to housing and healthcare. To identify factors influencing LSV-H implementation from the perspectives of veterans receiving and organizations delivering LSV-H-funded services, we used the RE-AIM (Reach, Effectiveness, Adoption, Implementation, Maintenance) framework to guide participant selection for interviews (distinct from RE-AIM’s common use for summative evaluation), which we subsequently analyzed using the i-PARIHS (Integrated Promoting Action on Research Implementation in Health Services) framework and RE-AIM’s Effectiveness domain.

Methods: We conducted semi-structured interviews with 21 veterans, as well as with 18 administrators and 15 legal service providers across 15 LSV-H grantee organizations purposively selected to vary in geography, organization type, and RE-AIM-aligned characteristics – number of veterans served [Reach], representativeness of target communities (e.g., rural) [Adoption], delivery of intended services [Implementation], and continued pursuit of LSV-H funding [Maintenance]. Rapid qualitative analysis extracted themes aligned with i-PARIHS constructs (Innovation, Recipients, Context, Facilitation) and RE-AIM’s Effectiveness domain.

Findings: Participants viewed LSV-H as making legal services more accessible to underserved veterans, including those previously ineligible due to income thresholds. LSV-H was perceived to prevent eviction, secure housing, reinstate benefits, and reduce anxiety and stress [Effectiveness]. LSV-H funds increased grantees’ service delivery capacity, while some grantees with less familiarity with LSV-H’s federal reporting requirements found them challenging [Innovation]. Veterans’ engagement and reported improvements reflected LSV-H’s high acceptability and alignment with their goals [Recipients]. Grantees having co-located services with VA settings fostered veteran trust [Context]. Grantees’ expertise with legal needs of veterans and subpopulations (e.g., tribal) helped tailor service delivery [Facilitation].

Implications for D&I Research

Combining RE-AIM and i-PARIHS enabled comprehensive understanding of LSV-H implementation dynamics. RE-AIM-guided participant selection helped identify implementation-relevant grantee characteristics, while i-PARIHS’ emphasis on evolving, interrelated constructs and RE-AIM-aligned perceived effectiveness illuminated how contextual factors shaped implementation. Findings suggest that pairing these frameworks can enhance explanatory power and provide actionable insights. This evaluation adds to the growing literature on legal services’ contributions to health, and how combining complementary frameworks can inform implementation of complex programs.

Primary Funding Source: Department of Veterans Affairs

## S134 Integrating RE-AIM and CFIR to evaluate the implementation of a national intimate partner violence screening expansion

## Galina Portnoy1,2, Mark Relyea1,2, Melissa Dichter3,4, Candice Presseau1,2, Katherine Iverson5, Joshua Price-Crist1, Melissa Skanderson1,2, Cynthia Brandt1,2, Steve Martino1,2

## 1Veterans Health Administration, West Haven, CT, USA; 2Yale School of Medicine, New Haven, CT, USA; 3Veterans Health Administration, Philadelphia, PA, USA; 4Temple University School of Social Work, Philadelphia, PA, USA; 5Veterans Health Administration, Boston, MA, USA

## Correspondence: Galina Portnoy (galina.portnoy@va.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S134

Background: The healthcare system plays an integral role in the detection and response of intimate partner violence (IPV), a significant public health problem with far-reaching consequences. The majority of IPV screening initiatives have historically targeted women of reproductive age, with little known about IPV screening experiences among other patient populations, including men. The Veterans Health Administration (VHA) has called for an expansion of IPV screening, providing a unique opportunity for a large-scale evaluation of IPV screening and response across patient populations. This presentation describes mixed-methods findings from a highly-partnered evaluation with VHA IPV operations partners, guided by an integration of the RE-AIM and CFIR frameworks.

Methods: Using medical record data from 6,970,533 patients (89.8% male) across 135 VHA facilities, we conducted analyses across RE-AIM components to examine the reach of IPV screening, including differences and disparities between those who were and were not screened across patient characteristics (e.g., gender, age, race, ethnicity, sexual orientation, marital status); effectiveness of screening; adoption across VHA facilities, provider types, and clinical settings; and implementation fidelity. Using data from CFIR-grounded qualitative interviews with providers (n = 32) and patients (n = 24), we identified multilevel barriers and facilitators of the screening expansion.

Findings: Over 1.5 million patients are being screened annually for IPV across VHA, yet some patient subgroups are more likely to be missed than others, such as men and Asian American patients (reach). Rates of screening adoption varies significantly by facility and clinical setting, as does screening implementation, particularly related to fidelity of screening per protocol among men. CFIR components across domains enabled us to categorize multilevel factors critical to screening expansion, potential barriers and enablers of implementation, and impact/effectiveness of screening on patient outcomes.

Implications for D&I Research: Integrating RE-AIM and CFIR allowed for an in depth and rich evaluation of VHA’s IPV screening and response expansion. Findings yield clinical, policy, and research implication including recommendations for screening implementation initiatives and/or adaptations, optimization of strategies for screening implementation across patient populations, and underscoring the value of integrating D&I frameworks for conducting rigorous evaluations.

Primary Funding Source: Department of Veterans Affairs

## S135 Benefits of integrating a novel resilience framework with CFIR to better understand adaptations during stressful times

## Abigail Baim-Lance1,2,3, Diane Addison2,4, Norman Archer5, Peter Gordon6, Sharen Duke7, Virginia Shubert8, Denis Nash2, McKaylee Robertson9

## 1Veterans Health Administration, New York, NY, USA; 2City University of New York, New York, NY, USA; 3Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai, New York, NY, USA; 4Graduate School of Public Health and Health Policy, City University of New York, New York, NY, USA; 5University of California San Francisco, San Francisco, USA; 6Division of Infectious Disease, Department of Internal Medicine, Columbia University Irving Medical Center, New York, NY; and, New York, USA; 7Veterans Health Administration, New York, USA; 8Housing Works, New York, NY, New York, USA; 9Institute for Implementation Science in Population Health, City University of New York, New York, NY;, New York, USA

## Correspondence: Abigail Baim-Lance (abigail.baim-lance@va.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S135

Background: Between 2019–2022, the Bottom Up Pilot Project leveraged a health information exchange and the ability to send real-time alerts to mobilize community-based outreach teams to quickly find and re-engage HIV positive patients back into healthcare. A collaboration of clinical, community, and academic partners located in New York City, Bottom Up can be characterized as a complex intervention with numerous tasks and coordination steps. Bottom Up faced a range of implementation challenges including the COVID-19 pandemic, and yet was successfully operationalized during the 3 year period. We explored using a novel implementation science framework incorporating organizational resilience to describe the processes by which partners overcame challenges experienced during implementation.

Methods: We conducted semi-structured interviews with implementation staff (n = 6) and meeting minutes and protocols document reviews (N = 35). The Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research (CFIR) combined with a novel organizational resilience framework guided thematic and process analyses. CFIR included construct assessment within all 5 of the domains. The resilience framework consisted of two attributes associated with challenges (acute, chronic) and three resilience types: absorptive to cope with adversity, adaptive to adjust as short-term solutions, and transformative to pro-actively adopt system-wide changes.

Findings: Bottom Up experienced 20 major challenges, 2–5 challenges per step. Challenges were multi-level, and chronic and crisis in nature. Implementers overcame challenges by leveraging multilevel factors that were absorptive, adaptive (most common), and transformative. Adaptive strategies were characterized by the project’s ability to maintain consistency to core operations while also employing flexibility by strategically simplifying or "downshifting" activities to fit their capacities in the face of stresses. The project leveraged the CFIR-defined constructs of leadership and flexibility to respond to arising challenges. Transformational responses to upgrade technology functionality suggests that initiatives can catalyze broader organizational change.

Implications for D&I Research

Combining a process-oriented framework like organizational resilience with CFIR can enhance understanding the active and dynamic processes promoting and impeding the growth and success of implementation. This analysis is particularly useful to understand implementation during volatile periods and with complex interventions.

Primary Funding Source: Funding for the Evaluation of the Bottom Up pilot is supported by the Einstein-Rockefeller-CUNY Center for AIDS Research (P30AI124414) and the New York City Council

## S136 Designing flexible tools for budget impact analysis to support adoption of innovations

## Douglas Levy1, Natalie Smith2

## 1Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, USA; 2University of Pittsburgh, Pittsburgh, PA, USA

## Correspondence: Douglas Levy (dlevy3@mgh.harvard.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S136

Background

Intervention and implementation costs are recognized in most major implementation science frameworks as key determinants of innovation adoption and success. Cost per quality-adjusted life year (QALY) saved is a common metric used in economic evaluations, but it fails implementers in the U.S. for at least two reasons. First, by taking a societal perspective, it encompasses broad costs and benefits that may not be relevant to individual institutions making adoption decisions. Second, the cost per QALY metric informs what innovations should be adopted, but not which innovations are affordable and thus feasible to implementers. Budget impact analysis (BIA) is a method for estimating context-specific implementation costs, aiding decision-makers in determining the affordability of evidence-based programs. Still, BIAs remain scarce in implementation science. As a case study, we describe the development of a pilot BIA tool tailored to the Smokefree Support Study (SSS), an evidence-based smoking cessation program for cancer patients, as well as potential future refinements and adaptations. The tool was designed to ensure accessibility, flexibility, and contextual adaptability through the incorporation of user-centered design principles.

Methods

Using publicly accessible R Shiny software, the BIA tool was constructed based on data from the original SSS cost-effectiveness study. The prototype allowed users to input context-specific variables such as hourly wages, enrollment estimates, and staffing models. We conducted usability testing of the tool through interviews with participants from nine sites within the National Cancer Institute’s Cancer Center Cessation Initiative.

Findings

Participant feedback provided information on potential refinements to enhance usability, functionality, and alignment with program adoption needs. The finalized tool provided dynamic cost estimates for training, staffing, recruitment, and material expenses, allowing users to customize inputs for their specific contexts. Usability testing revealed the need for a tool designed with flexibility to reflect local adaptation. Feedback highlighted the necessity of integrating contextualized cost estimates with other decision criteria to support adoption.

Implications for D&I Research

This study illustrates the value of BIA and the importance of user-centered design in creating tools that meaningfully facilitate evidence-based program adoption. Findings emphasize the need for adaptability in tool design/inputs and underscore the importance of combining cost estimates with broader decision-making frameworks.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S137 Applying Program Budgeting and Marginal Analysis (PBMA) for substance use prevention: a pragmatic approach to inform resource allocation decisions

## Andria Eisman

## Wayne State University College of Education, Detroit, MI, USA

## Correspondence: Andria Eisman (aeisman@wayne.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S137

Background: Vaping among youth poses a public health challenge, yet schools face resource constraints in prevention. Traditional cost-effectiveness analysis may not be well-suited due to difficulties in quantifying diverse benefits and limitations in youth-focused outcomes. We introduce Program Budgeting and Marginal Analysis (PBMA) as a pragmatic alternative, focusing on resource allocation trade-offs. PBMA is valuable because the entity investing in prevention may not directly realize the long-term cost savings.

Methods: We employed a PBMA framework to assess the economic implications of vaping prevention interventions. PBMA is well-suited for implementation science, as it facilitates economically grounded decision-making by quantifying the marginal benefits of alternative implementation strategies. Our application involved: 1) defining scope, 2) identifying data sources, 3) estimating marginal costs of strategy options, 4) identifying cost offsets, 5) conducting marginal analysis, and 6) sharing findings.

Findings: Our analysis included multi-level partner perspectives, using pilot study data as well as school and district administrative data and results from other published research. We found the total cost of $24,860 for the 4-school RAPD implementation, or $6,215 per school. The coordinating body bore $9,656 of the cost, followed by the RESA, who bore 32.7% of the cost. We estimated that the community partners' time was $4,305, and the schools bore 11.2% of the cost ($2,776). We determined that the cost of vape detector initiation and installation to be $6850 per set of male/female bathrooms, plus time for administration and school staff to be trained on response methods and procedures for responding to alerts. Potential cost offsets for RAPD include reduced disciplinary incidents and need for reactive interventions, increased collaboration; for vape detectors, offsets may include deterring students from vaping, reducing immediate risk behaviors. We also consider potential longer term cost offsets.

Implications for D&I Research

PBMA offers a pragmatic framework for evaluating implementation strategies in school-based vaping prevention. By emphasizing value-for-money and opportunity cost considerations, and incorporate partner central partner considerations and competing demands in complex environments, PBMA facilitates transparent allocation decisions, highlighting relative costs, benefits, feasibility, and long-term system impact. PBMA helps align prevention investments with priorities of schools and communities.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S138 Decision Analysis for Intervention Value Efficiency (DAIVE): a tool for cost-informed decision-making in intervention optimization

## Jillian Strayhorn1, Jennifer Cantrell2, Linda Collins3, David J. Vanness4, Michael Fiore5, Timothy Baker6, Megan Piper5

## 1New York University, New York, USA; 2New York University School of Global Public Health, New York, USA; 3New York University School of Global Public Health, New York, NY, USA; 4Pennsylvania State University, University Park, PA, USA; 5University of Wisconsin, Madison, WI, USA; 6School of Medicine and Public Health, University of Wisconsin, Center for Tobacco Research and Intervention, Madison, WI, USA

## Correspondence: Jillian Strayhorn (jillian.strayhorn@nyu.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S138

Background

Intervention optimization is a research paradigm in which alternative versions of a multicomponent intervention are rigorously compared in an optimization phase of research that precedes subsequent evaluation. This optimization process is “implementation-forward” in that its motivating goal is to empirically inform the construction of an intervention that is not only effective but also readily implementable. To aid determinations as to which intervention version (e.g., comprising which type or dose of intervention content) is optimal, given finite resources, we have advanced Decision Analysis for Intervention Value Efficiency (DAIVE), a decision analytic framework for selecting optimized interventions based on data from factorial optimization trials. In this presentation, we illustrate DAIVE using the empirical example of a 26–1 fractional factorial trial to optimize a tobacco cessation intervention. We consider how DAIVE fits into a larger toolkit of economic evaluation approaches, including traditional CEA.

Methods: Using data from a 26–1 fractional factorial optimization trial, we estimate the individual and combined effects of six candidate intervention components, each with two levels: 1) preparation nicotine patch vs. none; 2) preparation nicotine gum vs. none; 3) preparation counseling vs. none; 4) intensive cessation in-person counseling vs. minimal; 5) intensive cessation phone counseling vs. minimal; and 6) 16 vs. 8 weeks of combination nicotine replacement therapy (nicotine patch + nicotine gum). We apply DAIVE to summarize the performance of the 64 resulting intervention versions, each comprising some combination of candidate component levels, and identify the intervention that accomplishes the best strategic balance of effectiveness, in terms of cessation, and cost-to-implement, in dollars.

Findings: In this factorial optimization trial, results indicate that not all components have to be set to their higher, more resource-intensive levels to achieve the best expected effectiveness. DAIVE facilitates the identification of which intervention versions remain effective while conserving implementation resources, as well as the selection among those, given opportunity costs.

Implications for D&I Research: Optimizing for value efficiency using DAIVE can support the construction of effective interventions that do not include needless intervention components (or needlessly intense component doses), given opportunity costs, minimizing waste of implementation resources.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S139 Evaluation of implementation efficiency using stochastic frontier analysis: case example from an adaptive trial to implement medications for opioid use disorder

## Hannah Cheng1, Mark McGovern1, Jay Ford2, Hélène Chokron Garneau1, Todd Wagner3

## 1Stanford University School of Medicine, Palo Alto, CA, USA; 2University of Wisconsin-Madison, Madison, WI, USA; 3Stanford-Surgery Policy Improvement Research and Education Center (S-SPIRE), Stanford, CA, USA

## Correspondence: Hannah Cheng (chenghh@stanford.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S139

Background: Effective and efficient implementation strategies are crucial for scaling evidence-based practices (EBPs). While studies increasingly have begun to document the cost of implementation strategies, few have examined how these costs translate to improvement in implementation outcomes. This study estimates total implementation costs and applies stochastic frontier analysis (SFA) to assess the relative efficiency of implementation strategies in improving access to and quality of medications for opioid use disorder (MOUD).

Methods: Sixty-nine addiction treatment programs and primary care clinics in Washington state participated in an adaptive trial offering sequential strategies: Audit and Feedback (A&F), Workshop, Internal Facilitation, and External Facilitation. Using activity-based microcosting, we estimated delivery and participation costs of implementation strategies prospectively. Total implementation costs were linked to monthly RE-AIM outcomes: Reach (patients prescribed MOUD), Effectiveness (MOUD initiation within 72 h of diagnosis), Adoption (active MOUD prescribers), and Implementation (implementation capability scale total). All cost and outcomes data have been collected; we will apply SFA to estimate cost-efficiency using A&F as a proof of concept by November 2025.

Findings: As part of the SFA model, inputs will include the delivery and participation costs of A&F, which incurred a setup cost of $32,266 and an average annual recurring cost of $4,231 per clinic. Outputs will include Reach (mean = 41.66%, range: 0–100%), Effectiveness (mean = 36.32%, range: 0–100%), Adoption (mean = 4, range: 0–16), and Implementation (mean = 3.57, range: 2.21–5.00). We will present the average cost-efficiency score across clinics and compare how the most efficient clinics differ in RE-AI outcomes per dollar spent compared to the average clinics.

Implications for D&I Research: Applying SFA helps identify clinics that achieve the greatest improvement in implementation outcomes per dollar spent, which supports resource allocation decisions based on efficiency, rather than cost or effectiveness alone. This efficiency-driven approach can maximize sustainment by investing in efficient systems that do more with less while conserving resources that can be redirected to those who need it most.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S140 From context to strategy: Developing the Context Compass Framework and Tool to operationalize context and guide implementation strategy selection

## Sobia Khan, Julia Moore

## The Center for Implementation, Toronto, ON, Canada

## Correspondence: Julia Moore (julia.moore@thecenterforimplementation.com)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S140

Background

Context is widely recognized as critical to implementation success, yet commonly used frameworks like the Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research (CFIR) have been critiqued due to its limited focus on system-level factors and challenges with applicability in other settings, like in low- and middle-income countries. In addition, while attempts have been made to align ERIC strategies with CFIR constructs (CFIR-ERIC matching tool), selecting strategies to address context remains challenging. To address these issues, we developed the Context Compass Framework and tool—designed to consolidate key contextual domains across existing contextual frameworks, center usability, and support selection of implementation strategies that address contextual determinants.

Methods

Drawing on a scoping review (Nilsen & Bernhardsson, 2019) of contextual frameworks, we identified factors from 13 established context frameworks commonly used in implementation science (e.g., CFIR, EPIS). We identified and grouped recurring contextual domains into a framework using an inductive coding approach and collaborative review. Key domains were refined through iterative consultation with implementation researchers and practitioners, ensuring theoretical alignment and usability. We developed an interactive tool to identify implementation strategies to address contextual barriers or facilitators. Strategy mapping was grounded in established taxonomies (e.g., ERIC, Behaviour Change Techniques) and experiential evidence.

Findings

The Context Compass Framework consists of a simplified structure comprising 15 factors and 39 sub-factors that can be assessed at the site or system level. By describing factors in ways that can address multiple levels of the system, we were able to reduce the total number of factors. Factors were also grouped based on relevance across the implementation stages (fit, readiness, implementation, sustainability).
Contextual factors and sub-factors were linked to implementation strategies through the underlying functions. The interactive online tool allows users to identify high-priority contextual challenges and explore implementation strategies that can address those challenges. Early user feedback indicates the framework improves clarity, accessibility, and actionability of context assessments.

Implications for D&I Research

This work offers a more practical and structured way to bridge context assessment with implementation strategy selection. The Context Compass Framework can support research design and implementation, offering a foundation for future empirical validation studies. The next steps include conducting usability testing with the tool.

## S141 From a blank slate to contextual fit: iterative macroergonomic observations guiding pharmacy intervention design

## Emily Hoffins1, Katherine G. Moore2, Maria E. Berbakov1, Jay Ford II1, Aaron M. Gilson1, Jamie A. Stone2, Stephanie M. Resendiz1, Shiying Mai1, Rutuja Gadgil1, Michelle A. Chui2

## 1University of Wisconsin—Madison School of Pharmacy, Madison, WI, USA; 2University of Wisconsin—Madison School of Nursing, Madison, WI, USA

## Correspondence: Michelle A. Chui (michelle.chui@wisc.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S141

Background

Expanding services within community pharmacies is a national public health priority, yet most interventions are developed based on assumptions about how workflows function rather than a true understanding of daily practice. Researchers often believe they know what changes are needed to improve systems, but, without direct, open-ended observation, these assumptions can miss critical realities. Community pharmacy workflows are dynamic with frequent interruptions and competing demands for staff time. As pharmacists offer more clinical services, these services must be integrated into the workflow without compromising medication safety or overwhelming staff. Adopting a “blank slate” approach through field observations allows for an unbiased, system-level depiction of pharmacy work, ensuring that future interventions fit naturally rather than being retrofitted within existing workflows.

Methods

The Macroergonomic Analysis and Design (MEAD) 10-Step Process, a systematic iterative method for analyzing and optimizing complex work systems, was adapted to a three-phase, observation protocol in six community pharmacies. Observation 1 captured a systems-level view of the entire pharmacy environment, including staff roles, tools and technology, and environmental conditions. Observation 2 detailed the prescription dispensing process by documenting task sequences, interruptions, and role delegation. Observation 3 examined non-dispensing services (such as medication management) to understand how these activities are performed concurrently with dispensing work. Between observation cycles, analytical pauses were used to refine data collection and identify disruptions in the workflow.

Findings

The iterative observation process produced a detailed depiction of the current pharmacy workflow and identified bottlenecks to inform future intervention design. Across all sites, prescription dispensing was a primary, constant workflow. Non-dispensing services were routinely layered onto the dispensing workflow without formal integration or task delegation, which created role ambiguity, documentation challenges, and cognitive burden for pharmacy staff. By systematically observing the sequence of tasks and interruptions, the observation cycles captured how staff navigated competing responsibilities in real time.

Implications for D&I Research

This study demonstrates how taking a “blank slate approach” and conducting iterative observations can guide the design of sustainable interventions. Generating a comprehensive understanding of the work system without preconceived notions promotes contextual fit, reduces cognitive burden, and improves the likelihood of adoption and long-term sustainment in healthcare settings.

Primary Funding Source: Agency for Healthcare Research and Quality

## S142 Human-centered redesign of evidence-based interventions and implementation strategies to enhance usability: cross-project outcomes from the UW ALACRITY center

## Aaron R. Lyon1, Sean A. Munson2, Kathryn Bruzios2, Katie P. Osterhage2, Michael D. Pullmann2, Ryan Allred2, Brittany Mosser3, Alejandra Lopez2, John Fortney4, Brittany Blanchard2, Tricia Aung2, Doyanne Darnell2, Patricia Areán3, UW Alacrity Researchers3

## 1Psychiatry and Behavioral Sciences, University of Washington, Seattle, WA, USA; 2University of Washington, Seattle, WA, USA; 3University of Washington, Seattle, USA; 4Veterans Health Administration, Seattle, WA, USA

## Correspondence: Aaron R. Lyon (lyona@uw.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S142

Background: Implementation strategies and patient-facing interventions both tend to be complex psychosocial processes. When these innovations are not readily usable or aligned with their destination contexts, the potential for communities and the field to benefit from advances in both intervention and implementation science is significantly reduced. The NIMH-funded University of Washington ALACRITY Center (UWAC) applies human-centered design (HCD) methods to adapt implementation strategies and mental health interventions to ensure their usability in community settings. UWAC leverages the three-phase Discover, Design/Build, Test (DDBT) process to drive iterative redesign based on information about innovation usability and contextual constraints. Using data farate UWAC projects this presentation will describe the types of redesign solutions made to implementation strategies and interventions to improve their usability and adoption potential.

Methods: All 11 projects employed user testing and evaluation methods (e.g., usability testing, cognitive walkthroughs, user interviews) to identify usability issues, which served as modification targets for implementation strategy and intervention redesign. Redesign solutions were articulated by project teams using the Framework for Reporting Adaptations and Modifications – Expanded (FRAME) for evidence-based interventions and its extension for implementation strategies (FRAME-IS).

Findings: Of the projects, eight sought redesign solutions for a patient-facing intervention, two sought redesign solutions for an implementation strategy, and two projects sought redesign solutions for both an intervention and an implementation strategy. Projects reported usability issues across 12 categories which were linked to 15 types of content modifications. Redesign most often included the FRAME category of tailoring or refining intervention/strategy content. Of the 11 projects, four identified usability issues that were not able to be addressed in a redesign. These issues were highly variable but some of the most common issues left unaddressed were categorized as inadequate scaffolding for intervention/strategy recipients or that the intervention/strategy did not build adequate buy-in by demonstrating its value to its intended users.

Implications for D&I Research: Integration of data across 11 UWAC projects allowed for identification of common redesign solutions. These findings can inform future intervention and implementation strategy development and redesign to advance the science of adaptation.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S143 From partnership to practice: community-informed strategies for sustainable preconception counseling implementation

## Angie Suarez1, Calvin Foster2, Oluyemi Farinu1, Adenike McDonald1, Madison Haiman3, Morehouse Medicine1, Tiyamika Williams1, Jaydon Taylor1, Amy Huebschmann4, Meredith Fort5, Danette McLaurin-Glass6, Rasheeta Chandler7, Rasheeta Chandler7, Cheryl Franklin1, Latrice Rollins1, Natalie Hernandez-Green1

## 1Morehouse School of Medicine, Atlanta, GA, USA; 2Project IMPACT Implementer, Greensboro, NC, USA; 3University of Texas at Austin, Austin, TX, USA; 4University of Colorado School of Medicine, General Internal Medicine, General Internal Medicine, Aurora, CO, USA; 5Colorado School of Public Health, Aurora, CO, USA; 6First Team America, Charlotte, NC, USA; 7Emory University, Atlanta, GA, USA

## Correspondence: Oluyemi Farinu (ofarinu@msm.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S143

Background: The underutilization of preconception counseling (PC) represents a missed opportunity to reduce high rates of maternal morbidity and mortality in the Southeastern U.S. Recent policy shifts have reduced funding for family planning and PC resources, disproportionately affecting communities. In this evolving political landscape, adapting evidence-based health interventions and developing implementation strategies through a community-engaged lens is essential. The objective of Project IMPACT (IMproving Preconception Actions and Choices for Tomorrow) is to utilize the Practical Robust Implementation and Sustainability Model (PRISM) and the Implementation Research Logic Model (IRLM) tool alongside partner community-based organizations (CBO) to guide the collaborative development of an implementation strategy package for PC delivery.

Methods: Project IMPACT is a joint effort between academic and partner CBOs testing the delivery of a PC intervention within community-based settings as a type II hybrid effectiveness trial. The study team developed a preliminary implementation strategy package in an IRLM developed from a formative mixed methods assessment of PRISM determinants that also identified Screening, Brief Intervention and Referral to Treatment (SBIRT) as the community’s preferred PC intervention to deliver. We developed a regional accountability board (RAB) of community stakeholders that met quarterly to further refine the implementation strategies.

Findings: The final IRLM co-created with the RAB and site implementer input included 3 overarching themes for implementation strategies: 1) to ensure the SBIRT intervention was reflective of the PRISM community perspectives and needs/characteristics (Engage participants), 2) include a strong standard of operations (SOP) and other implementation materials (Technical assistance), and 3) implementer capacity-building. Through collaborative engagement with partner CBOs on implementation strategies, the Project IMPACT SOP and other materials were further iteratively refined using insights gathered from annual strategy sessions, RAB meetings, and researcher-implementer meetings.

Implications for D&I Research: Our findings reflect the innovative use of both PRISM and the IRLM to guide our community-based approach to allow for novel and feasible strategies for delivering PC through Project IMPACT. This approach has yielded culturally relevant, sustainable solutions that we expect will lead to translational benefits to Southeastern communities with a high burden of maternal mortality: improvements in PC access, maternal health outcomes and strengthened community resilience.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S144 Utilizing Artificial Intelligence (AI) rapid qualitative methods for implementation of Veterans Health Administration National AI Institute initiatives

## Cassie Cunningham Goedken1,2, David Hook2, Donna Hill2, Jessica Holman2,3, Daniel Morgan4,5, Katlyn Brinkley2, Evan Carey2,6, Westyn Branch-Elliman2,7

## 1Veterans Health Administration, Iowa City, IA, USA; 2Veterans Health Administration, Washington DC, USA; 3Veterans Health Administration, Seattle, WA, USA; 4Veterans Health Administration, Baltimore, MD, USA; 5University of Maryland, Baltimore, MD, USA; 6Veterans Health Administration, Denver, CO, USA; 7Veterans Health Administration, Los Angeles, CA, USA

## Correspondence: Cassie Cunningham Goedken (cassie.goedken@va.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S144

Background: Qualitative methods gather information to better understand contextual factors and perceptions influencing implementing interventions. While these methods are traditionally resource intensive, rapid qualitative methods have been successfully utilized to efficiently analyze qualitative data. Artificial intelligence (AI) has increasingly been used in health care settings for text summarization. Generative AI, which creates output based on user supplied inputs, can increase the efficiency of qualitative work. The United States Department of Veterans Affairs (VA) developed VA GPT(Beta); a generative AI tool housed behind the VA firewall to use with sensitive data. We used VA GPT(Beta) to assist in rapid qualitative analysis of data for two VA National AI Institute (NAII) initiatives.

Methods: NAII supports operationalizing AI technologies to improve Veteran health outcomes and alleviate workload burden. Rapid qualitative evaluation of initiatives is critical to inform leadership decisions. From April–May 2025, we implemented novel rapid AI-assisted qualitative analysis methods to evaluate two independent AI-focused pilots: Pilot1-VAGPT(Beta), Pilot2-CLABSI AI detection. Evaluation methods included structured interviews (pilot1) and surveys (pilot2). Interviews (n = 12) were recorded and transcribed using MS Teams integrated functionality for pilot1. Surveys (n = 20) were sent post-pilot via MS Forms, comprised of closed and open-ended questions for pilot2. AI-assisted rapid analysis for both pilots involved: 1) cleaning and formatting participant open-ended survey/interview responses into a raw dataset, 2) selecting and refining prompts, 3) inputting interview/MS form questions, tailored prompts, and participant raw data into VA GPT (Beta), 4) human validation of AI-generated output against the raw dataset.

Findings: VA GPT(Beta) was successfully integrated into rapid qualitative analyses, identifying key themes, providing insights and producing effective summaries of open-ended survey responses and qualitative interviews. This novel method delivered timely outputs to enable data-informed decision making.

Implications for D&I Research: AI-assisted qualitative research methods may offer greater efficiency than traditional approaches. We found this method to be successful at organizing, categorizing, and summarizing qualitative responses. However, trained qualitative researchers and content experts are needed to provide context and identify nuances within analyses to ensure AI output accuracy. Future research should explore refining prompts and AI’s use for in-depth thematic qualitative analysis.

Primary Funding Source: Department of Veterans Affairs

## S145 Documenting and troubleshooting challenges to experimental trial designs in implementation science

## Cathleen Willging1, Daniel Shattuck1, Marisa Sklar2, Erika Crable3, Joella Friedman4, Mark Ehrhart5, Manon Ironside4, Alexandra Kandah6, Gregory Aarons4

## 1Pacific Institute for Research and Evaluation, Albuquerque, NM, USA; 2University of California, San Diego, La Jolla, CA, USA; 3University of California, Los Angeles, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 4University of California San Diego, La Jolla, CA, USA; 5ImplementatioN Science and Team Effectiveness in Practice (IN STEP) Children’s Mental Health Research Center, San Diego, USA; 6University of Central Florida, Orlando, FL, USA

## Correspondence: Cathleen Willging (cwillging@pire.org)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S145

Background

Experimental trial designs are favored in implementation science because they provide a rigorous structure for generating credible evidence about both the outcomes of interventions and the processes that influence their adoption, fidelity, and sustainment. This qualitative study explores the perspectives and responses of researchers to multilevel factors affecting the use of a complex stepped-wedge trial that tested the Leadership and Organizational Change for Implementation – System Level (LOCI-SL) strategy to facilitate the adoption of Motivational Interviewing (MI) and a natural language processing artificial intelligence (NLP/AI) fidelity feedback platform to promote positive implementation outcomes in substance use disorder treatment.

Methods

We conducted monthly periodic reflections and weekly ethnographic observations involving ten research team members who participated in the trial and implemented LOCI-SL over a three-year period across 33 clinics within a single state’s behavioral healthcare system in the Western United States. Observational data included detailed field notes, and periodic reflections were audio-recorded and transcribed. We analyzed these qualitative data iteratively using open and focused coding techniques, along with NVivo software, to identify patterns and themes related to implementing and maintaining the integrity of the stepped-wedge design.

Findings

Recurring factors influencing the stepped-wedge trial comprised five main themes: (1) site recruitment challenges; (2) variable buy-in and adoption among implementers for the EBP and the NLP/AI platform; (3) turnover and internal transitions within sites and the research team; (4) inconsistent participation in quantitative and qualitative data collection throughout the multiyear study; and (5) the nuances of maintaining multilevel communication and coordination across sites and system partners. These themes highlight a range of interrelated factors that research teams may need to troubleshoot when initially engaging and then sustaining both site and implementer involvement in a large-scale trial.

Implications for D&I Research

Multisite experimental trials are regarded as the gold standard for evaluating implementation outcomes in real-world settings. However, they require flexibility and dedication from research teams to succeed. This trial’s multilevel focus on testing the LOCI-SL strategy to implement both MI and an NLP/AI technology platform elucidates the struggles that researchers encounter and their potential solutions when applying these demanding implementation trial designs.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S146 Focusing qualitative inquiries through a PRISM lens to sharpen the rigor of implementation research

## Emily Dunston1,2,3, Amy Huebschmann1,3,4,5, Katy Trinkley6, Samantha Harden7, James Pittman8,9, Rebecca Guerin10, Marcia Ory11,12, Emily Treichler8,13, Paul Estabrooks14, Russell Glasgow3,6, Borsika Rabin8,15
