## 1University of Colorado School of Medicine, Department of Medicine, Division of General Internal Medicine, Aurora, CO, USA; 2University of Colorado Cancer Center, Aurora, CO, USA; 3Adult and Child Center for Outcomes Research and Delivery Science (ACCORDS), Aurora, CO, USA; 4Ludeman Family Center for Women’s Health Research, Aurora, CO, USA; 5University of Colorado School of Medicine, General Internal Medicine, General Internal Medicine, Aurora, CO, USA; 6University of Colorado Anschutz Medical Campus, Aurora, CO, USA; 7Virginia Tech, Blacksburg, VA, USA; 8University of California San Diego, San Diego, CA, USA; 9VA Center of Excellence for Stress and Mental Health, San Diego, CA, USA; 10Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, Cincinnati, OH, USA; 11Department of Environmental and Occupational Health, School of Public Health, Texas A&M University, College Station, TX, USA; 12Center for Population Health and Aging, Texas A&M University, College Station, TX, USA; 13VA San Diego, San Diego, USA; 14University of Utah, Salt Lake City, UT, USA; 15University of California San Diego, Altman Clinical and Translational Research Center, San Diego, CA, USA

## Correspondence: Emily Dunston (emily.dunston@cuanschutz.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S146

Background: Qualitative data collection is an important component of dissemination and implementation (D&I) science, yet some view qualitative research as less rigorous than quantitative research. The application of a D&I theory, model, or framework (TMF) can enhance the rigor of qualitative methods for D&I research by providing systematic guidance. The Pragmatic, Robust Implementation and Sustainability Model (PRISM) is well-positioned for use in qualitative D&I research because it facilitates the assessment of multi-level contextual factors and dynamically connects outcomes with context. While some TMFs have qualitative guidance, there is a notable lack of formal guidance on how to apply PRISM for qualitative research.

Methods: We convened a group of D&I scientists (n = 10) with PRISM and qualitative research expertise to develop a set of general considerations for qualitative data collection and analysis. First, the expert group held a round-table discussion on the use of PRISM for qualitative research, informed by recent reviews of PRISM. Further, each expert shared qualitative interview and focus group guides using PRISM to develop a library of qualitative data collection instruments. From this library, we compiled each individual question and its attendant PRISM domains into a database.

Findings: The expert panel roundtable outlined four key ways PRISM can be used to inform qualitative D&I research:1) defining research questions, 2) informing interview and focus group guides, 3) conducting qualitative data analysis, and 4) developing intervention and implementation strategies to address multi-level contextual determinants. Additionally, the panel emphasized provision of guidance for use of PRISM across study phases including exploration, development, planning, implementation, and sustainment. We collected 29 interview and focus group guides that utilized PRISM from 13 distinct research studies, cataloged example interview items by PRISM domain, and developed example open-access interview guides and coding templates to support implementers and researchers.

Implications for D&I Research: Robust guidance for utilizing PRISM in qualitative research will significantly enhance the rigor of contextual inquiry within D&I science. The example interview guides and coding templates developed to date will support a more systematic approach to understanding contextual factors influencing PRISM outcomes such as reach, adoption and maintenance, thereby accelerating the translation of evidence-based practices into real-world settings.

## S147 Developing analytic profiles for multi-site trials: layering multi-method qualitative data to identify contextual factors impacting longitudinal implementation

## Lauren Hoffmann1, Eleni Skaperdas1, Maharshi Rawal1, Talia Panadero1, Erica Fletcher1, Matthew McCoy2, Agatha Palma1, Taylor Harris3, Jenny Barnard1, Sonya Gabrielian1, Kristina Cordasco4, Erin Finley5

## 1Veterans Health Administration, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 2Veterans Health Administration, Los Angeles, USA; 3Veterans Health Administration, North Hills, CA, USA; 4David Geffen School of Medicine, University of California, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 5Long School of Medicine, University of Texas Health Science Center, San Antonio, TX, USA

## Correspondence: Lauren Hoffmann (Lauren.Hoffmann@va.gov)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S147

Background

Although qualitative data is often used to assess contextual factors impacting implementation of evidence-based practices (EBPs), single-method study designs may be inadequate to capture shifting conditions over time, particularly in under-resourced settings with high staff turnover. In a hybrid type 3 implementation effectiveness study of Critical Time Intervention (CTI)—a time-limited, case management EBP—we developed site profiles to layer multi-method data across multiple time points to understand how shifting contextual factors at 32 community-based homeless service agencies affected CTI implementation.

Methods

Over three years, we conducted 161 interviews with case managers and supervisors across 32 agencies, and VA social workers (“liaisons”) who coordinate activities between the agencies and their local VA facilities. Interviews were conducted at baseline (pre-implementation),12 months, and 18 months after CTI implementation. Additionally, we gathered periodic reflections (brief, ethnographic snapshots of implementation activities) from VA liaisons at 3-, 6-, 9-, 12-, and 18- months (n = 92) post-implementation. Drawing on rapid qualitative analytic methods (i.e., structured interview and reflection summaries used to conduct matrix analyses) and informed by the updated Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research (CFIR), we integrated interview and reflections data across sources and timepoints to develop a longitudinal “site profile” of each agency.

Findings

Site profiles were templated as tables, with rows ascending chronologically from pre-implementation through 18 months, capturing data from all respondents summarized into five columns reflecting updated CFIR domains: Innovation, Outer Setting, Inner Setting, Characteristics of Individuals, and Implementation Process. Interview and reflection data were viewable by domain sequentially from pre- to post- implementation, highlighting changes in staff, infrastructure, and policies that reflect the dynamic nature of site-level implementation. Each profile distilled ≥ 15 data sources collected over 18 months into an accessible resource facilitating cross-site analysis and comparison.

Implications for D&I Research

Understanding contextual factors that impact implementation in multi-site trials can enhance the scalability and sustainability of evidence-based practices in under-resourced settings. We leveraged multi-method qualitative data across multiple time points, sources, and perspectives to develop pragmatic and analytically concentrated site profiles. Future implementation efforts may consider similar approaches to capture variability in context over time, particularly in large longitudinal trials in dynamic environments.

Primary Funding Source: Department of Veterans Affairs

## Prevention and public health

## S148 Strategies to improve the reach of evidence-based lifestyle interventions: Expanded insights from a scoping review

## Paul Estabrooks

## University of Utah, Salt Lake City, UT, USA

## Correspondence: Paul Estabrooks (paul.estabrooks@health.utah.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S148

Background: Improving the reach of evidence-based interventions is foundational to dissemination science and achieving population level impact. Indeed, evidence-based lifestyle interventions that often struggle with reach when implemented in typical community or clinical settings. This presentation will examine the utility of reach strategies to increase the individual-level dissemination of lifestyle interventions.

Methods: Using JBI and PRISMA-ScR guidance we identified 98 studies reporting a strategy designed to improve reach and at least one reach outcome measure (i.e., number, proportion, or representativeness). Strategies were inductively grouped into 15 categories by the research team and participation rate, representativeness, and information on the actor, action, channel, dose, timing, theory, mechanism, and cost of strategies were extracted. Exploratory cross-tabulations examined reach of evidence-based interventions that differed in characteristics that could influence participation.

Findings: Only 8/98 studies (8%) used randomized comparisons, and one-third (32%) bundled multiple strategies without reporting unique reach outcomes. Major reporting gaps included timing/dose (50% unreported), theory (93%), mechanism (95%), and cost (87%). Denominators for eligible and exposed groups were inconsistently defined, limiting comparability. Participation rates varied widely by strategy type (median 3%–41%), with population health management, place-based, and opt-out approaches often highest when denominators were clear. Exploratory cross tabulations suggest that when the reach strategy is tied to shorter, less intensive lifestyle interventions, participation rates were higher based on hours of contact (≤ 10 h = 35% vs > 20 h = 13%), length of the program (≤ 3 months = 30% vs > 12 months = 12%), number of contacts, (0–4 contacts = 37% vs > 12 contacts = 17%), and length of sessions (≤ 30 min = 39% vs 31–60 min = 19%). Differences by setting, format, and delivery channel were modest but variable. Finally, representativeness for subgroups (e.g., rurality, language, insurance status) was reported in fewer than one-third of studies.

Implications for D&I Research: Our results suggest that advancing individual-level dissemination science depends on three priorities. First, researchers should systematically report denominators for who is eligible and exposed to each reach strategy. Second, strategies must be clearly specified (actor, action, dose, timing, channel, mechanism) to enable replication and comparison. Third, more experimental and hybrid studies are needed to directly test strategies and establish causal effects.

Primary Funding Source: Margolis Foundation

## S149 Multi-level strategies for increasing reach of evidence-based tobacco cessation at FQHCs

## Chelsey Schlechter

## University of Utah School of Medicine, Salt Lake City, UT, USA

## Correspondence: Chelsey Schlechter (chelsey.schlechter@hci.utah.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S149

Background: Tobacco use remains the leading cause of death in the USA and is disproportionately concentrated among low socioeconomic status (SES) populations. Community Health Centers (CHCs) are a key venue for reaching low SES populations with evidence-based tobacco cessation treatment such as Quitlines. Electronic health record (EHR)-based strategies at the point-of-care, text messaging (TM), and phone counseling have the potential to increase Quitline reach.

Methods: QuitSMART Utah was a multilevel, two-phase SMART trial conducted with CHCs (n = 10 systems; 32 clinics) and CHC patients (n = 11,605). All clinics implemented Ask – Advise – Connect (AAC) to assess patient tobacco use, advise patients to quit, and directly and electronically connect patients to the Quitline. Patients who enrolled in the Quitline in response to AAC received no further strategies. Among individuals who did not enroll in the Quitline in response to AAC, phases one and two were patient-level randomizations. In phase one, patients who were non-responders were randomized to receive either text messaging (TM) or the clinic only (CO) AAC strategy. In phase two, patients in the TM condition who were non-responders in Phase one (i.e., did not enroll in Quitline) were randomized receive either continued-TM (TM-Cont) or TM + Motivation And Problem Solving (MAPS) phone coaching (TM + MAPS).

Findings: Patients were 54% male, 28% Latino, 18% spoke Spanish as preferred language, and 35% were uninsured. Sixty-three of the 11,605 QuitSMART Utah patients enrolled in the Quitline following AAC. 11,542 patients were randomized in Phase 1 to TM or CO. At 6 months, patients in TM were significantly more likely to enroll in the Quitline compared to CO (2.0% in TM vs 0.7% in CO; OR = 3.09; 97.5% CI = 1.77,5.98; P < 0.001). In Phase 2, 6,604 patients were randomized to TM + MAPS or TM-Cont. At 12 months, patients in TM + MAPS were significantly more likely to enroll in the Quitline compared to TM-Cont (2.2% TM + MAPS vs 0.9% TM-Cont; OR = 2.54; 97.5% CI = 1.57,4.26; P < 0.001).

Implications for D&I Research: Strategies such as proactive text messaging and phone coaching are effective at increasing reach of evidence-based tobacco cessation treatment among diverse and low SES patients seen at CHCs.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S150 SCALE-UP II: pragmatic randomized trial examining population health management interventions to increase reach of at-home COVID-19 testing among Community Health Center patients

## David Wetter

## University of Utah, Salt Lake City, UT, USA

## Correspondence: David Wetter (david.wetter@hci.utah.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S150

Background: Racial/ethnic minorities, low socioeconomic status (SES), and rural populations experienced lower COVID-19 vaccination rates, higher hospitalization rates, and higher mortality. While the availability of at-home tests provides a convenient and rapid option for self-testing, significant disparities persist in the reach of at-home testing among populations such as those served by Community Health Centers (CHCs). Scalable strategies are needed to increase the reach of at-home COVID-19 testing among CHC patients.

Methods: SCALE-UP II was a multi-site, randomized pragmatic clinical trial. Eligible patients were ≥ 18 years old, with a primary care visit in the last three years, and valid cellphone in the CHC electronic health record. Participants who self-reported that they had a smartphone were randomized in a 2 × 2x2 factorial (Smartphone study) to receive (i) Chatbot or Text Messaging; (ii) option to request PN (yes or no); and (iii) intervention frequency every 10 or 30 days. All other participants were randomized in a 2 × 2 factorial (Non-Smartphone study) to receive (i) option to request PN (yes or no); and (ii) intervention frequency every 10 or 30 days. The primary outcome was the proportion of participants requesting at-home COVID-19 tests by 90-days.

Findings: The trial enrolled 2,117 in the Smartphone study and 31,439 in the Non-Smartphone study. Overall, 2,341 out of 33,556 (7.0%) participants requested a test kit. In the Smartphone study, the proportion of participants who requested test kits in the Chatbot arm was lower than in TM (16.6% vs. 52.1%, aRR = 0.317 [98.33% CI 0.27–0.38], P < 0.0001). In the Non-Smartphone study, the proportion of participants who requested test kits was higher if messaged every 10 days rather than every 30 days (5.5% vs 4.8%, aRR = 1.144 [97.5% CI 1.03–1.28], P = 0.0055) and lower for participants who could request access to PN compared to those in No PN (4.3% vs 5.9%, aRR = 0.729 [97.5% CI 0.65–0.81], P < 0.0001).

Implications for D&I Research: Simple bidirectional text messaging every 10 days was effective at increasing the reach of COVID-19 testing. Text messaging is a low cost, scalable strategy that is effective at increasing reach of at-home COVID-19 testing.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S151 Evaluation of reach strategies in real world settings: impact of agency-selected reach strategies

## Jennie Hill

## University of Utah, Salt Lake City, UT, USA

## Correspondence: Jennie Hill (jennie.hill@hsc.utah.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S151

Background: The Supplemental Nutrition Program for Women, Infants, and Children (WIC) provides nutritious supplemental food, nutrition education, breastfeeding support, and referrals to health and community resources for pregnant, postpartum, and breastfeeding women, infants, and children up to age five. Despite a robust body of evidence that demonstrates WIC improves participant outcomes, WIC remains underutilized, with only half of eligible individuals participating. To increase reach—defined as the number, proportion, and representativeness of those who enroll and are retained—community-driven strategies are needed. Projects funded within a WIC innovation-initiative presents a unique opportunity to evaluate strategies developed by state and local WIC agencies to increase reach and retention.

Methods: REAIM and implementation strategy taxonomies (i.e., ERIC) were used to specify, track, and evaluate reach strategies within a type III hybrid effectiveness-implementation (HEI) evaluation. Data sources included a retrospective review of application materials submitted by 36 innovation projects and prospective reporting of reach outcomes during implementation. Each workplan was analyzed using framework-aligned protocols, codebooks, and tools to identify 1) reach to individuals—including current WIC participants and eligible non-participants—and 2) penetration to organizations through new or strengthened outreach partnerships.

Findings: A total of 1,015 non-unique implementation strategies were identified across work plans. Most focused on developing/distributing educational materials, hosting outreach events, and establishing referral pathways. Reach strategy categories engaging priority populations (n = 347), training/educating collaborators (n = 180), changing infrastructure (n = 144), developing partner relationships (n = 130), supporting delivery agents (n = 83), tailoring to context (n = 25), evaluation (n = 28), and providing financial incentives (n = 5). Engaging priority populations and building partner relationships were foundational strategies as demonstrated by active referral pathways and personalized follow-up yielding > 15% enrollment versus 2–8% from mass media. All subgrantees reported at least one reach or penetration outcome (median = 4), yet denominators were inconsistently defined making some comparisons challenging.

Implications for D&I Research: Given the scarcity of pragmatic, non-randomized evaluations suited to existing community evidence-based interventions such as WIC, our findings demonstrate how multilevel, partner-co-designed approaches can identify “what works” when conventional randomized trials are not feasible. Tailoring strategies to local context and engaging system partners therefore shows particular promise for improving WIC’s reach.

Primary Funding Source: USDA Food & Nutrition Services

## S152 Building trust through community-informed dissemination: a roadmap for connecting science and community

## Kristi Lambert1, Sharon Sanders2,3, Anna Huff Davis2,3, Taren Massey-Swindle2,3

## 1Community Member, Little Rock, AR, USA; 2University of Arkansas for Medical Sciences, Little Rock, AR, USA; 3Arkansas Children's Research Institute, Little Rock, AR, USA

## Correspondence: Taren Massey-Swindle (tswindle@uams.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S152

Background: While implementation strategies have been developed and tested extensively, dissemination strategies, particularly those grounded in community perspectives, remain underexplored. This gap limits research impact and weakens trust between communities and researchers. This project sought to understand community preferences for receiving research-based information and to develop a roadmap to guide community-centered dissemination.

Methods: The LINK (Leveraging Implementation and eNgagement for Knowledge impact) is a service core that connects science with communities, aiming to support community-engaged implementation and enhance dissemination as a means of building trust and engagement. The LINK Community Advisory Board (CAB) co-designed and piloted a guide for community listening sessions. A CAB member of The LINK led 7 listening sessions with 60 community members across a Southern U.S. state. Participants responded to questions regarding perceived barriers to engagement in research and preferred formats for receiving research information. Using directed content analysis informed by Bauman et al. (2022), we coded transcripts across the domains of dissemination: (1) Processes, (2) Determinants, (3) Strategies, and (4) desired Outcomes. Results informed the development of a structured Dissemination Roadmap for The LINK and its partnering researchers, designed to support planning and reflection.

Findings: Based on community input, the Roadmap Process begins with relationship-building and continues through co-creation, feedback, and ongoing evaluation. Key Determinants included trust, relevance, clear language, and structural supports (e.g., transportation, childcare). Preferred Strategies involved in-person updates, research briefs, digital tools (e.g., newsletters, resource hubs, social media), and capacity-building sessions to apply findings. Desired outcomes ranged from increased research awareness and use to long-term improvements in child health and community-led research. Planned products include a podcast platform, educational events, plain-language materials (e.g., infographics), and an online hub for access and communication.

Implications for D&I Research: This study provides a replicable, community-informed model for improving dissemination based on community preferences. By operationalizing community perspectives into a roadmap, this work can support future empirical testing of dissemination strategies and the broader goal of building sustainable research-community partnerships. This model can be adapted by dissemination and implementation or community engagement cores to improve the relevance, reach, and use of research findings.

Primary Funding Source: Arkansas Biosciences Institute

## S153 Engaging community-driven coalitions to improve uptake of prevention and public health interventions

## Ruth Rechis1, Katherine Oestman2, Haley Gardiner1, Travis Anthony1, Jacqueline Dan-Jumbo1, Stephanie Nutt1

## 1The University of Texas M.D. Anderson Cancer Center, Houston, TX, USA; 2Daybreak Consulting, Las Vegas, NV, USA

## Correspondence: Katherine Oestman (katy@daybreakway.com)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S153

Background

Multi-sector community-wide interventions are effective in addressing numerous health outcomes especially when guided by a community-driven coalition. Coalitions ensure community needs are integrated into evidence-based intervention (EBI) implementation. While many coalitions exist, they do not always sustain or result in improved uptake of prevention and public health interventions. Be Well Communities™ is MD Anderson's place-based strategy for cancer prevention and control, working with communities to reduce modifiable cancer risk factors. Be Well Communities works with community and clinical practice settings to fund, deliver and evaluate EBIs. Over 9 years, across three communities, we have developed a model for creating and evaluating coalitions that guide EBI implementation and improve health outcomes.

Methods: Annually, 50 + organizations participated across communities in a survey assessing the initiatives goals, effectiveness, outcomes, and sustainability. Survey responses were aggregated across communities, and descriptive statistics were calculated at baseline and endline. Coalition members also report annually on implementation and sustainability. Coalition effectiveness and EBI implementation are analyzed as part of the overall evaluation methodology which utilizes the RE-AIM framework.

Findings: Survey results related to coalition effectiveness include the following:95% agreed that the MD Anderson team is trusted by all organizations at baseline and 96% agreed at endline
88% agreed that coalition members support each other to carry out programs effectively at baseline and 91% agreed at endline
84% indicated confidence in long term sustainability of the community collaborations and 95% indicated confidence at endline.

Since 2017, 45 EBIs have been implemented, reaching over 111,000 people improving health outcomes related to obesity, cancer screening, and vaccination. Notably, one year post funding, 95% of interventions are sustained.

Implications for D&I Research

Understanding how to develop, manage, and evaluate effective coalitions can inform integrating EBIs into a multitude of clinical and community settings. This approach and methodology can inform other D&I efforts seeking to sustainably implement EBIs aimed at improving population health outcomes. Further, the components of this coalition survey and the evaluation strategy can be utilized by others to inform dissemination and implementation processes focused on how the context of relationships impact results.

Primary Funding Source: MD Anderson Cancer Center

## S154 Integrating preferences from primary caregivers and community partners for a multicomponent lifestyle intervention to address childhood obesity and cardiometabolic risk in Samoa

## Courtney Choy1, Rochelle Rosen2, Christina Soti-Ulberg3, Maria Arorae4, Vaimoana Filipo4, Kima Savusa4, Nicola Hawley1, Mona Sharifi5, Siufaga Simi3

## 1Yale School of Public Health, New Haven, CT, USA; 2The Miriam Hospital, Providence, RI, USA; 3Ministry of Health, Apia, Samoa; 4Samoan Obesity, Lifestyle and Genetic Adaptations Study (OLaGA) Group, Apia, Samoa; 5Yale University School of Medicine, New Haven, CT, USA

## Correspondence: Courtney Choy (courtney.choy@yale.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S154

Background: Preventative interventions are critical to reduce cardiometabolic diseases (CMD). In Samoa, childhood obesity prevalence has doubled over the past decade, accompanied by rising risk factors. Interventions have been slow to reach Pacific communities, with knowledge gaps about optimal design. We aimed to understand community preferences for the development of an intervention to address CMDs among Samoan children.

Methods: From 2024–25, we purposively sampled Samoan primary caregivers who were potential end users and key stakeholders involved with child programs in Samoa. Focus group discussions and interview agendas were guided by the Active Implementation Framework, using a Hexagon tool to explore options. Participants assessed six contextual fit and feasibility indices (need, evidence, fit, usability, capacity, and support for implementation) for common components of existing interventions identified in a scoping review. Using a Framework Matrix Analysis, we charted qualitative data and summarized preferences for preferences in intervention age group, setting, implementers, delivery mode, duration, components, and goals.

Findings: We conducted eight focus groups (n = 74 female primary caregivers, median age: 42 years, IQR: 36–49) and 19 interviews (84% female stakeholders, range: 5–40 years of work experience). Many participants endorsed the feasibility of implementing an intervention before the age of 9. They suggested leveraging existing community assets and collective mindset to protect the next generation. Participants preferred an intervention design and delivery that includes families, offers both group and individual in-person sessions bi-weekly for up to six months, and focuses on nutrition, physical activity, sleep, and mental wellness. A team approach and holistic programs that focus on multiple aspects of a child’s life to support their physical, mental, spiritual, and emotional health were emphasized. These insights directly informed a conceptual model (key components and processes to support healthful behaviors with family involvement) for an intervention to be pilot tested in Samoa.

Implications for D&I Research: Engaging primary caregivers and key stakeholders to share feedback on existing components can help to refine intervention design within the context of communities and enhance the likelihood for high feasibility and acceptability. D&I science facilitates the translation of community feedback into action for testing interventions, which can lead to improved implementation and health outcomes.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S155 The EPICC implementation capacity survey: identifying challenges for implementing evidence-based health promotion programs in 30 African American congregations

## Jemal Gishe1, David Schlundt2, Leah Alexander3, Sharon Jones2, Rebecca Selove1

## 1Tennessee State University, Nashville, TN, USA; 2Vanderbilt University, Nashville, TN, USA; 3Meharry Medical College, Nashville, TN, USA

## Correspondence: Jemal Gishe (jgishe@tnstate.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S155

Background

Implementing evidence-based health promotion programs (EBPs) in community settings is an effective approach to preventing chronic disease and reducing health disparities. Community organizations offer benefits of proximity and trust for engaging individuals in EBPs to change behavior. The Engaging Partners in Caring Communities (EPICC) Project was developed to provide technical assistance (TA) to churches serving predominantly Black communities to enhance their capacity to implement one of six EBPs identified by EPICC researchers. The EPICC survey was designed using the Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research (CFIR) as a tool for assessing implementation capacity and identifying congregations’ strengths as well as potential challenges they might encounter in implementing the EBP they selected.

Methods:

Teams from 30 African American congregations completed the online EPICC survey. Automated scoring identified potential implementation challenges across CFIR constructs. Each team received an individualized report which was discussed in a recorded feedback interview. Descriptive statistics summarized the findings for the research team.

Findings

Congregation sizes ranged from 18 to 1,000 members (median 62.5, IQR 126.3; mean 136.7, SD 196.9). The number of challenges per congregation (out of 31 CFIR constructs) ranged from 1 to 19 (median 4.5, IQR 4; mean 5.6, SD 4). The most common challenges were cost (n = 28), design quality and packaging (n = 24), and readiness for implementation (n = 13). Based on number of challenges identified in survey responses, congregations were categorized as beginner (n = 4), intermediate (n = 16), or advanced (n = 10) in implementation capacity. Congregation size was not correlated with the number of challenges (r = –0.04). The Project’s TA teams used the results to provide feedback to the church’s implementation team and used the feedback interview to plan and tailor their approach to supporting each congregation.

Implications for D&I Research

We developed a process for identifying potential challenges in churches implementing EBPs, and providing theory-driven, evidence-based results to church partners. While challenges can be identified during implementation, our approach anticipates these challenges and suggests strategies for overcoming them during the planning stages of program implementation. This approach can be adapted to other community groups attempting to implement a wide range of strategic initiatives.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S156 Advancing equitable access to HIV/HCV testing among people who inject drugs: A mixed-method study of a multicomponent implementation strategy to increase adoption and implementation of opt-out testing at syringe services programs

## Tyler Bartholomew1, Marina Plesons1, Hansel Tookes2

## 1University of Miami Miller School of Medicine, Miami, FL, USA; 2University of Miami, Miami, FL, USA

## Correspondence: Tyler Bartholomew (tsb61@miami.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S156

Background

Improving HIV and Hepatitis C (HCV) testing among people who inject drugs (PWID) remains fundamental in attaining the UNAIDS and WHO goals for 2030. Syringe services programs (SSPs) are ideal venues for reaching PWID with comprehensive testing services. In 2023, The US CDC endorsed opt-out testing for HIV/HCV in the SSP setting. This study examined the impact of a multicomponent implementation strategy – ACCESS – on adoption and implementation of opt-out HIV/HCV testing at two newly sanctioned SSPs in Florida.

Methods

A community-driven implementation mapping approach was used to describe the most significant barriers to HIV/HCV testing within SSPs (financial resources, organizational capacity, staff training, data systems, and technical support) and identify discrete implementation strategies (low-threshold funding and practice facilitation) for the ACCESS strategy. Two SSPs in Florida then participated in a 24-month pilot of ACCESS. Quantitative data on participant-level demographics, HIV/HCV testing uptake, and testing outcomes were obtained from SSP administrative records at 12 and 24-months post-baseline. Qualitative interviews with SSP directors/staff were conducted to ascertain ACCESS’s acceptability and feasibility.

Findings

Mean funding amounts provided for each 12-month period was $75,962 per site. Mean HIV/HCV testing uptake was 70.1% and 69.3%, respectively, among SSP participants (n = 475). There was significant variation between sites, with Site 1 testing 99.5% and 99.1% of participants for HIV/HCV, respectively, compared to 44.3% and 43.1% at Site 2. Interviews elucidated implementation fidelity issues, with Site 2 highlighting significant staff turnover during the initial 12-month period. Strategy adaptations (monthly check-in meetings and real-time data reports) were applied, after which HIV/HCV testing uptake at Site 2 significantly increased to 74.8% and 64.8%, respectively, at 24 months.

Implications for D&I Research

Our ACCESS strategy (low threshold funding and practice facilitation) was acceptable, feasible, and showed preliminary efficacy within the SSP context to influence adoption and sustainment of opt-out HIV/HCV testing.

Primary Funding Source: GILEAD Sciences

## S157 Implementation strategies and adjunctive interventions for HIV testing and linkage-to-care in the United States: a systematic review

## James Merle1, Amelia Van Pelt2, Olutobi Sanuade3, Timothy Levengood4, McKenzie Swan5, Ana Michaela Pachicano6, Alithia Zamantakis2, J.D. Smith1, Virginia McKay7, Dennis Li2, Brian Mustanski6, Nanette Benbow2

## 1University of Utah, Salt Lake City, UT, USA; 2Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine, Chicago, IL, USA; 3University of Utah Spencer Fox Eccles School of Medicine, Salt Lake City, UT, USA; 4Boston University School of Public Health, Boston, MA, USA; 5Washington University in St. Louis, Saint Louis, USA; 6Northwestern University, Chicago, IL, USA; 7Center for Public Health Systems Science, Brown School, Washington University in Saint Louis, St. Louis, MO, USA

## Correspondence: James Merle (james.merle@utah.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S157

Background: Despite advances in HIV prevention, over 30,000 new HIV diagnoses occur annually in the U.S., with many individuals unaware of their status. The Ending the HIV Epidemic initiative aims to reduce new infections to 3,000 per year by 2030. Achieving this goal requires effective implementation of HIV testing and linkage-to-care (LTC) strategies. However, the field lacks a comprehensive synthesis of implementation strategies and adjunctive interventions that support these efforts.

Methods: We conducted a systematic review following PRISMA guidelines to identify U.S.-based studies evaluating implementation strategies (targeting system- or provider-level change) or adjunctive interventions (targeting individual-level behavior change) for HIV testing/LTC. Searches were conducted across MEDLINE, PsycINFO, and Web of Science, with updates through June 2024. Studies were screened and coded using established frameworks (ERIC taxonomy for implementation strategies and the Theoretical Domains Framework (TDF) and COM-B model for adjunctive interventions). Descriptive statistics and post-hoc analyses were used to assess strategy use and effectiveness.

Findings: From 190 included studies, we identified 756 discrete implementation strategies. The most common ERIC domains were “Train and Educate Stakeholders” and “Change Infrastructure.” Reach was the most frequently measured outcome (88%), followed by adoption (35%). Few studies conducted an experimental design (8%). Among 93 studies eligible for effectiveness coding, 74% reported positive effects. Moreover, 39 adjunctive interventions were mapped to TDF and COM-B domains, with most targeting motivation and capability; however, few included implementation strategies to support adjunctive intervention delivery.

Implications for D&I Research: This review catalogs the breadth of strategies used to promote HIV testing/LTC. Effective strategies included integrating HIV testing/LTC programs into new settings and utilizing peer and social network strategies. A persistent gap in rigorous strategy evaluation emphasizes the need for more rigorous study designs and use of D&I frameworks, such as the Implementation Research Logic Model to ensure that change methods match their target level and the Longitudinal Implementation Strategy Tracking System to improve reporting, transparency, and reproducibility. The integration of behavioral frameworks like COM-B can enhance the design of adjunctive interventions, however, separate strategies are needed to implement adjunctive interventions. These insights guide future implementation research and practice, supporting more effective and equitable HIV prevention efforts.

Primary Funding Source: Centers for Disease Control and Prevention

## S158 Utility of PrEP training in Title X-funded safety-net family planning clinics for increasing HIV testing and PrEP reach among women in Atlanta: lessons learned from provider focus groups

## Jessica Sales1, Katherine Anderson2, Deja Er3, Peyton Williams4, Sara Sullivan4, Michael Brooks4, Melissa Kottke5, Anandi Sheth6

## 1Emory University, Rollins School of Public Health, Atlanta, GA, USA; 2Emory University Rollins School of Public Health, Atlanta, GA, USA; 3Emory University, Atlanta, GA, USA; 4The Family Health Centers of Georgia, Inc., Georgia Family Planning System, Atlanta, GA, USA; 5Emory University School of Medicine, Atlanta, GA, USA; 6Emory School of Medicine, Atlanta, GA, USA

## Correspondence: Jessica Sales (jmcderm@emory.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S158

Background: PrEP remains underutilized by women in the US, in part, because PrEP is not widely available in women’s health care settings. Based on formative findings, we developed a bundle of tailored implementation strategies (trainings, technical assistance, resources) to support PrEP delivery in Title X-funded family planning clinics across Atlanta, Georgia (GA) – a high priority jurisdiction for the Ending the HIV Epidemic initiative. Guided by the Interactive Systems Framework, we partnered with GAs Title X grant holder to provide these strategies to this clinical network. We conducted a comprehensive evaluation of Title X clinic strategy use and describe pre- and post-strategy engagement PrEP practices.

Methods: Between January 2022-November 2023, 47 staff, providers and clinic leadership from 11 clinical groups representing 22 of 31 Atlanta Title X clinics (71%) participated in baseline (pre-training) focus groups (FGs). 21 trainings were provided to the 11 clinical groups (n = 415 participants). Ten groups participated in FGs 1-year post-training(s). Rapid qualitative analysis of FGs was performed; themes describe PrEP delivery landscape at baseline and 1-year follow-up, utility of training and remaining barriers to PrEP care. We are currently analyzing clinic-level aggregate HIV testing and PrEP prescribing pre- and post-strategy engagement data; results will be added to the presentation.

Findings: At baseline, few clinics (< 5; 22%) were providing PrEP. After training, seven clinical groups were offering PrEP, while one offered referrals to a PrEP-specific clinic. Three clinics were able to provide injectable PrEP, though noted challenges navigating insurance barriers. Two clinical groups were not offering PrEP comprehensively/consistently, citing patient barriers and capacity limitations due to staff turnover. Overall, clinics reported trainings helped increase their readiness/capacity to provide PrEP. Particularly beneficial trainings included sexual history taking, creation of safe spaces/cultural sensitivity, and the overview of PrEP care. Five clinical groups noted “refresher” trainings would be beneficial because of staff turnover; four expressed a desire for additional trainings.

Implications for D&I Research: The implementation strategies were utilized by Atlanta Title X clinics. Brief, tailored trainings can improve PrEP provision in safety-net family planning settings and sustainable access to them via a centralized agency is necessary to overcome ongoing capacity-related barriers.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S159 Co-designing a model for home delivery of long-acting injectable pre-exposure prophylaxis in Chicago

## Amelia Van Pelt1,2, Elizabeth Casline1,2, Byrd G. M. Cook1, Jorge Cestou3, Brian Mustanski2,4, Gregory Phillips1,4, Rinad Beidas1

## 1Northwestern University, Chicago, USA; 2Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine, Chicago, IL, USA; 3Chicago Department of Public Health, Chicago, USA; 4Northwestern University, Chicago, IL, USA

## Correspondence: Amelia Van Pelt (amelia.vanpelt@northwestern.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S159

Background: Participatory design can center the voice of recipients and improve operationalization of implementation strategies. To maximize uptake of a new form of long-acting, injectable PrEP (CAB-LA), co-designing implementation strategies is critical. A recent innovation tournament in Chicago crowdsourced ideas from the community on CAB-LA implementation and selected delivery of CAB-LA in the home as the top strategy. Through a partnership with key constituents, this research aimed to operationalize this approach for sexual minority men (SMM) (≥ 13 years) through user-centered design (UCD).

Methods: The UCD process involved two steps: 1) workshop to draft the home-based model and 2) semi-structured interviews to iterate the model. First, transdisciplinary constituents (e.g., clinicians, public health leadership, community-based organizations, implementation scientists, SMM) convened in an in-person four-hour meeting to outline a blueprint for the home-based delivery model. Second, virtual semi-structured interviews were conducted to elicit additional feedback from workshop participants. Rapid qualitative analysis was completed to finalize the model.

Findings: Nineteen constituents participated in the workshop. Key phases of the model included: pre-implementation (e.g., attainment of buy-in, procurement of shots, financing), implementation (e.g., enrollment of patients, identification of staff, scheduling, administration in homes), and maintenance (e.g., post-administration/re-engagement). Patient actions focused on indicating preferences for visit, preparing home for visit, and scheduling follow-up care. Deliverer actions emphasized prescribing CAB-LA, sharing financing information, and administration and at-home procedures. Ten workshop participants completed follow-up interviews. Constituents suggested additional considerations for clarification (e.g., specification of lab requirements) and potential barriers to implementation given change in context (e.g., stigma toward SMM, concerns visiting homes).
Implications for D&I Research: This research produced a blueprint for a co-designed model of “low-barrier PrEP,” which can guide plans for CAB-LA integration in Chicago. Future efforts will test the success of this model on increasing the uptake of CAB-LA among SMM. Further, the approach to utilize UCD to generate a community-informed strategy can provide a framework for participatory approaches in implementation science. Given that the co-design of implementation efforts often does not involve the participation of individuals with lived experience, this work centered the voices of those who will benefit most.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S160 Sustaining the positive impacts of Physically Active Children in Education (PACE): a cluster randomised controlled trial

## Alix Hall1, Adam Shoesmith1, Luke Wolfenden2, Edward Riley-Gibson1, William Pascoe1, Rebecca Jackson2, Nicole McCarthy2, Adrian Bauman3, Rachel Sutherland2, James Boyer4, Bernadette Duggan5, Nicole Nathan2

## 1University of Newcastle, Callaghan, NSW, Australia; 2Hunter New England Population Health, Wallsend, NSW, Australia; 3University of Sydney, Sydney, NSW, Australia; 4New South Wales Department of Education, Turrella, NSW, Australia; 5Catholic Schools Office Maitland Newcastle, Newcastle West, NSW, Australia

## Correspondence: Alix Hall (Alix.Ivers@health.nsw.gov.au)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S160

Background: Despite policies that mandate schools deliver a minimum amount of physical activity (PA) per week, many schools find it difficult to meet such requirements. We have developed an effective, award winning, multicomponent implementation intervention, Physically Active Children in Education (PACE), to help teacher’s increase their delivery of PA across the school week. However, evidence suggests that following the withdrawal of active implementation support, less than 20% of health promotion programs continue to be delivered (i.e., sustained). To ensure the positive benefits of PACE continue, we co-designed and evaluated a multi-component sustainment strategy to support teachers to continue implementing a school PA policy 12 months after initial adoption.

Methods: We conducted a clustered randomised controlled trial with 25 primary schools across four health jurisdictions in New South Wales, Australia. Schools were randomised to receive a multi-component sustainment strategy or to usual care. The sustainment strategy included formal Principal commitment, in-school champions, prompts and reminders, resource packs, school recognition materials, and parent-facing communication tools. Minutes of PA scheduled by classroom teachers was collected via completion of a daily activity logbook over one school week. Linear mixed models were used to assess between group differences in the change of teacher’s scheduling of PA from baseline to 12-month follow-up. Approval was obtained from Hunter New England and University of Newcastle Human Research Ethics Committees.

Findings: 100 teachers at baseline and 81 at 12-month follow-up, provided valid scheduling data. From baseline to 12-month follow-up, a significant difference in the change of PA scheduled by intervention and control teachers was observed (p = 0.002). Specifically, preliminary data indicates intervention teachers increased their scheduling by ~15 min per week while control teachers decreased their scheduling by ~21 min per week.

Implications for D&I Research: This is one of the first trials globally to test a strategy to sustain a school PA policy. Findings show that a co-designed, multi-component strategy, developed based on Implementation Science principles, can support schools to sustain their delivery of PA over time. Continued evaluation at 24 months will determine whether these effects are maintained long term.

Primary Funding Source: NHMRC (National Health & Medical Research Council)

## S161 Enhancing fidelity in school-based prevention: results from a randomized controlled trial of getting to outcomes to support Project ALERT implementation by middle school teachers

## Matthew Chinman1,2, Andrea Phillips3, Jill Cannon4, Michelle Bongard4, Jacobo Pereira-Pacheco3, Courtney Kase2

## 1Veterans Health Administration, Pittsburgh, PA, USA; 2RAND Corporation, Pittsburgh, PA, USA; 3RAND CORPORATION, Santa Monica, CA, USA; 4RAND, Santa Monica, CA, USA

## Correspondence: Matthew Chinman (chinman@rand.org)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S161

Background: It is critical to conduct substance use prevention with middle school youth to prevent risky use of alcohol, marijuana, nicotine, and misuse of prescription medication in high school. Project ALERT (PA) is an 11-session evidence-based prevention program (EBPP) targeting all 7th graders that is widely adopted, cost-free, and accessible (online teacher training). Data show, like with many substance use EBPPs, PA is often poorly implemented (e.g., truncated or omitted lessons), undermining its effectiveness. Getting To Outcomes (GTO)—an implementation strategy bundle of coaching, training, and tools that stimulate quality improvement—improved EBPP fidelity in community-based organizations delivering programs to middle school youth. This is the first test of GTO with an EBPP in the school context.

Methods: As part of a larger RCT evaluating PA and GTO, we assessed the fidelity of PA implementation comparing middle school teachers randomly assigned to implement Project ALERT on their own (N = 9) to teachers assigned to receive GTO-based support (N = 8). In two successive school years for each teacher, research staff rated a subset of PA sessions (PA-alone: NY1 = 29; NY2 = 22; PA + GTO: NY1 = 24; NY2 = 21) for adherence to session activities (0 = none, 1 = partial, 2 = full). On a 1 = least to 5 = most scale, ratings were also made on each teacher’s pace, engagement effort, class management, enthusiasm, empathy, and on students’ engagement and interest. We conducted linear mixed-effects models, accounting for repeated teacher observations, to test for fidelity differences between groups and across years. We also interviewed the GTO teachers about their experience after their second year and extracted common themes.

Findings: In Year 1, GTO teachers’ adherence ratings were significantly higher than PA-alone, and then significantly improved further in Year 2. In Year 2, teacher pace, engagement effort, management, empathy, and student interest were significantly higher for GTO teachers than PA-alone, and management and student interest also significantly improved for GTO teachers from Year 1. In interviews, teachers said GTO helped them plan and deliver lessons, set goals, and reflect on lesson delivery to improve future lessons.

Implications for D&I Research: This study demonstrates the value of implementation strategies like GTO for bridging the gap between EBPP availability and effective delivery in real-world educational contexts.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S162 Prevention of parental substance use and mental health disorders by intervening on social risk factors: implementation of a continuum of care prevention program in child welfare settings

## Lisa Saldana1, Gracelyn Cruden1, Jeffrey Peterson2, Ryan Singh1, Maria Bybee3

## 1Chestnut Health Systems-Lighthouse Institute, Eugene, OR, USA; 2Chestnut Health Systems- Lighthouse Institute, Eugene, OR, USA; 3Chestnut Health Systems—Lighthouse Institute, Eugene, OR, USA

## Correspondence: Lisa Saldana (lsaldana@chestnut.org)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S162

Background

Parents with opioid and/or methamphetamine use (O/MU) and mental health (MH) disorders have a high likelihood of adverse social risk factors in all five areas identified by Healthy People 2030 as being essential for healthy development and quality of life. Just Care for Families (JCFF) is an emerging practice developed for parents along a continuum of preventive care need (primary to tertiary) involved with, or at-risk for involvement with, the child welfare system (CWS). In partnership with Oregon’s CWS, JCFF is being implemented in five rural counties.

Methods

JCFF was developed out of an identified need, from a different hybrid trial, for a continuum of care prevention model. A multi-level implementation roadmap was followed and measured implementation process fidelity. Two of the counties self-initiated JCFF implementation, after their respective agencies successfully implemented in other counties in the first trial.
While parent data collection was designed for hybrid trial research, it now has been integrated into practice to inform clinical care. Parents are assessed weekly for 18 months, via a survey received by text, email, or phone about the social risk factors they experience as well as O/MU and MH outcomes. Survey questions span all five categories of social risk.

Findings

All counties have implemented JCFF and sustained for two years post-launch. To date,192 of a targeted 250 parents have consented to participate. Most parents elect to receive their weekly survey via text (76%) versus email (24%) or phone (n = 1) and most (74%) have completed 85–100% of the weeks. Preliminary analyses show a significant change in social risk factor needs and clinical outcomes, in the first 6-months. In collaboration with CWS, expansion into four more counties, following the same implementation roadmap, is planned to commence this Fall.

Implications for D&I Research

Through collaboration with public systems, such as CW, implementation of preventive interventions can successfully reach a range of parents, along a continuum of care need, to address public health crises such as O/MU and MH disorders. Following a well-defined implementation roadmap, agencies can learn how to implement well and replicate in other regions, allowing the potential for system-wide scale-up and public health impact.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S163 Predicting implementation fidelity across two statewide school mental health initiatives: findings from REACH and RSSI in Illinois

## David W. Johnson1, Grace Daley2, Beatriz Cabello1, Danielle Hatchimonji1, Mashana Smith1, Colleen Cicchetti1

## 1Lurie Children's Hospital of Chicago, Chicago, IL, USA; 2Center for Childhood Resilience, Ann & Robert H. Lurie Children's, Chicago, IL, USA

## Correspondence: David W. Johnson (dajohnson@luriechildrens.org)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S163

Background: Previous research attests to the important role that schools play in addressing the unmet need for youth mental health services; however, more research is needed to understand school-level predictors of engagement in initiatives supporting the implementation of evidence-based mental health supports. The Resilience Education to Advance Community Healing (REACH) initiative and its successor, Resilience Supportive Schools Illinois (RSSI), represent statewide efforts to increase schools’ capacity to implement trauma-responsive, equitable, and culturally relevant strategies for supporting student mental health and resilience. Both initiatives use structured processes and statewide data tools to guide needs assessment, action planning, and continuous improvement. To support more effective dissemination, implementation, and scale-up strategies, it is critical to identify what school-level characteristics are associated with participation and completion of key implementation milestones. This reflects a core question in hybrid implementation-effectiveness research: understanding organizational and contextual factors that influence implementation success alongside downstream outcomes.

Methods: We conducted multivariate regression analyses using two datasets: (1) REACH data from 2019–2024 (n = 1,169 schools) and (2) RSSI pilot data from the 2024–25 school year (n = 400 schools). For both initiatives, we examined school-level predictors of two outcomes: participation in REACH or RSSI and completion of key implementation milestones, including needs assessment, action plan development, and engagement with asynchronous online professional learning. Predictor variables include measures of school context and characteristics from publicly available administrative data.

Findings: School participation in REACH and completion of key milestones are associated with improved academic and workforce outcomes, including increased teacher retention, reductions in student chronic absenteeism, and reductions in out-of-school suspensions. Preliminary findings indicate that participation in the RSSI pilot is associated with reductions in chronic absenteeism. The current study tests hypotheses regarding school-level characteristics associated with both participation and engagement in REACH and RSSI, including school size, grade level composition, Title I status, student demographics, urbanicity, geographic region, and adversity index score.

Implications for D&I Research: This study advances dissemination and implementation science by identifying predictors of uptake and engagement with school mental health capacity-building initiatives. Findings contribute actionable insights for scaling systems-level, trauma-responsive, and equity-focused models across diverse school contexts.

Primary Funding Source: Blended funding

## S164 Advancing preventive care through Medicaid policy innovation: Florida’s cancer screening incentive initiative

## Jaclyn Hall1, Rahma Mkuu1, Peyton Lurk2, Curtis Warren3, Belal Ghaffari3, Christopher Cogle4

## 1University of Florida, Gainesville, FL, USA; 2Florida State Universit, Tallahassee, FL, USA; 3Knowli Data Science, Tallahassee, FL, USA; 4Florida Medicaid, Gainesville, FL, USA

## Correspondence: Jaclyn Hall (Jaclynha@ufl.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S164

Background: The COVID-19 pandemic caused a nationwide disruption in preventive services, including an estimated 3.8 million missed colorectal cancer (CRC) screenings in 2020. To address this screening gap among vulnerable populations, Florida Medicaid implemented a financial incentive for managed care plans: Alleviation of Liquidated Damages if ≥ 50% of eligible enrollees completed CRC screening between October 1, 2022, and June 30, 2023. This novel statewide initiative leveraged administrative claims data, health plan-specific screening metrics, public–private partnerships, and bi-weekly cross-sector meetings with state officials, health plans, and external stakeholders to increase screening utilization.

Methods: This quasi-experimental pre-post policy evaluation used Medicaid claims data to identify adults aged 45–75 (by October 1st in each period) continuously enrolled for 12 previous months and not up-to-date with colorectal cancer screening guidelines, excluding those with frailty or advanced illness. During bi-weekly meetings Plans shared practice-level screening rates, incentivized physicians to achieve screening targets, contacted enrollees, and hosted community awareness events. Medicaid claims data were analyzed for three cohorts: a pre-initiative year, pilot year, and post-initiative year. Secondary administrative data were used to compare colorectal cancer screening rates before and during the initiative, with descriptive statistics and two-sided chi-squared tests (P < 0.05) used to assess differences.

Findings: During the initiative, the statewide CRC screening rate among Medicaid enrollees increased to 51.3%, up from 46.9% in the prior year, equating to an estimated 6267 additional patients screened, with the largest gains seen in those aged 45–65. The total number of colonoscopies and stool-based screenings increased in the subsequent year, yet, due to an increase in the number of enrollees eligible, the rate of those screened dropped to 45.1%. Notably, the use of noninvasive stool-DNA testing (Cologuard) grew in all age groups from 1.3% of total tests pre-initiative to 7.4% post-initiative and contributed to an increase in follow-up screenings.

Implications for D&I Research: This case highlights how state-level financial incentives along with health plan level operational changes catalyzed large-scale improvements in preventive care. Financial incentives should be integrated with accessible screening modalities and upstream plan level drivers of access and utilization to sustain gains, increase follow-up screenings, and reduce costs beyond the intervention period.

## S165 Comparing the cost of harm reduction vending machine implementation models in the HEALing Communities Study: the New York State experience

## Jazmine Li1, Aurora Yuan2, James David3, Nabila El-Bassel4, Bruce Schackman5, Laura Starbird6

## 1Weill Cornell Medicine, New York, NY, USA; 2University of Pennsylvania, Philadelphia, PA, USA; 3Columbia University, New York, NY, USA; 4Columbia University, New York, USA; 5Weill Cornell Medical College, New York, NY, USA; 6University of Pennsylvania School of Nursing, Philadelphia, PA, USA

## Correspondence: Laura Starbird (starbird@nursing.upenn.edu)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S165

Background

Harm reduction vending machines provide free and anonymous access to supplies such as naloxone, fentanyl and xylazine test strips, and safe sex supplies. They facilitate community access to products that lower the risk of opioid-related morbidity and mortality. We identified different approaches to implement harm reduction vending machines that were selected by communities in New York State (NYS) and estimated the cost of each approach.

Methods

We collected invoice, tracking, and key informant interview data from five communities in NYS that participated in Wave 2 of the HEALing Communities Study in NYS and installed harm reduction vending machines during the intervention period (2022–2023). We classified the planning, installation, and maintenance strategies employed by each community and conducted an activity-based micro-costing analysis to estimate the one-time start-up and ongoing implementation cost of each harm reduction vending machine strategy.

Findings

Across the five communities, we identified three unique community-selected approaches to implementing harm reduction vending machines: 1) electronic vending machines with tracking software; 2) refurbished vending machines; and 3) naloxone housing unit-adjacent implementation (e.g., newspaper box or free-vend machine like a restroom tampon dispenser). The two communities that installed electronic vending machines with tracking software incurred a higher cost over a 1-year period per machine ($3,816 and $1,863) compared with the community that chose a refurbished vending machine ($1,098). Electronic vending machines also incurred the highest one-time cost per machine ($19,808 and $16,620) compared to installing a refurbished vending machine ($11,626). The two communities that selected a naloxone housing unit-adjacent approach incurred the lowest 1-year implementation cost per machine ($498 and $328). These two communities also had the lowest one-time costs per machine ($2,452 and $6,828).

Implications for D&I Research

Harm reduction vending machines provide an opportunity for communities to offer publicly available naloxone and other safe drug use equipment to reduce fatal overdoses. Depending on a community’s resources, we offer multiple implementation options for harm reduction vending machines that range in costs, maintenance needs, and stock. The wide range of costs depending on the implementation approach allows stakeholders to choose the strategy that fits their budget, time, and personnel needs, with considerations for scalability and sustainability.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health

## S166 Is flexibility in decision-making a catalyst for innovation adoption? Rethinking organizational drivers of implementation during public health crises

## Sapna Mendon-Plasek1, Gabriel Hassler2, Carl Berdahl3, Christina Huber4, Catherine Cohen2, Peter Mendel5, Shira H. Fischer1

## 1RAND Corporation, Boston, MA, USA; 2RAND, Santa Monica, CA, USA; 3Cedars-Sinai Medicine, Los Angeles, CA, USA; 4University of California, Los Angeles, Los Angeles, USA; 5RAND Corporation, Santa Monica, CA, USA

## Correspondence: Sapna Mendon-Plasek (smendon@rand.org)

Implementation Science 2026, 21(2):S166

Background

During 2020, over 100,000 patients were hospitalized for acute COVID-19, most through emergency departments (EDs). Rapidly evolving infection control challenges demanded adaptations to protocols to combat shortages in resources and to optimize treatments. Although positive leadership support, learning climate, and collaborations are known to promote adoption of interventions during steady state conditions, their role in facilitating implementation of innovations during public health crises is less clear. This study examines how organizational context and clinician attitudes influence the adoption to use innovations in ED settings.

Methods

Between February and September 2024, we conducted a nationally representative survey of 1,556 ED clinicians (MDs and RNs). The survey, based on focus groups with clinicians in 8 EDs across the US, assessed organizational characteristics and use of innovations. We examined adoption of three purposively selected innovations during the COVID-19 pandemic: personal protective equipment (PPE) reuse, high flow oxygen (HFO) to treat hypoxia, and ivermectin prescribing. For each innovation, we fit a mediation model measuring the effects of learning climate, policy dissemination, leadership support, and collaboration on innovation adoption, with flexibility in decision-making as a mediator.

Findings

PPE reuse had the most adoption (93%) compared to HFO (80%) and ivermectin (6.6%). The mediation model revealed significant mediation effects for MDs: unit increases in learning climate and collaboration resulted in 1.7% and 1.2% increases in HFO use via their effects on flexibility. Unit increases in leadership support resulted in 0.6% decreases in PPE reuse mediated through flexibility. We observed no significant effects for nurses. Direct effects of flexibility on adoption were much greater, with unit increases in flexibility significantly associated with 13.8% (MDs) and 16.8% (RNs) increases in HFO, 4.3% (MDs) and 9.1% (RNs) decreases in PPE reuse, and 3.9% (RNs) increases in ivermectin use.

Implications for D&I Research

These unexpected findings challenge the established view that organizational factors like climate and leadership support drive intervention adoption, suggesting these constructs may be less relevant during public health emergencies. Rapidly evolving crises require real-time support and decision-making; implementation science methods and frameworks give us insights into what is relevant to focus on during public health emergencies and can inform strategies to improve adoption.

Primary Funding Source: National Institutes of Health
